## Practical skills, apparatus, errors and data handling

Every board examines practical skills in the written papers and assesses a practical endorsement. This file covers planning, carrying out experiments, recording and analysing results, uncertainties and errors, and evaluation, and lists the apparatus and techniques students must use. The individual required practicals are in the topic files.

### Overview

Applies across the whole course. The practical activities for each board are listed in `map.md` with the file that covers each one.

#### Board differences

- AQA: section 5 (practical assessment, apparatus and techniques AT a to l, practical skills) and section 6 (mathematical requirements). Required practicals RP1 to RP12 are in files 2, 4, 5, 9, 10, 11, 14, 16, 21, 22, 24 and 31.
- Edexcel: practical skills and mathematical skills in the appendices; core practicals CP1 to CP16 in files 2, 4, 9, 10, 11, 14, 15, 16, 19, 21 and 24.
- OCR A and OCR B: Module 1 (1.1.1 Planning, 1.1.2 Implementing, 1.1.3 Analysis, 1.1.4 Evaluation, 1.2.1 Practical skills for the endorsement, 1.2.2 Use of apparatus and techniques). Practical activity groups PAG1 to PAG12 sit in the topic files.
- Eduqas: estimating percentage error (C1.3(l)), the specified practical work listed through the topics, and Component 3, Chemistry in practice, a practical examination.

### 1.1.1 Planning

*Refs: OCR A 1.1.1(a) to (c) · OCR B 1.1.1(a) to (c) · AQA Section 5 · Edexcel Appendix · Eduqas Component 3. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Design experiments to test a hypothesis, including choosing apparatus, techniques and quantities.
- Identify independent, dependent and control variables.
- Assess risks and plan to reduce them.

#### Explained

**Variables.** The independent variable is the one you change. The dependent variable is the one you measure. Control variables are kept constant so the test is fair.

**Choosing apparatus.** Pick apparatus with the right precision for the quantity:

| Quantity | Apparatus | Typical uncertainty |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mass | Two-decimal balance | ±0.005 g (one reading); ±0.01 g by difference |
| Volume (fixed, accurate) | Pipette (25 cm³) | ±0.06 cm³ |
| Volume (variable) | Burette | ±0.05 cm³ per reading; ±0.10 cm³ for a titre |
| Volume (approximate) | Measuring cylinder | ±0.5 cm³ (100 cm³ cylinder) |
| Volume of solution made up | Volumetric flask (250 cm³) | ±0.2 cm³ |
| Gas volume | Gas syringe | ±0.5 cm³ |
| Temperature | Thermometer | ±0.5 °C (1 °C divisions) |
| Time | Stopwatch | Limited by reaction time, about ±0.5 s |

**Risk assessment.** For each hazard, say the risk and how to reduce it: flammable organic liquids (no naked flames, use a water bath or heating mantle); corrosive acids and alkalis (goggles, gloves, dilute where possible); toxic gases (fume cupboard); harmful or irritant chemicals (gloves, avoid skin contact); hot apparatus (allow to cool, tongs).

#### Common mistakes

- Using a measuring cylinder when an accurate volume is needed.
- Writing "be careful" as a safety precaution. Name the hazard and the specific control.

### 1.1.2 Implementing

*Refs: OCR A 1.1.2(a) to (c) · OCR B 1.1.2(a) to (c) · AQA Section 5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Use apparatus and techniques correctly and safely.
- Record results in tables with headings and units, to an appropriate precision.
- Use correct units and significant figures.

#### Explained

**Results tables.** Independent variable in the first column. Column headings include the quantity and unit separated by a slash, for example "Volume of NaOH / cm³". Units go in the heading, not in each cell. Record all readings to the same precision, matching the apparatus (burette readings to 2 decimal places, ending in 0 or 5).

**Significant figures.** Give answers to the same number of significant figures as the least precise data used. Keep full values during calculation and round at the end.

**Units.** Know standard units: mol, mol dm⁻³, g mol⁻¹, kJ mol⁻¹, J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹, Pa, m³, K, s⁻¹. Convert prefixes: kilo (10³), centi (10⁻²), milli (10⁻³), micro (10⁻⁶), nano (10⁻⁹).

### 1.1.3 Analysis

*Refs: OCR A 1.1.3(a) to (d) · OCR B 1.1.3(a) to (d) · AQA Section 6 · Edexcel Appendix · Eduqas C1.3(l). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Process data: means, rates, percentage yield, gradients, intercepts.
- Plot graphs with suitable scales and lines of best fit; tangents to curves.
- Calculate uncertainties and percentage uncertainties; combine them.
- Identify anomalies.

#### Explained

**Graphs.** Independent variable on the x-axis. Use more than half the grid. Label axes with quantity and unit. Plot points accurately (small crosses). Draw a smooth line or curve of best fit with points evenly spread either side; ignore anomalies but identify them. For rates, draw a tangent and find its gradient with a large triangle.

**Uncertainty.** The uncertainty in a reading is usually half the smallest division. If two readings are needed (a titre is a final minus an initial reading, a temperature change is two readings), the uncertainties add.

Percentage uncertainty = (absolute uncertainty ÷ measured value) × 100.

For a calculated result that multiplies or divides measured quantities, add the percentage uncertainties.

**Reducing percentage uncertainty.** Measure a larger quantity: use a larger mass, a larger titre (more dilute titrant), or a bigger temperature change.

**Percentage error** compares an experimental result with an accepted value: (|experimental − accepted| ÷ accepted) × 100. If the percentage error is larger than the total percentage uncertainty, there are systematic errors as well.

#### Worked example

A titre is 23.40 cm³ (burette ±0.05 cm³ per reading). The pipetted volume is 25.0 cm³ (±0.06 cm³). Find the percentage uncertainty in each, and the total.

Titre: ±0.10 ÷ 23.40 × 100 = 0.43%. Pipette: 0.06 ÷ 25.0 × 100 = 0.24%. Total = 0.67%.

### 1.1.4 Evaluation

*Refs: OCR A 1.1.4(a) to (e) · OCR B 1.1.4(a) to (e) · AQA Section 5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Evaluate results and draw conclusions.
- Identify random and systematic errors and suggest improvements.
- Accuracy, precision, repeatability and reproducibility.

#### Explained

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Accurate | Close to the true value |
| Precise | Repeated readings close to each other (small spread) |
| Repeatable | Same person, same method and equipment, gets similar results |
| Reproducible | Different people or methods get similar results |
| Random error | Unpredictable variation (reading a scale, judging an end point); reduced by repeating and averaging |
| Systematic error | Same direction and size each time (heat loss, a zero error, an uncalibrated meter); not reduced by repeating |

**Improving an experiment.** Name the specific problem and its fix: heat loss → insulate and add a lid, use extrapolation; gas escapes before the bung is fitted → put the solid in a small tube inside the flask and tip it over after sealing; end point hard to judge → use a colorimeter or a white tile; volatile product lost → use a condenser.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying "human error". Name the error.
- Saying repeating improves accuracy. It improves precision and reduces random error.

### 1.2.1 Practical skills (practical endorsement)

*Refs: OCR A 1.2.1(a) to (j) · OCR B 1.2.1(a) to (j) · AQA Section 5 · Edexcel Appendix · Eduqas Component 3. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

The endorsement (common to all boards) is assessed against five competencies across at least 12 practical activities:

1. Follows written procedures.
2. Applies investigative approaches and methods when using instruments and equipment.
3. Safely uses a range of practical equipment and materials.
4. Makes and records observations.
5. Researches, references and reports.

Students keep a record of their practical work (a lab book). The endorsement is reported as Pass or Not classified, separately from the grade.

#### Explained

Good lab records include the date, aim, method followed (and any changes), risk assessment, raw results with units, calculations, conclusions and references to sources used (with full details for websites and books).

### 1.2.2 Use of apparatus and techniques

*Refs: OCR A 1.2.2(a) to (l) · OCR B 1.2.2(a) to (l) · AQA Section 5 (AT a to l). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

The techniques students must use, and where they appear in these notes:

| Technique | Where |
| --- | --- |
| Measuring mass, volume of solids, liquids and gases, temperature | Files 2, 4 |
| Measuring pH with a pH probe or chart | File 11 |
| Heating safely: water bath, electric heater, Bunsen | Files 4, 13, 21 |
| Volumetric flask and standard solutions | File 2 |
| Burette titrations (acid–base, redox) | Files 2, 15 |
| Distillation and heating under reflux | Files 19, 21, 24 |
| Separating funnel, drying, purifying a liquid | Files 21, 24 |
| Filtration (gravity and reduced pressure), recrystallisation, melting point | Files 24, 29 |
| Qualitative tests for ions and functional groups | Files 13, 14, 16, 22 |
| Electrochemical cells and EMF | File 10 |
| Thin-layer or paper chromatography | File 31 |
| Following a reaction: colorimetry, gas collection, continuous monitoring, initial rates | Files 5, 9 |
| Data logger or computer for collecting data | Files 9, 11 |

#### Explained

**Electrolysis (OCR B ES(c)) and other storyline techniques** are in file 36.

**Mathematical skills** used across the course: standard form, significant figures, ratios and percentages, logarithms (pH, Arrhenius), rearranging equations, plotting y = mx + c, gradients and tangents, 3D shapes (geometry of molecules), and simple probability (mass spectra of Cl₂).

### Check questions

1. A student weighs 0.250 g of solid on a balance reading to ±0.001 g, by difference. Calculate the percentage uncertainty. (2 marks)
2. A temperature rises from 21.5 °C to 27.0 °C, measured with a thermometer reading to ±0.5 °C. Calculate the percentage uncertainty in the temperature change and suggest how it could be reduced. (3 marks)
3. Explain the difference between accuracy and precision. (2 marks)
4. Identify one systematic error in measuring the enthalpy of combustion of ethanol with a spirit burner, and suggest an improvement. (2 marks)
5. Give the correct heading for a column of burette readings, and state the precision they should be recorded to. (2 marks)
6. A student's value for Ka was 1.9 × 10⁻⁵ mol dm⁻³; the accepted value is 1.74 × 10⁻⁵. The total uncertainty was 3%. Calculate the percentage error and comment. (3 marks)
7. Explain why a 250 cm³ measuring cylinder is unsuitable for making a standard solution. (1 mark)

### Mark scheme

1. Uncertainty = 2 × 0.001 = 0.002 g (1); 0.002 ÷ 0.250 × 100 = 0.8% (1).
2. ΔT = 5.5 °C, uncertainty ±1.0 °C (1); 18% (1); use more concentrated reagents / larger mass to give a larger temperature change, or a more precise thermometer (1).
3. Accuracy: closeness to the true value (1); precision: closeness of repeated values to each other (1).
4. Heat loss to surroundings / incomplete combustion (1); use a draught shield / lid / insulated can / bomb calorimeter / ensure good oxygen supply (1).
5. "Burette reading / cm³" (1); to 0.05 cm³, two decimal places (1).
6. (1.9 − 1.74) ÷ 1.74 × 100 = 9.2% (1)(1 for working); larger than the 3% uncertainty, so systematic errors present / method not accurate (1).
7. Measuring cylinder not precise enough / uncertainty too large compared with a volumetric flask (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/02-amount-of-substance.md -->

## Amount of substance, formulae, equations and titrations

The mole lets chemists count particles by weighing them. This file covers relative masses, the mole, gas volumes and the ideal gas equation, empirical and molecular formulae, balanced equations, concentration, percentage yield, atom economy and titrations. It includes AQA required practical 1.

### Overview

AS content for every board. Almost every calculation later in the course (energetics, equilibria, rates, acids) uses the ideas here. Percentage error and uncertainty are in file 32.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.2.1 to 3.1.2.5 and RP1. AQA uses the ideal gas equation with SI units and asks for atom economy and percentage yield.
- Edexcel 5.1 to 5.16 and CP1 (molar volume of a gas), CP2 (standard solution and titration), CP3 (finding the concentration of hydrochloric acid). Edexcel asks for concentrations in g dm⁻³ and ppm (5.11) and for the molar gas volume at room temperature (24.0 dm³ mol⁻¹, 5.9) as well as pV = nRT (5.10).
- OCR A 2.1.2 to 2.1.4 include naming and writing formulae of common ions (2.1.2), water of crystallisation (2.1.3(d)), sustainability and atom economy (2.1.3(j)) and the reactions of acids with bases, carbonates and metals (2.1.4).
- OCR B EL(a) to (d), EL(t), DF(a), ES(a) place the same calculations in storylines: masses and titrations in Elements of life, gas volumes in Developing fuels, and moles in Elements from the sea.
- Eduqas C1.1(a), (c), C1.3(d) to (k), C1.6(r), C2.1(d), (f) and specified practicals: gravimetric analysis, preparing a soluble salt by titration, standardising an acid, back titration and double titration. Eduqas asks for correcting gas volumes for temperature and pressure (C1.3(h)).

### 3.1.2.1 Relative atomic mass and relative molecular mass

*Refs: AQA 3.1.2.1 · Edexcel 5.3 · OCR A 2.1.1(e), 2.1.3(a) · OCR B EL(a) · Eduqas C1.3(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Aᵣ and Mᵣ are measured relative to one twelfth of the mass of a carbon-12 atom.
- Mᵣ is the sum of the Aᵣ values in the formula. For ionic compounds the term is relative formula mass.
- Molar mass, M, is the mass of one mole, in g mol⁻¹. Numerically it equals Mᵣ.

#### Explained

Relative masses have no units because they are ratios. Molar mass does have units: g mol⁻¹.

Mᵣ of CuSO₄·5H₂O = 63.5 + 32.1 + 4(16.0) + 5(18.0) = 249.6. The dot means the water is part of the crystal and is counted in the formula.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving Mᵣ units of g. Mᵣ has no units; molar mass is in g mol⁻¹.
- Forgetting the water of crystallisation in a hydrated salt.

### 3.1.2.2 The mole and the Avogadro constant

*Refs: AQA 3.1.2.2 · Edexcel 5.1 to 5.3, 5.7, 5.11 · OCR A 2.1.3(a), (e) · OCR B EL(b), EL(c) · Eduqas C1.3(e) to (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The Avogadro constant, L or Nₐ, is 6.022 × 10²³ mol⁻¹: the number of particles in one mole.
- The mole is the unit of amount of substance.
- n = m ÷ M, and number of particles = n × L.
- For solutions, n = c × V, with V in dm³ and c in mol dm⁻³.

#### Explained

A mole of any substance contains the same number of particles. One mole of carbon-12 atoms has a mass of exactly 12 g. (Since 2019 the mole has been defined by fixing the value of the Avogadro constant; either definition gives the same numbers.)

Three key equations:

| To find | Use | Units |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Moles from mass | n = m ÷ M | m in g, M in g mol⁻¹ |
| Moles in solution | n = c × V | c in mol dm⁻³, V in dm³ (cm³ ÷ 1000) |
| Particles | N = n × L | L = 6.02 × 10²³ mol⁻¹ |

**Concentration in g dm⁻³** = mol dm⁻³ × M.

**Parts per million (Edexcel).** ppm by mass = (mass of solute ÷ mass of solution) × 10⁶. In dilute water solutions 1 ppm is about 1 mg dm⁻³.

#### Worked example

1. How many water molecules are in 9.0 g of water?

   n = 9.0 ÷ 18.0 = 0.50 mol. N = 0.50 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 3.0 × 10²³.

2. How many ions in total are in 0.20 mol of CaCl₂?

   Each formula unit gives three ions. 0.20 × 3 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 3.6 × 10²³ ions.

3. What mass of NaOH is needed to make 250 cm³ of 0.100 mol dm⁻³ solution?

   n = 0.100 × 0.250 = 0.0250 mol. m = 0.0250 × 40.0 = 1.00 g.

#### Common mistakes

- Using cm³ in n = cV. Divide by 1000 first.
- Counting particles in a compound as molecules when the question asks for atoms or ions.

### 3.1.2.3 The ideal gas equation

*Refs: AQA 3.1.2.3 · Edexcel 5.9, 5.10, CP1 · OCR A 2.1.3(a)(v), (e), (f) · OCR B DF(a) · Eduqas C1.3(h), (i). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- pV = nRT, with p in Pa, V in m³, T in K, R = 8.31 J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹.
- Molar gas volume: one mole of any gas at room temperature and pressure occupies about 24.0 dm³ (Edexcel, OCR, Eduqas).
- Volumes of reacting gases are in the same ratio as their moles (Avogadro's law).

#### Explained

Unit conversions are where marks go:

| Quantity | Convert to SI |
| --- | --- |
| Pressure | kPa × 1000 = Pa |
| Volume | cm³ × 10⁻⁶ = m³; dm³ × 10⁻³ = m³ |
| Temperature | °C + 273 = K |

The ideal gas equation assumes molecules have no volume and no forces between them. Real gases behave nearly ideally at high temperature and low pressure.

**Correcting a volume (Eduqas).** Rearranging for a fixed amount of gas: p₁V₁/T₁ = p₂V₂/T₂.

**Molar volume method (Edexcel CP1).** Gas volume = n × 24.0 dm³ at room temperature and pressure. To measure it, react a known mass of a metal such as magnesium (or a carbonate) with excess acid and collect the gas in a gas syringe or an upturned burette over water. Divide the volume collected by the moles of gas formed.

#### Worked example

1. What volume does 0.350 g of nitrogen gas occupy at 25 °C and 101 kPa?

   n = 0.350 ÷ 28.0 = 0.0125 mol. V = nRT ÷ p = (0.0125 × 8.31 × 298) ÷ 101 000 = 3.06 × 10⁻⁴ m³ = 306 cm³.

2. A gas has mass 0.482 g and occupies 150 cm³ at 100 kPa and 300 K. Find Mᵣ.

   n = pV ÷ RT = (100 000 × 150 × 10⁻⁶) ÷ (8.31 × 300) = 6.02 × 10⁻³ mol. M = 0.482 ÷ 6.02 × 10⁻³ = 80.1 g mol⁻¹.

3. 50 cm³ of propane burns completely: C₃H₈ + 5O₂ → 3CO₂ + 4H₂O. What volume of oxygen is used, and what volume of CO₂ forms (same conditions)?

   O₂: 5 × 50 = 250 cm³. CO₂: 3 × 50 = 150 cm³.

#### Common mistakes

- Leaving temperature in °C or pressure in kPa.
- Converting cm³ to m³ by dividing by 1000 (that gives dm³). Divide by 10⁶.
- Using 24 dm³ mol⁻¹ for a liquid or solid product (water at room temperature is a liquid).

### 3.1.2.4 Empirical and molecular formula

*Refs: AQA 3.1.2.4 · Edexcel 5.4, 5.5 · OCR A 2.1.3(b), (c), (d) · OCR B EL(b) · Eduqas C1.3(d). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Empirical formula: the simplest whole-number ratio of atoms of each element in a compound.
- Molecular formula: the actual number of atoms of each element in a molecule.
- Calculate empirical formula from masses or percentages, including combustion data, and the molecular formula from the empirical formula and Mᵣ.
- Anhydrous, hydrated and water of crystallisation; find the formula of a hydrated salt from heating data (OCR A, OCR B).

#### Explained

Method for empirical formula:

1. Write mass (or %) of each element.
2. Divide by Aᵣ to get moles.
3. Divide all by the smallest.
4. If not whole numbers, multiply up (×2 for 0.5, ×3 for 0.33 or 0.67, ×4 for 0.25).

Molecular formula = empirical formula × (Mᵣ ÷ empirical formula mass).

**Water of crystallisation.** Heat a weighed hydrated salt in a crucible to constant mass (heat, cool, weigh, repeat until two readings agree). The mass lost is water. Moles of water ÷ moles of anhydrous salt gives x in MX·xH₂O.

#### Worked example

1. A hydrocarbon is 85.7% carbon. Its Mᵣ is 56. Find its molecular formula.

   C: 85.7 ÷ 12.0 = 7.14. H: 14.3 ÷ 1.0 = 14.3. Ratio 1 : 2, so CH₂ (mass 14). 56 ÷ 14 = 4, so C₄H₈.

2. 0.600 g of an organic compound containing C, H and O burns to give 1.32 g CO₂ and 0.720 g H₂O. Find its empirical formula.

   C: 1.32 ÷ 44.0 = 0.0300 mol C = 0.360 g. H: 2 × (0.720 ÷ 18.0) = 0.0800 mol H = 0.0800 g. O: 0.600 − 0.360 − 0.080 = 0.160 g = 0.0100 mol. Ratio C : H : O = 3 : 8 : 1, so C₃H₈O.

3. 2.50 g of hydrated MgSO₄·xH₂O is heated to constant mass, leaving 1.22 g of anhydrous MgSO₄. Find x.

   Water = 1.28 g = 0.0711 mol. MgSO₄ = 1.22 ÷ 120.4 = 0.01013 mol. x = 0.0711 ÷ 0.01013 = 7.0, so MgSO₄·7H₂O.

#### Common mistakes

- Rounding 1.5 to 2 instead of doubling all values.
- Using the mass of H₂O instead of the mass of H when working from combustion data, or forgetting there are two H in each H₂O.
- Not heating to constant mass, so some water remains and x comes out too low.

### 3.1.2.5 Balanced equations and associated calculations

*Refs: AQA 3.1.2.5, RP1 · Edexcel 5.6 to 5.8, 5.11 to 5.15, CP2, CP3 · OCR A 2.1.2(a), (b), 2.1.3(e), (g) to (j), 2.1.4(a) to (e) · OCR B EL(c), EL(d), EL(t), ES(a) · Eduqas C1.1(a), (c), C1.3(j), (k), C1.6(r), C2.1(d), (f). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Write balanced full and ionic equations with state symbols.
- Use balanced equations to find reacting masses, volumes of gases and solution concentrations.
- Percentage yield and percentage atom economy, and why high atom economy matters for sustainability and cost.
- Titration calculations, including making a standard solution.
- Formulae of common acids and their ions; neutralisation of acids by bases, alkalis and carbonates, and reactions of acids with metals (OCR A 2.1.4).
- Eduqas: preparing salts, back titrations, double titrations and gravimetric analysis.

#### Explained

**Formulae of ions to learn.**

| Positive | Negative |
| --- | --- |
| NH₄⁺ ammonium | OH⁻ hydroxide |
| H⁺ hydrogen | NO₃⁻ nitrate |
| Zn²⁺, Ag⁺ | CO₃²⁻ carbonate, HCO₃⁻ hydrogencarbonate |
| | SO₄²⁻ sulfate, SO₃²⁻ sulfite |
| | PO₄³⁻ phosphate |

Common acids: HCl, H₂SO₄, HNO₃, CH₃COOH. Common alkalis: NaOH, KOH, NH₃(aq).

**Ionic equations** show only the species that change. Spectator ions are left out. For any strong acid with any strong alkali: H⁺(aq) + OH⁻(aq) → H₂O(l). For silver nitrate with sodium chloride: Ag⁺(aq) + Cl⁻(aq) → AgCl(s).

**Reactions of acids (OCR A 2.1.4, Eduqas C2.1(d)).**

- Acid + metal hydroxide or oxide → salt + water.
- Acid + carbonate → salt + water + carbon dioxide. Ionic: 2H⁺ + CO₃²⁻ → H₂O + CO₂.
- Acid + reactive metal → salt + hydrogen. This is also a redox reaction (file 7).
- Acid + ammonia → ammonium salt: NH₃ + HCl → NH₄Cl.

An acid releases H⁺ ions in water; a base accepts H⁺; an alkali is a soluble base that releases OH⁻ ions. Strong acids fully dissociate in water; weak acids only partly dissociate (file 11).

**Percentage yield** = (actual mass of product ÷ theoretical mass) × 100. Yields are below 100% because of incomplete reactions, side reactions and losses on transfer and purification.

**Atom economy** = (Mᵣ of desired product ÷ total Mᵣ of all reactants) × 100. Addition reactions have 100% atom economy. A high atom economy means less waste, fewer raw materials used, and lower cost of disposal. It is a theoretical figure from the equation, and is not the same as yield.

**Titration (RP1, Edexcel CP2 and CP3, OCR A 2.1.4(d), Eduqas).**

*Making a standard solution:*

1. Weigh the solid accurately in a weighing boat on a two-decimal (or better) balance. Reweigh the boat after tipping out the solid so the mass transferred is known (weighing by difference).
2. Dissolve in a beaker in less distilled water than the final volume. Stir.
3. Transfer to a volumetric flask with a funnel, rinsing the beaker, rod and funnel into the flask.
4. Make up to the mark with distilled water so the bottom of the meniscus sits on the line at eye level.
5. Stopper and invert several times to mix.

*Doing the titration:*

1. Rinse the pipette with the solution it will deliver, and the burette with its solution. Rinse the conical flask with distilled water only.
2. Pipette 25.0 cm³ of one solution into the flask. Add 2 to 3 drops of indicator.
3. Fill the burette (below eye level, funnel removed, tap filled with no air bubble). Read the initial volume to 0.05 cm³.
4. Do a rough titration, then accurate ones, adding dropwise near the end point and swirling. Wash the sides with distilled water (this does not change the moles).
5. Repeat until two titres are concordant (within 0.10 cm³). Average the concordant titres.

Indicators: methyl orange (red in acid, yellow in alkali; end point orange) or phenolphthalein (colourless in acid, pink in alkali). Do not use universal indicator; its colour change is gradual.

**Back titration (Eduqas).** Used when a solid reacts slowly or is insoluble, such as calcium carbonate in limestone. Add a known excess of acid, then titrate the leftover acid with alkali. Acid used by the solid = acid added − acid left.

**Double titration (Eduqas).** For a mixture of NaOH and Na₂CO₃ titrated with HCl: phenolphthalein loses its colour when all the NaOH is neutralised and the carbonate has become hydrogencarbonate (CO₃²⁻ + H⁺ → HCO₃⁻). Methyl orange is then added; the second titre turns HCO₃⁻ into CO₂ and water. From the two titres the amounts of each base can be found.

**Gravimetric analysis (Eduqas).** Precipitate an ion as an insoluble compound (for example sulfate as BaSO₄ by adding barium chloride), filter, wash, dry to constant mass and weigh. The mass of precipitate gives the moles of the ion.

**Preparing a salt (Eduqas C1.6(r)).** Soluble salt from an alkali: titrate to find the exact volume, then repeat without indicator, evaporate to the point of crystallisation and leave to crystallise. Soluble salt from an insoluble base or carbonate: add excess solid to acid, filter off the excess, crystallise. Insoluble salt: mix two solutions, filter, wash, dry.

#### Worked example

1. 25.0 cm³ of sodium hydroxide is neutralised by 22.40 cm³ of 0.100 mol dm⁻³ sulfuric acid. Find the concentration of NaOH.

   H₂SO₄ + 2NaOH → Na₂SO₄ + 2H₂O. n(H₂SO₄) = 0.100 × 0.02240 = 2.24 × 10⁻³ mol. n(NaOH) = 4.48 × 10⁻³ mol. c = 4.48 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.0250 = 0.179 mol dm⁻³.

2. Find the atom economy for making ethanol by fermentation: C₆H₁₂O₆ → 2C₂H₅OH + 2CO₂.

   Atom economy = (2 × 46.0) ÷ 180.0 × 100 = 51.1%.

3. 5.00 g of impure limestone is added to 100 cm³ of 1.00 mol dm⁻³ HCl (excess). The solution is made up to 250 cm³. 25.0 cm³ portions need 14.50 cm³ of 0.200 mol dm⁻³ NaOH. Find the percentage of CaCO₃.

   HCl left in 25.0 cm³ = 0.200 × 0.01450 = 2.90 × 10⁻³ mol, so in 250 cm³ = 0.0290 mol. HCl added = 0.100 mol. HCl used = 0.0710 mol. CaCO₃ + 2HCl, so n(CaCO₃) = 0.0355 mol, mass = 3.55 g. Percentage = 3.55 ÷ 5.00 × 100 = 71.0%.

4. 4.80 g of magnesium reacts with excess HCl. Find the volume of hydrogen at room temperature and pressure.

   Mg + 2HCl → MgCl₂ + H₂. n(Mg) = 4.80 ÷ 24.3 = 0.198 mol = n(H₂). V = 0.198 × 24.0 = 4.74 dm³.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting the mole ratio from the equation.
- Using non-concordant titres in the mean.
- Rinsing the conical flask with the solution it will hold (this adds extra moles).
- Mixing up percentage yield (practical) and atom economy (from the equation).
- Writing spectator ions in an ionic equation.

#### Practical

**AQA RP1: make up a volumetric solution and carry out a simple acid–base titration.** Method as above. Typical errors: balance ±0.005 g, pipette ±0.06 cm³, burette ±0.05 cm³ per reading (so ±0.10 cm³ for a titre), volumetric flask ±0.2 cm³ for 250 cm³. Percentage uncertainty = (uncertainty ÷ measured value) × 100 (file 32). To reduce the percentage uncertainty in the titre, use a more dilute titrant so the titre is larger.

**Edexcel CP1, CP2, CP3; OCR A PAG1, PAG2; Eduqas standardisation, back titration, double titration, gravimetric analysis, salt preparation.** Covered in the sections above.

### Check questions

1. Calculate the number of oxygen atoms in 4.40 g of carbon dioxide. (2 marks)
2. A gas sample of mass 0.320 g occupies 240 cm³ at 298 K and 1.01 × 10⁵ Pa. Calculate its molar mass. (3 marks)
3. A compound contains 40.0% carbon, 6.7% hydrogen and 53.3% oxygen by mass. Its Mᵣ is 180. Find the empirical and molecular formulae. (3 marks)
4. 1.50 g of hydrated sodium carbonate Na₂CO₃·xH₂O is heated to constant mass, leaving 0.556 g. Find x. (3 marks)
5. 25.0 cm³ of 0.0500 mol dm⁻³ Ca(OH)₂ is neutralised by hydrochloric acid. 18.60 cm³ of acid is needed. Find the acid concentration. (3 marks)
6. Ethanol can be made from ethene and steam, or by fermentation. Explain why the first has the higher atom economy. (2 marks)
7. Describe how to make 250 cm³ of a standard solution of a solid acid. (4 marks)
8. Write the ionic equation, with state symbols, for the reaction of a carbonate with an acid. (1 mark)
9. A student used 2.00 g of magnesium carbonate to make magnesium sulfate. The theoretical yield was 2.86 g and the student obtained 2.12 g. Calculate the percentage yield and give one reason it is below 100%. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. n(CO₂) = 4.40 ÷ 44.0 = 0.100 mol (1); O atoms = 0.200 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.20 × 10²³ (1).
2. n = pV ÷ RT = (1.01 × 10⁵ × 240 × 10⁻⁶) ÷ (8.31 × 298) (1) = 9.79 × 10⁻³ mol (1); M = 32.7 g mol⁻¹ (1).
3. Mole ratio C 3.33 : H 6.7 : O 3.33 = 1 : 2 : 1 (1); CH₂O (1); C₆H₁₂O₆ (1).
4. Water lost = 0.944 g = 0.0524 mol (1); n(Na₂CO₃) = 0.556 ÷ 106 = 5.25 × 10⁻³ mol (1); x = 10 (1).
5. n(Ca(OH)₂) = 1.25 × 10⁻³ mol (1); n(HCl) = 2.50 × 10⁻³ mol (1); c = 0.134 mol dm⁻³ (1).
6. Ethene + steam is an addition reaction with one product, atom economy 100% (1); fermentation also gives CO₂, which is a waste product (1).
7. Weigh solid accurately / by difference (1); dissolve in a beaker in a smaller volume of distilled water (1); transfer with rinsings to a 250 cm³ volumetric flask (1); make up to the mark, bottom of meniscus at eye level, then invert to mix (1).
8. 2H⁺(aq) + CO₃²⁻(s or aq) → H₂O(l) + CO₂(g) (1).
9. 74.1% (1); any one: losses on filtering / transfer; incomplete reaction; product left in solution when crystallising (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/01-atomic-structure.md -->

## Atomic structure, isotopes, mass spectrometry and electron configuration

Everything in chemistry comes back to electrons: how many an atom has, where they sit, and how hard they are to remove. This file covers the particles in an atom, isotopes, the time of flight mass spectrometer, relative masses, electron configurations and ionisation energies.

### Overview

AS content for all boards. Ionisation energy trends across Period 3 and down groups appear again in file 12 (periodicity) and file 13 (Group 2). Mass spectrometry of organic molecules is in file 22. Atomic emission spectra and radioactivity are in file 33.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.1.1 to 3.1.1.3 name the time of flight (TOF) mass spectrometer and expect the four stages: ionisation, acceleration, ion drift, detection. They also expect the two ionisation methods: electron impact and electrospray.
- Edexcel 1.1 to 1.23 do not require the workings of the spectrometer but do ask for predicting the mass spectrum of Cl₂ including relative peak heights (1.9), and how emission spectra and ionisation energy data give evidence for shells and sub-shells (1.15).
- OCR A 2.1.1 and 2.2.1 ask for relative isotopic mass from mass spectra and electrons-in-boxes. OCR A puts the ionisation energy trend in 3.1.1(c).
- OCR B EL(a), (e), (f), (g), (q), (x) cover the same ideas in the Elements of life storyline. OCR B calls ionisation energy "ionisation enthalpy".
- Eduqas C1.2(e) to (g) and C1.3(a) to (c) cover ionisation energies, orbitals and mass spectra including chlorine gas. Eduqas also asks about radioactivity and atomic spectra in C1.2 (file 33).

### 3.1.1.1 Fundamental particles

*Refs: AQA 3.1.1.1 · Edexcel 1.1 to 1.4 · OCR A 2.1.1(b) · OCR B EL(a), EL(g) · Eduqas C1.3(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- An atom has a small, dense nucleus of protons and neutrons, surrounded by electrons in shells (energy levels).
- Relative charge and relative mass of the three particles.
- Atomic number Z = number of protons. Mass number A = protons + neutrons.
- How to work out the numbers of each particle in atoms and ions.
- The model of the atom has changed as new evidence appeared.

#### Explained

| Particle | Relative charge | Relative mass | Where |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Proton | +1 | 1 | Nucleus |
| Neutron | 0 | 1 | Nucleus |
| Electron | −1 | 1/1840 (about 0.0005) | Shells around the nucleus |

Almost all the mass is in the nucleus, but almost all the volume is the space where the electrons are. A neutral atom has equal numbers of protons and electrons. An ion has gained or lost electrons; the number of protons never changes in a chemical reaction.

**Notation.** In ²³Na written with 23 at the top left and 11 at the bottom left, 23 is A and 11 is Z. Neutrons = A − Z = 12.

**For ions**, adjust only the electrons. ³²S²⁻ has 16 protons, 16 neutrons and 18 electrons. ²⁷Al³⁺ has 13 protons, 14 neutrons and 10 electrons.

**How the model developed.** Dalton pictured atoms as solid indivisible spheres. Thomson found the electron and proposed a "plum pudding" of negative electrons in a spread-out positive charge. Rutherford's gold foil experiment showed most alpha particles passed straight through but a few bounced back, so the positive charge and the mass must be in a tiny nucleus. Bohr put the electrons in fixed energy levels to explain line spectra. Chadwick later found the neutron. Each model was replaced because it could not explain new evidence. That is how science works: models are kept while they explain the data.

#### Worked example

How many protons, neutrons and electrons are in ⁵⁶Fe³⁺ (Z = 26)?

- Protons = Z = 26.
- Neutrons = A − Z = 56 − 26 = 30.
- Electrons = 26 − 3 = 23 (a 3+ ion has lost three).

#### Common mistakes

- Changing the number of protons when forming an ion. Only electrons change.
- Giving the electron a relative mass of 0. It is small (about 1/1840), not zero.
- Adding electrons for a positive ion. Positive means electrons were lost.

### 3.1.1.2 Mass number and isotopes

*Refs: AQA 3.1.1.2 · Edexcel 1.5 to 1.10 · OCR A 2.1.1(a), (c), (d), (e) · OCR B EL(a), EL(x) · Eduqas C1.3(a) to (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Isotopes are atoms of the same element with the same number of protons but different numbers of neutrons.
- Isotopes have the same chemical properties (same electron configuration) but slightly different physical properties (mass, density, rate of diffusion).
- Relative isotopic mass and relative atomic mass are measured on the carbon-12 scale.
- AQA: the principles of a simple time of flight (TOF) mass spectrometer, limited to ionisation, acceleration to give all ions constant kinetic energy, ion drift, ion detection, and data analysis.
- Interpret simple mass spectra of elements; calculate relative atomic mass from isotopic abundance (and the reverse).
- Use mass spectrometry to identify elements, and to find the relative molecular mass from the molecular ion peak.

#### Explained

**Definitions.**

- Relative isotopic mass: the mass of an atom of an isotope compared with one twelfth of the mass of an atom of carbon-12.
- Relative atomic mass, Aᵣ: the weighted mean mass of an atom of an element compared with one twelfth of the mass of an atom of carbon-12.

The weighting is by abundance. That is why Aᵣ values are rarely whole numbers.

**The time of flight mass spectrometer (AQA).**

1. **Ionisation.** The sample is turned into positive ions. Two methods:
   - *Electron impact*: the sample is vaporised and high-energy electrons from an electron gun knock an electron off each particle: X(g) + e⁻ → X⁺(g) + 2e⁻. Used for elements and small molecules with low Mᵣ. It often breaks molecules into fragments.
   - *Electrospray ionisation*: the sample is dissolved in a volatile solvent and pushed through a fine, high-voltage needle. Each molecule gains a proton: X(g) + H⁺ → XH⁺(g). Used for large molecules such as proteins, because it causes little fragmentation. The peak you see is at Mᵣ + 1.
2. **Acceleration.** An electric field gives all the ions the same kinetic energy.
3. **Ion drift.** The ions travel through a flight tube with no field. Since KE = ½mv², lighter ions move faster and arrive first.
4. **Detection.** Ions hit a negatively charged plate and gain electrons. This makes a current proportional to the number of ions hitting it. The time of flight gives m/z; the current gives abundance.
5. **Data analysis.** A computer plots relative abundance against m/z.

Time of flight comes from KE = ½mv² and v = d/t, so t = d√(m/2KE). Heavier ions take longer.

**Why ions?** Only charged particles are accelerated by the field and only charged particles make a current at the detector.

**Diatomic molecules (Edexcel, Eduqas).** Chlorine has two isotopes, ³⁵Cl (about 75%) and ³⁷Cl (about 25%). Cl₂ gives three molecular ion peaks:

| Molecule | m/z | Probability | Ratio |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ³⁵Cl–³⁵Cl | 70 | 0.75 × 0.75 = 0.5625 | 9 |
| ³⁵Cl–³⁷Cl (either order) | 72 | 2 × 0.75 × 0.25 = 0.375 | 6 |
| ³⁷Cl–³⁷Cl | 74 | 0.25 × 0.25 = 0.0625 | 1 |

There are also peaks at 35 and 37 (ratio 3 : 1) from Cl⁺ ions formed when the molecule splits.

**Relative molecular mass from a mass spectrum.** For a molecular substance, the peak with the highest m/z (ignoring small isotope peaks) is the molecular ion, M⁺. Its m/z equals Mᵣ, because the charge is +1.

**Relative molecular mass and relative formula mass.** Mᵣ is the sum of Aᵣ values for a simple molecule. For giant ionic compounds such as NaCl, use "relative formula mass" because there are no molecules.

#### Worked example

1. A sample of boron gives peaks at m/z 10 (relative abundance 19.9) and m/z 11 (80.1). Find Aᵣ.

   Aᵣ = (10 × 19.9 + 11 × 80.1) ÷ (19.9 + 80.1) = (199 + 881.1) ÷ 100 = 10.8.

2. Copper has Aᵣ = 63.55 and two isotopes, ⁶³Cu and ⁶⁵Cu. Find the percentage of ⁶³Cu.

   Let x = % of ⁶³Cu. 63x + 65(100 − x) = 6355. 6500 − 2x = 6355, so x = 72.5%.

3. In a TOF spectrometer, a ²⁴Mg⁺ ion takes 1.20 × 10⁻⁵ s to cross the flight tube. How long does a ²⁶Mg⁺ ion take, given both have the same kinetic energy?

   t ∝ √m, so t = 1.20 × 10⁻⁵ × √(26 / 24) = 1.25 × 10⁻⁵ s.

#### Common mistakes

- Defining relative atomic mass without "weighted mean" or without "one twelfth of carbon-12".
- Saying isotopes have different chemical properties. Same electron configuration, same reactions.
- Saying ions are deflected by a magnetic field in the TOF spectrometer. In the TOF design they are separated by time, not deflection.
- Forgetting that electrospray gives MH⁺, so the peak is at Mᵣ + 1.
- Missing the factor of 2 for the mixed ³⁵Cl–³⁷Cl molecule.

### 3.1.1.3 Electron configuration

*Refs: AQA 3.1.1.3 · Edexcel 1.11 to 1.23 · OCR A 2.2.1(a) to (d), 3.1.1(c) · OCR B EL(e), EL(f), EL(q) · Eduqas C1.2(e) to (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Electron configurations of atoms and ions up to Z = 36 in terms of shells and sub-shells (orbitals) s, p and d.
- Define first ionisation energy; write equations for first and successive ionisation energies.
- How first and successive ionisation energies give evidence for electron configuration and the group of an element.
- Trends in first ionisation energy across Period 3 and down Group 2 (AQA), across Periods 2 and 3 and down a group (other boards).
- Shapes of s and p orbitals; orbitals hold up to two electrons with opposite spins; electrons fill singly before pairing.

#### Explained

**Shells, sub-shells and orbitals.**

| Shell n | Sub-shells | Orbitals | Max electrons |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1s | 1 | 2 |
| 2 | 2s, 2p | 1 + 3 | 8 |
| 3 | 3s, 3p, 3d | 1 + 3 + 5 | 18 |
| 4 | 4s, 4p, 4d, 4f | 1 + 3 + 5 + 7 | 32 |

An orbital is a region around the nucleus that can hold up to two electrons with opposite spins. An s orbital is spherical. A p orbital is dumbbell shaped; there are three at right angles (pₓ, p_y, p_z). A d sub-shell has five orbitals.

**Filling order.** 1s, 2s, 2p, 3s, 3p, 4s, 3d, 4p. The 4s fills before 3d because it is lower in energy when empty. In a sub-shell, electrons occupy orbitals singly with parallel spins before pairing (pairing costs energy because paired electrons repel).

Examples:

- Na (11): 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s¹, or [Ne] 3s¹.
- Fe (26): 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 3d⁶ 4s².
- **Chromium and copper are exceptions.** Cr: [Ar] 3d⁵ 4s¹. Cu: [Ar] 3d¹⁰ 4s¹. A half-full or full 3d sub-shell is more stable.
- **Transition metal ions lose 4s first.** Fe²⁺ is [Ar] 3d⁶, not [Ar] 3d⁴ 4s².

**Electrons in boxes.** Draw a box per orbital and an arrow per electron, arrows in a pair pointing opposite ways. Nitrogen 2p is three boxes each with one up arrow.

**Blocks.** The sub-shell holding the highest-energy electron gives the block: s block (Groups 1, 2), p block (Groups 3 to 0), d block (transition metals). The configuration decides the chemistry, which is why elements in a group behave alike.

**Ionisation energy.**

First ionisation energy: the energy needed to remove one electron from each atom in one mole of gaseous atoms to form one mole of gaseous 1+ ions.

Na(g) → Na⁺(g) + e⁻

Second ionisation energy: Na⁺(g) → Na²⁺(g) + e⁻. Always include (g).

Three things decide ionisation energy:

1. **Nuclear charge.** More protons, stronger attraction.
2. **Distance from nucleus (atomic radius).** Further out, weaker attraction.
3. **Shielding.** Inner electrons repel the outer electron and cut the attraction it feels.

**Successive ionisation energies** always increase, because each electron is removed from an increasingly positive ion, and the remaining electrons are pulled closer. A big jump shows the next electron comes from a shell nearer the nucleus. Count the electrons before the first big jump to find the group.

For aluminium the first three are fairly close; there is a huge jump between the 3rd and 4th. So Al is in Group 3.

**Trend down a group.** First ionisation energy decreases. The outer electron is in a shell further from the nucleus and more shielded. The extra protons are outweighed.

**Trend across a period.** First ionisation energy generally increases. Nuclear charge rises, the electron is in the same shell with similar shielding, so attraction increases and atomic radius falls.

**The two dips across Period 3 (and Period 2):**

- **Mg to Al (Be to B):** the drop happens because Al's outer electron is in a 3p orbital, higher in energy and slightly further out than 3s, and partly shielded by the 3s electrons. This is evidence for sub-shells.
- **P to S (N to O):** sulfur's fourth 3p electron goes into an orbital that already holds one. The two electrons in the same orbital repel, so one is easier to remove. This is evidence for electrons pairing in orbitals.

Helium has the highest first ionisation energy of all; noble gases are at the peaks.

**Evidence for shells and sub-shells (Edexcel 1.15, OCR B).** Successive ionisation energies on a log scale fall into groups (2, 8, 8, 2 for calcium), which shows shells. The pattern of first ionisation energies across a period, with the dips above, shows sub-shells. Line emission spectra (file 33) show electrons occupy fixed energy levels.

#### Worked example

1. Write the configuration of Mn²⁺ (Mn is Z = 25).

   Mn: [Ar] 3d⁵ 4s². Remove the two 4s electrons: Mn²⁺ is [Ar] 3d⁵.

2. Successive ionisation energies (kJ mol⁻¹) of element X: 738, 1451, 7733, 10 541, 13 630. Which group?

   The jump is between the 2nd (1451) and 3rd (7733). Two electrons in the outer shell, so Group 2 (X is magnesium).

3. Write the equation for the third ionisation energy of aluminium.

   Al²⁺(g) → Al³⁺(g) + e⁻

#### Common mistakes

- Writing 4s electrons after 3d is full for every element. Remember Cr and Cu.
- Removing 3d electrons before 4s when forming transition metal ions.
- Leaving out (g) or writing X(g) + e⁻ → X⁻(g) (that is electron affinity, not ionisation).
- Explaining the Mg/Al drop by "full sub-shell is stable". Say the 3p electron is higher in energy and shielded by 3s.
- Explaining the P/S drop by "half-full sub-shell is stable". Say the paired electrons repel.
- Saying shielding increases across a period. It stays about the same: the new electrons go into the same shell.

### Check questions

1. State the relative mass and relative charge of a neutron and an electron. (2 marks)
2. Give the numbers of protons, neutrons and electrons in ⁸⁰Br⁻ (Z = 35). (2 marks)
3. Define relative atomic mass. (2 marks)
4. Gallium has two isotopes. ⁶⁹Ga has abundance 60.1% and ⁷¹Ga has abundance 39.9%. Calculate the relative atomic mass of gallium to one decimal place. (2 marks)
5. Describe how a time of flight mass spectrometer separates ions and measures their abundance. (4 marks)
6. Explain why electrospray ionisation is used for a protein and why the main peak is at m/z = Mᵣ + 1. (2 marks)
7. Bromine has two isotopes, ⁷⁹Br and ⁸¹Br, in roughly equal amounts. Predict the m/z values and relative heights of the molecular ion peaks for Br₂. (3 marks)
8. Give the full electron configuration of Cu and of Cu²⁺ (Z = 29). (2 marks)
9. Explain why the first ionisation energy of sulfur is lower than that of phosphorus. (2 marks)
10. Explain the general increase in first ionisation energy across Period 3. (3 marks)
11. The first five successive ionisation energies of element Y in kJ mol⁻¹ are 578, 1817, 2745, 11 577, 14 842. Deduce the group of Y and explain your answer. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Neutron: mass 1, charge 0 (1). Electron: mass 1/1840 (accept negligible / 0.0005), charge −1 (1).
2. 35 protons, 45 neutrons (1); 36 electrons (1).
3. Weighted mean (average) mass of an atom of the element (1); compared with one twelfth of the mass of an atom of carbon-12 (1).
4. (69 × 60.1 + 71 × 39.9) ÷ 100 (1) = 69.8 (1).
5. Ions accelerated by an electric field so all have the same kinetic energy (1); lighter ions travel faster / reach detector first (1); ions hit detector and gain electrons, producing a current (1); size of current proportional to abundance (1).
6. Little or no fragmentation of large molecules (1); molecule gains a proton / H⁺, so ion is MH⁺ (1).
7. Peaks at 158, 160, 162 (1); ratio 1 : 2 : 1 (2 marks: 1 for correct masses, 1 for ratio with reason, e.g. two ways of making ⁷⁹Br–⁸¹Br).
8. Cu: 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 3d¹⁰ 4s¹ (1). Cu²⁺: 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 3d⁹ (1).
9. In S, the electron removed is from a 3p orbital that contains two electrons (paired) (1); repulsion between paired electrons makes it easier to remove (1).
10. Nuclear charge increases / more protons (1); electrons added to the same shell, so similar shielding (1); stronger attraction / smaller atomic radius, so more energy needed (1).
11. Group 3 (1); large jump between 3rd and 4th ionisation energies, so three electrons in outer shell (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/33-atomic-spectra-radiation-and-nuclear-chemistry.md -->

## Atomic spectra, the electromagnetic spectrum, radioactivity and fusion

This file covers content that only Eduqas and OCR B examine: the electromagnetic spectrum and E = hf, atomic emission and absorption spectra, the hydrogen spectrum and its convergence limit, radiation promoting electrons and breaking bonds, radioactive decay and half-life, uses and dangers of radioisotopes, and nuclear fusion as the origin of the elements.

### Overview

AS content for Eduqas and OCR B. It extends atomic structure (file 1). Edexcel 1.15 mentions emission spectra as evidence for shells; that idea is explained here too. Flame tests are in file 13.

#### Board differences

- Eduqas C1.2(a) to (d): radioactive decay including positron emission and electron capture; α, β and γ in fields and their penetration; half-life; effects on living cells and uses of radioisotopes. C1.2(h) to (l): emission and absorption spectra; the hydrogen spectrum; E = hf and c = fλ; order of IR, visible and UV; the Lyman series convergence limit and the ionisation energy of hydrogen.
- OCR B EL(h): fusion reactions and how elements formed, with nuclear equations. EL(v), (w): the electromagnetic spectrum in order of frequency, energy and wavelength; absorption and emission spectra and their similarities and differences. OZ(t), (u): UV and visible radiation promoting electrons and breaking bonds; calculations of frequency, wavelength and energy.

### C1.2 Radioactivity and nuclear changes

*Refs: Eduqas C1.2(a) to (d) · OCR B EL(h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Types of radioactive decay and the changes in atomic number and mass number: α, β⁻, β⁺ (positron emission), electron capture, γ.
- Behaviour of α, β and γ in electric and magnetic fields; penetrating power.
- Half-life, and simple calculations.
- Harmful effects of radiation and uses of radioisotopes.
- Fusion: light nuclei join to form heavier nuclei at very high temperature and pressure; this formed the elements in stars.

#### Explained

**Types of decay.**

| Decay | Particle | Change in nucleus | Change in A | Change in Z |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Alpha (α) | Helium nucleus, ⁴₂He | Loses 2 protons and 2 neutrons | −4 | −2 |
| Beta minus (β⁻) | Electron from the nucleus | A neutron becomes a proton | 0 | +1 |
| Positron (β⁺) | Positron (e⁺) | A proton becomes a neutron | 0 | −1 |
| Electron capture | Inner electron captured | A proton becomes a neutron | 0 | −1 |
| Gamma (γ) | High-energy electromagnetic radiation | No change in particles; nucleus loses energy | 0 | 0 |

Examples:

- ²²⁶₈₈Ra → ²²²₈₆Rn + ⁴₂He
- ¹⁴₆C → ¹⁴₇N + ⁰₋₁e
- ¹¹₆C → ¹¹₅B + ⁰₊₁e
- ⁴⁰₁₉K + ⁰₋₁e → ⁴⁰₁₈Ar (electron capture)

In nuclear equations both mass numbers and atomic numbers balance.

**Properties.**

| Radiation | Charge | Penetration | Stopped by | In electric field |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| α | +2 | Low; most ionising | Paper, a few cm of air | Deflected slightly towards negative plate (heavy) |
| β⁻ | −1 | Medium | A few mm of aluminium | Deflected strongly towards positive plate (light) |
| γ | 0 | High; least ionising | Thick lead or concrete reduces it | Not deflected |

In a magnetic field, α and β bend in opposite directions; β bends more because it is much lighter.

**Half-life** is the time for half the nuclei in a sample to decay (or for the activity to halve). It is constant for an isotope. After n half-lives, the fraction left is (½)ⁿ.

**Harm.** Ionising radiation damages DNA in cells, which can kill cells or cause mutations leading to cancer. α is most dangerous inside the body (swallowed or breathed in); γ and β are dangerous from outside.

**Uses of radioisotopes.**

- Medicine: technetium-99m (γ, short half-life) as a tracer for imaging; iodine-131 to treat thyroid cancer; cobalt-60 γ rays to kill cancer cells and sterilise equipment.
- Dating: carbon-14 (half-life about 5730 years) to date once-living material; potassium-40/argon-40 to date rocks.
- Industry: thickness gauges (β for paper), finding leaks in pipes (γ tracers), smoke alarms (americium-241, α).
- Analysis: radioactive tracers follow the path of atoms in reactions.

**Fusion (OCR B EL(h)).** Under the extreme temperatures and pressures in stars, light nuclei fuse to form heavier ones, releasing energy. Hydrogen fuses to helium: ²₁H + ²₁H → ³₂He + ¹₀n, and overall four protons become one helium-4 nucleus. In older stars helium fuses to carbon (3 ⁴₂He → ¹²₆C) and then oxygen and heavier elements up to iron. Elements heavier than iron form in supernova explosions. High temperature is needed so the positive nuclei have enough energy to overcome their repulsion.

#### Worked example

A sample of a radioisotope with a half-life of 8 days has an activity of 640 Bq. What will its activity be after 32 days?

32 ÷ 8 = 4 half-lives. 640 × (½)⁴ = 40 Bq.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting that in β⁻ decay the mass number does not change.
- Saying γ radiation changes the element.
- Saying half-life is half the time for all the nuclei to decay.

### C1.2 Atomic spectra and the electromagnetic spectrum

*Refs: Eduqas C1.2(h) to (l) · OCR B EL(v), EL(w), OZ(t), OZ(u). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The order of infrared, visible and ultraviolet radiation by frequency, energy and wavelength.
- E = hf (or E = hν) and c = fλ.
- Emission spectra arise when excited electrons fall to lower energy levels and emit photons; absorption spectra when electrons absorb photons and move up.
- The hydrogen emission spectrum: series of lines converging at higher frequency; the Lyman series (transitions to n = 1).
- The convergence limit of the Lyman series gives the ionisation energy of hydrogen.
- UV and visible radiation can promote electrons and sometimes break bonds.

#### Explained

**The electromagnetic spectrum.** In order of increasing frequency and energy (decreasing wavelength): radio, microwave, infrared, visible (red to violet), ultraviolet, X-ray, gamma.

E = hf, where h = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ J s. c = fλ, where c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹. So E = hc/λ. Per mole, multiply by L (6.02 × 10²³).

**Energy levels.** Electrons in an atom can only have certain energies (quantised levels, n = 1, 2, 3 …). The levels get closer together as n increases.

- **Emission spectrum:** when atoms are heated or given electrical energy, electrons are excited to higher levels. When they drop back, each transition emits a photon with energy equal to the gap: ΔE = hf. This gives coloured lines on a black background.
- **Absorption spectrum:** when white light passes through a cool gas, electrons absorb photons of exactly the energies of the gaps. This gives black lines on a continuous coloured background.

Similarities: both are line spectra, with lines at the same frequencies for a given element, converging at higher frequency. Differences: bright lines on dark for emission; dark lines on bright for absorption.

Each element has its own pattern of lines, used to identify elements (for example in stars: the Sun's spectrum showed helium before it was found on Earth).

**The hydrogen spectrum.** Lines come in series, each ending on one level:

- Lyman series: electrons fall to n = 1; ultraviolet.
- Balmer series: electrons fall to n = 2; visible.

Within each series, lines get closer together at higher frequency because the energy levels get closer at high n.

**Convergence limit and ionisation energy.** The lines in the Lyman series converge to a limit. This corresponds to an electron falling from n = ∞ (outside the atom) to n = 1. The reverse, from n = 1 to n = ∞, is ionisation. So the frequency of the convergence limit gives the ionisation energy of hydrogen: IE = hf × L.

**Radiation and bonds (OCR B OZ(t)).** Visible and UV photons have enough energy to promote electrons to higher levels in molecules. UV photons can have enough energy to break covalent bonds (photodissociation), as when UV breaks Cl–Cl, O=O or the C–Cl bond in CFCs (files 18, 19, 35).

#### Worked example

1. The convergence limit of the Lyman series is at a frequency of 3.29 × 10¹⁵ Hz. Calculate the ionisation energy of hydrogen in kJ mol⁻¹.

   E per atom = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ × 3.29 × 10¹⁵ = 2.18 × 10⁻¹⁸ J. Per mole: × 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.31 × 10⁶ J mol⁻¹ = 1310 kJ mol⁻¹.

2. The bond enthalpy of Cl–Cl is 243 kJ mol⁻¹. Find the longest wavelength of light that can break the bond.

   E per bond = 243 000 ÷ 6.02 × 10²³ = 4.04 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. λ = hc/E = (6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ × 3.00 × 10⁸) ÷ 4.04 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 4.92 × 10⁻⁷ m = 492 nm (visible light).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying lines are produced when electrons move up (for emission).
- Mixing up which series is UV and which is visible.
- Forgetting to multiply by the Avogadro constant to get a molar value.
- Using the wavelength in nm without converting to m.

### Check questions

1. Write a nuclear equation for the α decay of uranium-238 (Z = 92). (2 marks)
2. Write a nuclear equation for the β⁻ decay of strontium-90 (Z = 38). (2 marks)
3. Describe how α, β and γ radiation behave in an electric field. (3 marks)
4. Iodine-131 has a half-life of 8 days. What fraction of a sample remains after 24 days? (1 mark)
5. Give two uses of radioisotopes and explain why each isotope is suitable. (4 marks)
6. Explain how elements heavier than helium formed. (2 marks)
7. Put infrared, ultraviolet and visible radiation in order of increasing energy. (1 mark)
8. Explain how an emission spectrum arises and why it consists of lines. (3 marks)
9. Explain why the lines in the hydrogen emission spectrum get closer together at higher frequency. (1 mark)
10. Light of wavelength 254 nm is absorbed by a molecule. Calculate the energy absorbed in kJ mol⁻¹. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. ²³⁸₉₂U → ²³⁴₉₀Th + ⁴₂He (2: products 1, numbers balance 1).
2. ⁹⁰₃₈Sr → ⁹⁰₃₉Y + ⁰₋₁e (2).
3. α deflected slightly towards negative plate (1); β deflected more strongly towards positive plate (1); γ not deflected (1).
4. ⅛ (1).
5. Any two uses with reasons, e.g. Tc-99m tracer: γ passes out of body, short half-life (2); C-14 dating: half-life suits archaeological timescales, taken in by living things (2); Co-60 radiotherapy: γ penetrates to tumour (2).
6. Fusion of lighter nuclei (1); at very high temperature and pressure in stars (1).
7. Infrared < visible < ultraviolet (1).
8. Electrons excited to higher energy levels (1); fall back to lower levels emitting photons (1); energy levels are fixed / quantised, so only certain frequencies emitted (1).
9. Energy levels get closer together at higher n (1).
10. E = hc/λ = (6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ × 3.00 × 10⁸) ÷ 2.54 × 10⁻⁷ = 7.83 × 10⁻¹⁹ J (1); × 6.02 × 10²³ = 4.71 × 10⁵ J mol⁻¹ (1); 471 kJ mol⁻¹ (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/03-bonding-and-structure.md -->

## Bonding, structure, shapes and intermolecular forces

How particles are held together decides melting points, conductivity, solubility and shape. This file covers ionic, covalent, dative and metallic bonding, the four types of structure, shapes of molecules by electron pair repulsion, bond polarity and the forces between molecules.

### Overview

AS content for every board. Shapes come back with complex ions (file 15), and intermolecular forces explain boiling points of alkanes (file 18), alcohols (file 21) and haloalkanes (file 19).

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.3.1 to 3.1.3.7.
- Edexcel 2.1 to 2.27 add: the effect of ionic radius and charge on ionic bond strength (2.2), trends in ionic radii (2.5), the link between bond length and bond strength (2.9), choosing solvents (2.21), and the structures of graphene and nanotubes (2.25).
- OCR A 2.2.2(a) to (o), and 3.1.1(d) to (f) for metallic bonding and giant covalent lattices.
- OCR B EL(i) to (l) (bonding, giant lattices, shapes, the sodium chloride structure) and OZ(a) to (d) (electronegativity and intermolecular bonds, set in the ozone story).
- Eduqas C1.4(a) to (i) and C1.5(a) to (e). Eduqas names the caesium chloride structure, and the structures of iodine and ice.

### 3.1.3.1 Ionic bonding

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3.1 · Edexcel 2.1 to 2.6 · OCR A 2.2.2(a), (b) · OCR B EL(i), EL(j), EL(l) · Eduqas C1.4(a), C1.5(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Ionic bonding is the electrostatic attraction between oppositely charged ions in a lattice.
- Formulae of compound ions (sulfate, hydroxide, nitrate, carbonate, ammonium).
- Predict the charge on an ion from the element's position in the periodic table.
- Dot-and-cross diagrams of ionic compounds.
- Ionic bond strength increases with ionic charge and decreases with ionic radius (Edexcel).
- Trends in ionic radii (Edexcel): ions get larger down a group; isoelectronic ions get smaller as nuclear charge rises.

#### Explained

Metals lose electrons to form positive ions; non-metals gain electrons to form negative ions. Groups 1, 2, 3 form 1+, 2+, 3+ ions. Groups 5, 6, 7 form 3−, 2−, 1− ions.

The ions pack into a **giant ionic lattice**: each ion is surrounded by oppositely charged ions, and attractions act in all directions. In sodium chloride each Na⁺ has six Cl⁻ neighbours and each Cl⁻ has six Na⁺ (6 : 6 coordination). In caesium chloride the larger Cs⁺ ion has room for eight Cl⁻ neighbours (8 : 8 coordination, Eduqas).

**Ionic radius.** Positive ions are smaller than their atoms (they lose a shell). Negative ions are larger than their atoms (more electron repulsion, same nuclear charge). For the isoelectronic series N³⁻, O²⁻, F⁻, Na⁺, Mg²⁺, Al³⁺ (all 10 electrons), radius decreases from N³⁻ to Al³⁺ because the nuclear charge rises from 7 to 13 while the electron arrangement stays the same.

**Strength.** Smaller, more highly charged ions attract more strongly, so MgO (2+, 2−) has a much higher melting point than NaCl (1+, 1−).

**Evidence for ions (Edexcel).** When a coloured salt such as copper(II) chromate(VI) is electrolysed on damp filter paper, the blue colour (Cu²⁺) moves to the negative electrode and the yellow colour (CrO₄²⁻) moves to the positive one.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling an ionic lattice "molecules of NaCl".
- Saying the bond forms because the atoms "want a full shell". The bond is the attraction between the ions.
- Drawing ionic dot-and-cross diagrams without square brackets and charges.

### 3.1.3.2 Nature of covalent and dative covalent bonds

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3.2 · Edexcel 2.7 to 2.9 · OCR A 2.2.2(d), (e), (f) · OCR B EL(i) · Eduqas C1.4(b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A single covalent bond is a shared pair of electrons. Multiple bonds share two or three pairs.
- A dative covalent (coordinate) bond is a shared pair in which both electrons come from one atom.
- Dot-and-cross diagrams of molecules, including those with expanded octets (SF₆, PCl₅) and fewer than eight electrons (BF₃).
- Bond length and bond strength (Edexcel): shorter bonds are generally stronger.

#### Explained

A covalent bond is the strong electrostatic attraction between a shared pair of electrons and the nuclei of the two bonded atoms.

**Dative bonds.** In the ammonium ion, the lone pair on N in NH₃ forms a bond to H⁺: NH₃ + H⁺ → NH₄⁺. Once formed, all four N–H bonds are identical. Other examples: H₃O⁺ (from H₂O + H⁺), and the Al₂Cl₆ dimer (a lone pair on Cl of one AlCl₃ donates to the Al of another). Show a dative bond with an arrow from the donor to the acceptor.

**Breaking the octet rule.** Boron in BF₃ has only six outer electrons. Elements in Period 3 and beyond can use d orbitals and have more than eight: phosphorus in PCl₅ (10), sulfur in SF₆ (12).

**Length and strength.** C≡C (0.120 nm) is shorter and stronger than C=C (0.134 nm), which is shorter and stronger than C–C (0.154 nm). More shared electrons pull the nuclei closer.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting lone pairs in dot-and-cross diagrams.
- Thinking a dative bond is weaker or different once made. In NH₄⁺ all four bonds are the same.

### 3.1.3.3 Metallic bonding

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3.3 · Edexcel 2.22 · OCR A 3.1.1(d) · OCR B EL(j) · Eduqas C1.5(d). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Metallic bonding is the attraction between a lattice of positive metal ions and delocalised electrons.

#### Explained

Metal atoms lose their outer electrons into a "sea" of delocalised electrons shared by the whole structure. The positive ions sit in a regular giant lattice. Metallic bond strength increases with more delocalised electrons per atom and smaller ions with higher charge. So magnesium (2 electrons per atom, Mg²⁺) has a higher melting point than sodium (1, Na⁺).

- Metals conduct electricity because the delocalised electrons move through the structure when a voltage is applied.
- They conduct heat because the electrons and the vibrating ions pass on energy.
- They are malleable and ductile because layers of ions can slide over each other without breaking the bonding.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying metals contain "free electrons" with no mention of the positive ions they attract.
- Saying metal ions move to conduct electricity. The electrons move.

### 3.1.3.4 Bonding and physical properties

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3.4 · Edexcel 2.6, 2.23 to 2.27 · OCR A 2.2.2(b), (c), (n), (o), 3.1.1(e), (f) · OCR B EL(j), EL(l) · Eduqas C1.5(a) to (e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The four types of crystal structure: ionic, metallic, macromolecular (giant covalent) and molecular.
- Diamond and graphite structures; ice and iodine as molecular crystals.
- Explain energy changes in melting and boiling from the bonding and structure.
- Predict structure from physical data and the reverse.

#### Explained

| Structure | Examples | Particles and forces | Melting point | Conducts? | Soluble in water? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Giant ionic | NaCl, MgO | Ions; strong attractions in all directions | High | Only when molten or dissolved (ions free to move) | Often |
| Giant metallic | Na, Fe | Positive ions and delocalised electrons | Usually high | Yes, solid and liquid | No |
| Giant covalent (macromolecular) | Diamond, graphite, SiO₂ | Atoms joined by covalent bonds throughout | Very high | No (except graphite) | No |
| Simple molecular | I₂, ice, CO₂ | Molecules; covalent inside, weak forces between | Low | No | Varies |

**Diamond.** Each carbon is covalently bonded to four others in a tetrahedral arrangement. All electrons are in bonds, so it does not conduct. Very hard and very high melting point because many strong covalent bonds must break.

**Graphite.** Each carbon bonds to three others in flat hexagonal layers. The fourth electron of each carbon is delocalised across the layer, so graphite conducts along the layers. Weak London forces between layers let them slide, so it is soft. High melting point because the covalent bonds in the layers must break.

**Graphene (Edexcel)** is a single layer of graphite: very strong, light and an excellent conductor. **Carbon nanotubes** are graphene rolled into a cylinder; strong, conducting, and used to carry drugs and in electronics.

**Silicon dioxide** is like diamond with an O between each pair of Si atoms.

**Iodine** forms a molecular lattice of I₂ molecules held by London forces. It sublimes on gentle heating.

**Ice** is a molecular lattice in which each water molecule forms hydrogen bonds to four others, in an open tetrahedral arrangement.

**Melting a molecular substance breaks the forces between molecules, not the covalent bonds.** When iodine sublimes, I₂ molecules separate; the I–I bond stays.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying covalent bonds break when a simple molecular substance melts.
- Saying ionic solids conduct because electrons move. Ions move, and only when molten or in solution.
- Saying graphite conducts because of "free ions".

### 3.1.3.5 Shapes of simple molecules and ions

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3.5 · Edexcel 2.10 to 2.12 · OCR A 2.2.2(g), (h) · OCR B EL(k) · Eduqas C1.4(h), (i). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Electron pairs around the central atom repel and move as far apart as possible.
- Lone pair–lone pair repulsion > lone pair–bonding pair > bonding pair–bonding pair.
- Predict shapes and bond angles for molecules and ions with up to six electron pairs (including lone pairs) around the central atom.

#### Explained

**Method.**

1. Count the outer electrons on the central atom.
2. Add one for each atom bonded (for single bonds, each bonded atom provides one electron).
3. Adjust for charge: add one per negative charge, subtract one per positive charge.
4. Divide by two to get the number of electron pairs.
5. Bonding pairs = number of bonded atoms. Lone pairs = the rest.
6. Treat a double bond as one "region" of electron density for shape.

| Pairs | Bonding | Lone | Shape | Angle | Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 | 2 | 0 | Linear | 180° | BeCl₂, CO₂ |
| 3 | 3 | 0 | Trigonal planar | 120° | BF₃ |
| 4 | 4 | 0 | Tetrahedral | 109.5° | CH₄, NH₄⁺ |
| 4 | 3 | 1 | Trigonal pyramidal | 107° | NH₃ |
| 4 | 2 | 2 | Bent (V-shaped) | 104.5° | H₂O |
| 5 | 5 | 0 | Trigonal bipyramidal | 90° and 120° | PCl₅ |
| 6 | 6 | 0 | Octahedral | 90° | SF₆ |
| 6 | 4 | 2 | Square planar | 90° | XeF₄ |
| 3 | 2 | 1 | Bent | about 118° | SO₂ |

Each lone pair closes the angle between bonding pairs by about 2.5°, because lone pairs are held closer to the central atom and repel more.

To explain a shape in an exam: state the number of bonding pairs and lone pairs, say the pairs repel as far apart as possible, say lone pairs repel more than bonding pairs, then give the shape and angle.

#### Worked example

Predict the shape of ClF₄⁻.

Cl has 7 outer electrons, + 4 from F, + 1 for the charge = 12, so 6 pairs. 4 bonding, 2 lone. Square planar, 90°.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to adjust for the charge of an ion.
- Calling water "linear". It has two lone pairs.
- Giving 109° for NH₃. It is 107°.

### 3.1.3.6 Bond polarity

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3.6 · Edexcel 2.13 to 2.15 · OCR A 2.2.2(i), (j) · OCR B OZ(a) · Eduqas C1.4(c), (d). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Electronegativity is the power of an atom to attract the pair of electrons in a covalent bond.
- Electronegativity difference causes bond polarity; a big enough difference gives ionic bonding.
- A molecule with polar bonds is non-polar if it is symmetrical and the dipoles cancel.

#### Explained

Electronegativity increases across a period (more protons, similar shielding, smaller atoms) and decreases down a group (larger atoms, more shielding). Fluorine is the most electronegative element (Pauling value 4.0), then oxygen (3.5), nitrogen and chlorine (3.0).

In H–Cl the electrons are pulled towards Cl, which becomes δ−; H becomes δ+. The bond has a dipole.

Bonding is a continuum: pure covalent (equal sharing, e.g. Cl₂) → polar covalent → ionic. Ionic compounds can have some covalent character when a small, highly charged positive ion distorts a large negative ion (file 8).

**Polar or non-polar molecule?** CO₂ is linear, so its two C=O dipoles cancel: non-polar. CCl₄ is tetrahedral and symmetrical: non-polar. H₂O is bent and CHCl₃ is not symmetrical: polar.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying electronegativity is the attraction for "an electron". It is for a bonding pair in a covalent bond.
- Saying CO₂ has no polar bonds. It has polar bonds that cancel.

### 3.1.3.7 Forces between molecules

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3.7 · Edexcel 2.16 to 2.21 · OCR A 2.2.2(k), (l), (m) · OCR B OZ(b), OZ(c), OZ(d) · Eduqas C1.4(e) to (g), C1.5(c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Three types of intermolecular force: induced dipole–dipole (London or van der Waals) forces, permanent dipole–dipole forces, and hydrogen bonding.
- How each arises, and their relative strength.
- Explain boiling points and other physical properties, including the anomalous properties of water.
- Edexcel: how intermolecular forces affect the choice of solvent and solubility.

#### Explained

**London forces (induced dipole–dipole).** Electrons move constantly, so at any instant the electron cloud may be uneven, giving a temporary dipole. This induces a dipole in a neighbouring molecule, and the two attract. They act between all molecules. They get stronger with more electrons (larger molecules) and with more contact between molecules (straight chains pack closer than branched ones).

**Permanent dipole–dipole forces** act between polar molecules, such as HCl or propanone: the δ+ end of one attracts the δ− end of another. They act as well as London forces.

**Hydrogen bonding** is the strongest. It needs:

1. H bonded directly to N, O or F (very electronegative, so the H is strongly δ+ and its electron is pulled away, leaving an almost bare proton);
2. a lone pair on an N, O or F of another molecule.

Draw it with the δ charges, the lone pair and a dashed line between the H and the lone pair; the O–H···O arrangement is close to 180°.

**Boiling point trends.**

- Noble gases and halogens: boiling point rises down the group as the number of electrons, and so London forces, increase.
- Alkanes: boiling point rises with chain length; branched isomers boil lower.
- Group 6 hydrides: H₂O boils far higher (100 °C) than H₂S (−60 °C), because of hydrogen bonds. From H₂S to H₂Te the boiling point rises with London forces.

**Anomalous properties of water.**

1. High melting and boiling points for its size: hydrogen bonds need extra energy to break.
2. Ice is less dense than liquid water: in ice, hydrogen bonds hold the molecules in an open lattice with more space than in the liquid, so ice floats.
3. Also high surface tension and high specific heat capacity.

**Solvents (Edexcel 2.21).** A substance dissolves when the new attractions to the solvent are comparable to the ones broken. Ionic solids dissolve in water because the ions are attracted to the polar water molecules (hydration). Alcohols dissolve in water because they hydrogen bond to it; solubility falls as the hydrocarbon chain gets longer. Non-polar substances dissolve in non-polar solvents such as hexane, because London forces are similar on both sides; they do not dissolve in water because they cannot break the hydrogen bonds between water molecules.

#### Worked example

Explain why propan-1-ol (Mᵣ 60) boils at 97 °C but butane (Mᵣ 58) boils at −1 °C.

Both have similar numbers of electrons, so similar London forces. Propan-1-ol has an O–H group so it forms hydrogen bonds between molecules; butane only has London forces. More energy is needed to overcome hydrogen bonds.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying hydrogen bonds form with any H atom. The H must be on N, O or F.
- Saying boiling breaks covalent bonds inside molecules.
- Ranking forces only by type without thinking about size: a large non-polar molecule can have stronger total London forces than a small polar one.

### Check questions

1. Explain why magnesium oxide has a higher melting point than sodium chloride. (3 marks)
2. Draw a dot-and-cross diagram for the ammonium ion and identify the dative bond. (2 marks)
3. Predict the shape and bond angle of PF₆⁻ and of H₂S. (4 marks)
4. Explain why graphite conducts electricity but diamond does not. (3 marks)
5. Explain why the bond angle in water is smaller than in methane. (3 marks)
6. Explain why tetrachloromethane is non-polar although its C–Cl bonds are polar. (2 marks)
7. Explain why the boiling point of HF is higher than that of HCl. (3 marks)
8. Explain why ice is less dense than liquid water. (2 marks)
9. Explain why sodium has a lower melting point than magnesium. (3 marks)
10. Arrange the ions F⁻, Na⁺, O²⁻, Mg²⁺ in order of increasing radius and explain. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Mg²⁺ and O²⁻ have higher charges than Na⁺ and Cl⁻ (1); ions also smaller (1); stronger electrostatic attraction / more energy to break lattice (1).
2. Correct diagram with 4 bonds, 8 electrons around N, square brackets and + charge (1); one bond shown with both electrons from N (1).
3. PF₆⁻: octahedral (1), 90° (1). H₂S: bent / V-shaped (1), about 92° to 104° (accept less than 109.5° with reason: two lone pairs) (1).
4. Graphite: each C bonded to three others (1); one electron per C delocalised and free to move along layers (1). Diamond: each C bonded to four, all outer electrons in bonds / none delocalised (1).
5. Water has two lone pairs, methane none (1); lone pairs repel more than bonding pairs (1); so bonding pairs pushed closer: 104.5° vs 109.5° (1).
6. Molecule is symmetrical / tetrahedral (1); dipoles cancel (1).
7. HF forms hydrogen bonds (H bonded to F, which is very electronegative) (1); HCl has only permanent dipole–dipole and London forces (1); hydrogen bonds are stronger, need more energy to overcome (1).
8. In ice, hydrogen bonds hold molecules in an open lattice / fixed further apart (1); in liquid the molecules are closer together, so ice has lower density (1).
9. Mg²⁺ has a higher charge (and is smaller) than Na⁺ (1); Mg has two delocalised electrons per atom, Na one (1); stronger attraction between ions and delocalised electrons (1).
10. Mg²⁺ < Na⁺ < F⁻ < O²⁻ (1); all have the same number of electrons / isoelectronic (1); more protons means greater nuclear charge pulling electrons closer (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/12-periodicity-and-period-3.md -->

## Periodicity and the Period 3 elements and their oxides

The periodic table repeats its patterns every period. This file covers how the table is arranged, the s, p and d blocks, trends in atomic radius, ionisation energy and melting point across Period 3, and (AQA A-level) the reactions of the Period 3 elements with water and oxygen and the acid–base nature of their oxides.

### Overview

Periodicity is AS. The reactions of Period 3 elements and oxides are AQA A-level only. Ionisation energy explanations are in file 1; structure and bonding types are in file 3.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.1.1, 3.2.1.2 (AS) and 3.2.4 (A-level).
- Edexcel 1.24 to 1.26: periodicity across Periods 2 and 3, including melting temperatures and ionisation energies, illustrated with data. Edexcel does not require 3.2.4.
- OCR A 3.1.1(a), (b), (g): arrangement of the table, blocks, and melting point trend across Periods 2 and 3. OCR A ionisation energy trend is in file 1; metallic and giant covalent lattices are in file 3.
- OCR B EL(m) to (o): the table as a list in order of atomic number, melting point trends, and links between position and electron configuration (including Mendeleev's work).
- Eduqas C1.6(a), (b), (d): arrangement, electronic structure and position, and general trends in ionisation energy, melting temperature and electronegativity.

### 3.2.1.1 Classification

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.1 · Edexcel 1.24 · OCR A 3.1.1(a), (b) · OCR B EL(m), EL(o) · Eduqas C1.6(a), (b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The periodic table is arranged in order of atomic (proton) number, in periods (rows) and groups (columns).
- Elements are classified into s, p, d (and f) blocks by the sub-shell of their highest-energy electron.
- Elements in a group have the same outer electron configuration and similar chemistry.

#### Explained

Mendeleev arranged elements by atomic mass but swapped some (such as tellurium and iodine) to keep chemical families together, and left gaps for undiscovered elements, predicting their properties. When the gaps were filled (gallium, germanium), the predictions were confirmed. The modern table uses atomic number, which removes the anomalies.

- s block: Groups 1 and 2 (and He).
- p block: Groups 3 to 0 (13 to 18).
- d block: the transition metals, Sc to Zn in Period 4.
- f block: lanthanides and actinides.

The period number is the number of the outer shell; the group number (for s and p blocks) is the number of outer electrons.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the modern table is ordered by atomic mass.
- Putting helium in the p block. Its electrons are 1s².

### 3.2.1.2 Physical properties of Period 3 elements

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.2 · Edexcel 1.25, 1.26 · OCR A 3.1.1(g) · OCR B EL(n) · Eduqas C1.6(d). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Trends in atomic radius, first ionisation energy and melting point across Period 3 (Na to Ar).
- Explain the melting point trend in terms of structure and bonding.

#### Explained

**Atomic radius decreases** across the period. Nuclear charge increases; the electrons are added to the same shell with similar shielding; so the outer electrons are pulled closer.

**First ionisation energy** generally increases, with dips at Al and S (file 1).

**Electronegativity** increases across the period for the same reasons as radius decreases.

**Melting points:**

| Element | Na | Mg | Al | Si | P (P₄) | S (S₈) | Cl (Cl₂) | Ar |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Structure | Giant metallic | Giant metallic | Giant metallic | Giant covalent | Simple molecular | Simple molecular | Simple molecular | Atoms |
| m.p. / K | 371 | 923 | 933 | 1683 | 317 | 392 | 172 | 84 |

- Na to Al: metallic bonding gets stronger. Ions have charges 1+, 2+, 3+, are smaller, and there are 1, 2, 3 delocalised electrons per atom. Stronger attraction.
- Si: giant covalent (like diamond). Many strong covalent bonds must break, so the highest melting point.
- P₄, S₈, Cl₂, Ar: simple molecules held by London forces. The bigger the molecule (more electrons), the stronger the London forces. S₈ > P₄ > Cl₂ > Ar.
