Period 2 shows the same pattern: Li, Be metallic; B, C (diamond/graphite) giant covalent with very high melting points; N₂, O₂, F₂, Ne simple molecular.

#### Worked example

Explain why sulfur has a higher melting point than phosphorus.

Sulfur exists as S₈ molecules and phosphorus as P₄ molecules. S₈ has more electrons, so stronger London forces between molecules, which need more energy to overcome.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying covalent bonds break when sulfur melts.
- Explaining silicon's high melting point with London forces.
- Saying atomic radius decreases because of "more shells". It is the same shell.

### 3.2.4 Properties of Period 3 elements and their oxides

*Refs: AQA 3.2.4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Reactions of Na and Mg with water.
- Reactions of Na, Mg, Al, Si, P and S with oxygen, the formulae of the oxides and their structure and bonding.
- Trend in melting points of the oxides.
- Reactions of the oxides with water and the pH of the solutions.
- The acid–base character of the oxides; amphoteric aluminium oxide; equations for reactions with acids and bases.

#### Explained

**Reactions with water.**

- Sodium reacts vigorously with cold water, fizzing and moving on the surface: 2Na + 2H₂O → 2NaOH + H₂. Solution pH about 14.
- Magnesium reacts very slowly with cold water: Mg + 2H₂O → Mg(OH)₂ + H₂. Mg(OH)₂ is only slightly soluble, so pH about 10. With steam it reacts quickly with a bright white flame: Mg + H₂O → MgO + H₂.

**Reactions with oxygen.**

| Element | Observation | Oxide | Bonding and structure |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Na | Yellow flame, white solid | Na₂O | Giant ionic |
| Mg | Brilliant white flame, white solid | MgO | Giant ionic |
| Al | White flame (powder), white solid | Al₂O₃ | Giant ionic with some covalent character |
| Si | Burns when heated strongly | SiO₂ | Giant covalent |
| P | Burns with a white flame, white smoke | P₄O₁₀ | Simple molecular |
| S | Blue flame, choking gas | SO₂ (some SO₃ with a catalyst) | Simple molecular |

**Melting points of oxides.** MgO is highest of the ionic oxides (2+ and 2− ions; strong attraction), higher than Na₂O. Al₂O₃ is lower than expected for 3+ ions because of covalent character. SiO₂ is high (giant covalent). P₄O₁₀ and SO₂ are low (London and permanent dipole forces between molecules), with P₄O₁₀ higher because it is a larger molecule.

**Oxides with water.**

| Oxide | Reaction with water | pH |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Na₂O | Na₂O + H₂O → 2NaOH | 13 to 14 |
| MgO | MgO + H₂O → Mg(OH)₂ (slightly soluble) | 9 to 10 |
| Al₂O₃ | Insoluble | 7 |
| SiO₂ | Insoluble | 7 |
| P₄O₁₀ | P₄O₁₀ + 6H₂O → 4H₃PO₄ | 0 to 1 |
| SO₂ | SO₂ + H₂O → H₂SO₃ | 2 to 3 |
| SO₃ | SO₃ + H₂O → H₂SO₄ | 0 to 1 |

Ionic oxides contain O²⁻, which reacts with water as a base: O²⁻ + H₂O → 2OH⁻. Non-metal oxides are covalent and form acids.

**Acid–base character.**

- Basic: Na₂O, MgO. Na₂O + 2HCl → 2NaCl + H₂O; MgO + H₂SO₄ → MgSO₄ + H₂O.
- Amphoteric: Al₂O₃ reacts with both.
  - With acid: Al₂O₃ + 6HCl → 2AlCl₃ + 3H₂O.
  - With base: Al₂O₃ + 2NaOH + 3H₂O → 2NaAl(OH)₄.
- Acidic: SiO₂, P₄O₁₀, SO₂, SO₃.
  - SiO₂ + 2NaOH → Na₂SiO₃ + H₂O (only with hot concentrated alkali).
  - P₄O₁₀ + 12NaOH → 4Na₃PO₄ + 6H₂O.
  - SO₂ + 2NaOH → Na₂SO₃ + H₂O.
  - SO₃ + 2NaOH → Na₂SO₄ + H₂O.

**The structures of the acids.** H₃PO₄ has P bonded to one =O and three –OH groups; H₂SO₄ has S bonded to two =O and two –OH groups. The H⁺ ions come from the O–H groups. The anions (PO₄³⁻, SO₄²⁻, SO₃²⁻) are stabilised by charge spread over the oxygens.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Al₂O₃ is basic only. It is amphoteric.
- Writing SiO₂ + H₂O as a reaction. It does not react with water.
- Saying MgO solution has pH 14. Mg(OH)₂ is only slightly soluble.

### Check questions

1. Explain why atomic radius decreases from sodium to chlorine. (2 marks)
2. Explain why the melting point of aluminium is higher than that of sodium. (3 marks)
3. Explain why silicon has the highest melting point in Period 3. (2 marks)
4. Explain why argon has the lowest melting point in Period 3. (2 marks)
5. Write equations for the reactions of sodium with water and of magnesium with steam. (2 marks)
6. Give the formulae of the oxides of phosphorus and sulfur formed on burning in excess oxygen and state their type of structure. (2 marks)
7. Write equations to show that aluminium oxide is amphoteric. (2 marks)
8. Explain why MgO has a higher melting point than P₄O₁₀. (3 marks)
9. Give the approximate pH of the solutions formed when sodium oxide and sulfur dioxide are added to water, with equations. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Nuclear charge / proton number increases (1); electrons in same shell, similar shielding, so greater attraction (1).
2. Al³⁺ has higher charge and is smaller than Na⁺ (1); three delocalised electrons per atom compared with one (1); stronger metallic bonding / attraction between ions and delocalised electrons (1).
3. Giant covalent structure (1); many strong covalent bonds to break (1).
4. Exists as single atoms with fewest electrons of the molecular elements (1); weakest London forces (1).
5. 2Na + 2H₂O → 2NaOH + H₂ (1); Mg + H₂O → MgO + H₂ (1).
6. P₄O₁₀, SO₂ (and SO₃) (1); simple molecular (1).
7. Al₂O₃ + 6HCl → 2AlCl₃ + 3H₂O (1); Al₂O₃ + 2NaOH + 3H₂O → 2NaAl(OH)₄ (1).
8. MgO giant ionic (1); strong attraction between Mg²⁺ and O²⁻ in all directions (1); P₄O₁₀ simple molecular with weak intermolecular forces (1).
9. Na₂O: pH 13 to 14 (1); Na₂O + H₂O → 2NaOH (1). SO₂: pH 2 to 3 (1); SO₂ + H₂O → H₂SO₃ (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/13-groups-1-and-2.md -->

## Group 2 (and Group 1): the s-block metals

The Group 2 metals, magnesium to barium, show clear trends down the group. This file covers trends in atomic radius, ionisation energy, melting point and reactivity, reactions with water and oxygen, solubility of hydroxides and sulfates, thermal stability of carbonates and nitrates, flame tests, and uses of Group 2 compounds. Group 1 is included for the boards that ask for it.

### Overview

AS content. Ionisation energy ideas come from file 1; tests for sulfate and ammonium ions are with the other ion tests in file 14 (AQA RP4).

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.2: trends in radius, first ionisation energy, melting point; reactions with water; solubility of hydroxides and sulfates; uses (Mg in titanium extraction, Ca(OH)₂ in agriculture, CaO/CaCO₃ in flue gas desulfurisation, BaSO₄ in medicine and in the sulfate test, Mg(OH)₂ in medicine). Melting point trend is AQA only.
- Edexcel 4.1 to 4.8 add Group 1, thermal stability of Group 1 and 2 nitrates and carbonates (4.6), flame colours (4.7) and experimental procedures for thermal decomposition and flame tests (4.8).
- OCR A 3.1.2(a) to (e): reactivity with oxygen and water, oxides with water, and uses as bases (Ca(OH)₂ in agriculture, Mg(OH)₂ and CaCO₃ as antacids).
- OCR B EL(p), (r), (s), (u): Group 2 properties including thermal stability of carbonates explained by charge density, solubility of many compounds and precipitate colours, and the basic nature of the oxides and hydroxides.
- Eduqas C1.6(e) to (k): Group 1 and 2 reactivity, reactions of aqueous Mg²⁺, Ca²⁺, Ba²⁺, flame colours, thermal stability of Group 2 carbonates and nitrates, solubility of Group 2 hydroxides and sulfates, basic oxides and hydroxides.

### 3.2.2 Group 2, the alkaline earth metals

*Refs: AQA 3.2.2 · Edexcel 4.1 to 4.8 · OCR A 3.1.2(a) to (e) · OCR B EL(p), EL(r), EL(s), EL(u) · Eduqas C1.6(e) to (k). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Trends down Group 2 in atomic radius, first ionisation energy, melting point and reactivity.
- Reactions of the elements with water (and oxygen) and the trend in reactivity.
- Solubility trends of the hydroxides and the sulfates.
- Uses of Group 2 compounds.
- Thermal stability of carbonates and nitrates (Edexcel, OCR B, Eduqas).
- Flame test colours (Edexcel, Eduqas).
- Group 1 reactivity trend (Edexcel, Eduqas).

#### Explained

**Physical trends down the group (Mg → Ba).**

- Atomic radius increases: each element has an extra shell.
- First ionisation energy decreases: the outer electrons are further from the nucleus and more shielded, so less attracted, despite more protons.
- Melting point generally decreases (AQA): the metal ions are larger, so the attraction between the delocalised electrons and the ions is weaker. (Magnesium is a slight exception because of its different crystal structure.)

**Reactivity increases down the group.** Group 2 metals react by losing two electrons. The first and second ionisation energies both fall down the group, so the electrons are lost more easily.

**With water.**

- Mg + 2H₂O(l) → Mg(OH)₂(aq) + H₂(g): very slow with cold water.
- Mg + H₂O(g) → MgO(s) + H₂(g): fast with steam, bright white glow.
- Ca + 2H₂O → Ca(OH)₂ + H₂: steady fizzing, a cloudy white suspension forms.
- Sr and Ba react faster still.

**With oxygen.** All burn to form white ionic oxides MO: 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO (brilliant white flame). Calcium burns with a brick-red flame, strontium crimson, barium apple green. Barium can also form the peroxide BaO₂ in excess oxygen.

**Oxides and hydroxides are basic.**

- Oxides react with water to form hydroxides: CaO + H₂O → Ca(OH)₂. The O²⁻ ion takes a proton from water.
- They neutralise acids: MgO + 2HCl → MgCl₂ + H₂O; Ca(OH)₂ + 2HNO₃ → Ca(NO₃)₂ + 2H₂O.
- The hydroxide solutions become more alkaline down the group because the hydroxides become more soluble, so [OH⁻] is higher.

**Solubility.**

| Compound | Mg | Ca | Sr | Ba | Trend |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hydroxide | Sparingly soluble | Slightly soluble | More soluble | Soluble | Increases down |
| Sulfate | Soluble | Slightly soluble | Sparingly soluble | Insoluble | Decreases down |

So adding NaOH(aq) to Mg²⁺(aq) gives a white precipitate of Mg(OH)₂; adding sulfate ions to Ba²⁺(aq) gives a white precipitate of BaSO₄. Ca²⁺ gives a slight white precipitate with both. Carbonates of Group 2 are all insoluble: Mg²⁺, Ca²⁺ and Ba²⁺ all give white precipitates with carbonate ions (Eduqas C1.6(f)).

Ionic equations: Mg²⁺(aq) + 2OH⁻(aq) → Mg(OH)₂(s); Ba²⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq) → BaSO₄(s).

**Uses (AQA, OCR A).**

| Compound | Use | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mg(OH)₂ | Antacid (milk of magnesia) | Weak base, neutralises stomach acid, only slightly soluble so not too alkaline |
| CaCO₃ | Antacid; in farming | Neutralises acid, giving CO₂ |
| Ca(OH)₂ (slaked lime) | Neutralises acid soil | Base: Ca(OH)₂ + 2H⁺ → Ca²⁺ + 2H₂O |
| CaO or CaCO₃ | Removes SO₂ from power station flue gases | CaO + SO₂ → CaSO₃; then oxidised to CaSO₄ (gypsum) |
| BaSO₄ | "Barium meal" for X-ray of gut | Insoluble, so the toxic Ba²⁺ ions are not absorbed; absorbs X-rays |
| BaCl₂ (acidified) | Test for sulfate ions | White BaSO₄ precipitate (file 14) |
| Mg | Extracting titanium | TiCl₄ + 2Mg → Ti + 2MgCl₂, reduces TiCl₄ |

**Thermal stability of carbonates and nitrates (Edexcel, OCR B, Eduqas).**

Group 2 carbonates decompose on heating to the oxide and carbon dioxide: CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂. They become **more stable** down the group (need higher temperatures).

Group 2 nitrates decompose to the oxide, nitrogen dioxide (brown gas) and oxygen: 2Mg(NO₃)₂ → 2MgO + 4NO₂ + O₂. They also become more stable down the group.

**Explanation: charge density and polarisation.** The cation at the top of the group (Mg²⁺) is small with a 2+ charge, so it has a high charge density. It polarises (distorts) the large carbonate or nitrate anion, weakening the bonds inside it, so the anion breaks up more easily. Down the group the cation is larger, its charge density falls, it polarises the anion less, so the compound is more stable.

**Group 1** compounds are more stable than Group 2 because 1+ ions have lower charge density. Most Group 1 carbonates do not decompose at Bunsen temperatures; Li₂CO₃ does (Li⁺ is very small): Li₂CO₃ → Li₂O + CO₂. Group 1 nitrates (except lithium) decompose only to the nitrite and oxygen: 2NaNO₃ → 2NaNO₂ + O₂. Lithium nitrate behaves like Group 2: 4LiNO₃ → 2Li₂O + 4NO₂ + O₂.

**Experimental procedure (Edexcel 4.8).** Heat equal amounts of each carbonate in a boiling tube fitted with a delivery tube into limewater. Time how long until the limewater turns cloudy (CO₂). For nitrates, time until brown NO₂ appears (in a fume cupboard), or until a glowing splint relights (O₂). Keep the Bunsen flame, mass of solid and distance from flame constant.

**Group 1 reactivity (Edexcel, Eduqas).** Reactivity increases from Li to Cs: the single outer electron is further away and more shielded, so the first ionisation energy falls. Lithium fizzes steadily in water; sodium melts into a ball and moves around; potassium ignites with a lilac flame. 2M + 2H₂O → 2MOH + H₂. Group 1 oxides and hydroxides are strongly basic and soluble.

**Flame tests (Edexcel 4.7, Eduqas C1.6(g)).**

| Ion | Colour |
| --- | --- |
| Li⁺ | Red (crimson) |
| Na⁺ | Yellow-orange |
| K⁺ | Lilac |
| Rb⁺ | Red-violet |
| Cs⁺ | Blue-violet |
| Ca²⁺ | Brick red (orange-red) |
| Sr²⁺ | Crimson |
| Ba²⁺ | Apple green |
| Cu²⁺ | Blue-green |

Method: clean a nichrome wire by dipping in concentrated hydrochloric acid and holding it in a roaring Bunsen flame until no colour shows. Dip it in acid then into the solid, and hold in the edge of the flame.

Why: the heat promotes electrons to higher energy levels. When they fall back, they emit energy as light of specific frequencies (ΔE = hf). The energy gaps differ for each ion, so each gives its own colour. Magnesium gives no flame colour because its emitted light is outside the visible region.

#### Worked example

1. Explain why a saturated solution of barium hydroxide has a higher pH than a saturated solution of magnesium hydroxide.

   Ba(OH)₂ is much more soluble than Mg(OH)₂, so more dissolves. The concentration of OH⁻ ions is higher, so the pH is higher.

2. 0.500 g of impure calcium carbonate is heated to constant mass. The mass falls by 0.198 g. Find the percentage of CaCO₃ (impurities do not decompose).

   CO₂ lost = 0.198 ÷ 44.0 = 4.50 × 10⁻³ mol = n(CaCO₃). Mass CaCO₃ = 4.50 × 10⁻³ × 100.1 = 0.450 g. Percentage = 90.1%.

**Solubility rules for precipitation tests (OCR B EL(s)).** Useful facts for predicting precipitates:

| Anion | Soluble with | Precipitates (colour) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| NO₃⁻ | All cations | None |
| Cl⁻, Br⁻, I⁻ | Most | AgCl white, AgBr cream, AgI yellow; PbCl₂ white, PbI₂ yellow |
| SO₄²⁻ | Most | BaSO₄ white, PbSO₄ white; CaSO₄ slightly soluble |
| CO₃²⁻ | Group 1 and NH₄⁺ | Most others: CaCO₃, BaCO₃ white; CuCO₃ blue-green; ZnCO₃ white |
| OH⁻ | Group 1, NH₄⁺, Ba²⁺ | Cu(OH)₂ blue, Fe(OH)₂ green, Fe(OH)₃ orange-brown, Zn(OH)₂, Al(OH)₃, Pb(OH)₂ white, Mg(OH)₂ white |

All Group 1 and ammonium compounds are soluble. These facts make the basis of ion tests: Ba²⁺ for SO₄²⁻, Ag⁺ for halides, OH⁻ for many metal ions (files 14 and 16).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying reactivity increases because "more shells means more electrons to lose". Say the outer electrons are lost more easily because ionisation energy decreases.
- Reversing the solubility trends: hydroxides become more soluble down the group, sulfates less soluble.
- Saying BaSO₄ is safe because barium is not toxic. Barium ions are toxic; BaSO₄ is safe because it is insoluble.
- Explaining thermal stability without mentioning polarisation of the anion by the cation.
- Saying flame colours come from electrons being "given energy" without saying that light is emitted when they fall back.

#### Practical

**OCR A PAG4, OCR B PAG4 and Eduqas: test-tube reactions of Group 2 ions.** Add dilute sodium hydroxide, dilute sulfuric acid (or sodium sulfate) and sodium carbonate solutions to solutions of Mg²⁺, Ca²⁺, Sr²⁺ and Ba²⁺, and record which give precipitates. This links to the qualitative analysis scheme in file 14.

### Check questions

1. Explain why the first ionisation energy decreases down Group 2. (3 marks)
2. Describe what you would see when calcium reacts with water, and write an equation. (3 marks)
3. Explain why barium sulfate is used in X-ray imaging even though barium ions are toxic. (2 marks)
4. Write an equation for the removal of sulfur dioxide from flue gases using calcium oxide. (1 mark)
5. State the trend in solubility of the Group 2 hydroxides and describe a test that shows Mg(OH)₂ is the least soluble. (2 marks)
6. Explain why magnesium carbonate decomposes at a lower temperature than barium carbonate. (3 marks)
7. Write an equation for the thermal decomposition of calcium nitrate and state one observation. (2 marks)
8. Explain how a flame colour is produced. (3 marks)
9. Give the use of magnesium in the extraction of titanium, with an equation. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Outer electrons further from nucleus / larger atomic radius (1); more shielding by inner shells (1); weaker attraction outweighs increase in nuclear charge (1).
2. Fizzing / bubbles; calcium sinks and dissolves; cloudy white solution forms (any two, 2); Ca + 2H₂O → Ca(OH)₂ + H₂ (1).
3. BaSO₄ is insoluble (1); so Ba²⁺ ions are not absorbed into the blood / it absorbs X-rays and outlines the gut (1).
4. CaO + SO₂ → CaSO₃ (1).
5. Solubility increases down the group (1); add NaOH(aq) to each solution: Mg²⁺ gives a white precipitate, Ba²⁺ none / least (1).
6. Mg²⁺ is smaller (same charge) so higher charge density (1); polarises / distorts the carbonate ion more (1); weakens C–O bonds so decomposes more easily (1).
7. 2Ca(NO₃)₂ → 2CaO + 4NO₂ + O₂ (1); brown gas / white solid melts / glowing splint relights (1).
8. Electrons are promoted / excited to higher energy levels by heat (1); they fall back to lower levels (1); emitting energy as light of a particular frequency / wavelength (1).
9. Reducing agent for TiCl₄ (1); TiCl₄ + 2Mg → Ti + 2MgCl₂ (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/17-introduction-to-organic-chemistry.md -->

## Introduction to organic chemistry: names, formulae, mechanisms and isomerism

Organic chemistry is the chemistry of carbon compounds. This file sets up the language used in every later organic file: types of formula, homologous series and functional groups, IUPAC naming, types of reaction and bond fission, curly arrows, and structural and E/Z isomerism.

### Overview

AS content. Optical isomerism is in file 23 (A-level).

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.1.1 to 3.3.1.3.
- Edexcel 6.1 to 6.7: includes classifying reactions as addition, elimination, substitution, oxidation, reduction and polymerisation (6.5), and E/Z and cis–trans isomerism with CIP rules (6.7).
- OCR A 4.1.1(a) to (i) and 4.1.3(c), (d): adds the terms aliphatic, alicyclic and aromatic, and homolytic and heterolytic fission.
- OCR B DF(b), (c), (l), (m), (r) to (t) and WM(a): σ and π bonds and shapes in organic molecules, formula types, structural and stereoisomerism, and the main homologous series.
- Eduqas C3.1(a) to (e) and C3.2(f): formulae, naming, effect of chain length and functional groups on physical properties, structural isomerism, electrophiles, nucleophiles and radicals, E/Z isomerism.

### 3.3.1.1 Nomenclature

*Refs: AQA 3.3.1.1 · Edexcel 6.1 to 6.4 · OCR A 4.1.1(a) to (d) · OCR B DF(l), DF(m), DF(r), WM(a) · Eduqas C3.1(a) to (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Types of formula: empirical, molecular, general, structural, displayed, skeletal.
- Homologous series and functional groups.
- IUPAC naming of compounds with up to six carbons in the main chain (larger ones for some boards), with one or more functional groups.
- Terms: saturated, unsaturated, aliphatic, alicyclic, aromatic.

#### Explained

**Formulae for butan-1-ol.**

| Type | Example | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Molecular | C₄H₁₀O | Actual number of atoms |
| Empirical | C₄H₁₀O | Simplest ratio |
| General | CₙH₂ₙ₊₁OH | Algebraic formula for the series |
| Structural | CH₃CH₂CH₂CH₂OH | Arrangement of atoms, carbon by carbon |
| Displayed | All atoms and bonds drawn | Every bond shown |
| Skeletal | Zig-zag line ending in OH | Carbon skeleton; C and H on carbon not shown; functional groups shown |

**Homologous series**: a family with the same functional group and general formula; each member differs by CH₂; similar chemical properties and a gradual trend in physical properties. **Functional group**: the group of atoms responsible for the characteristic reactions.

**Other terms.** Saturated: only C–C single bonds. Unsaturated: contains C=C (or C≡C). Aliphatic: carbon atoms in straight or branched chains or non-aromatic rings. Alicyclic: aliphatic arranged in a ring (cyclohexane). Aromatic: contains a benzene ring.

**Naming.**

1. Find the longest carbon chain containing the main functional group: stem meth (1), eth (2), prop (3), but (4), pent (5), hex (6).
2. Add the suffix or prefix for the functional group, with a number for its position, giving the lowest numbers.
3. Name side chains as prefixes (methyl, ethyl) with positions; list prefixes alphabetically; use di, tri for repeats.

| Series | Functional group | Prefix / suffix | Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Alkane | C–C | -ane | propane |
| Alkene | C=C | -ene | but-2-ene |
| Halogenoalkane | –X | chloro-, bromo-, iodo- | 2-bromopropane |
| Alcohol | –OH | -ol (hydroxy-) | propan-2-ol |
| Aldehyde | –CHO | -al | ethanal |
| Ketone | C=O in the chain | -one | butanone |
| Carboxylic acid | –COOH | -oic acid | propanoic acid |
| Ester | –COO– | -yl -oate | methyl ethanoate |
| Acyl chloride | –COCl | -oyl chloride | ethanoyl chloride |
| Amine | –NH₂ | -amine (amino-) | ethylamine / ethanamine |
| Nitrile | –C≡N | -nitrile | propanenitrile (C counted) |
| Amide | –CONH₂ | -amide | ethanamide |

When there are two groups, the higher-priority one gives the suffix: carboxylic acid > ester > acyl chloride > amide > nitrile > aldehyde > ketone > alcohol > amine > alkene > halogenoalkane. For example, HOCH₂CH₂COOH is 3-hydroxypropanoic acid.

**Physical properties (Eduqas C3.1(c)).** Boiling points rise with chain length (more London forces) and fall with branching. Polar functional groups raise boiling points (dipole–dipole forces), and –OH, –COOH and –NH₂ much more so (hydrogen bonds). Short-chain alcohols, acids and amines dissolve in water; solubility falls as the chain grows.

#### Worked example

Name CH₃CH(CH₃)CH(Cl)CH₃.

Longest chain: 4 carbons (butane). Substituents: methyl and chloro on carbons 2 and 3, or 3 and 2. Lowest set: 2,3 either way; alphabetical first gets the lower number when there is a tie: 2-chloro-3-methylbutane.

#### Common mistakes

- Choosing a chain that is not the longest one through the functional group.
- Writing commas and hyphens wrongly: numbers are separated by commas, numbers and words by hyphens (2,3-dimethylpentane).
- Counting the carbon of –CHO, –COOH or –CN as extra to the chain. It is part of the chain.

### 3.3.1.2 Reaction mechanisms

*Refs: AQA 3.3.1.2 · Edexcel 6.5 · OCR A 4.1.1(f) to (i) · OCR B DF(b) · Eduqas C3.1(e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Reactions are classified as addition, elimination, substitution, oxidation, reduction, hydrolysis, condensation and polymerisation.
- Homolytic fission forms radicals; heterolytic fission forms ions.
- A radical has an unpaired electron; a nucleophile is an electron pair donor; an electrophile is an electron pair acceptor.
- Curly arrows show the movement of an electron pair. Half-headed arrows (fish hooks) show one electron (not required by all boards).
- σ and π bonds (OCR B, also file 20).

#### Explained

**Bond fission.**

- **Homolytic:** each atom keeps one electron of the bond: Cl–Cl → 2Cl•. Needs energy, often UV light. Gives radicals, which are very reactive.
- **Heterolytic:** one atom takes both electrons: H–Br → H⁺ + Br⁻. Gives ions; the more electronegative atom takes the pair.

**Reagent types.**

| Type | Definition | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nucleophile | Donates a lone pair to form a bond; attracted to δ+ carbon | OH⁻, CN⁻, NH₃, H₂O |
| Electrophile | Accepts a lone pair; attracted to areas of high electron density | H⁺, NO₂⁺, Br₂ (polarised), carbocations |
| Radical | Species with an unpaired electron | Cl•, CH₃• |

**Curly arrows.** An arrow starts from a lone pair or a bond (the source of the electron pair) and ends where the new bond forms or on the atom that receives the pair. Show the relevant lone pairs, δ+ and δ− charges and the full charges of ions.

**σ and π bonds (OCR B DF(b)).** A σ bond is formed by head-on overlap of orbitals between two nuclei. A π bond is formed by sideways overlap of p orbitals above and below the axis. A single bond is one σ bond; a double bond is one σ and one π bond. Shapes in organic molecules: carbon with four single bonds is tetrahedral (109.5°); carbon in C=C or C=O is trigonal planar (120°); carbon in C≡C or C≡N is linear (180°).

**Types of reaction.**

| Type | Example |
| --- | --- |
| Addition | CH₂=CH₂ + Br₂ → CH₂BrCH₂Br |
| Substitution | CH₃CH₂Br + OH⁻ → CH₃CH₂OH + Br⁻ |
| Elimination | CH₃CH₂OH → CH₂=CH₂ + H₂O |
| Oxidation | CH₃CH₂OH + [O] → CH₃CHO + H₂O |
| Reduction | CH₃CHO + 2[H] → CH₃CH₂OH |
| Hydrolysis | Ester + water → acid + alcohol |
| Condensation | Two molecules join, losing a small molecule such as water |
| Polymerisation | Many monomers join |

#### Common mistakes

- Starting a curly arrow from an atom or a charge instead of a lone pair or bond.
- Calling Cl⁻ a radical.
- Confusing nucleophile (attacks δ+ carbon) and electrophile (attacks the C=C electrons).

### 3.3.1.3 Isomerism

*Refs: AQA 3.3.1.3 · Edexcel 6.6, 6.7 · OCR A 4.1.1(e), 4.1.3(c), (d) · OCR B DF(c), DF(s), DF(t) · Eduqas C3.1(d), C3.2(f). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Structural isomers have the same molecular formula but different structural formulae: chain, position and functional group isomers.
- Stereoisomers have the same structural formula but a different arrangement of atoms in space.
- E/Z isomerism arises from restricted rotation about C=C with two different groups on each carbon.
- Cahn–Ingold–Prelog (CIP) priority rules.
- Cis–trans as a special case of E/Z when one group on each carbon is the same.

#### Explained

**Structural isomers of C₄H₁₀O** (examples):

- Chain: butan-1-ol and 2-methylpropan-1-ol.
- Position: butan-1-ol and butan-2-ol.
- Functional group: butan-1-ol and ethoxyethane (an ether, C₂H₅OC₂H₅).

Other functional group pairs: aldehydes and ketones (propanal and propanone), carboxylic acids and esters (propanoic acid and methyl ethanoate), alkenes and cycloalkanes (but-1-ene and cyclobutane).

**E/Z isomerism.** The π bond stops rotation about the C=C. If each carbon of the double bond has two different groups, two isomers exist.

**CIP rules.**

1. On each carbon of the C=C, look at the two atoms directly attached. The atom with the higher atomic number has priority.
2. If they are the same, move along each chain to the first point of difference.
3. If the two higher-priority groups are on the same side, the isomer is Z (zusammen, together). If on opposite sides, it is E (entgegen, opposite).

Example: CH₃CH=CHCl (1-chloropropene). On C1: Cl (17) beats H (1). On C2: CH₃ (C, 6) beats H. If Cl and CH₃ are on the same side, it is Z.

But-2-ene: cis-but-2-ene is Z (both CH₃ on the same side). When the groups compared are the same on both carbons, cis = Z and trans = E. With different groups (for example 1-bromo-2-chloro-1-fluoroethene), cis/trans cannot be used and E/Z is needed.

E/Z isomers can have different physical properties: Z-but-2-ene boils at 4 °C, E-but-2-ene at 1 °C.

#### Worked example

How many structural isomers are there with the formula C₅H₁₂? Draw and name them.

Three: pentane; 2-methylbutane; 2,2-dimethylpropane.

#### Common mistakes

- Claiming E/Z isomers exist for but-1-ene (C1 has two H atoms).
- Using mass of the whole group instead of the atomic number of the first atom attached.
- Drawing the same molecule twice rotated and calling them isomers.

### Check questions

1. Give the skeletal formula and the name of CH₃CH₂CH(OH)CH₃. (2 marks)
2. Name (CH₃)₂CHCH₂CHO. (1 mark)
3. Define the term homologous series. (2 marks)
4. Explain the difference between homolytic and heterolytic fission, giving an example of each. (4 marks)
5. Classify each reagent as nucleophile, electrophile or radical: NH₃, NO₂⁺, Br•, CN⁻. (2 marks)
6. Draw and name a chain isomer and a position isomer of pentan-1-ol. (2 marks)
7. Explain why pent-2-ene has E/Z isomers but pent-1-ene does not. (2 marks)
8. Assign E or Z to the isomer of 2-bromobut-2-ene in which Br and the CH₃ on carbon 3 are on the same side. Explain using CIP rules. (3 marks)
9. State the number of σ and π bonds in a molecule of propene. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Skeletal zig-zag of four carbons with OH on carbon 2 (1); butan-2-ol (1).
2. 3-methylbutanal (1).
3. Same functional group and general formula (1); each member differs by CH₂; similar chemical properties / trend in physical properties (1).
4. Homolytic: each atom gets one electron of the bonding pair, forming radicals (1); e.g. Cl₂ → 2Cl• in UV light (1). Heterolytic: one atom gets both electrons, forming ions (1); e.g. HBr → H⁺ + Br⁻ / C–Br bond in nucleophilic substitution (1).
5. NH₃ and CN⁻ nucleophiles (1); NO₂⁺ electrophile, Br• radical (1).
6. Chain: e.g. 2-methylbutan-1-ol / 3-methylbutan-1-ol (1). Position: pentan-2-ol / pentan-3-ol (1).
7. Restricted rotation about C=C (1); in pent-1-ene carbon 1 has two identical groups (H, H), so no isomers; in pent-2-ene each carbon has two different groups (1).
8. On C2: Br has higher priority than CH₃ (1); on C3: CH₃ has higher priority than H (1); same side, so Z (1).
9. 8 σ bonds (6 C–H, 2 C–C) (1); 1 π bond (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/04-energetics.md -->

## Energetics: enthalpy changes, calorimetry, Hess's law and bond enthalpies

Reactions give out or take in heat. This file covers enthalpy change, the standard enthalpy definitions, measuring enthalpy changes by calorimetry, Hess's law cycles, and mean bond enthalpies. It includes AQA required practical 2.

### Overview

AS content for every board. Born–Haber cycles, enthalpies of solution and entropy follow in file 8 (A-level). Activation energy and reaction profiles connect to file 5.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.4.1 to 3.1.4.4 and RP2.
- Edexcel 8.1 to 8.11 and CP8 (finding an enthalpy change that cannot be measured directly, using Hess's law). Edexcel defines standard enthalpy change of atomisation in 8.4 as well.
- OCR A 3.2.1(a) to (h), with enthalpy change of neutralisation (3.2.1(d)(v)) and activation energy on enthalpy profile diagrams (3.2.1(b), (c)).
- OCR B DF(d) to (g) in the Developing fuels storyline: measuring enthalpy of combustion of fuels, and bond enthalpies.
- Eduqas C2.2(a) to (e) and specified practicals: an indirect determination using Hess's law, and an enthalpy change of combustion.

### 3.1.4.1 Enthalpy change

*Refs: AQA 3.1.4.1 · Edexcel 8.1 to 8.4 · OCR A 3.2.1(a) to (d) · OCR B DF(d) · Eduqas C2.2(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Enthalpy change, ΔH, is the heat energy change measured at constant pressure.
- Exothermic reactions have negative ΔH; endothermic reactions have positive ΔH.
- Standard conditions: 100 kPa and a stated temperature, usually 298 K. Substances in their standard states.
- Definitions of standard enthalpy of combustion, ΔcH⦵, and standard enthalpy of formation, ΔfH⦵ (AQA). Others: reaction, neutralisation, atomisation (other boards).
- Enthalpy profile (level) diagrams with activation energy.

#### Explained

**Sign convention.** Exothermic: the system loses energy to the surroundings, the surroundings warm up, ΔH is negative. Endothermic: the system takes energy in, the surroundings cool, ΔH is positive.

**Definitions** (learn the wording exactly in meaning):

| Term | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| ΔrH⦵ | Enthalpy change when the amounts in the equation react under standard conditions, all in standard states |
| ΔfH⦵ | Enthalpy change when one mole of a compound is formed from its elements in their standard states under standard conditions |
| ΔcH⦵ | Enthalpy change when one mole of a substance is completely burned in oxygen under standard conditions, all in standard states |
| ΔneutH⦵ | Enthalpy change when acid and alkali react to form one mole of water under standard conditions |
| ΔatH⦵ | Enthalpy change when one mole of gaseous atoms is formed from the element in its standard state |

ΔfH⦵ of an element in its standard state is zero by definition.

Example equations:

- ΔfH⦵ of ethanol: 2C(s) + 3H₂(g) + ½O₂(g) → C₂H₅OH(l).
- ΔcH⦵ of ethanol: C₂H₅OH(l) + 3O₂(g) → 2CO₂(g) + 3H₂O(l).
- ΔatH⦵ of chlorine: ½Cl₂(g) → Cl(g).

**Enthalpy profile diagrams** show reactants and products as horizontal lines with ΔH as the vertical gap. A hump between them shows the activation energy, Eₐ: the minimum energy needed for a reaction to happen. For an exothermic reaction the products are below the reactants.

#### Common mistakes

- Defining ΔfH⦵ for "one mole of product" without "from its elements in their standard states".
- Writing ΔcH⦵ equations with water as a gas. Under standard conditions water is a liquid.
- Using fractions wrongly: one mole of the substance of interest must appear, so other species may need fractions.

### 3.1.4.2 Calorimetry

*Refs: AQA 3.1.4.2, RP2 · Edexcel 8.5, 8.6, CP8 · OCR A 3.2.1(e), (h) · OCR B DF(f), DF(g) · Eduqas C2.2(d), (e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- q = mcΔT, where m is the mass of the substance heated (usually water or solution), c its specific heat capacity, ΔT the temperature change.
- ΔH = −q ÷ n, in kJ mol⁻¹, with n the moles of the limiting reactant (or of fuel burned).
- Methods for reactions in solution and for combustion, and their sources of error.
- Using a cooling curve to correct ΔT.

#### Explained

**Reactions in solution.** Use a polystyrene cup with a lid (to cut heat loss) in a beaker for stability. Measure the starting temperature of each solution, mix, stir, and record the temperature every 30 seconds. Assume the density of the solution is 1.00 g cm⁻³ and c = 4.18 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹.

**Combustion.** Burn a fuel in a spirit burner under a copper can of water. Weigh the burner before and after to find the mass burned. Measure the temperature rise of the water. Shield from draughts. Large heat losses and incomplete combustion make the result far less exothermic than data-book values.

**Correcting for heat loss (extrapolation).** Record the temperature for a few minutes before mixing, then after. Plot temperature against time and extend the cooling line back to the moment of mixing. The difference between this extrapolated value and the starting temperature is a better ΔT, because it estimates the rise if no heat were lost.

**Sources of error.** Heat lost to surroundings (the biggest), heat absorbed by the container and thermometer, incomplete combustion (soot on the can), evaporation of fuel from the wick, assuming the solution has the same c and density as water.

#### Worked example

1. 50.0 cm³ of 1.00 mol dm⁻³ HCl and 50.0 cm³ of 1.00 mol dm⁻³ NaOH are mixed. The temperature rises by 6.8 K. Find ΔneutH.

   q = 100 × 4.18 × 6.8 = 2842 J. n(H₂O) = 0.0500 mol. ΔH = −2842 ÷ 0.0500 = −56 800 J mol⁻¹ = −56.8 kJ mol⁻¹.

2. Burning 0.46 g of ethanol heats 200 g of water from 20.0 °C to 35.5 °C. Find ΔcH.

   q = 200 × 4.18 × 15.5 = 12 958 J = 12.96 kJ. n = 0.46 ÷ 46.0 = 0.0100 mol. ΔcH = −12.96 ÷ 0.0100 = −1296 kJ mol⁻¹ (−1.3 × 10³ kJ mol⁻¹). The data value is −1367 kJ mol⁻¹; heat losses explain the difference.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the mass of the fuel or solid in mcΔT. Use the mass of the water or solution.
- Forgetting the negative sign for an exothermic reaction.
- Forgetting to convert J to kJ.
- Using the total volume of one solution only, instead of both added together.

#### Practical

**AQA RP2: measurement of an enthalpy change.** Typical task: displacement (zinc powder and copper(II) sulfate), neutralisation, or dissolving. Use excess of the solid so the solution is limiting. Plot temperature against time and extrapolate. Percentage uncertainty in a thermometer reading of ±0.5 °C on a rise of 6.8 °C: each reading contributes, so ±1.0 °C over ΔT, giving 15%. Use a larger rise (more concentrated solutions) or a more precise thermometer to reduce it.

**Edexcel CP8 and Eduqas indirect determination.** Find ΔH for a reaction that cannot be measured directly, for example the thermal decomposition of potassium hydrogencarbonate, or MgO + CO₂ → MgCO₃, by measuring the enthalpy changes of two reactions with hydrochloric acid and combining them in a Hess cycle (see 3.1.4.3).

**Eduqas: enthalpy change of combustion** of a series of alcohols with a spirit burner and calorimeter.

### 3.1.4.3 Applications of Hess's law

*Refs: AQA 3.1.4.3 · Edexcel 8.7, 8.8, CP8 · OCR A 3.2.1(g) · OCR B DF(g) · Eduqas C2.2(b), (d). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Hess's law: the enthalpy change for a reaction is independent of the route taken, provided the initial and final conditions are the same.
- Construct cycles and calculate ΔH from enthalpies of formation, from enthalpies of combustion, and from experimental data.

#### Explained

Hess's law follows from conservation of energy. If you could go round a cycle and gain energy, you would have made energy from nothing.

**From enthalpies of formation:** ΔrH = ΣΔfH(products) − ΣΔfH(reactants). The arrows in the cycle point up from the elements to both reactants and products.

**From enthalpies of combustion:** ΔrH = ΣΔcH(reactants) − ΣΔcH(products). The arrows point down from both reactants and products to the combustion products.

Multiply each value by the number of moles in the equation.

#### Worked example

1. Find ΔrH for C₂H₄(g) + H₂O(g) → C₂H₅OH(l). Data (kJ mol⁻¹): ΔfH C₂H₄ +52, H₂O(g) −242, C₂H₅OH(l) −277.

   ΔrH = −277 − (+52 + (−242)) = −277 + 190 = −87 kJ mol⁻¹.

2. Find ΔfH of methane. ΔcH (kJ mol⁻¹): C(s) −394, H₂(g) −286, CH₄(g) −890.

   C + 2H₂ → CH₄. ΔfH = [−394 + 2(−286)] − (−890) = −966 + 890 = −76 kJ mol⁻¹.

3. (Indirect method) MgO + 2HCl → MgCl₂ + H₂O, ΔH₁ = −146 kJ mol⁻¹. MgCO₃ + 2HCl → MgCl₂ + H₂O + CO₂, ΔH₂ = −98 kJ mol⁻¹. Find ΔH for MgCO₃ → MgO + CO₂.

   ΔH = ΔH₂ − ΔH₁ = −98 − (−146) = +48 kJ mol⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Reversing the subtraction for combustion data.
- Forgetting the stoichiometric multipliers.
- Including elements in their standard states with a non-zero ΔfH.

### 3.1.4.4 Bond enthalpies

*Refs: AQA 3.1.4.4 · Edexcel 8.9 to 8.11 · OCR A 3.2.1(f) · OCR B DF(e) · Eduqas C2.2(c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Mean bond enthalpy: the enthalpy needed to break one mole of a covalent bond in gaseous molecules, averaged over many compounds.
- Breaking bonds is endothermic; making bonds is exothermic.
- ΔH = Σ(bonds broken) − Σ(bonds made).
- Why values calculated from mean bond enthalpies differ from data values.

#### Explained

A C–H bond in methane is not quite the same as one in ethanol, so tables list an average. Results from mean bond enthalpies are therefore approximate. They are also only valid for gases; if a substance is a liquid, energy to vaporise it is ignored.

A reaction is exothermic if the bonds made release more energy than the bonds broken take in.

#### Worked example

1. Use mean bond enthalpies to find ΔH for CH₄(g) + 2O₂(g) → CO₂(g) + 2H₂O(g). Data (kJ mol⁻¹): C–H 413, O=O 498, C=O 805, O–H 464.

   Broken: 4(413) + 2(498) = 1652 + 996 = 2648. Made: 2(805) + 4(464) = 1610 + 1856 = 3466. ΔH = 2648 − 3466 = −818 kJ mol⁻¹.

2. Find the mean N–H bond enthalpy given ΔfH(NH₃, g) = −46 kJ mol⁻¹, N≡N 945 and H–H 436 kJ mol⁻¹.

   ½N₂ + 1½H₂ → NH₃: broken = 472.5 + 654 = 1126.5. −46 = 1126.5 − 3E(N–H). E = 390.8 ≈ 391 kJ mol⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Doing made − broken.
- Forgetting that a double bond (O=O, C=O) is a single entry with its own value.
- Comparing a value calculated for gaseous water with a data value for liquid water and not commenting on it.

### Check questions

1. Define standard enthalpy of formation. (2 marks)
2. Write an equation, with state symbols, for the standard enthalpy of combustion of propane. (2 marks)
3. 25.0 cm³ of 2.00 mol dm⁻³ copper(II) sulfate reacts with excess zinc. The temperature rises from 21.0 °C to 66.5 °C. Calculate ΔH for the reaction in kJ mol⁻¹. (3 marks)
4. Explain how a temperature–time graph gives a more accurate value of ΔT. (2 marks)
5. Give two reasons why an experimental enthalpy of combustion of a fuel is less negative than the data book value. (2 marks)
6. Use ΔfH values (kJ mol⁻¹) to find ΔrH for 2NO₂(g) → N₂O₄(g): NO₂ +33, N₂O₄ +9. (2 marks)
7. Use ΔcH values (kJ mol⁻¹): C(s) −394, H₂(g) −286, C₃H₈(g) −2220 to find ΔfH of propane. (3 marks)
8. Using mean bond enthalpies (kJ mol⁻¹: C=C 612, C–C 348, C–H 413, H–H 436), find ΔH for C₂H₄ + H₂ → C₂H₆. (3 marks)
9. Explain why your answer to question 8 differs from the data value. (1 mark)

### Mark scheme

1. Enthalpy change when one mole of a compound is formed from its elements (1); in their standard states under standard conditions (1).
2. C₃H₈(g) + 5O₂(g) → 3CO₂(g) + 4H₂O(l) (1 balanced; 1 state symbols).
3. q = 25.0 × 4.18 × 45.5 = 4755 J (1); n(CuSO₄) = 0.0500 mol (1); ΔH = −95.1 kJ mol⁻¹ (1).
4. Extrapolate the cooling line back to the time of mixing (1); allows for heat lost while the temperature was rising (1).
5. Any two: heat lost to surroundings; heat absorbed by apparatus; incomplete combustion; evaporation of fuel; not standard conditions.
6. ΔrH = 9 − 2(33) (1) = −57 kJ mol⁻¹ (1).
7. 3C + 4H₂ → C₃H₈ (1); ΔfH = 3(−394) + 4(−286) − (−2220) (1) = −106 kJ mol⁻¹ (1).
8. Broken: C=C + 4 C–H + H–H = 612 + 1652 + 436 = 2700 (1); made: C–C + 6 C–H = 348 + 2478 = 2826 (1); ΔH = −126 kJ mol⁻¹ (1).
9. Mean bond enthalpies are averages over many compounds, not the exact values in these molecules (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/05-kinetics.md -->

## Kinetics: collision theory, Maxwell–Boltzmann and catalysts

Kinetics is about how fast reactions go. This file covers collision theory, the Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution, the effects of temperature, concentration, pressure and surface area, measuring rates, and how catalysts work. It includes AQA required practical 3.

### Overview

AS content for every board. Rate equations, orders and the Arrhenius equation are in file 9 (A-level). Industrial catalysts using transition metals are in file 15.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.5.1 to 3.1.5.5 and RP3.
- Edexcel 9.1 to 9.9 add calculating rate from graphs (9.3), heterogeneous catalysis on surfaces (9.7, 9.8) and the economic benefits of catalysts (9.9).
- OCR A 3.2.2(a) to (g) add homogeneous and heterogeneous catalysts with examples, and the sustainability benefits of catalysts (3.2.2(d)).
- OCR B DF(h), DF(i) (catalysts and a model of heterogeneous catalysis, including catalyst poisons) and OZ(e) to (h) (activation enthalpy, concentration and pressure, homogeneous catalysis by radicals in the ozone layer).
- Eduqas C2.3(a) to (f), (h) and specified practicals: rate by gas collection, and an iodine clock reaction. Colorimetry (C2.3(g)) is in file 9.

### 3.1.5.1 Collision theory

*Refs: AQA 3.1.5.1 · Edexcel 9.1, 9.2 · OCR A 3.2.2(a) · OCR B OZ(e), OZ(f) · Eduqas C2.3(a), (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Reactions happen only when particles collide with enough energy (at least the activation energy) and in the right orientation.
- Activation energy, Eₐ, is the minimum energy for a reaction to occur.
- Most collisions do not lead to reaction.

#### Explained

Rate of reaction is the change in concentration of a reactant or product per unit time (mol dm⁻³ s⁻¹). It depends on the frequency of **successful** collisions. Anything that makes collisions more frequent, or gives a larger fraction of them enough energy, speeds up the reaction.

The energy is needed to start breaking bonds. Even exothermic reactions need it, which is why a match is needed to light methane.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying "more collisions" without "per unit time" or "more frequent".
- Leaving out orientation or energy when defining a successful collision.

### 3.1.5.2 Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution

*Refs: AQA 3.1.5.2 · Edexcel 9.4 · OCR A 3.2.2(f) · Eduqas C2.3(d). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution shows the spread of molecular energies in a gas.
- Draw and explain the curve; mark Eₐ on it.

#### Explained

The curve plots number of molecules against energy.

- It starts at the origin: no molecules have zero energy.
- It rises to a peak (the most probable energy), then falls.
- It never touches the energy axis at high energy (asymptote): a few molecules have very high energies.
- The area under the curve equals the total number of molecules.
- The area to the right of Eₐ is the number of molecules with enough energy to react.

The mean energy is slightly to the right of the peak.

#### Common mistakes

- Starting the curve above zero or making it touch the axis at high energy.
- Drawing the curve symmetrical like a bell.

### 3.1.5.3 Effect of temperature on reaction rate

*Refs: AQA 3.1.5.3, RP3 · Edexcel 9.1, 9.4 · OCR A 3.2.2(g) · Eduqas C2.3(d), (h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A small rise in temperature gives a large increase in rate.
- Explain with the Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution.
- Draw the higher-temperature curve.

#### Explained

At a higher temperature the curve shifts to the right and becomes flatter and lower, with the same area (same number of molecules). The peak moves to higher energy.

The key point: **many more molecules have energy at least Eₐ.** The area to the right of Eₐ grows much more than the average energy does. That is why a 10 K rise can roughly double the rate. Collisions are also slightly more frequent because molecules move faster, but this is a small effect compared with the energy change.

#### Common mistakes

- Making the higher-temperature curve taller.
- Saying the main reason is "more collisions". It is that more collisions have enough energy.
- Moving Eₐ. Temperature does not change the activation energy.

#### Practical

**AQA RP3: investigate how the rate of a reaction changes with temperature.** A common method is sodium thiosulfate with hydrochloric acid: Na₂S₂O₃ + 2HCl → 2NaCl + SO₂ + S + H₂O. The sulfur makes the mixture cloudy. Time how long it takes for a cross under the flask to disappear. Warm the thiosulfate in a water bath to the chosen temperature before adding the acid, and measure the temperature of the mixture. Rate ∝ 1/time. Keep the volumes and concentrations constant. The end point is judged by eye, so use the same observer, lighting and cross. Work in a ventilated room because SO₂ is toxic.

**Eduqas: rate by gas collection.** Collect the gas (for example CO₂ from marble chips and acid, or H₂ from magnesium and acid) in a gas syringe and record the volume every 10 to 30 seconds. Alternatively place the flask on a balance and record the mass loss.

**Eduqas: iodine clock.** In one version, hydrogen peroxide oxidises iodide ions to iodine in acid. A small, fixed amount of thiosulfate turns the iodine back into iodide as fast as it forms. When the thiosulfate is used up, iodine builds up and turns starch blue-black suddenly. The time taken is the time to make a fixed amount of iodine, so 1/t is proportional to the initial rate.

### 3.1.5.4 Effect of concentration and pressure

*Refs: AQA 3.1.5.4 · Edexcel 9.1, 9.3 · OCR A 3.2.2(a), (b) · OCR B OZ(f) · Eduqas C2.3(a), (b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Higher concentration (solutions) or higher pressure (gases) increases collision frequency, so increases rate.
- Larger surface area of a solid also increases rate (Edexcel, OCR, Eduqas).
- Calculate rate from the gradient of a concentration–time (or volume–time) graph.

#### Explained

More particles in the same volume means more frequent collisions, so more successful collisions per second. The fraction of collisions with enough energy does not change.

For a solid, only particles on the surface can react. Powder has a much bigger surface area than lumps of the same mass.

**Rate from a graph.** On a graph of concentration (or volume of gas) against time, the rate at any time is the gradient of the tangent at that point. The initial rate is the gradient of the tangent at t = 0. The rate falls as the reaction proceeds because the reactant concentration falls. If a measurement is taken over a whole reaction, mean rate = change ÷ time.

#### Worked example

A tangent drawn to a volume–time curve at 20 s passes through (0 s, 12 cm³) and (50 s, 62 cm³). Find the rate at 20 s.

Gradient = (62 − 12) ÷ (50 − 0) = 1.0 cm³ s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying that higher concentration gives particles more energy.
- Drawing a tangent that cuts the curve instead of touching it.

### 3.1.5.5 Catalysts

*Refs: AQA 3.1.5.5 · Edexcel 9.5 to 9.9 · OCR A 3.2.2(c), (d), (g) · OCR B DF(h), DF(i), OZ(g), OZ(h) · Eduqas C2.3(e), (f). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A catalyst increases rate without being used up overall, by providing an alternative reaction route with a lower activation energy.
- Show this on a reaction profile and on a Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution.
- Homogeneous catalysts are in the same phase as the reactants; heterogeneous catalysts are in a different phase.
- How a heterogeneous catalyst works: adsorption, reaction, desorption; catalyst poisoning (OCR B, Edexcel).
- Economic and environmental benefits of catalysts.

#### Explained

**On a reaction profile**, the catalysed route has a lower hump. ΔH is unchanged.

**On a Maxwell–Boltzmann curve**, the curve does not move. Mark a second, lower Eₐ to the left: a larger area lies to its right, so more molecules can react.

A catalyst takes part in the reaction (it forms an intermediate or bonds to the surface) but is regenerated at the end.

**Heterogeneous catalysis.** Usually a solid catalysing gas reactions. Reactant molecules are **adsorbed** on to active sites on the surface, forming weak bonds. Bonds in the reactants weaken and the molecules are held close together in a good orientation. They react, and the products **desorb**, freeing the site. A good catalyst adsorbs strongly enough to weaken bonds but not so strongly that products cannot leave. Catalysts are spread thinly on a support (a ceramic honeycomb in catalytic converters) to give a large surface area and reduce the amount of expensive metal.

**Catalyst poisons** are impurities that adsorb strongly to active sites and block them. Lead in old petrol poisoned catalytic converters; sulfur compounds poison the iron catalyst in the Haber process.

Examples:

| Catalyst | Type | Process |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Iron | Heterogeneous | Haber process, N₂ + 3H₂ ⇌ 2NH₃ |
| Platinum, rhodium, palladium | Heterogeneous | Catalytic converters |
| Nickel | Heterogeneous | Hydrogenation of alkenes |
| Concentrated sulfuric acid | Homogeneous | Esterification |
| Cl• radicals | Homogeneous | Ozone breakdown in the stratosphere (file 35) |
| Zeolites | Heterogeneous | Catalytic cracking |

**Benefits.** Catalysts let reactions run at lower temperatures, saving energy and fuel, cutting CO₂ emissions and costs. They can make a process selective, giving more of the wanted product and less waste, which improves atom economy. Enzymes work at body temperature.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying catalysts "lower the activation energy of the reaction". Better: they provide an alternative route with a lower Eₐ.
- Moving the Maxwell–Boltzmann curve when a catalyst is added.
- Saying catalysts change the yield at equilibrium. They speed up both directions equally (file 6).

### Check questions

1. Define activation energy. (1 mark)
2. Sketch a Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution for a gas at temperature T and add a second curve at a higher temperature. Label the axes. (3 marks)
3. Use your sketch to explain why a small temperature rise causes a large rise in rate. (2 marks)
4. Explain why the rate of reaction between magnesium ribbon and hydrochloric acid decreases as the reaction proceeds. (2 marks)
5. Explain why powdered calcium carbonate reacts faster than lumps of the same mass with hydrochloric acid. (2 marks)
6. Explain how a heterogeneous catalyst speeds up a gas reaction. (3 marks)
7. Give two benefits of using a catalyst in an industrial process. (2 marks)
8. In the thiosulfate and acid reaction, a student found that the cross disappeared after 64 s at 20 °C and 32 s at 30 °C. Calculate the relative rate at each temperature and comment. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. The minimum energy needed for a reaction to occur / for a collision to be successful (1).
2. Axes: number of molecules (y), energy (x) (1); curve starts at origin, peak, does not touch x-axis at high energy (1); higher T curve peak lower and to the right, same area (1).
3. Many more molecules have energy greater than or equal to Eₐ (1); so more frequent successful collisions (1).
4. Concentration of acid decreases (1); fewer collisions per unit time / less frequent collisions (1).
5. Larger surface area (1); more frequent collisions (1).
6. Reactants adsorb on to active sites on the surface (1); bonds weaken / molecules held in suitable orientation, so lower Eₐ route (1); products desorb (1).
7. Any two: lower temperature so less energy / fuel used; lower costs; less CO₂ emitted; faster production; higher selectivity / less waste.
8. Rate = 1/t: 0.0156 s⁻¹ and 0.0313 s⁻¹ (1); rate roughly doubles for a 10 °C rise (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/18-alkanes.md -->

## Alkanes: crude oil, cracking, combustion and radical substitution

Alkanes are saturated hydrocarbons and the main fuels. This file covers their structure and properties, fractional distillation of crude oil, thermal and catalytic cracking, complete and incomplete combustion, pollutants and catalytic converters, alternative fuels, and the free-radical substitution of alkanes by chlorine.

### Overview

AS content. Ozone depletion by chlorine radicals is in file 19; the wider environmental story (greenhouse gases, ozone) is in file 35.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.2.1 to 3.3.2.4.
- Edexcel 6.8 to 6.17: adds cycloalkanes (6.9), reforming (6.10), alternative fuels including biodiesel and alcohols (6.14), and the limits of radical substitution (6.17). Also bromine in radical substitution.
- OCR A 4.1.2(a) to (g): adds the tetrahedral shape and σ bonds, and the limits of radical substitution in synthesis.
- OCR B DF(j), (k), (n), (u) (cracking, pollutants, combustion equations, benefits and risks of fuels) and OZ(o), (p) (radicals and their formation).
- Eduqas C3.2(a) to (c): combustion with benefits and drawbacks of fossil fuels, σ bonds, and the radical substitution mechanism.

### 3.3.2.1 Fractional distillation of crude oil

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2.1 · Edexcel 6.8 to 6.10 · OCR A 4.1.2(a) to (d) · Eduqas C3.2(b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Alkanes are saturated hydrocarbons, general formula CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ (cycloalkanes CₙH₂ₙ).
- Each carbon is tetrahedral with σ bonds; bond angle 109.5°.
- Crude oil is a mixture mostly of alkanes, separated by fractional distillation.
- Boiling points rise with chain length; branched chains boil lower.
- Alkanes are unreactive: C–C and C–H bonds are strong and non-polar.

#### Explained

**Fractional distillation.** Crude oil is heated until most of it vaporises and fed into the bottom of a fractionating column, which is hot at the bottom and cooler at the top. Vapours rise; each fraction condenses at the level where the temperature falls below its boiling point and is drawn off. Short-chain alkanes (low boiling point) leave at the top as gases (refinery gas); long-chain ones condense lower (petrol, naphtha, kerosene, diesel, then fuel oil); the residue (bitumen) leaves at the bottom.

**Boiling point trend.** Longer chains have more electrons and more surface contact, so stronger London forces. Branched isomers cannot pack as closely, so have fewer points of contact and weaker London forces: 2,2-dimethylpropane boils at 10 °C; pentane at 36 °C.

**Reactivity.** Alkanes have strong C–C and C–H σ bonds with almost no polarity (C and H have similar electronegativities), so they do not attract nucleophiles or electrophiles. They burn and react with halogens in UV light.

**Reforming (Edexcel 6.10).** Straight-chain alkanes are converted to branched alkanes and cyclic hydrocarbons (including arenes) using a platinum catalyst. These burn more smoothly in engines (less knocking).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying fractions boil off at the top. Each condenses at its level.
- Explaining boiling points with "stronger bonds". The C–C bonds do not break when an alkane boils.

### 3.3.2.2 Modification of alkanes by cracking

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2.2 · Edexcel 6.10 · OCR B DF(j) · Eduqas C3.2(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Cracking breaks C–C bonds in long-chain alkanes to make shorter, more useful alkanes and alkenes.
- Thermal cracking: high pressure and high temperature; mainly alkenes.
- Catalytic cracking: slight pressure, high temperature, zeolite catalyst; mainly motor fuels and aromatic hydrocarbons.
- Economic reasons for cracking.

#### Explained

Fractional distillation gives more long-chain fractions than are needed, and too few short-chain ones (petrol, which is in high demand). Cracking matches supply to demand and also makes alkenes, which are feedstocks for polymers and other chemicals.

| | Thermal cracking | Catalytic cracking |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Conditions | 700 to 1200 K, up to 7000 kPa | About 720 K, slight pressure, zeolite catalyst |
| Main products | High proportion of alkenes | Branched alkanes, cycloalkanes, aromatics: motor fuels |
| Mechanism | Radicals | Carbocations (not needed) |

Example: C₁₀H₂₂ → C₈H₁₈ + C₂H₄. There must be at least one alkene, because an alkane has the highest H : C ratio.

**Catalysts (OCR B DF(j)).** Zeolites are aluminosilicates with a honeycomb structure and a large surface area. They lower the temperature needed, saving energy.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing a cracking equation with only alkane products.
- Mixing up the conditions of the two types.

### 3.3.2.3 Combustion of alkanes

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2.3 · Edexcel 6.11 to 6.14 · OCR A 4.1.2(e) · OCR B DF(k), DF(n), DF(u) · Eduqas C3.2(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Alkanes are used as fuels.
- Complete and incomplete combustion; products and their problems.
- Pollutants: CO, NOₓ, SO₂, carbon particulates, unburnt hydrocarbons.
- Catalytic converters remove CO, NOₓ and unburnt hydrocarbons.
- SO₂ removal from flue gases using CaO or CaCO₃.
- Edexcel: alternative fuels (biodiesel, alcohols) and carbon neutrality.

#### Explained

**Complete combustion** (plenty of oxygen): C₈H₁₈ + 12½O₂ → 8CO₂ + 9H₂O.

**Incomplete combustion** (limited oxygen): C₈H₁₈ + 8½O₂ → 8CO + 9H₂O; or soot: CH₄ + O₂ → C + 2H₂O.

**Pollutants.**

| Pollutant | Source | Problem |
| --- | --- | --- |
| CO | Incomplete combustion | Toxic: binds to haemoglobin, reducing oxygen transport |
| Carbon particulates (soot) | Incomplete combustion | Breathing problems, cancer, global dimming |
| Unburnt hydrocarbons | Engines | Photochemical smog, with NOₓ |
| NOₓ | N₂ and O₂ react at the high temperature in engines: N₂ + O₂ → 2NO; 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂ | Acid rain, photochemical smog, toxic |
| SO₂ | Sulfur impurities in fuels | Acid rain: SO₂ + ½O₂ + H₂O → H₂SO₄ |
| CO₂ | All combustion | Greenhouse gas: global warming (file 35) |

**Catalytic converters** in car exhausts: honeycomb ceramic coated in platinum, palladium and rhodium.

- 2CO + 2NO → 2CO₂ + N₂
- C₈H₁₈ + 25NO → 8CO₂ + 12½N₂ + 9H₂O
- 2CO + O₂ → 2CO₂

**Flue gas desulfurisation.** In power stations, SO₂ is removed by reacting with calcium oxide or calcium carbonate slurry: CaO + SO₂ → CaSO₃; CaCO₃ + SO₂ → CaSO₃ + CO₂. Calcium sulfite is oxidised to calcium sulfate (gypsum), used for plasterboard.

**Alternative fuels (Edexcel 6.14, OCR B DF(u)).**

- **Biodiesel** is made by reacting vegetable oils (triglycerides) with methanol to form methyl esters. **Bioethanol** is made by fermenting sugars.
- They are renewable. They are described as carbon neutral because the CO₂ released when they burn was absorbed by the plants as they grew. In practice they are not fully carbon neutral, because fossil fuels are used in farming, fertiliser production, processing and transport.
- Drawbacks: they need land that could grow food, may lead to deforestation, and need large amounts of water and fertiliser.
- Hydrogen is another option (file 10).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying catalytic converters remove CO₂. They make CO₂.
- Saying NOₓ comes from nitrogen in the fuel. It comes from nitrogen in the air.
- Calling biofuels completely carbon neutral without qualification.

### 3.3.2.4 Chlorination of alkanes

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2.4 · Edexcel 6.15 to 6.17 · OCR A 4.1.2(f), (g) · OCR B OZ(o), OZ(p) · Eduqas C3.2(c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Alkanes react with chlorine (or bromine) in UV light by free-radical substitution.
- The mechanism: initiation, propagation, termination.
- The reaction gives a mixture of products, which limits its use in synthesis.

#### Explained

**Mechanism for methane and chlorine** (overall CH₄ + Cl₂ → CH₃Cl + HCl).

*Initiation.* UV light breaks the Cl–Cl bond by homolytic fission:

Cl₂ → 2Cl•

*Propagation.* A radical is used and a new one is made, so the chain continues:

Cl• + CH₄ → HCl + •CH₃
•CH₃ + Cl₂ → CH₃Cl + Cl•

*Termination.* Two radicals join; radicals are removed:

Cl• + Cl• → Cl₂
•CH₃ + Cl• → CH₃Cl
•CH₃ + •CH₃ → C₂H₆

Why UV? The Cl–Cl bond (243 kJ mol⁻¹) is weaker than C–H (413), so UV light has enough energy to break Cl–Cl. Bromine reacts in the same way.

**Further substitution.** With excess chlorine, CH₃Cl reacts further:

Cl• + CH₃Cl → HCl + •CH₂Cl
•CH₂Cl + Cl₂ → CH₂Cl₂ + Cl•

and so on to CHCl₃ and CCl₄.

**Limitations.**

- A mixture of mono-, di-, tri- and tetrachloroalkanes forms.
- For longer alkanes, substitution happens at any carbon, giving position isomers (propane gives both 1-chloropropane and 2-chloropropane).
- Termination makes longer chains (ethane from methane).
- Products must be separated by fractional distillation, so yield of any one product is low. Using excess alkane favours the monosubstituted product.

#### Common mistakes

- Showing H• as a product of propagation. The Cl• takes an H atom to form HCl.
- Writing initiation with a curly arrow drawn as a full arrow for both electrons; homolytic fission uses half-headed arrows if arrows are drawn.
- Forgetting to write the dot for the unpaired electron.

### Check questions

1. Explain why the boiling point of hexane is higher than that of 2,2-dimethylbutane. (3 marks)
2. Give two reasons why cracking is carried out. (2 marks)
3. Write an equation for the cracking of C₁₂H₂₆ to give octane and one other product. (1 mark)
4. Give the conditions and main type of product of catalytic cracking. (2 marks)
5. Write an equation for the incomplete combustion of propane to form carbon monoxide. (1 mark)
6. Explain how nitrogen monoxide forms in an engine and write an equation for its removal in a catalytic converter. (3 marks)
7. Explain why biodiesel is not completely carbon neutral. (2 marks)
8. Write the mechanism for the reaction of ethane with bromine in UV light to form bromoethane, naming each stage. (5 marks)
9. Give two reasons why radical substitution is not a good way to make 1-chloropropane. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Hexane straight chain, more surface contact (1); stronger / more London forces (1); more energy needed to overcome (1).
2. Any two: demand for short-chain fractions such as petrol exceeds supply; long-chain fractions in excess; makes alkenes for polymers / feedstock.
3. C₁₂H₂₆ → C₈H₁₈ + 2C₂H₄ (accept C₈H₁₈ + C₄H₈) (1).
4. About 450 °C, slight pressure, zeolite catalyst (1); branched alkanes / aromatic hydrocarbons / motor fuels (1).
5. C₃H₈ + 3½O₂ → 3CO + 4H₂O (1).
6. High temperature in engine (1); N₂ + O₂ from air react: N₂ + O₂ → 2NO (1); 2CO + 2NO → 2CO₂ + N₂ (1).
7. CO₂ absorbed when crops grow equals that released on burning (1); but fossil fuels used in farming / making fertiliser / transport / processing (1).
8. Initiation: Br₂ → 2Br• in UV (1). Propagation: Br• + C₂H₆ → HBr + •C₂H₅ (1); •C₂H₅ + Br₂ → C₂H₅Br + Br• (1). Termination: any two of Br• + Br• → Br₂; •C₂H₅ + Br• → C₂H₅Br; 2•C₂H₅ → C₄H₁₀ (2).
9. Mixture of products / further substitution (1); 2-chloropropane also forms / substitution at any position; low yield, separation needed (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/20-alkenes-and-addition-polymers.md -->

## Alkenes, electrophilic addition and addition polymers

The C=C double bond makes alkenes far more reactive than alkanes. This file covers the σ and π bonds in alkenes, electrophilic addition of bromine, hydrogen halides and sulfuric acid, Markovnikov's rule and carbocation stability, other addition reactions, the bromine water test, addition polymers, and dealing with waste polymers.

### Overview

AS content. E/Z isomerism is in file 17. Condensation polymers are in file 27.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.4.1 to 3.3.4.3.
- Edexcel 6.18 to 6.29: adds oxidation with acidified potassium manganate(VII) to a diol (6.22), and the energy and resource use over the life of polymers, recycling, incineration and feedstock (6.27 to 6.29).
- OCR A 4.1.3(a), (b), (e) to (l): adds hydrogenation with nickel, steam with phosphoric acid, and the sustainability of waste polymer processing and biodegradable or photodegradable polymers (4.1.3(k), (l)).
- OCR B DF(o) to (q): addition reactions, addition polymerisation and the mechanism with the terms electrophile and carbocation.
- Eduqas C3.2(d), (e), (g) to (k): includes the KMnO₄ test, Markovnikov addition of HBr to propene, catalytic hydrogenation conditions and the economic importance of addition polymers.

### 3.3.4.1 Structure, bonding and reactivity

*Refs: AQA 3.3.4.1 · Edexcel 6.18 to 6.21 · OCR A 4.1.3(a), (b), (e), (g) · OCR B DF(q) · Eduqas C3.2(d), (e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Alkenes are unsaturated hydrocarbons, CₙH₂ₙ, with a C=C bond made of one σ and one π bond.
- The C=C is a region of high electron density, so alkenes are attacked by electrophiles.
- The π bond has a lower bond enthalpy than the σ bond, so it breaks more readily.

#### Explained

Each carbon in C=C uses three orbitals for σ bonds (trigonal planar, 120°). The remaining p orbitals overlap sideways above and below the plane to form the π bond. The π electrons are further from the nuclei and exposed, so they attract electrophiles. The π bond also prevents rotation (E/Z isomerism).

C=C bond enthalpy (612 kJ mol⁻¹) is less than twice C–C (348), so the π bond is weaker than the σ bond.

An **electrophile** is an electron pair acceptor: H⁺, Br⁺ (from polarised Br₂), NO₂⁺.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the C=C bond is weaker than C–C. The whole double bond is stronger; the π part is weaker than the σ part.

### 3.3.4.2 Addition reactions of alkenes

*Refs: AQA 3.3.4.2 · Edexcel 6.22 to 6.25 · OCR A 4.1.3(f), (h), (i) · OCR B DF(o), DF(q) · Eduqas C3.2(g) to (j). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Electrophilic addition of HBr, Br₂ and concentrated H₂SO₄; mechanisms.
- Bromine water test for C=C.
- Markovnikov's rule and carbocation stability (primary < secondary < tertiary).
- Hydration with steam (H₃PO₄ catalyst) and hydrogenation (Ni catalyst).
- Edexcel, Eduqas: oxidation by acidified KMnO₄ to a diol (and as a test).

#### Explained

**Bromine.** CH₂=CH₂ + Br₂ → CH₂BrCH₂Br (1,2-dibromoethane).

Mechanism:

1. As Br₂ approaches the C=C, the π electrons repel the electrons in Br–Br, inducing a dipole: Brᵟ⁺–Brᵟ⁻.
2. Curly arrow from the C=C to the δ+ Br; curly arrow from the Br–Br bond to the δ− Br.
3. A carbocation forms (CH₂Br–CH₂⁺) and a Br⁻ ion.
4. Curly arrow from a lone pair on Br⁻ to the positive carbon.

**Test for C=C.** Shake with bromine water: orange to colourless. (In bromine water, some bromohydrin CH₂BrCH₂OH also forms.) Alkanes do not decolorise it without UV.

**Hydrogen bromide.** CH₂=CH₂ + HBr → CH₃CH₂Br. H–Br is permanently polar: H is δ+. Arrow from C=C to H; arrow from H–Br bond to Br; carbocation forms; Br⁻ lone pair attacks the carbocation.

**Unsymmetrical alkenes: Markovnikov's rule.** With propene and HBr, two carbocations could form:

- CH₃CH⁺CH₃ (secondary): more stable.
- CH₃CH₂CH₂⁺ (primary): less stable.

Alkyl groups push electron density towards the positive carbon (positive inductive effect), spreading the charge and stabilising the ion. More alkyl groups, more stable: tertiary > secondary > primary. The more stable carbocation forms faster, so the major product is 2-bromopropane and the minor product 1-bromopropane. In words: the H adds to the carbon that already has more hydrogens.

**Concentrated sulfuric acid.** CH₂=CH₂ + H₂SO₄ → CH₃CH₂OSO₂OH (ethyl hydrogensulfate). Same mechanism with H⁺ from H–O. Adding water then gives ethanol and regenerates the acid: CH₃CH₂OSO₂OH + H₂O → CH₃CH₂OH + H₂SO₄. Sulfuric acid is a catalyst overall.

**Steam.** CH₂=CH₂ + H₂O ⇌ CH₃CH₂OH. Conditions: 300 °C, 60 to 70 atm, phosphoric acid catalyst (file 21).

**Hydrogen.** CH₂=CH₂ + H₂ → CH₃CH₃. Nickel catalyst, about 150 °C (Eduqas C3.2(j)). Used to harden vegetable oils into margarine.

**Other halogens.** Cl₂ adds in the same way to give dichloroalkanes; I₂ adds slowly.

**Acidified potassium manganate(VII) (Edexcel, Eduqas).** Shaken with cold dilute acidified KMnO₄, alkenes are oxidised to diols: CH₂=CH₂ + [O] + H₂O → HOCH₂CH₂OH (ethane-1,2-diol). The purple solution goes colourless. This is another test for C=C.

#### Worked example

Predict the major product of 2-methylpropene with HBr and explain.

The H adds to the CH₂ end, giving the tertiary carbocation (CH₃)₂C⁺CH₃, which is more stable than the primary one. Major product: 2-bromo-2-methylpropane.

#### Common mistakes

- Drawing the first arrow from the Br to the C=C. It goes from the C=C to the electrophile.
- Forgetting the δ charges on Br₂ or HBr.
- Saying the major product forms because the minor carbocation "cannot form". Both form; the more stable one forms faster.

### 3.3.4.3 Addition polymers

*Refs: AQA 3.3.4.3 · Edexcel 6.26 to 6.29 · OCR A 4.1.3(j) to (l) · OCR B DF(p) · Eduqas C3.2(k). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Addition polymers form from alkenes and substituted alkenes.
- Draw repeat units from monomers and identify monomers from polymers.
- Properties of polymers depend on chain length and the forces between chains.
- Poly(chloroethene) (PVC) and the use of plasticisers.
- Addition polymers are unreactive and non-biodegradable.
- Disposal and recycling of polymers.

#### Explained

n CH₂=CHCl → –[CH₂–CHCl]ₙ–

To draw a repeat unit: change C=C to C–C, keep the substituents on the same carbons, add bonds sticking out each side, put brackets and n.

| Monomer | Polymer | Use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Ethene | Poly(ethene) | Bags, bottles |
| Propene | Poly(propene) | Crates, ropes |
| Chloroethene | Poly(chloroethene), PVC | Window frames, pipes, cable insulation |
| Tetrafluoroethene | PTFE | Non-stick coatings |
| Phenylethene | Poly(phenylethene), polystyrene | Packaging, insulation |

**Properties.** Long chains with more contact have stronger London forces, so higher melting points and strength. PVC has polar C–Cl bonds, so permanent dipole–dipole forces between chains: rigid PVC is hard. Adding a **plasticiser** (small molecules that fit between the chains) pushes the chains apart, so they slide more easily: flexible PVC for cable insulation and clothing.

**Unreactive.** Saturated C–C and C–H backbones with non-polar bonds, so most addition polymers are non-biodegradable and last for centuries in landfill.

**Dealing with waste polymers.**

| Method | Benefits | Drawbacks |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Landfill | Cheap | Uses land; persists for centuries |
| Recycling (sorting by type, melting, remoulding) | Saves crude oil and energy; reduces landfill | Polymers must be sorted (by density or infrared), quality can fall |
| Incineration for energy | Energy used to make steam and electricity | Releases CO₂; PVC releases HCl (removed by scrubbers with a base), and possibly dioxins |
| Feedstock recycling (cracking back to monomers or small molecules) | Products used to make new polymers | Energy-intensive |
| Biodegradable and photodegradable polymers (OCR A 4.1.3(l)) | Break down naturally (from starch, or with light) | May need special composting conditions; made from crops that need land |

**Life cycle (Edexcel 6.28).** Judging a polymer's environmental impact means looking at energy and resources used in extraction, manufacture, use and disposal.

#### Common mistakes

- Including the C=C in the repeat unit.
- Putting substituents on the wrong carbon, or leaving out the side bonds.
- Saying addition polymers are biodegradable because they "contain carbon".

### Check questions

1. Explain why alkenes are more reactive than alkanes. (2 marks)
2. Outline the mechanism for the reaction of propene with bromine. (4 marks)
3. Propene reacts with hydrogen bromide to give two products. Name them, identify the major one, and explain why it is major. (4 marks)
4. Give the reagent, conditions and type of reaction for converting ethene into ethanol industrially. (3 marks)
5. Describe a chemical test to distinguish cyclohexane from cyclohexene. (2 marks)
6. Draw the repeat unit of the polymer formed from but-2-ene. (1 mark)
7. Draw the monomer used to make –[CH₂–C(CH₃)(COOCH₃)]ₙ–. (1 mark)
8. Explain how a plasticiser makes PVC flexible. (2 marks)
9. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of incinerating waste polymers. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. C=C has high electron density / π electrons exposed (1); π bond weaker than σ bond, so breaks more easily / attracts electrophiles (1).
2. Induced dipole on Br₂ shown (1); arrow from C=C to Brᵟ⁺ and from Br–Br bond to Brᵟ⁻ (1); correct carbocation (1); arrow from lone pair of Br⁻ to C⁺; product 1,2-dibromopropane (1).
3. 2-bromopropane and 1-bromopropane (1); 2-bromopropane major (1); formed via secondary carbocation (1); more stable due to more alkyl groups pushing electrons / inductive effect (1).
4. Steam (1); phosphoric acid catalyst, 300 °C, 60 to 70 atm (1); addition / hydration (1).
5. Add bromine water (1); cyclohexene decolorises it (orange to colourless), cyclohexane does not (1).
6. –[CH(CH₃)–CH(CH₃)]– with side bonds (1).
7. CH₂=C(CH₃)COOCH₃ (1).
8. Plasticiser molecules get between chains (1); weaken forces between chains so they slide past each other (1).
9. Advantage: energy produced / less landfill (1). Disadvantage: CO₂ released / toxic gases such as HCl from PVC (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/36-industrial-chemistry-and-nitrogen.md -->

## Industrial chemistry, electrolysis and nitrogen chemistry

This file covers the OCR B (Salters) content set in the Elements from the sea and The chemical industry storylines: electrolysis of molten and aqueous salts with half-equations, extracting halogens from sea water, the chemistry of nitrogen and its compounds with tests for ammonium and nitrate ions, and how to analyse given information about an industrial process (costs, co-products, benefits and risks).

### Overview

OCR B only. Equilibrium and rate ideas used in industry are in files 6 and 9; redox and half-equations in file 7; halogens in file 14.

#### Board differences

- OCR B ES(b), (c) (AS): processes in extracting halogens from sea water (given information, no recall); electrolysis of molten salts and aqueous solutions, with half-equations.
- OCR B CI(i), (j), (k) (A-level): using given information about reactions in industrial processes; nitrogen chemistry (bonding in N₂, NH₃, NH₄⁺; oxides of nitrogen; interconversions of nitrate(V), nitrate(III), ammonium and nitrogen oxides; tests for nitrate(V) and ammonium); costs, co-products, by-products, benefits and risks of industrial processes.
- Other boards: AQA, OCR A and Eduqas meet industrial compromise in equilibrium (file 6). The ammonium test is in file 14.

### ES Electrolysis and extracting the halogens

*Refs: OCR B ES(b), ES(c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Electrolysis of molten salts: metal at the cathode, non-metal at the anode.
- Electrolysis of aqueous solutions: at the cathode, hydrogen forms for salts of Group 1, Group 2 and aluminium, otherwise the metal is plated; at the anode, a halogen forms from concentrated halide solutions, otherwise oxygen (or the anode metal dissolves if it is not inert).
- Half-equations at each electrode.
- Explain, from given information, how halogens are extracted from sea water.

#### Explained

**Electrolysis** uses electricity to decompose an ionic compound that is molten or dissolved. Positive ions move to the cathode (negative electrode) and gain electrons (reduction). Negative ions move to the anode (positive electrode) and lose electrons (oxidation).

**Molten salts.** Molten lead(II) bromide: cathode Pb²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Pb; anode 2Br⁻ → Br₂ + 2e⁻.

**Aqueous solutions.** Water provides H⁺ and OH⁻ (and can be reduced or oxidised itself).

| Electrode | Rule | Half-equation examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cathode | Metal plated if less reactive than hydrogen (Cu, Ag); hydrogen if Group 1, 2 or Al | Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu; 2H₂O + 2e⁻ → H₂ + 2OH⁻ (or 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂) |
| Anode (inert) | Halogen from concentrated halide; otherwise oxygen | 2Cl⁻ → Cl₂ + 2e⁻; 2H₂O → O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ (or 4OH⁻ → O₂ + 2H₂O + 4e⁻) |
| Anode (metal, e.g. copper) | The anode metal dissolves | Cu → Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ |

Brine (concentrated NaCl solution) gives hydrogen at the cathode, chlorine at the anode, and leaves sodium hydroxide in solution: the chlor-alkali industry.

**Practical (OCR B).** Electrolyse solutions in a small cell with carbon electrodes connected to a low-voltage DC supply. Test the gases: hydrogen pops with a lighted splint; oxygen relights a glowing splint; chlorine bleaches damp litmus paper. Notice copper plating on the cathode or colour changes from halogens at the anode.

**Extracting halogens from the sea (given information).** Chlorine is made by electrolysing brine. Bromine is extracted from concentrated sea water (bromide ions) by:

1. acidifying, then oxidising bromide with chlorine: Cl₂ + 2Br⁻ → 2Cl⁻ + Br₂;
2. blowing the bromine out with air or steam;
3. trapping it by reduction with sulfur dioxide and water: Br₂ + SO₂ + 2H₂O → 2HBr + H₂SO₄ (this concentrates it);
4. oxidising again with chlorine and distilling the pure bromine.

Iodine can be obtained from seaweed or from iodate(V) minerals by reduction. In questions, use the given information to write equations and explain each step (which species is oxidised or reduced, and why).

#### Common mistakes

- Putting oxidation at the cathode. Cathode = reduction.
- Predicting sodium metal at the cathode from aqueous NaCl. Hydrogen forms.

### CI Nitrogen chemistry and industrial processes

*Refs: OCR B CI(i), CI(j), CI(k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Bonding in N₂ (triple bond, very strong, so N₂ is unreactive), NH₃ (three bonds, one lone pair, pyramidal) and NH₄⁺ (dative bond, tetrahedral).
- The oxides N₂O, NO and NO₂: names, appearance, and oxidation states.
- Interconversions between nitrate(V), nitrate(III) (nitrite), ammonium and the oxides of nitrogen.
- Tests: ammonium (warm with NaOH, ammonia given off); nitrate(V) (warm with Devarda's alloy and NaOH, ammonia given off).
