- Use given information to explain reactions in industrial processes; costs, co-products, by-products, benefits and risks.

#### Explained

**Bonding.** N≡N has a bond enthalpy of 945 kJ mol⁻¹, so N₂ is very unreactive: it needs high temperatures (lightning, car engines) or catalysts (Haber process) or enzymes (nitrogen-fixing bacteria) to react. NH₃ is a base because of its lone pair, forming NH₄⁺ by a dative bond.

**Oxides of nitrogen.**

| Formula | Name | Oxidation state of N | Appearance |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| N₂O | Dinitrogen oxide (nitrous oxide) | +1 | Colourless gas |
| NO | Nitrogen monoxide (nitrogen(II) oxide) | +2 | Colourless gas; turns brown in air as it forms NO₂ |
| NO₂ | Nitrogen dioxide (nitrogen(IV) oxide) | +4 | Brown gas |

**Interconversions (nitrogen cycle chemistry).**

| Change | N oxidation state | How |
| --- | --- | --- |
| N₂ → NH₃ | 0 → −3 | Haber process; nitrogen-fixing bacteria |
| NH₄⁺ → NO₂⁻ → NO₃⁻ | −3 → +3 → +5 | Oxidation by nitrifying bacteria in soil |
| NO₃⁻ → N₂O / N₂ | +5 → +1 / 0 | Reduction by denitrifying bacteria in waterlogged soil |
| N₂ + O₂ → 2NO | 0 → +2 | High temperature (engines, lightning) |
| 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂ | +2 → +4 | In air |
| NH₃ → NO → NO₂ → HNO₃ | −3 → +2 → +4 → +5 | Industrial: ammonia oxidised over a platinum–rhodium catalyst, then NO₂ absorbed in water with oxygen |
| NO₃⁻ → NH₃ | +5 → −3 | Devarda's alloy (Al, Cu, Zn) in alkali (test) |

Nitrate(III) is the systematic name for nitrite, NO₂⁻; nitrate(V) is NO₃⁻.

**Tests.**

- Ammonium ions: warm with sodium hydroxide; ammonia is given off and turns damp red litmus blue. NH₄⁺ + OH⁻ → NH₃ + H₂O.
- Nitrate(V) ions: if the sample contains no ammonium ions, warm with sodium hydroxide and Devarda's alloy; ammonia is given off, turning damp red litmus blue. The aluminium reduces nitrate(V) to ammonia: 3NO₃⁻ + 8Al + 5OH⁻ + 18H₂O → 3NH₃ + 8[Al(OH)₄]⁻.

**Industrial processes (given information).** Exam questions describe a process and ask you to analyse it. Points to consider:

- **Raw materials:** cost, availability, renewable or not, purity (impurities can poison catalysts).
- **Energy:** temperature and pressure needed; is the reaction exothermic (heat recovered and used elsewhere in the plant) or endothermic?
- **Rate and yield:** choose conditions to balance them (files 5, 6, 9); catalysts; recycling of unreacted materials.
- **Plant costs:** building and maintaining equipment; high pressure is expensive. Continuous processes suit large volumes; batch processes suit small-volume products such as medicines.
- **Co-products** are formed alongside the main product by the reaction and can be sold (for example chlorine and hydrogen with sodium hydroxide from brine). **By-products** come from side reactions and may be waste.
- **Benefits and risks:** benefits of the product to society (fertilisers feed people; medicines; materials) against hazards (toxic, flammable or corrosive chemicals; high pressure; environmental release; transport risks). Plants are often sited near raw materials, transport links and a workforce, and away from housing.

**Example: the Haber process and fertilisers.** N₂ (from air) + 3H₂ (from methane and steam) ⇌ 2NH₃. Iron catalyst, about 450 °C, 200 atm, unreacted gases recycled. Ammonia is oxidised to nitric acid, and the two react to make ammonium nitrate fertiliser. Benefits: enough food for billions. Risks: the hydrogen comes from fossil fuels (CO₂ emissions); ammonia is toxic; ammonium nitrate is explosive; fertiliser run-off causes eutrophication of rivers.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying NO is brown. NO is colourless; NO₂ is brown.
- Testing for nitrate with Devarda's alloy without first checking for ammonium ions.
- Confusing co-products (from the main reaction) with by-products (from side reactions).

### Check questions

1. Write half-equations for the reactions at each electrode in the electrolysis of aqueous copper(II) sulfate with inert electrodes. (2 marks)
2. Predict the products at each electrode in the electrolysis of concentrated aqueous potassium bromide. (2 marks)
3. Bromine is released from sea water by adding chlorine. Write an ionic equation and explain the reaction in terms of redox. (3 marks)
4. Explain why nitrogen gas is unreactive. (2 marks)
5. Give the oxidation state of nitrogen in N₂O, NO₂⁻ and NH₄⁺. (3 marks)
6. Describe how to test a fertiliser for nitrate(V) ions. (3 marks)
7. In a process, the main reaction is exothermic. Suggest how a chemical company can use this to reduce costs. (2 marks)
8. Distinguish between a co-product and a by-product. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Cathode: Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu (1). Anode: 2H₂O → O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ (1).
2. Cathode: hydrogen (1). Anode: bromine (1).
3. Cl₂ + 2Br⁻ → 2Cl⁻ + Br₂ (1); chlorine gains electrons / reduced (1); bromide loses electrons / oxidised; chlorine is the stronger oxidising agent (1).
4. Triple bond (1); very high bond enthalpy, so lots of energy needed to break it (1).
5. N₂O +1 (1); NO₂⁻ +3 (1); NH₄⁺ −3 (1).
6. Check no ammonium present first / warm with NaOH alone, no ammonia (1); then warm with NaOH and Devarda's alloy (1); gas turns damp red litmus blue (ammonia) (1).
7. Use heat released to warm reactants / generate steam or electricity (1); reduces fuel / energy costs (1).
8. Co-product: formed in the main reaction alongside the desired product (1); by-product: formed in an unwanted side reaction (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/07-redox.md -->

## Oxidation, reduction, oxidation states and redox equations

Redox reactions move electrons from one species to another. This file covers oxidation and reduction, oxidising and reducing agents, oxidation states, naming with Roman numerals, disproportionation, and building full redox equations from half-equations.

### Overview

AS content. Redox runs through the halogens (file 14), electrode potentials (file 10), transition metal chemistry and redox titrations (file 15).

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.7 (AS).
- Edexcel 3.1 to 3.13 (AS) include disproportionation (3.7) and the idea that metals form positive ions and non-metals negative ions (3.11, 3.12).
- OCR A 2.1.5(a) to (f) (AS) include redox reactions of metals with acids (2.1.5(e)). OCR A 5.2.3(a) to (c) (A-level) revisit oxidising and reducing agents and half-equations for unfamiliar systems.
- OCR B ES(d), (e), (g) (AS) cover oxidation states and redox in the halogen context, including systematic names such as chlorate(I). Iodine–thiosulfate titrations (ES(f)) are in file 15. DM(c) (A-level) balances half-equations for transition metal redox.
- Eduqas C1.1(b) and C1.6(c) (AS) and PI1.1(a), (b), PI1.2(a), (b) (A-level).

### 3.1.7 Oxidation, reduction and redox equations

*Refs: AQA 3.1.7 · Edexcel 3.1 to 3.13 · OCR A 2.1.5(a) to (f), 5.2.3(a) to (c) · OCR B ES(d), (e), (g), DM(c) · Eduqas C1.1(b), C1.6(c), PI1.1(a), (b), PI1.2(a), (b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Oxidation is the loss of electrons; reduction is the gain of electrons (OIL RIG).
- An oxidising agent accepts electrons (and is reduced); a reducing agent donates electrons (and is oxidised).
- Rules for working out oxidation states.
- Identify oxidation and reduction from changes in oxidation state.
- Write half-equations and combine them into a full redox equation.
- Use Roman numerals in names (iron(III) chloride, chlorate(I)).
- Disproportionation: the same element is both oxidised and reduced.

#### Explained

**Oxidation state rules.**

1. An uncombined element is 0 (Na, O₂, Cl₂).
2. A simple ion has the oxidation state of its charge (Na⁺ +1, O²⁻ −2).
3. The oxidation states in a neutral compound add up to 0; in an ion they add up to the charge.
4. Fixed values: Group 1 +1, Group 2 +2, Al +3, F −1.
5. H is +1, except in metal hydrides such as NaH, where it is −1.
6. O is −2, except in peroxides (H₂O₂, −1), in OF₂ (+2) and in O₂ (0).
7. Cl is −1 except with O or F.

Examples:

| Species | Working | Oxidation state |
| --- | --- | --- |
| S in H₂SO₄ | 2(+1) + S + 4(−2) = 0 | +6 |
| Mn in MnO₄⁻ | Mn + 4(−2) = −1 | +7 |
| Cr in Cr₂O₇²⁻ | 2Cr + 7(−2) = −2 | +6 |
| N in NH₄⁺ | N + 4(+1) = +1 | −3 |
| Cl in ClO₃⁻ | Cl + 3(−2) = −1 | +5 |

**Roman numerals** give the oxidation state of the element that can vary: iron(II) sulfate FeSO₄, iron(III) oxide Fe₂O₃, sodium chlorate(I) NaClO, sodium chlorate(V) NaClO₃, potassium manganate(VII) KMnO₄, potassium dichromate(VI) K₂Cr₂O₇, sulfate(IV) is sulfite SO₃²⁻.

An increase in oxidation state is oxidation; a decrease is reduction.

**Writing half-equations in acid solution.**

1. Balance the element changing oxidation state.
2. Balance O by adding H₂O.
3. Balance H by adding H⁺.
4. Balance charge by adding electrons.

Example: MnO₄⁻ → Mn²⁺.

- Mn balanced.
- Add 4H₂O on the right: MnO₄⁻ → Mn²⁺ + 4H₂O.
- Add 8H⁺ on the left: MnO₄⁻ + 8H⁺ → Mn²⁺ + 4H₂O.
- Charge: left is +7, right is +2, so add 5e⁻ on the left: MnO₄⁻ + 8H⁺ + 5e⁻ → Mn²⁺ + 4H₂O.

Dichromate: Cr₂O₇²⁻ + 14H⁺ + 6e⁻ → 2Cr³⁺ + 7H₂O.

**Combining half-equations.** Multiply each so that the electrons are equal, add them, and cancel electrons and anything on both sides.

MnO₄⁻ + 8H⁺ + 5e⁻ → Mn²⁺ + 4H₂O
Fe²⁺ → Fe³⁺ + e⁻ (× 5)

MnO₄⁻ + 8H⁺ + 5Fe²⁺ → Mn²⁺ + 4H₂O + 5Fe³⁺

**Metals and acids (OCR A 2.1.5(e)).** Mg + 2HCl → MgCl₂ + H₂. Mg goes 0 → +2 (oxidised); H goes +1 → 0 (reduced). Ionic: Mg + 2H⁺ → Mg²⁺ + H₂.

**Disproportionation.** Chlorine with water: Cl₂ + H₂O ⇌ HCl + HClO. Cl goes from 0 to −1 (in HCl) and to +1 (in HClO). Chlorine with cold dilute sodium hydroxide: Cl₂ + 2NaOH → NaCl + NaClO + H₂O. Copper(I) in aqueous solution: 2Cu⁺ → Cu + Cu²⁺.

**Metals and non-metals (Edexcel 3.11, 3.12).** Metals generally lose electrons and are oxidised, forming positive ions; reactive metals are good reducing agents. Non-metals generally gain electrons and are reduced, forming negative ions; reactive non-metals such as fluorine and chlorine are good oxidising agents.

#### Worked example

1. Write the half-equation for the reduction of SO₄²⁻ to H₂S in acid.

   SO₄²⁻ → H₂S. Add 4H₂O on the right. Add 10H⁺ on the left (8 for water, 2 for H₂S). Charge: left 10 − 2 = +8, right 0, so add 8e⁻. SO₄²⁻ + 10H⁺ + 8e⁻ → H₂S + 4H₂O.

2. Combine with the oxidation of iodide (2I⁻ → I₂ + 2e⁻).

   Multiply iodide by 4: 8I⁻ → 4I₂ + 8e⁻. Full: SO₄²⁻ + 10H⁺ + 8I⁻ → H₂S + 4I₂ + 4H₂O.

3. Identify the oxidising agent in 2Fe³⁺ + 2I⁻ → 2Fe²⁺ + I₂.

   Fe³⁺ gains electrons (reduced), so Fe³⁺ is the oxidising agent. I⁻ is the reducing agent.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the oxidising agent is oxidised. It is reduced.
- Putting electrons on the wrong side: reduction half-equations have electrons on the left.
- Forgetting to multiply to balance electrons before adding.
- Giving oxidation states as charges written like "2+" instead of "+2".
- Saying a disproportionation reaction has two different elements changing.

### Check questions

1. Define oxidation in terms of electrons and in terms of oxidation state. (2 marks)
2. Give the oxidation state of: (a) N in NO₃⁻, (b) S in S₂O₃²⁻, (c) V in VO₂⁺, (d) O in H₂O₂. (4 marks)
3. Name NaClO₃ and Fe₂(SO₄)₃ using Roman numerals. (2 marks)
4. Write a half-equation for the reduction of NO₃⁻ to NO in acid solution. (2 marks)
5. Combine the half-equations Cr₂O₇²⁻ + 14H⁺ + 6e⁻ → 2Cr³⁺ + 7H₂O and Fe²⁺ → Fe³⁺ + e⁻ into a full equation. (2 marks)
6. Explain, using oxidation states, why the reaction of chlorine with cold aqueous sodium hydroxide is a disproportionation. (3 marks)
7. In the reaction Zn + Cu²⁺ → Zn²⁺ + Cu, identify the reducing agent and explain. (2 marks)
8. Hydrogen peroxide can be reduced to water in acid. Write the half-equation. (1 mark)

### Mark scheme

1. Loss of electrons (1); increase in oxidation state (1).
2. (a) +5 (1); (b) +2 (1); (c) +5 (1); (d) −1 (1).
3. Sodium chlorate(V) (1); iron(III) sulfate (1).
4. NO₃⁻ + 4H⁺ + 3e⁻ → NO + 2H₂O (1 for species and H⁺/H₂O balanced; 1 for electrons).
5. Cr₂O₇²⁻ + 14H⁺ + 6Fe²⁺ → 2Cr³⁺ + 7H₂O + 6Fe³⁺ (1 for ×6; 1 for correct equation).
6. Cl₂ + 2NaOH → NaCl + NaClO + H₂O (1); Cl 0 → −1 in NaCl, reduced (1); Cl 0 → +1 in NaClO, oxidised; same element both oxidised and reduced (1).
7. Zinc (1); it loses electrons / its oxidation state increases from 0 to +2, so it reduces Cu²⁺ (1).
8. H₂O₂ + 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → 2H₂O (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/15-transition-metals.md -->

## Transition metals: complexes, colour, oxidation states and catalysts

The transition metals Ti to Cu form complexes, coloured ions, several oxidation states and good catalysts, all because of their partly filled d sub-shell. This file covers complexes and their shapes, ligand substitution and the chelate effect, the origin of colour, variable oxidation states with vanadium, chromium, cobalt and manganese, redox titrations, and catalysis.

### Overview

A-level only. Precipitation reactions of aqua ions with hydroxide, ammonia and carbonate are in file 16. You need electron configurations (file 1), redox (file 7) and electrode potentials (file 10).

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.5.1 to 3.2.5.6.
- Edexcel 15.1 to 15.35 (except 15.24, 15.25 in file 16), 14.18, 14.19 (redox titrations), CP11 (redox titration), CP12 (preparing a complex). Edexcel adds chromium chemistry (15.22, 15.23), entropy and the chelate effect (15.28), and catalytic converters (15.32).
- OCR A 5.3.1(a) to (i), (k), (l) and 5.2.3(d), (e): includes the Fe²⁺/Fe³⁺ and Cr³⁺/Cr₂O₇²⁻ interconversions, optical isomerism in complexes, and iodine–thiosulfate titrations.
- OCR B DM(a), (b), (g) to (n) (Developing metals) and ES(f) (iodine–thiosulfate titrations, AS).
- Eduqas PI2.2(a) to (i) and PI1.2(c), (d): includes the most important oxidation states of Cr, Mn, Fe, Co and Cu, nickel and iron catalysts in hydrogenation and the Haber process, and the copper(II)/iodide titration. Specified practicals: a simple redox titration, and estimating copper in copper(II) salts.

### 3.2.5.1 General properties of transition metals

*Refs: AQA 3.2.5.1 · Edexcel 15.1 to 15.3, 15.6 · OCR A 5.3.1(a) to (c) · OCR B DM(g), DM(h) · Eduqas PI2.2(a), (b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Transition metal characteristics come from an incomplete d sub-shell in the atom or ions.
- The characteristics: complex formation, coloured ions, variable oxidation state, catalytic activity.
- A complex is a central metal atom or ion surrounded by ligands. A ligand is a molecule or ion that forms a coordinate bond with a transition metal by donating a pair of electrons. Coordination number is the number of coordinate bonds to the central metal.

#### Explained

**Definition.** A transition element is a d-block element that forms at least one stable ion with an incomplete d sub-shell. Scandium (only Sc³⁺, [Ar] 3d⁰) and zinc (only Zn²⁺, [Ar] 3d¹⁰) are in the d block but not transition elements.

Configurations: remove 4s electrons first. Fe [Ar] 3d⁶ 4s²; Fe²⁺ [Ar] 3d⁶; Fe³⁺ [Ar] 3d⁵. Cr [Ar] 3d⁵ 4s¹; Cu [Ar] 3d¹⁰ 4s¹; Cu²⁺ [Ar] 3d⁹.

**Why variable oxidation states?** The 4s and 3d sub-shells are close in energy, so different numbers of electrons can be lost (or shared) for similar energy.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling zinc a transition metal.
- Removing 3d electrons before 4s.

### 3.2.5.2 Substitution reactions

*Refs: AQA 3.2.5.2 · Edexcel 15.4, 15.5, 15.12, 15.17 to 15.19, 15.26 to 15.28, CP12 · OCR A 5.3.1(d), (h), (i) · OCR B DM(i), DM(j) · Eduqas PI2.2(e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- H₂O, NH₃ and Cl⁻ are monodentate ligands. H₂NCH₂CH₂NH₂ (en) and C₂O₄²⁻ (ethanedioate) are bidentate. EDTA⁴⁻ is hexadentate (multidentate).
- Ligand substitution with similar-sized ligands (H₂O and NH₃) has no change in coordination number. Substitution by Cl⁻ (larger) changes coordination number from 6 to 4.
- Incomplete substitution: [Cu(H₂O)₆]²⁺ with excess ammonia gives [Cu(NH₃)₄(H₂O)₂]²⁺.
- The chelate effect, explained by entropy.
- Haem: an iron(II) complex with a multidentate ligand; O₂ binds to Fe(II); CO binds more strongly and is toxic.

#### Explained

**Ligands** have at least one lone pair. Bidentate ligands have two donor atoms and form two coordinate bonds; en bonds through both N atoms, ethanedioate through two O⁻ atoms. EDTA⁴⁻ has six donor atoms (two N and four O⁻).

**Substitution, no change in coordination number.**

[Co(H₂O)₆]²⁺ + 6NH₃ → [Co(NH₃)₆]²⁺ + 6H₂O (pink to straw-yellow)

[Cu(H₂O)₆]²⁺ + 4NH₃ → [Cu(NH₃)₄(H₂O)₂]²⁺ + 4H₂O (pale blue to deep blue). This is incomplete substitution: only four waters are replaced.

[Cr(H₂O)₆]³⁺ + 6NH₃ → [Cr(NH₃)₆]³⁺ + 6H₂O (in excess concentrated ammonia; purple to violet) (OCR A).

**Substitution with chloride.** Cl⁻ is larger than H₂O, so only four fit around the metal:

[Cu(H₂O)₆]²⁺ + 4Cl⁻ ⇌ [CuCl₄]²⁻ + 6H₂O (blue to yellow-green to yellow, with concentrated HCl). Octahedral to tetrahedral.

[Co(H₂O)₆]²⁺ + 4Cl⁻ ⇌ [CoCl₄]²⁻ + 6H₂O (pink to blue).

**The chelate effect.** Multidentate ligands form more stable complexes than monodentate ones.

[Cu(H₂O)₆]²⁺ + 3en → [Cu(en)₃]²⁺ + 6H₂O

[Cu(H₂O)₆]²⁺ + EDTA⁴⁻ → [Cu(EDTA)]²⁻ + 6H₂O

The bonds broken and made are similar (Cu–O and Cu–N), so ΔH is small. But the number of particles increases (4 → 7 and 2 → 7), so ΔS is large and positive. So ΔG = ΔH − TΔS is negative: the reaction is feasible and the complex is stable. Reversing it would decrease entropy. EDTA is used to remove metal ions (in water softening, in treating metal poisoning).

**Haem and haemoglobin.** Haem is an iron(II) ion held by four nitrogen atoms of a flat multidentate ring (a porphyrin). The fifth position bonds to the protein globin. The sixth position can bind O₂ (coordinate bond to Fe²⁺), carrying oxygen round the body. Carbon monoxide binds to the same site more strongly than O₂ and is not released, so less oxygen is carried: CO is toxic. This is a ligand substitution (Edexcel 15.19).

#### Practical

**Edexcel CP12: preparation of a transition metal complex**, for example making a copper(II) ammine complex or a chromium(II) ethanoate complex. Typical steps: dissolve, react, crystallise by adding ethanol or cooling, filter by suction, wash with a little cold solvent, dry, weigh and work out the percentage yield. For chromium(II), oxygen must be kept out because Cr(II) is readily oxidised.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing [CuCl₄]²⁻ as octahedral.
- Forgetting the charge on a complex when ligands change (Cl⁻ is negative).
- Saying the chelate effect is due to stronger bonds. It is due to the entropy increase.

### 3.2.5.3 Shapes of complex ions

*Refs: AQA 3.2.5.3 · Edexcel 15.11, 15.13 to 15.16 · OCR A 5.3.1(e) to (g) · OCR B DM(b) · Eduqas PI2.2(c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Small ligands (H₂O, NH₃) usually give octahedral complexes (coordination number 6). Larger ligands (Cl⁻) give tetrahedral complexes (4).
- Square planar complexes (coordination number 4) such as cisplatin. Linear complexes such as [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺ (Tollens' reagent).
- Cis–trans isomerism in square planar and octahedral complexes. Optical isomerism in octahedral complexes with three bidentate ligands.
- Cisplatin as an anticancer drug (also file 28).

#### Explained

| Shape | Coordination number | Angle | Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Octahedral | 6 | 90° | [Fe(H₂O)₆]²⁺, [Co(NH₃)₆]²⁺ |
| Tetrahedral | 4 | 109.5° | [CuCl₄]²⁻, [CoCl₄]²⁻ |
| Square planar | 4 | 90° | [Pt(NH₃)₂Cl₂], [Ni(CN)₄]²⁻ |
| Linear | 2 | 180° | [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺ |

**Cis–trans isomerism.** In square planar [Pt(NH₃)₂Cl₂], the two Cl can be next to each other (cis, 90°) or opposite (trans, 180°). In octahedral [Co(NH₃)₄Cl₂]⁺ the same applies.

**Optical isomerism.** [Ni(en)₃]²⁺ and [Co(en)₃]³⁺ exist as two non-superimposable mirror images, like left and right hands (OCR A 5.3.1(f), Edexcel). Octahedral complexes with two bidentate ligands in cis positions, such as cis-[Co(en)₂Cl₂]⁺, are also optically active.

**Cisplatin.** cis-[Pt(NH₃)₂Cl₂]. In the cell, the Cl⁻ ligands are replaced by water, and the platinum then bonds to two nitrogen atoms on adjacent guanine bases in DNA. This bends the DNA, stopping it unwinding and replicating, so cancer cells cannot divide. It also affects healthy cells that divide quickly (hair follicles, gut lining), causing side effects. Small doses and targeted delivery reduce these. Only the cis isomer works; in the trans isomer the two binding sites are too far apart to bond to neighbouring bases.

#### Common mistakes

- Drawing a square planar complex as tetrahedral.
- Saying both isomers of [Pt(NH₃)₂Cl₂] are used as drugs.

### 3.2.5.4 Formation of coloured ions

*Refs: AQA 3.2.5.4 · Edexcel 15.7 to 15.10 · OCR A 5.3.1(c) · OCR B DM(m), DM(n) · Eduqas PI2.2(d), (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Transition metal ions are coloured because d electrons absorb visible light and move to a higher d orbital: ΔE = hν = hc/λ.
- Changes in oxidation state, coordination number and ligand change the colour.
- The colour seen is the complementary colour of the light absorbed.
- Colorimetry (visible spectroscopy) to find concentrations.
- Why Sc³⁺ and Zn²⁺ (and Cu⁺) are colourless.

#### Explained

In an isolated ion the five 3d orbitals have the same energy. When ligands bond, they split into two groups with an energy gap ΔE (in an octahedral complex, two higher and three lower). An electron in a lower d orbital can absorb a photon whose energy matches ΔE and jump up. The light that is not absorbed is transmitted or reflected, and that is the colour we see.

[Cu(H₂O)₆]²⁺ absorbs in the red/orange region, so it looks blue.

ΔE = hν, where h is the Planck constant (6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ J s) and ν the frequency. Also ν = c/λ.

The size of ΔE, and so the colour, depends on:

- the metal and its oxidation state ([Fe(H₂O)₆]²⁺ pale green, [Fe(H₂O)₆]³⁺ pale violet but usually seen yellow-brown due to hydrolysis);
- the ligand ([Cu(H₂O)₆]²⁺ blue, [Cu(NH₃)₄(H₂O)₂]²⁺ deep blue);
- the coordination number and shape ([CuCl₄]²⁻ yellow).

**No colour.** Sc³⁺ (3d⁰) has no d electrons to promote. Zn²⁺ and Cu⁺ (3d¹⁰) have a full d sub-shell, so there is no space for an electron to move into.

**Colorimetry.** Choose a filter of the complementary colour (the colour absorbed most). Make standard solutions of known concentration, measure their absorbance and plot a calibration graph. Measure the unknown and read off the concentration. If the ion is pale, add a ligand that gives a more intensely coloured complex (such as thiocyanate with Fe³⁺, or ammonia with Cu²⁺).

#### Worked example

A complex absorbs light of wavelength 500 nm. Calculate ΔE for one ion, and per mole.

ν = c/λ = 3.00 × 10⁸ ÷ 5.00 × 10⁻⁷ = 6.00 × 10¹⁴ Hz. ΔE = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ × 6.00 × 10¹⁴ = 3.98 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. Per mole: × 6.02 × 10²³ = 2.40 × 10⁵ J mol⁻¹ = 240 kJ mol⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the electrons emit the colour we see. They absorb; we see what is left.
- Saying the electron is promoted from 3d to 4s. It moves between split d orbitals.

### 3.2.5.5 Variable oxidation states

*Refs: AQA 3.2.5.5 · Edexcel 14.18, 14.19, 15.20 to 15.23, CP11 · OCR A 5.2.3(d), (e), 5.3.1(k), (l) · OCR B DM(a), ES(f) · Eduqas PI1.2(c), (d), PI2.2(b). Level: A-level only (OCR B ES(f) AS).*

#### What you need to know

- Transition elements show variable oxidation states.
- Vanadium(V) to (II) by zinc in acid, with colours.
- The redox potential of a change depends on pH and ligand.
- Tollens' reagent, [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺, reduced to silver by aldehydes.
- Redox titrations with KMnO₄ (Fe²⁺, ethanedioate) and K₂Cr₂O₇; iodine–thiosulfate titrations; calculations.
- Chromium (Edexcel, OCR A, Eduqas): Cr³⁺ ⇌ Cr₂O₇²⁻ interconversion; Cr³⁺ to Cr²⁺ with zinc; dichromate–chromate equilibrium.
- Iron(II)/iron(III) interconversion (OCR A); cobalt(II) oxidised in ammonia (Eduqas, AQA context).

#### Explained

**Vanadium.** Ammonium vanadate(V) in acid contains VO₂⁺ (yellow). Add zinc and acid; zinc is a strong reducing agent.

| Ion | Oxidation state | Colour |
| --- | --- | --- |
| VO₂⁺ | +5 | Yellow |
| VO²⁺ | +4 | Blue |
| V³⁺ | +3 | Green |
| V²⁺ | +2 | Violet |

The mixture goes yellow → green (yellow + blue mix) → blue → green → violet. Half-equations:

- VO₂⁺ + 2H⁺ + e⁻ → VO²⁺ + H₂O (E⦵ +1.00 V)
- VO²⁺ + 2H⁺ + e⁻ → V³⁺ + H₂O (+0.34 V)
- V³⁺ + e⁻ → V²⁺ (−0.26 V)
- Zn²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Zn (−0.76 V)

Zinc can reduce all three because all three are more positive than −0.76 V. V²⁺ is readily reoxidised by air, so the flask is fitted with a loose cotton wool plug or a valve.

**Effect of pH.** Many oxidations are easier in alkali and many reductions easier in acid. Half-equations with H⁺ shift right in acid (MnO₄⁻ is a stronger oxidising agent in acid). Cr(III) is oxidised to Cr(VI) by hydrogen peroxide in alkaline solution: 2[Cr(OH)₆]³⁻ + 3H₂O₂ → 2CrO₄²⁻ + 2OH⁻ + 8H₂O (green to yellow). In acid, the reverse happens.

**Effect of ligand.** Co²⁺ in water is hard to oxidise, but [Co(NH₃)₆]²⁺ is readily oxidised by air to [Co(NH₃)₆]³⁺ (straw to darker brown). Changing the ligand changes E.

**Chromium (Edexcel 15.22, 15.23; OCR A 5.3.1(k); Eduqas).**

- Dichromate(VI) in acid is reduced by zinc: Cr₂O₇²⁻ (orange) → Cr³⁺ (green) → Cr²⁺ (blue). Cr²⁺ is easily oxidised back by air.
- Cr³⁺ is oxidised to chromate(VI) by H₂O₂ in alkali (above). Adding acid turns chromate(VI) (yellow) to dichromate(VI) (orange): 2CrO₄²⁻ + 2H⁺ ⇌ Cr₂O₇²⁻ + H₂O. This is not a redox reaction: Cr stays +6.

**Iron (OCR A 5.3.1(k)).** Fe²⁺ (pale green) is oxidised to Fe³⁺ (yellow/orange-brown) by acidified MnO₄⁻, by H₂O₂ or slowly by air. Fe³⁺ is reduced to Fe²⁺ by I⁻ (iodine forms) or by zinc. Test: with NaOH, Fe²⁺ gives a green precipitate, Fe³⁺ an orange-brown one.

**Copper (OCR A, Eduqas).** Cu²⁺ is reduced by iodide: 2Cu²⁺ + 4I⁻ → 2CuI + I₂ (white precipitate of CuI in a brown iodine solution). Cu⁺ in water disproportionates to Cu and Cu²⁺.

**Main oxidation states (Eduqas PI2.2(b)).** Cr: +3, +6. Mn: +2, +4 (MnO₂), +7 (MnO₄⁻). Fe: +2, +3. Co: +2, +3. Cu: +1, +2.

**Tollens' reagent.** [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺ is reduced to silver (a mirror) by aldehydes, which are oxidised: Ag⁺ + e⁻ → Ag. Ketones give no change (file 23).

**Redox titrations.**

*Manganate(VII)* is self-indicating: its purple colour disappears while it reacts; the end point is the first permanent pale pink. It is used in acid (dilute sulfuric acid; not HCl, which MnO₄⁻ would oxidise to Cl₂; not nitric, which is itself an oxidant).

- With iron(II): MnO₄⁻ + 8H⁺ + 5Fe²⁺ → Mn²⁺ + 4H₂O + 5Fe³⁺ (ratio 1 : 5).
- With ethanedioate: 2MnO₄⁻ + 16H⁺ + 5C₂O₄²⁻ → 2Mn²⁺ + 8H₂O + 10CO₂ (ratio 2 : 5). Done warm (about 60 °C) because it is slow at the start.

*Dichromate(VI)* with iron(II): Cr₂O₇²⁻ + 14H⁺ + 6Fe²⁺ → 2Cr³⁺ + 7H₂O + 6Fe³⁺ (ratio 1 : 6). Needs an indicator (such as sodium diphenylaminesulfonate) because the green Cr³⁺ hides the orange.

*Iodine–thiosulfate* (OCR A, OCR B, Eduqas). An oxidising agent reacts with excess iodide to release iodine, which is titrated with sodium thiosulfate: I₂ + 2S₂O₃²⁻ → 2I⁻ + S₄O₆²⁻. Add starch near the end point, when the solution is pale straw: it goes blue-black, then colourless at the end point. Adding starch too early can trap iodine.

*Estimating copper (Eduqas).* Neutralise the copper(II) solution, add excess KI, then titrate the iodine released with thiosulfate. 2Cu²⁺ → I₂ → 2S₂O₃²⁻, so n(Cu²⁺) = n(S₂O₃²⁻).

#### Worked example

1. A 2.50 g iron tablet is dissolved in acid and made up to 250 cm³. 25.0 cm³ needs 12.40 cm³ of 0.0100 mol dm⁻³ KMnO₄. Find the percentage of iron.

   n(MnO₄⁻) = 1.24 × 10⁻⁴ mol. n(Fe²⁺) = 5 × 1.24 × 10⁻⁴ = 6.20 × 10⁻⁴ in 25.0 cm³; 6.20 × 10⁻³ in 250 cm³. Mass Fe = 6.20 × 10⁻³ × 55.8 = 0.346 g. Percentage = 13.8%.

2. 0.600 g of a brass is dissolved and treated with excess KI. The iodine needs 23.60 cm³ of 0.100 mol dm⁻³ thiosulfate. Find the percentage of copper.

   n(S₂O₃²⁻) = 2.36 × 10⁻³ = n(Cu). Mass = 2.36 × 10⁻³ × 63.5 = 0.150 g. Percentage = 25.0%.

#### Practical

**Edexcel CP11, Eduqas simple redox titration**, for example the iron content of iron tablets with KMnO₄. **Eduqas estimating copper** by iodine–thiosulfate as above. **OCR B DM(n)**: colorimetry to find the concentration of a coloured transition metal ion.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the 1 : 1 ratio for MnO₄⁻ and Fe²⁺.
- Adding starch at the start of an iodine–thiosulfate titration.
- Saying the chromate/dichromate change is redox.
- Trying to read the bottom of the meniscus in a KMnO₄ burette. The solution is so dark that the top of the meniscus is read instead.

### 3.2.5.6 Catalysts

*Refs: AQA 3.2.5.6 · Edexcel 15.29 to 15.35 · OCR A 5.3.1(c) · OCR B DM(l) · Eduqas PI2.2(g) to (i). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Transition metals and their compounds are good catalysts because they can change oxidation state readily.
- Heterogeneous catalysts: surface, adsorption, support media, poisoning. Examples: V₂O₅ in the Contact process, Fe in the Haber process, Ni in hydrogenation, Pt/Rh in catalytic converters.
- Homogeneous catalysts: intermediate species. Examples: Fe²⁺ in the reaction of I⁻ with S₂O₈²⁻; autocatalysis by Mn²⁺ in MnO₄⁻ with C₂O₄²⁻.

#### Explained

**V₂O₅ in the Contact process** (2SO₂ + O₂ ⇌ 2SO₃):

- SO₂ + V₂O₅ → SO₃ + V₂O₄ (V goes +5 to +4)
- 2V₂O₄ + O₂ → 2V₂O₅ (back to +5)

The variable oxidation state lets the catalyst take part and be regenerated.

**Iron in the Haber process** and **nickel in hydrogenation** of vegetable oils to margarine (Eduqas PI2.2(h)) are heterogeneous: reactants adsorb on the metal surface.

**Catalytic converters (Edexcel 15.32).** A ceramic honeycomb coated with a thin layer of platinum, palladium and rhodium. 2CO + 2NO → 2CO₂ + N₂. Unburnt hydrocarbons are also oxidised to CO₂ and H₂O. Large surface area for the small amount of expensive metal.

**Poisoning.** Impurities adsorb strongly on the active sites and block them: lead poisoned catalytic converters (so unleaded petrol was introduced); sulfur poisons the iron in the Haber process. Poisoning means the catalyst must be replaced or cleaned, adding cost; purifying the reactants helps.

**Fe²⁺ catalysing peroxodisulfate and iodide.** S₂O₈²⁻ + 2I⁻ → 2SO₄²⁻ + I₂. Without a catalyst this is slow, because two negative ions repel each other. With Fe²⁺:

- S₂O₈²⁻ + 2Fe²⁺ → 2SO₄²⁻ + 2Fe³⁺
- 2Fe³⁺ + 2I⁻ → 2Fe²⁺ + I₂

Each step involves oppositely charged ions, so the activation energy is lower. Fe³⁺ also works, because the steps simply happen in the other order.

**Autocatalysis by Mn²⁺.** In the MnO₄⁻/C₂O₄²⁻ titration, the reaction starts slowly (two negative ions). Mn²⁺ is a product and catalyses the reaction:

- 4Mn²⁺ + MnO₄⁻ + 8H⁺ → 5Mn³⁺ + 4H₂O
- 2Mn³⁺ + C₂O₄²⁻ → 2Mn²⁺ + 2CO₂

So the rate increases as Mn²⁺ builds up, then falls as the reactants run out: an S-shaped concentration–time curve for the product.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a catalyst is "not involved" in the reaction.
- Saying the Fe²⁺ catalysis works because Fe²⁺ "attracts" both ions; explain with the two steps between oppositely charged ions.
- Forgetting that both Fe²⁺ and Fe³⁺ can catalyse this reaction.

### Check questions

1. Explain why zinc is not a transition element. (2 marks)
2. Give the formula and shape of the complex formed when excess concentrated hydrochloric acid is added to aqueous copper(II) ions. (2 marks)
3. Explain the chelate effect for the reaction of [Ni(H₂O)₆]²⁺ with three en molecules. (3 marks)
4. Explain why carbon monoxide is toxic. (2 marks)
5. Draw the two isomers of [Pt(NH₃)₂Cl₂] and explain how the active isomer works as an anticancer drug. (4 marks)
6. Explain why [Cu(H₂O)₆]²⁺ is blue. (3 marks)
7. Describe the colour changes when zinc and acid are added to a solution of ammonium vanadate(V), giving the ions responsible. (4 marks)
8. 25.0 cm³ of an iron(II) solution reacts with 21.80 cm³ of 0.0200 mol dm⁻³ potassium manganate(VII). Find [Fe²⁺]. (3 marks)
9. Explain why the reaction between peroxodisulfate and iodide ions is catalysed by Fe²⁺. Include equations. (3 marks)
10. Explain why the rate of reaction between MnO₄⁻ and C₂O₄²⁻ increases after the reaction has started. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Only forms Zn²⁺ (1); which has a full d sub-shell, 3d¹⁰ (1).
2. [CuCl₄]²⁻ (1); tetrahedral (1).
3. Number of particles increases from 4 to 7 (1); entropy increases / ΔS positive (1); ΔH approximately zero (similar bonds), so ΔG negative (1).
4. CO forms a coordinate bond to Fe²⁺ in haem / substitutes O₂ (1); binds more strongly, so haemoglobin cannot carry oxygen (1).
5. Correct cis and trans square planar structures (2); cis isomer bonds to two nitrogen atoms on adjacent guanines in DNA / Cl ligands replaced (1); stops DNA replication so cell cannot divide (1).
6. Ligands split d orbitals (1); electrons absorb light of energy ΔE (red / orange) and are promoted (1); remaining (blue) light transmitted (1).
7. Yellow VO₂⁺ (1); to green (mixture) then blue VO²⁺ (1); to green V³⁺ (1); to violet V²⁺ (1).
8. n(MnO₄⁻) = 4.36 × 10⁻⁴ (1); n(Fe²⁺) = 2.18 × 10⁻³ (1); [Fe²⁺] = 0.0872 mol dm⁻³ (1).
9. Uncatalysed: two negative ions repel, high Eₐ (1); S₂O₈²⁻ + 2Fe²⁺ → 2SO₄²⁻ + 2Fe³⁺ (1); 2Fe³⁺ + 2I⁻ → 2Fe²⁺ + I₂ (1).
10. Mn²⁺ is a product (1); which catalyses the reaction / autocatalysis (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/14-group-7-halogens.md -->

## Group 7: the halogens, halides and tests for ions

The halogens are reactive non-metals that act as oxidising agents. This file covers trends in their physical properties, electronegativity and oxidising power, displacement reactions, the reducing power of halide ions with sulfuric acid, silver halides, chlorine in water treatment and bleach, and the test-tube tests for common ions. It includes AQA required practical 4.

### Overview

AS content. You need redox and oxidation states from file 7 and intermolecular forces from file 3. Halogenoalkanes are in file 19.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.3.1, 3.2.3.2 and RP4.
- Edexcel 4.9 to 4.15 and CP7: also predictions about fluorine and astatine (4.14), chlorine with hot alkali (4.12), and reactions of hydrogen halides with ammonia and water (4.13).
- OCR A 3.1.3(a) to (g) and 3.1.4(a): qualitative analysis including the sequence of anion tests.
- OCR B ES(h) to (n): physical properties, displacement, silver halides with ammonia, preparing HCl, HBr and HI, the properties of hydrogen halides, and the risks and uses of chlorine.
- Eduqas C1.6(l) to (q) (AS) and PI2.1(j) to (l) (A-level: chlorine with warm alkali, bleaching and bactericidal action, sodium halides with concentrated sulfuric acid). Specified practical: identifying unknown solutions by qualitative analysis.

### 3.2.3.1 Trends in properties

*Refs: AQA 3.2.3.1 · Edexcel 4.9 to 4.11, 4.13 to 4.15 · OCR A 3.1.3(a) to (d), (g) · OCR B ES(h) to (m) · Eduqas C1.6(l) to (p), PI2.1(l). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Trends in electronegativity and boiling point down Group 7, and their explanations.
- Trend in oxidising ability (F₂ > Cl₂ > Br₂ > I₂), shown by displacement reactions.
- Trend in reducing ability of halide ions (I⁻ > Br⁻ > Cl⁻ > F⁻), shown by reactions of solid sodium halides with concentrated sulfuric acid.
- Silver nitrate test for halide ions, and the solubility of the silver halides in ammonia.

#### Explained

**Physical properties.**

| Halogen | State at room temperature | Colour | In water | In hexane (organic) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| F₂ | Gas | Pale yellow | Reacts | — |
| Cl₂ | Gas | Pale green | Pale green | Pale green |
| Br₂ | Liquid | Red-brown | Orange / yellow | Orange / red |
| I₂ | Solid (shiny grey-black; purple vapour) | Grey-black | Brown (slightly soluble) | Violet / purple |

**Boiling point increases** down the group. The molecules get bigger with more electrons, so stronger London forces between them.

**Electronegativity decreases** down the group. The atoms get larger with more shielding, so the bonding pair is further from the nucleus and less attracted.

**Oxidising ability decreases** down the group. A halogen oxidises by gaining an electron. Down the group the atom is larger, the incoming electron is further from the nucleus and more shielded, so it is attracted less strongly.

**Displacement reactions.** A more reactive halogen oxidises the halide ion of a less reactive one.

| Added | Cl⁻(aq) | Br⁻(aq) | I⁻(aq) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Cl₂(aq) | — | Orange solution (Br₂) | Brown solution (I₂) |
| Br₂(aq) | No change | — | Brown solution (I₂) |
| I₂(aq) | No change | No change | — |

Cl₂ + 2Br⁻ → 2Cl⁻ + Br₂. Half-equations: Cl₂ + 2e⁻ → 2Cl⁻ (reduction); 2Br⁻ → Br₂ + 2e⁻ (oxidation).

Shaking with hexane makes the colours clearer: bromine orange-red, iodine purple in the upper organic layer.

**Halides with concentrated sulfuric acid (reducing ability).** The halide ions get better at losing electrons (stronger reducing agents) down the group, because they are larger and the outer electron is further from the nucleus.

| Solid | Products and observations | What happens |
| --- | --- | --- |
| NaF / NaCl | Misty white fumes of HF / HCl | Acid–base only: NaCl + H₂SO₄ → NaHSO₄ + HCl. Cl⁻ cannot reduce H₂SO₄. |
| NaBr | Misty fumes (HBr), orange-brown fumes (Br₂), colourless choking gas (SO₂) | NaBr + H₂SO₄ → NaHSO₄ + HBr; then 2HBr + H₂SO₄ → Br₂ + SO₂ + 2H₂O. S goes +6 → +4. |
| NaI | Misty fumes (HI), purple fumes / black solid (I₂), SO₂, yellow solid (S), rotten-egg smell (H₂S) | 2HI + H₂SO₄ → I₂ + SO₂ + 2H₂O; 6HI + H₂SO₄ → 3I₂ + S + 4H₂O; 8HI + H₂SO₄ → 4I₂ + H₂S + 4H₂O. S goes to +4, 0 and −2. |

Half-equations for iodide: 2I⁻ → I₂ + 2e⁻; H₂SO₄ + 8H⁺ + 8e⁻ → H₂S + 4H₂O.

**Hydrogen halides (OCR B, Edexcel).**

- Preparing: HCl from NaCl and concentrated sulfuric acid. HBr and HI cannot be made this way because they reduce the sulfuric acid; instead use the halide with concentrated phosphoric acid, which is not an oxidising agent.
- All are colourless gases that fume in moist air, dissolve in water to form strong acids (HCl, HBr, HI) and react with ammonia gas to form white smoke of ammonium halide: NH₃ + HCl → NH₄Cl.
- Thermal stability decreases from HF to HI, because the H–X bond gets longer and weaker. A hot wire plunged into HI gives purple iodine vapour; HCl does not decompose.

**Silver nitrate test.** Acidify the solution with dilute nitric acid (removes carbonate or hydroxide ions, which would also give precipitates), then add silver nitrate.

| Halide | Precipitate | Dilute NH₃ | Concentrated NH₃ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Cl⁻ | White AgCl | Dissolves | Dissolves |
| Br⁻ | Cream AgBr | Does not dissolve | Dissolves |
| I⁻ | Yellow AgI | Does not dissolve | Does not dissolve |
| F⁻ | No precipitate (AgF is soluble) | — | — |

Ag⁺(aq) + Cl⁻(aq) → AgCl(s). The precipitate dissolves when ammonia forms [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺. Do not acidify with HCl (it adds Cl⁻).

**Predicting fluorine and astatine (Edexcel 4.14).** Fluorine is a pale yellow gas, the strongest oxidising agent, and AgF is soluble. Astatine would be a black solid, the weakest oxidising agent, and AgAt would be insoluble even in concentrated ammonia.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying bromine "displaces" chloride. A halogen displaces a less reactive halide.
- Explaining oxidising power with "more shells, so more electrons". Say the incoming electron is further from the nucleus and more shielded.
- Forgetting to add nitric acid first in the silver nitrate test, or adding hydrochloric acid instead.
- Saying the sulfuric acid oxidises Cl⁻. It does not; only an acid–base reaction happens.

### 3.2.3.2 Uses of chlorine and chlorate(I)

*Refs: AQA 3.2.3.2, RP4 · Edexcel 4.12, 4.15, CP7 · OCR A 3.1.3(e), (f), 3.1.4(a) · OCR B ES(n) · Eduqas C1.6(q), PI2.1(j), (k). Level: AS & A (Eduqas PI2.1 A-level).*

#### What you need to know

- Reaction of chlorine with water, and with cold dilute sodium hydroxide (both disproportionations).
- Chlorine in water treatment: benefits and risks; chlorine in sunlight.
- Sodium chlorate(I) as bleach.
- Chlorine with hot alkali (Edexcel, Eduqas).
- Test-tube tests for ions (AQA RP4; OCR A 3.1.4).

#### Explained

**Chlorine with water.**

Cl₂ + H₂O ⇌ HCl + HClO. Chlorine goes from 0 to −1 and +1: disproportionation. Chloric(I) acid (HClO) kills bacteria. Universal indicator turns red then colourless (HClO bleaches it).

In bright sunlight chlorine water decomposes: 2Cl₂ + 2H₂O → 4HCl + O₂.

**Water treatment.** Small amounts of chlorine kill bacteria in drinking water and swimming pools, preventing diseases such as cholera and typhoid. Benefits outweigh the risks, but:

- chlorine is toxic, so the dose must be controlled;
- chlorine can react with organic matter to form chlorinated hydrocarbons, some of which may cause cancer.

Alternatives such as ozone or UV light have no lasting effect in the pipes. Fluoride ions are added to some water supplies to reduce tooth decay (Eduqas C1.6(q)), which raises questions about mass medication.

**Storage and transport (OCR B ES(n)).** Chlorine is a toxic gas, transported as a liquid under pressure. A leak can cause serious harm. Its uses (sterilising water, bleaching, making PVC and other chemicals) must be weighed against these risks.

**Chlorine with cold dilute sodium hydroxide.**

Cl₂ + 2NaOH → NaCl + NaClO + H₂O. The solution of sodium chlorate(I) is bleach.

**Chlorine with hot concentrated sodium hydroxide (Edexcel, Eduqas).**

3Cl₂ + 6NaOH → 5NaCl + NaClO₃ + 3H₂O. Chlorine goes 0 → −1 and 0 → +5. The chlorate(I) disproportionates when warm: 3ClO⁻ → 2Cl⁻ + ClO₃⁻.

**Bleaching and bactericidal action (Eduqas PI2.1(k)).** ClO⁻ and HClO are oxidising agents. They oxidise coloured dyes to colourless products and destroy enzymes and cell components in microbes.

**Tests for ions (AQA RP4, OCR A 3.1.4, Edexcel CP7, Eduqas).**

| Ion | Test | Positive result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Group 2 ions | Add NaOH(aq) dropwise, then excess; add dilute H₂SO₄ | Mg²⁺ white ppt with OH⁻; Ba²⁺ white ppt with SO₄²⁻; Ca²⁺ slight ppt with both |
| NH₄⁺ | Warm with NaOH(aq) | Gas turns damp red litmus blue (NH₃): NH₄⁺ + OH⁻ → NH₃ + H₂O |
| OH⁻ | Red litmus / indicator | Turns blue; pH above 7 |
| CO₃²⁻ | Add dilute acid; bubble gas through limewater | Effervescence; limewater goes cloudy |
| SO₄²⁻ | Add dilute HCl, then BaCl₂(aq) | White precipitate of BaSO₄ |
| Cl⁻, Br⁻, I⁻ | Add dilute HNO₃, then AgNO₃(aq), then NH₃ | White, cream, yellow precipitates (see above) |

**Order of tests (OCR A).** When testing for several anions in one solution, do carbonate first (add acid; any CO₃²⁻ is removed as CO₂), then sulfate (BaCO₃ would also be white, so carbonate must be gone first), then halides (Ag₂SO₄ can precipitate, so sulfate test comes before). Use HCl for the carbonate test only if you will not then test for chloride; use nitric acid otherwise.

#### Practical

**AQA RP4: test-tube reactions to identify cations (Group 2, NH₄⁺) and anions (Group 7 halides, OH⁻, CO₃²⁻, SO₄²⁻).** Use a few drops of each solution in a test tube, add the reagent dropwise and record observations including colours and whether a precipitate dissolves in excess. Carry out the ammonium test with warm (not boiling) mixture and hold damp red litmus at the mouth of the tube without touching the glass.

**Edexcel CP7 and Eduqas: analysis of unknowns.** Plan a sequence of these tests to identify an unknown salt, recording positive and negative results.

### Check questions

1. Explain the trend in boiling points down Group 7. (3 marks)
2. Explain why chlorine is a stronger oxidising agent than iodine. (3 marks)
3. Write an ionic equation and give the observation for the reaction of chlorine water with potassium bromide solution. (2 marks)
4. Solid sodium bromide reacts with concentrated sulfuric acid. Give two observations and write the equation for the redox step. (3 marks)
5. Explain why iodide ions reduce sulfuric acid further than bromide ions do. (2 marks)
6. Describe how to distinguish between solutions of sodium chloride and sodium bromide using silver nitrate. Include why nitric acid is added. (4 marks)
7. Write the equation for chlorine with water and explain why this is a disproportionation. (3 marks)
8. Give one benefit and one risk of chlorinating drinking water. (2 marks)
9. Describe a test for ammonium ions. (2 marks)
10. A solution may contain carbonate and sulfate ions. Describe tests, in order, to show both are present. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Boiling point increases (1); molecules larger with more electrons (1); stronger London forces need more energy to overcome (1).
2. Chlorine atom smaller (1); incoming electron closer to nucleus / less shielding (1); stronger attraction so electron gained more easily (1).
3. Cl₂ + 2Br⁻ → 2Cl⁻ + Br₂ (1); solution turns orange / yellow (1).
4. Any two of: misty white fumes, orange / brown fumes, colourless choking gas (2); 2HBr + H₂SO₄ → Br₂ + SO₂ + 2H₂O (1).
5. I⁻ larger / outer electrons further from nucleus and more shielded (1); so electrons lost more easily / stronger reducing agent (1).
6. Add dilute HNO₃ to remove carbonate / other ions that would precipitate (1); add AgNO₃: chloride white ppt, bromide cream ppt (1); add dilute NH₃ (1); AgCl dissolves, AgBr does not (1).
7. Cl₂ + H₂O ⇌ HCl + HClO (1); Cl goes from 0 to −1 (reduced) (1) and 0 to +1 (oxidised) (1).
8. Benefit: kills bacteria / prevents disease (1). Risk: toxic gas / forms chlorinated hydrocarbons that may cause cancer (1).
9. Warm with NaOH(aq) (1); gas turns damp red litmus blue (1).
10. Add dilute nitric (or hydrochloric) acid: effervescence, gas turns limewater cloudy shows carbonate (1); continue until no more fizzing so carbonate removed (1); then add BaCl₂ / Ba(NO₃)₂: white precipitate shows sulfate (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/06-equilibria-kc-and-kp.md -->

## Chemical equilibria, Le Chatelier's principle, Kc and Kp

Many reactions do not go to completion: they reach a balance where the forward and reverse reactions run at the same rate. This file covers dynamic equilibrium, Le Chatelier's principle, industrial compromise, the equilibrium constant Kc and, at A-level, Kp.

### Overview

The first two sections are AS. The Kp section and the effect of temperature on K are A-level only. Acid dissociation constants Ka are in file 11; ΔG and K are linked in file 8.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.6.1, 3.1.6.2 (AS) and 3.1.10 (A-level: Kp).
- Edexcel 10.1 to 10.4 (AS: includes Kc for heterogeneous systems) and 11.1 to 11.5 (A-level: Kp, effect of temperature on K, K unaffected by concentration, pressure and catalysts).
- OCR A 3.2.3(a) to (g) (AS: includes estimating the position of equilibrium from the size of Kc, and the techniques for investigating changes to an equilibrium) and 5.1.2(a) to (h) (A-level).
- OCR B ES(o) to (q) (AS) and CI(f) to (h) (A-level: choosing economical conditions; Kc and Kp calculations).
- Eduqas C2.1(a) to (c) (AS) and PI5.1(a) to (c) (A-level) with the specified practical: determining an equilibrium constant.

### 3.1.6.1 Chemical equilibria and Le Chatelier's principle

*Refs: AQA 3.1.6.1 · Edexcel 10.1 to 10.3 · OCR A 3.2.3(a) to (e) · OCR B ES(o), ES(q), CI(f), CI(g) · Eduqas C2.1(a), (b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Many reactions are reversible. In a closed system they reach dynamic equilibrium: forward and reverse rates are equal, and concentrations stay constant.
- Le Chatelier's principle: if a factor affecting an equilibrium is changed, the position of equilibrium shifts to oppose the change.
- Predict the effects of changing concentration, pressure and temperature, and of adding a catalyst.
- Industrial processes use compromise conditions.

#### Explained

"Dynamic" means both reactions are still happening. "Equilibrium" means no overall change. Concentrations are constant, not equal.

| Change | Shift | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Increase concentration of a reactant | Right, towards products | Uses up the added reactant |
| Remove a product | Right | Replaces the product |
| Increase pressure (gases) | Towards the side with fewer gas moles | Reduces the pressure |
| Increase temperature | In the endothermic direction | Absorbs the added heat |
| Decrease temperature | In the exothermic direction | Releases heat |
| Add a catalyst | No shift | Speeds up forward and reverse equally; equilibrium reached sooner |

If both sides have the same number of gas moles, pressure has no effect on position.

**Industrial compromise.** In the Haber process, N₂(g) + 3H₂(g) ⇌ 2NH₃(g), ΔH = −92 kJ mol⁻¹:

- Low temperature favours ammonia (exothermic forward), but the rate is too slow. About 450 °C is used with an iron catalyst.
- High pressure favours ammonia (4 gas moles → 2), but it is expensive and dangerous. About 200 atm is used.
- Unreacted gases are recycled.

Ethanol from ethene and steam, C₂H₄(g) + H₂O(g) ⇌ C₂H₅OH(g), ΔH = −46 kJ mol⁻¹: about 300 °C, 60 to 70 atm, phosphoric acid catalyst. Excess steam pushes the equilibrium right, but too much would dilute the catalyst. The conversion per pass is only about 5%, so unreacted ethene is recycled, giving an overall conversion of about 95%.

The Contact process step 2SO₂ + O₂ ⇌ 2SO₃ (ΔH negative) uses about 450 °C, slightly above atmospheric pressure (the equilibrium already lies far right), and a V₂O₅ catalyst.

**Investigating equilibria (OCR A 3.2.3(d)).** Coloured systems make shifts visible:

- 2NO₂(g, brown) ⇌ N₂O₄(g, colourless), ΔH negative. Warm the gas syringe and it goes darker brown (shift left, endothermic direction).
- [Co(H₂O)₆]²⁺ (pink) + 4Cl⁻ ⇌ [CoCl₄]²⁻ (blue) + 6H₂O, ΔH positive. Adding concentrated HCl or heating turns it blue; adding water or cooling turns it pink.
- Cr₂O₇²⁻ (orange) + H₂O ⇌ 2CrO₄²⁻ (yellow) + 2H⁺. Adding acid turns it orange; adding alkali removes H⁺ and turns it yellow.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying concentrations are equal at equilibrium.
- Saying a catalyst increases yield.
- Explaining pressure changes without counting gas moles.
- Writing "the equilibrium shifts to oppose the change" without saying which direction and why.

### 3.1.6.2 Equilibrium constant Kc for homogeneous systems

*Refs: AQA 3.1.6.2 · Edexcel 10.4, 11.2, 11.3, 11.5 · OCR A 3.2.3(f), (g), 5.1.2(b), (d) to (g) · OCR B ES(p), ES(q), CI(h) · Eduqas C2.1(c), PI5.1(a) to (c). Level: AS & A (temperature effect on Kc is A-level for Edexcel 11.3, OCR A 5.1.2(f), Eduqas PI5.1(a)).*

#### What you need to know

- For aA + bB ⇌ cC + dD, Kc = [C]ᶜ[D]ᵈ ÷ ([A]ᵃ[B]ᵇ), with equilibrium concentrations.
- Work out the units of Kc.
- Calculate Kc from equilibrium amounts, and amounts from Kc.
- Kc is changed only by temperature. Concentration, pressure and catalysts change the position, not Kc.
- The size of Kc indicates the position of equilibrium: Kc much greater than 1 means mostly products.
- Heterogeneous equilibria (Edexcel, OCR A): leave out solids (and pure liquids) from Kc.

#### Explained

**Units.** Put mol dm⁻³ for each concentration and cancel.

For N₂ + 3H₂ ⇌ 2NH₃: Kc = [NH₃]² ÷ ([N₂][H₂]³). Units: (mol dm⁻³)² ÷ (mol dm⁻³)⁴ = mol⁻² dm⁶.

For H₂ + I₂ ⇌ 2HI: Kc = [HI]² ÷ ([H₂][I₂]), no units.

**Temperature.** If the forward reaction is exothermic, raising the temperature shifts equilibrium left, so Kc decreases. If endothermic, Kc increases. This is the only way to change K.

**Why concentration does not change Kc.** If you add reactant, the ratio in the expression is temporarily below Kc. The system reacts forward until the ratio equals Kc again. So the position moves but Kc stays the same.

**Heterogeneous example.** CaCO₃(s) ⇌ CaO(s) + CO₂(g): Kc = [CO₂]. The solids have constant "concentration" and are absorbed into K.

**Method for calculations (the ICE table).**

1. Write initial moles.
2. Use the equation ratio to find the change.
3. Equilibrium moles = initial ± change.
4. Divide by volume to get concentrations (if the total moles on each side are equal, volume cancels, but show it).
5. Substitute into Kc.

#### Worked example

1. 1.00 mol of ethanoic acid and 1.00 mol of ethanol are mixed and left to reach equilibrium. 0.667 mol of ethyl ethanoate forms. Find Kc.

   CH₃COOH + C₂H₅OH ⇌ CH₃COOC₂H₅ + H₂O.

   | | Acid | Alcohol | Ester | Water |
   | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
   | Start | 1.00 | 1.00 | 0 | 0 |
   | Equilibrium | 0.333 | 0.333 | 0.667 | 0.667 |

   Kc = (0.667 × 0.667) ÷ (0.333 × 0.333) = 4.0 (no units; V cancels).

2. For H₂ + I₂ ⇌ 2HI, Kc = 49 at a certain temperature. 1.00 mol of H₂ and 1.00 mol of I₂ are mixed. Find the equilibrium moles of HI.

   Let x mol of H₂ react. 49 = (2x)² ÷ (1 − x)². Square root: 7 = 2x ÷ (1 − x). 7 − 7x = 2x, x = 0.778. HI = 1.56 mol.

#### Practical

**Eduqas: determining an equilibrium constant.** For ester hydrolysis: mix known amounts of ethyl ethanoate, water and a small known amount of hydrochloric acid catalyst in a sealed flask and leave for a week at constant temperature. Titrate the mixture with standard sodium hydroxide to find the total acid, subtract the HCl catalyst (found by titrating a blank), giving the moles of ethanoic acid at equilibrium. Work out the other amounts from the equation and calculate Kc. Titrate quickly and in a cold flask so the equilibrium does not shift during the titration.

#### Common mistakes

- Using initial amounts instead of equilibrium amounts.
- Forgetting the powers from the balanced equation.
- Writing solids in a Kc expression.
- Saying that increasing pressure changes Kc.

### 3.1.10 Equilibrium constant Kp for homogeneous systems

*Refs: AQA 3.1.10 · Edexcel 11.1 to 11.5 · OCR A 5.1.2(a) to (h) · OCR B CI(h) · Eduqas PI5.1(a) to (c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Mole fraction of a gas = moles of that gas ÷ total moles of gas.
- Partial pressure = mole fraction × total pressure. Partial pressures add up to the total pressure.
- For gases, Kp = p(C)ᶜ p(D)ᵈ ÷ (p(A)ᵃ p(B)ᵇ), and its units.
- Kp depends only on temperature. Effect of temperature on Kp for exothermic and endothermic reactions.
- Calculate Kp, or partial pressures, from data.

#### Explained

The partial pressure of a gas in a mixture is the pressure it would exert if it alone filled the container. Square brackets are not used in Kp; write p(X) or pX.

For N₂O₄(g) ⇌ 2NO₂(g): Kp = p(NO₂)² ÷ p(N₂O₄). Units: kPa² ÷ kPa = kPa (or Pa, or atm, matching the data).

**Kp and total pressure.** Raising the total pressure changes partial pressures, and the position of equilibrium shifts to the side with fewer moles, but Kp itself is unchanged. Only temperature changes Kp, in the same way as for Kc.

**Heterogeneous Kp (Edexcel, OCR A).** Only gases appear. For C(s) + H₂O(g) ⇌ CO(g) + H₂(g): Kp = p(CO) p(H₂) ÷ p(H₂O).

#### Worked example

0.80 mol of N₂O₄ is heated in a closed vessel. At equilibrium 0.30 mol has dissociated: N₂O₄ ⇌ 2NO₂. The total pressure is 200 kPa. Find Kp.

- N₂O₄ left: 0.50 mol. NO₂ formed: 0.60 mol. Total = 1.10 mol.
- Mole fractions: N₂O₄ 0.50 ÷ 1.10 = 0.455; NO₂ 0.60 ÷ 1.10 = 0.545.
- Partial pressures: N₂O₄ = 0.455 × 200 = 90.9 kPa; NO₂ = 0.545 × 200 = 109 kPa.
- Kp = 109² ÷ 90.9 = 131 kPa.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the initial moles in the total.
- Forgetting to multiply mole fractions by total pressure.
- Stating that higher pressure increases Kp.
- Giving Kp units that do not match the pressure unit used.

### Check questions

1. State two features of a dynamic equilibrium. (2 marks)
2. For 2SO₂(g) + O₂(g) ⇌ 2SO₃(g), ΔH = −196 kJ mol⁻¹, state and explain the effect on the yield of SO₃ of (a) increasing pressure, (b) increasing temperature. (4 marks)
3. Explain why a catalyst does not change the yield of an equilibrium reaction. (2 marks)
4. Write the Kc expression and units for 2SO₂(g) + O₂(g) ⇌ 2SO₃(g). (2 marks)
5. In a 2.00 dm³ flask at equilibrium there are 0.40 mol PCl₅, 0.20 mol PCl₃ and 0.20 mol Cl₂: PCl₅ ⇌ PCl₃ + Cl₂. Calculate Kc with units. (3 marks)
6. State and explain the effect of increasing the temperature on Kc for an exothermic reaction. (2 marks)
7. In the equilibrium N₂(g) + 3H₂(g) ⇌ 2NH₃(g) at 15.0 MPa, the mole fractions are N₂ 0.20, H₂ 0.60, NH₃ 0.20. Calculate Kp with units. (3 marks)
8. Write a Kc expression for the heterogeneous equilibrium 3Fe(s) + 4H₂O(g) ⇌ Fe₃O₄(s) + 4H₂(g). (1 mark)

### Mark scheme

1. Rate of forward reaction equals rate of reverse reaction (1); concentrations remain constant / closed system (1).
2. (a) Yield increases (1); fewer gas moles on the right (3 → 2), so equilibrium shifts right to reduce pressure (1). (b) Yield decreases (1); forward reaction exothermic, so equilibrium shifts left / in endothermic direction to absorb heat (1).
3. Catalyst increases rates of forward and reverse reactions equally (1); so position unchanged, only reached faster (1).
4. Kc = [SO₃]² ÷ ([SO₂]²[O₂]) (1); mol⁻¹ dm³ (1).
5. Concentrations 0.20, 0.10, 0.10 mol dm⁻³ (1); Kc = (0.10 × 0.10) ÷ 0.20 = 0.050 (1); mol dm⁻³ (1).
6. Kc decreases (1); equilibrium shifts in endothermic (reverse) direction, so less product / more reactant (1).
7. Partial pressures: N₂ 3.0 MPa, H₂ 9.0 MPa, NH₃ 3.0 MPa (1); Kp = 3.0² ÷ (3.0 × 9.0³) = 4.1 × 10⁻³ (1); MPa⁻² (1).
8. Kc = [H₂]⁴ ÷ [H₂O]⁴ (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/35-atmosphere-ozone-and-green-chemistry.md -->

## The atmosphere, ozone, the greenhouse effect and green chemistry

Chemistry explains, and can help fix, the problems humans cause in the atmosphere. This file covers measuring gas concentrations in percentages and ppm, ozone in the stratosphere and troposphere, the radical chemistry of ozone depletion and the role of bond enthalpy, the greenhouse effect and how greenhouse gases absorb infrared, ocean acidification, and the principles of green chemistry and sustainability.

### Overview

AS for most of the boards that include it. Ozone depletion by CFCs is also in file 19 (AQA, OCR A, Eduqas); pollution from combustion and catalytic converters is in file 18; IR spectroscopy in file 22; photon energies in file 33.

#### Board differences

- OCR A 4.2.4(b): absorption of infrared radiation by atmospheric gases containing C=O, O–H and C–H bonds (CO₂, H₂O, CH₄), the link to global warming, and changes to legislation.
- OCR B OZ(i) (percentage and ppm composition of gas mixtures), OZ(q) (ozone depletion and ease of photodissociation of haloalkanes), OZ(r) (ozone formation and destruction in the stratosphere and troposphere, sunscreen effect, photochemical smog), OZ(s) (principal radiations of the Sun and Earth), WM(g) (principles of green chemistry), O(n) (the greenhouse effect in detail).
- Eduqas C2.4(a), (b): social, economic and environmental impact of chemical synthesis and energy production; green chemistry and sustainability.
- AQA and Edexcel do not have a separate topic; related ideas are in files 18 and 19.

### OZ Gas concentrations, ozone and radicals

*Refs: OCR B OZ(i), OZ(q), OZ(r), OZ(s). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Calculate percentage by volume and parts per million (ppm) for gases.
- Ozone forms and breaks down naturally in the stratosphere, absorbing high-energy UV.
- In the troposphere, ozone is a pollutant that contributes to photochemical smog.
- Haloalkanes release halogen radicals by photodissociation; the ease depends on C–X bond enthalpy (C–I easiest, C–F hardest).
- Chlorine radicals catalyse ozone breakdown; other radicals (NO•, HO•) do too.
- The Sun emits mainly UV, visible and some IR; the Earth radiates IR.

#### Explained

**Gas composition.** For gases, volume percentage = mole percentage. ppm = (volume of component ÷ total volume) × 10⁶. Air is about 78% N₂, 21% O₂, 0.9% Ar, and about 0.04% (420 ppm) CO₂.

**Ozone in the stratosphere** (about 10 to 50 km up).

- Formation: O₂ + UV → 2O; O + O₂ + M → O₃ + M (M is any third molecule that carries away energy).
- Breakdown: O₃ + UV → O₂ + O; O + O₃ → 2O₂.

These reactions absorb high-energy UV (UV-B and UV-C), acting as a sunscreen. UV-B causes sunburn, skin cancer and cataracts, and damages crops and plankton.

**Ozone depletion.** Halogen radicals formed when UV breaks C–X bonds in haloalkanes catalyse ozone breakdown:

CF₂Cl₂ → •CF₂Cl + Cl•
Cl• + O₃ → ClO• + O₂
ClO• + O → Cl• + O₂
Overall: O₃ + O → 2O₂

**Ease of photodissociation.** The C–X bond enthalpy falls from C–F to C–I. C–F bonds are too strong to be broken by the UV that reaches the stratosphere, so fluoroalkanes (HFCs) do not deplete ozone. C–Cl bonds break, so CFCs do. C–Br and C–I break even more easily, but iodo- and bromoalkanes break down lower in the atmosphere before reaching the stratosphere; bromine from halons is still very damaging.

**Ozone in the troposphere.** Near the ground ozone is a pollutant: it irritates eyes and lungs and damages plants. It forms when sunlight breaks NO₂ (from vehicles): NO₂ → NO + O; O + O₂ → O₃. With unburnt hydrocarbons, this produces photochemical smog.

**Radiation from the Sun and Earth.** The Sun, being very hot, emits mostly visible light with some UV and IR. The Earth's surface, being much cooler, emits IR.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying ozone is always harmful or always useful. It depends where it is.
- Saying CFCs deplete ozone because C–F bonds break.

### O(n) The greenhouse effect

*Refs: OCR A 4.2.4(b) · OCR B O(n). Level: AS & A (OCR B O(n) A-level).*

#### What you need to know

- Solar energy reaches the Earth mainly as visible and UV radiation; the Earth warms and radiates IR.
- Greenhouse gases absorb some of this IR, increasing the vibrational energy of their bonds.
- They pass the energy to other molecules by collisions (raising the temperature) and re-emit IR in all directions, some back to Earth.
- Bonds such as C=O, O–H and C–H absorb IR; CO₂, H₂O and CH₄ are greenhouse gases. N₂ and O₂ are not.
- Human activity has increased CO₂ and CH₄ levels, enhancing the greenhouse effect; this links to global warming and climate change.

#### Explained

A molecule absorbs IR only if its vibration changes its dipole. N₂ and O₂ are symmetrical diatomics with no change of dipole when they stretch, so they do not absorb IR. CO₂ (asymmetric stretch and bending), H₂O and CH₄ do.

How much a gas contributes depends on: how well each molecule absorbs IR, how much of the gas is present, and how long it stays in the atmosphere (residence time). Methane is a much stronger absorber per molecule than CO₂ but is present at much lower concentration and lasts about 12 years; CO₂ lasts for centuries.

Some IR wavelengths are not absorbed by water vapour (the "IR window"); CO₂ and CH₄ absorb in parts of this window, which is why rising levels matter.

**Responses.** International agreements (the Kyoto Protocol, the Paris Agreement) set targets to cut emissions. Approaches include renewable energy, carbon capture and storage (pumping CO₂ into old oil fields or deep rock, or reacting it with metal oxides to form carbonates), and more efficient processes.

**Ocean acidification (OCR B Oceans context).** The oceans absorb CO₂: CO₂(g) ⇌ CO₂(aq); CO₂(aq) + H₂O ⇌ H⁺ + HCO₃⁻; HCO₃⁻ ⇌ H⁺ + CO₃²⁻. More CO₂ makes the sea slightly more acidic, and H⁺ removes CO₃²⁻ ions, making it harder for shellfish and corals to build calcium carbonate shells. The HCO₃⁻/CO₃²⁻ system also buffers the oceans (file 11).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying greenhouse gases "trap heat" without explaining absorption of IR by bond vibrations.
- Confusing the greenhouse effect with the hole in the ozone layer.

### C2.4 The wider impact of chemistry and green chemistry

*Refs: Eduqas C2.4(a), (b) · OCR B WM(g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Chemical industry and energy production have social, economic and environmental impacts.
- Principles of green chemistry and how they improve sustainability; apply them to given processes.

#### Explained

**Impacts.** Chemical industry provides medicines, fertilisers, materials and fuels, and employs many people. It also uses finite resources and energy, and can release waste and pollutants. Burning fossil fuels for energy releases CO₂, SO₂, NOₓ and particulates.

**Principles of green chemistry** (summarised; apply them to the information given):

1. Prevent waste rather than treat it afterwards.
2. Use reactions with high atom economy.
3. Use and make substances with low toxicity.
4. Use safer solvents (water, supercritical CO₂) or none.
5. Use less energy: lower temperatures and pressures, and catalysts.
6. Use renewable raw materials (feedstocks from plants).
7. Avoid unnecessary steps such as protecting groups.
8. Use catalysts rather than reagents used in large amounts.
9. Design products that break down after use (biodegradable).
10. Monitor processes to prevent pollution.
11. Design processes to reduce the risk of accidents.

**Examples.** Ibuprofen's industrial route was shortened from six steps to three, raising atom economy from about 40% to almost 80%. Supercritical CO₂ replaces organic solvents for decaffeinating coffee. Enzymes catalyse reactions at body temperature in water.

**Sustainability** means meeting present needs without harming the ability of future generations to meet theirs.

#### Common mistakes

- Treating percentage yield and atom economy as the same thing.
- Calling a process "green" just because it uses a catalyst, without considering raw materials, energy and waste.

### Check questions

1. A sample of air contains 0.042% carbon dioxide by volume. Express this in ppm. (1 mark)
2. Write equations to show how ozone forms in the stratosphere. (2 marks)
3. Explain why ozone in the stratosphere is beneficial but ozone in the troposphere is harmful. (3 marks)
4. Write equations to show how chlorine radicals from CFCs catalyse the breakdown of ozone. (3 marks)
5. Explain why HFCs do not deplete the ozone layer. (2 marks)
6. Explain how carbon dioxide molecules in the atmosphere cause an increase in temperature. (4 marks)
7. Explain why nitrogen and oxygen are not greenhouse gases. (1 mark)
8. Give three ways the chemical industry can make a process more sustainable. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. 420 ppm (1).
2. O₂ → 2O (UV) (1); O + O₂ → O₃ (1).
3. Stratosphere: absorbs harmful high-energy UV, protecting from skin cancer etc. (1). Troposphere: toxic / irritates lungs and eyes (1); contributes to photochemical smog (1).
4. CF₂Cl₂ → •CF₂Cl + Cl• (1); Cl• + O₃ → ClO• + O₂ (1); ClO• + O → Cl• + O₂ (1).
5. C–F bond too strong to be broken by UV (1); so no halogen radicals formed / no C–Cl bonds (1).
6. Earth radiates IR (1); CO₂ absorbs IR, increasing vibrational energy of its bonds (1); energy transferred to other molecules by collisions, increasing kinetic energy / temperature (1); some IR re-emitted back towards Earth (1).
7. Symmetrical diatomic molecules; no change in dipole when bonds vibrate, so do not absorb IR (1).
8. Any three: high atom economy; renewable feedstocks; catalysts / lower energy; safer solvents; less waste; biodegradable products; fewer steps.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/19-halogenoalkanes.md -->

## Halogenoalkanes: substitution, elimination and ozone depletion

Halogenoalkanes have a polar carbon–halogen bond that attracts nucleophiles. This file covers their classification, nucleophilic substitution with hydroxide, cyanide and ammonia, the effect of bond enthalpy on rate, SN1 and SN2 mechanisms, elimination to form alkenes, the competition between the two, uses, and how CFCs destroy ozone.

### Overview

AS content. Rate equations for SN1 and SN2 are in file 9 (A-level). Nitriles and amines formed here are used in files 26 and 29. The wider ozone story is in file 35.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.3.1 to 3.3.3.3.
- Edexcel 6.30 to 6.36 and CP4: adds the SN1 and SN2 mechanisms (6.36), primary, secondary and tertiary reactivity (6.34), and reactions with aqueous silver nitrate in ethanol and ethanolic ammonia.
- OCR A 4.2.2(a) to (e): hydrolysis by aqueous alkali and by water with AgNO₃ (rate depends on C–X bond enthalpy), and ozone depletion by Cl• and NO•.
- OCR B OZ(j) to (n): properties of haloalkanes, substitution, nucleophile, the SN2 mechanism, and how relative reactivity depends on bond enthalpy and polarity.
- Eduqas C3.3(a) to (g) and specified practical (nucleophilic substitution, for example 1-bromobutane with aqueous sodium hydroxide): includes uses as solvents, anaesthetics and refrigerants (C3.3(e)), and reflux (C3.3(g)).

### 3.3.3.1 Nucleophilic substitution

*Refs: AQA 3.3.3.1 · Edexcel 6.30 to 6.36, CP4 · OCR A 4.2.2(a) to (d) · OCR B OZ(j) to (n) · Eduqas C3.3(b) to (e), (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Halogenoalkanes contain a polar C–X bond; the carbon is δ+.
- Primary, secondary and tertiary classification.
- Nucleophilic substitution with OH⁻ (aqueous), CN⁻ (ethanolic) and NH₃ (excess, ethanolic, under pressure); mechanisms.
- Reactivity depends on C–X bond enthalpy: C–I is the weakest and reacts fastest, even though C–F is the most polar.
- SN1 and SN2 (Edexcel, OCR B): tertiary halogenoalkanes react by SN1, primary by SN2.

#### Explained

**Classification.** Primary: the carbon bonded to X is attached to one other carbon. Secondary: two. Tertiary: three. 1-bromobutane is primary, 2-bromobutane secondary, 2-bromo-2-methylpropane tertiary.

**Physical properties (OCR B).** Boiling points are higher than alkanes of similar size because of permanent dipole–dipole forces, and rise from F to I (more electrons, stronger London forces). They do not mix with water because they cannot form hydrogen bonds with it.

**Mechanism (SN2) with hydroxide.** CH₃CH₂Br + OH⁻ → CH₃CH₂OH + Br⁻.

1. The lone pair on OH⁻ is attracted to the δ+ carbon. Curly arrow from the lone pair on O to the carbon.
2. At the same time the C–Br bond breaks heterolytically. Curly arrow from the C–Br bond to Br.
3. Products: ethanol and Br⁻.

Conditions: warm aqueous NaOH or KOH, under reflux. This is also called hydrolysis.

**With cyanide.** CH₃CH₂Br + CN⁻ → CH₃CH₂CN + Br⁻. Conditions: KCN in ethanol, warm under reflux. Product: propanenitrile. The carbon chain grows by one carbon, which is useful in synthesis (file 29). The lone pair is on the carbon of C≡N⁻.

**With ammonia.** CH₃CH₂Br + 2NH₃ → CH₃CH₂NH₂ + NH₄Br. Conditions: excess ammonia in ethanol, heated in a sealed tube (under pressure). Mechanism: the lone pair on N attacks the δ+ carbon; Br⁻ leaves; the intermediate CH₃CH₂NH₃⁺ loses a proton to a second NH₃ molecule. Excess ammonia reduces further substitution (the amine product is itself a nucleophile, file 26).

**Rate and bond enthalpy.**

| Bond | Bond enthalpy / kJ mol⁻¹ | Polarity | Rate of hydrolysis |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| C–F | 467 | Most polar | Slowest (almost none) |
| C–Cl | 346 | | Slow |
| C–Br | 290 | | Faster |
| C–I | 228 | Least polar | Fastest |

Polarity alone would predict C–F reacts fastest. In fact the rate is decided by how easily the C–X bond breaks, so bond enthalpy is the dominant factor.

**Comparing rates experimentally (OCR A 4.2.2(d), Edexcel CP4).** Add a few drops of each halogenoalkane to ethanol (a common solvent for water and the halogenoalkane) in test tubes in a water bath at 50 °C. Add warm aqueous silver nitrate and start timing. Water acts as the nucleophile, releasing X⁻, which forms a silver halide precipitate. Time how long until a precipitate appears: iodo (yellow, fastest), bromo (cream), chloro (white, slowest).

R–X + H₂O → R–OH + H⁺ + X⁻; Ag⁺ + X⁻ → AgX(s).

**SN1 and SN2 (Edexcel 6.36, OCR B OZ(m)).**

- **SN2** (substitution, nucleophilic, bimolecular): one step, the nucleophile attacks as the halide leaves. Rate = k[RX][OH⁻]. Typical of primary halogenoalkanes. The transition state has five groups around carbon.
- **SN1** (unimolecular): two steps. The C–X bond breaks first to give a carbocation (slow, rate-determining), then the nucleophile attacks (fast). Rate = k[RX]. Typical of tertiary halogenoalkanes because the three alkyl groups push electrons towards the positive carbon (positive inductive effect), stabilising the tertiary carbocation, and because they crowd the carbon so a nucleophile cannot reach it from the back.
- Secondary halogenoalkanes react by both.

So the order of reactivity in hydrolysis is tertiary > secondary > primary (Edexcel 6.34).

**Uses (Eduqas C3.3(e)).** Solvents (dichloromethane in paint stripping and decaffeinating); anaesthetics (halothane, CF₃CHBrCl); refrigerants and aerosol propellants (CFCs, now replaced by HFCs and hydrocarbons); fire retardants; making PVC and PTFE.

#### Practical

**Eduqas: nucleophilic substitution**, for example warming 1-bromobutane with aqueous sodium hydroxide under reflux to make butan-1-ol. Reflux (Eduqas C3.3(g)): heat the mixture in a round-bottomed flask with anti-bumping granules and a vertical condenser, so vapours condense and drip back; the reaction can be heated for a long time without losing volatile reactants or products. Then distil the product (file 21).

#### Common mistakes

- Curly arrow from the negative charge instead of the lone pair of OH⁻.
- Showing the C–Br bond breaking towards carbon.
- Saying C–F reacts fastest because it is most polar.
- Using aqueous KCN (it gives some hydrolysis to alcohol); use ethanolic KCN.

### 3.3.3.2 Elimination

*Refs: AQA 3.3.3.2 · Edexcel 6.32 · Eduqas C3.3(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- OH⁻ can act as a base and remove HX to form an alkene.
- Conditions decide whether substitution or elimination dominates.
- The elimination mechanism.

#### Explained

CH₃CHBrCH₃ + KOH → CH₂=CHCH₃ + KBr + H₂O. Conditions: KOH dissolved in ethanol, heated under reflux.

**Mechanism.**

1. The lone pair on OH⁻ attacks an H atom on the carbon next to the C–Br carbon (acting as a base). Curly arrow from the lone pair to that H.
2. The C–H bond electrons move to form the C=C. Curly arrow from the C–H bond to between the two carbons.
3. The C–Br bond breaks and Br⁻ leaves. Curly arrow from the C–Br bond to Br.

**Substitution or elimination?**

| Favours substitution | Favours elimination |
| --- | --- |
| Aqueous solvent | Ethanolic solvent |
| Lower temperature | Higher temperature |
| Primary halogenoalkane | Tertiary halogenoalkane |

OH⁻ in water acts mainly as a nucleophile; OH⁻ in ethanol acts mainly as a base.

**Isomers.** Eliminating HBr from 2-bromobutane gives but-1-ene and but-2-ene (as E and Z isomers): three alkenes.

#### Common mistakes

- Attacking the carbon bonded to the halogen in elimination. The base removes an H from the neighbouring carbon.
- Forgetting the extra alkene isomers.

### 3.3.3.3 Ozone depletion

*Refs: AQA 3.3.3.3 · OCR A 4.2.2(e) · Eduqas C3.3(f). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Ozone in the upper atmosphere absorbs harmful UV radiation.
- CFCs break down in UV to give Cl• radicals that catalyse ozone decomposition.
- Equations for the chain reaction; NO• radicals do the same (OCR A).
- Legislation to ban CFCs, and alternatives (HFCs).

#### Explained

**Ozone is useful.** In the stratosphere, ozone absorbs UV-B and UV-C radiation, which would otherwise cause skin cancer, cataracts and crop damage. It forms and breaks down naturally: O₂ → 2O (UV); O + O₂ → O₃; O₃ → O₂ + O (UV).

**CFCs** (chlorofluorocarbons) are very unreactive, so they last long enough to reach the stratosphere. There, UV light breaks the C–Cl bond (weaker than C–F):

CF₂Cl₂ → •CF₂Cl + Cl•

Cl• catalyses ozone breakdown:

Cl• + O₃ → ClO• + O₂
ClO• + O₃ → 2O₂ + Cl•

Overall: 2O₃ → 3O₂. Cl• is regenerated, so one radical can destroy many thousands of ozone molecules before termination.

**NO• radicals** from aircraft and lightning act the same way: NO• + O₃ → NO₂• + O₂; NO₂• + O → NO• + O₂ (overall O₃ + O → 2O₂).

**Action taken.** The Montreal Protocol (1987) phased out CFCs. Chemists developed replacements: HFCs (hydrofluorocarbons such as CH₂FCF₃), which contain no C–Cl bonds, and hydrocarbons such as butane for aerosols and refrigerators. HFCs are, however, greenhouse gases. The ozone layer is slowly recovering. This is an example of scientists' evidence informing legislation.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying CFCs break down because C–F bonds break. C–Cl breaks; C–F is too strong.
- Writing Cl⁻ instead of Cl•.
- Confusing ozone depletion with global warming.

### Check questions

1. Classify 2-chloro-2-methylbutane as primary, secondary or tertiary. (1 mark)
2. Outline the mechanism for the reaction of 1-bromopropane with aqueous sodium hydroxide. Name the mechanism. (4 marks)
3. Give the reagent, conditions and product of the reaction of bromoethane with potassium cyanide. Why is this reaction useful? (3 marks)
4. Explain why 1-iodobutane is hydrolysed faster than 1-chlorobutane. (2 marks)
5. Describe an experiment to compare the rates of hydrolysis of 1-chlorobutane, 1-bromobutane and 1-iodobutane. (4 marks)
6. Outline the mechanism for the formation of propene from 2-bromopropane with ethanolic KOH. (3 marks)
7. State the conditions that favour elimination over substitution. (2 marks)
8. Write equations to show how chlorine radicals catalyse the decomposition of ozone. (3 marks)
9. Explain why the rate equation for hydrolysis of 2-bromo-2-methylpropane is first order. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Tertiary (1).
2. Nucleophilic substitution (1); curly arrow from lone pair on OH⁻ to δ+ carbon (1); curly arrow from C–Br bond to Br (1); products propan-1-ol and Br⁻ with δ+/δ− shown on C–Br (1).
3. KCN in ethanol, heat under reflux (1); propanenitrile, CH₃CH₂CN (1); increases carbon chain length by one (1).
4. C–I bond weaker / lower bond enthalpy than C–Cl (1); breaks more easily (1).
5. Same volume of each halogenoalkane with ethanol in test tubes in a water bath at the same temperature (1); add equal volumes of warm aqueous AgNO₃ and start timing (1); time for precipitate to appear (1); iodide fastest (yellow), chloride slowest (white) (1).
6. OH⁻ lone pair to H on carbon next to C–Br (1); arrow from C–H bond to form C=C (1); arrow from C–Br bond to Br (1).
7. Ethanolic (not aqueous) solvent (1); higher temperature / tertiary halogenoalkane (1).
8. Cl• + O₃ → ClO• + O₂ (1); ClO• + O₃ → 2O₂ + Cl• (1); Cl• regenerated / overall 2O₃ → 3O₂ (1).
9. SN1: rate-determining step involves only the halogenoalkane (1); C–Br breaks to form a carbocation before OH⁻ attacks (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/21-alcohols.md -->

## Alcohols: production, oxidation and elimination

Alcohols contain the –OH group. This file covers their properties and classification, making ethanol by hydration and by fermentation, combustion, oxidation with acidified dichromate, dehydration to alkenes, substitution to halogenoalkanes, other reactions, and the practical techniques of reflux, distillation and purifying a liquid. It includes AQA required practical 5.

### Overview

AS content. Tests that tell primary, secondary and tertiary alcohols and aldehydes apart are in file 22. Esterification is in file 24.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.5.1 to 3.3.5.3 and RP5.
- Edexcel 6.37 to 6.39, CP5 (oxidation of ethanol) and CP6 (chlorination of 2-methylpropan-2-ol with concentrated hydrochloric acid). Edexcel names halogenating agents: PCl₅, KBr with 50% sulfuric acid, and red phosphorus with iodine; and the reaction with sodium.
- OCR A 4.2.1(a) to (e) and 4.2.3(a): adds polarity and water solubility, combustion, and Quickfit techniques (reflux, distillation, separating funnel, drying).
- OCR B WM(b), (d), (h): classification, reactions of alcohols (oxidation, dehydration, ester formation, substitution) and the term elimination.
- Eduqas C3.4(a) to (f), (i) (AS) and OA2.1(a), (b) (A-level): industrial preparation of ethanol, fermentation, dehydration, oxidation, the dichromate test and distillation. Specified practical: preparing an ester and separating it by distillation.

### 3.3.5.1 Alcohol production
