*Refs: AQA 3.3.5.1 · OCR A 4.2.1(a) · OCR B WM(b) · Eduqas C3.4(a), (b), (d), OA2.1(a). Level: AS & A (Eduqas OA2.1 A-level).*

#### What you need to know

- Alcohols are primary, secondary or tertiary depending on how many carbons are attached to the carbon bearing the –OH.
- Ethanol is made industrially by the hydration of ethene with steam (phosphoric acid catalyst) and by fermentation of glucose.
- Advantages and disadvantages of each method; biofuels and carbon neutrality.
- Alcohols are polar and form hydrogen bonds, so small alcohols are soluble in water and have relatively high boiling points.

#### Explained

**Classification.** Primary: CH₃CH₂OH (the C–OH carbon has one carbon attached). Secondary: CH₃CH(OH)CH₃. Tertiary: (CH₃)₃COH.

**Hydration of ethene.** CH₂=CH₂(g) + H₂O(g) ⇌ CH₃CH₂OH(g). Conditions: 300 °C, 6 to 7 MPa, phosphoric acid on silica. Mechanism is electrophilic addition; H⁺ from the acid catalyst adds first:

1. Arrow from C=C to H⁺ (from H₃PO₄ / H₃O⁺): carbocation forms.
2. Lone pair on a water molecule attacks the carbocation.
3. The oxonium ion (CH₃CH₂OH₂⁺) loses H⁺, regenerating the catalyst.

**Fermentation.** C₆H₁₂O₆(aq) → 2C₂H₅OH(aq) + 2CO₂(g). Conditions: yeast enzymes, 30 to 40 °C (too cold is slow; too hot denatures the enzymes), anaerobic (with air, the ethanol would be oxidised to ethanoic acid). Stops at about 15% ethanol when the yeast dies; the ethanol is then separated by fractional distillation.

| | Hydration of ethene | Fermentation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Raw material | Crude oil (finite) | Sugar from crops (renewable) |
| Process | Continuous | Batch |
| Rate | Fast | Slow |
| Purity | Pure ethanol | Dilute; needs distilling |
| Atom economy | 100% | 51% (CO₂ waste) |
| Energy cost | High temperature and pressure | Low temperature, but distillation uses energy |

**Carbon neutrality of bioethanol (AQA).** Plants take in 6CO₂ in photosynthesis to make one glucose. Fermentation releases 2CO₂ and burning the two ethanols releases 4CO₂: total 6. So in theory it is carbon neutral. In practice, energy for farming machinery, fertilisers, transport and distillation usually comes from fossil fuels, so overall CO₂ is released.

**Other routes (Eduqas OA2.1(a)).** Halogenoalkane + aqueous NaOH under reflux gives an alcohol (file 19). Reduction of aldehydes by NaBH₄ gives primary alcohols and of ketones gives secondary alcohols (file 23).

**Properties (OCR A 4.2.1(a)).** The O–H bond is polar. Alcohols form hydrogen bonds with each other (high boiling points compared with alkanes of similar Mᵣ) and with water (small alcohols mix completely with water). As the chain gets longer, the non-polar part dominates and solubility falls.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying fermentation is carbon neutral without qualification.
- Using phosphoric acid for fermentation or yeast for hydration.
- Classifying an alcohol by the number of H atoms rather than C atoms.

### 3.3.5.2 Oxidation of alcohols

*Refs: AQA 3.3.5.2 · Edexcel 6.38, CP5 · OCR A 4.2.1(b), (c) · OCR B WM(d) · Eduqas C3.4(e), (f). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Primary alcohols are oxidised to aldehydes, then to carboxylic acids. Secondary alcohols are oxidised to ketones. Tertiary alcohols are not oxidised.
- Oxidising agent: potassium dichromate(VI) acidified with dilute sulfuric acid; orange Cr₂O₇²⁻ turns green Cr³⁺.
- Distil to collect the aldehyde; reflux with excess oxidant for the carboxylic acid.
- Combustion of alcohols (OCR A, Edexcel).

#### Explained

Use [O] for the oxidising agent in equations.

- Primary to aldehyde: CH₃CH₂OH + [O] → CH₃CHO + H₂O.
- Aldehyde to acid: CH₃CHO + [O] → CH₃COOH.
- Primary to acid in one: CH₃CH₂OH + 2[O] → CH₃COOH + H₂O.
- Secondary to ketone: CH₃CH(OH)CH₃ + [O] → CH₃COCH₃ + H₂O.

**Why tertiary alcohols are not oxidised.** Oxidation removes the H from the O–H and an H from the carbon bearing the OH. A tertiary alcohol has no H on that carbon.

**Getting the aldehyde.** Use excess alcohol and heat gently with the dichromate, distilling off the product as it forms. The aldehyde has the lowest boiling point in the mixture (no hydrogen bonding between its molecules), so it leaves before it can be oxidised further.

**Getting the acid.** Use excess oxidising agent and heat under reflux, so any aldehyde vapour condenses back and is oxidised to the acid. Then distil.

**Combustion.** C₂H₅OH + 3O₂ → 2CO₂ + 3H₂O. Alcohols burn with a clean blue flame and are used as fuels.

#### Practical

**Edexcel CP5: the oxidation of ethanol.** Add acidified potassium dichromate dropwise to ethanol with cooling (exothermic), then (a) set up for distillation to collect ethanal, or (b) reflux then distil to collect ethanoic acid. Test the distillate: ethanal gives a red precipitate with Fehling's or Benedict's solution, or a silver mirror with Tollens' (file 22); ethanoic acid fizzes with sodium carbonate.

#### Common mistakes

- Refluxing when the aldehyde is wanted.
- Writing the colour change backwards. Orange to green.
- Using [O] as O₂.

### 3.3.5.3 Elimination

*Refs: AQA 3.3.5.3, RP5 · Edexcel 6.38, 6.39, CP6 · OCR A 4.2.1(d), (e), 4.2.3(a) · OCR B WM(h) · Eduqas C3.4(c), (i), OA2.1(b). Level: AS & A (Eduqas OA2.1 A-level).*

#### What you need to know

- Alcohols are dehydrated to alkenes by heating with concentrated phosphoric or sulfuric acid (or passing the vapour over hot Al₂O₃). Mechanism (AQA).
- Alkenes made from renewable ethanol can make polymers without crude oil.
- Substitution of –OH by halogen (OCR A, Edexcel, Eduqas).
- Reaction with sodium (Edexcel).
- Techniques: reflux, distillation, separating funnel, drying, boiling point.

#### Explained

**Dehydration.** CH₃CH₂OH → CH₂=CH₂ + H₂O. Conditions: excess concentrated H₃PO₄ (or H₂SO₄) at about 170 °C, or vapour over hot Al₂O₃.

Mechanism (acid-catalysed elimination):

1. A lone pair on the O of the –OH bonds to H⁺: the alcohol is protonated.
2. Water leaves, taking the C–O bond electrons, to give a carbocation.
3. A C–H bond on the neighbouring carbon breaks; its electrons form the C=C; H⁺ is lost (regenerating the catalyst).

Secondary alcohols can give more than one alkene: butan-2-ol gives but-1-ene, E-but-2-ene and Z-but-2-ene.

This route makes ethene from bioethanol, so poly(ethene) can be made without crude oil.

**Substitution to halogenoalkanes.**

| Reagent | Product | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| HCl (concentrated), shaken | Chloroalkane | Works well for tertiary alcohols (Edexcel CP6) |
| KBr + concentrated (or 50%) H₂SO₄ | Bromoalkane | HBr made in situ |
| PCl₅ | Chloroalkane | Steamy fumes of HCl: ROH + PCl₅ → RCl + POCl₃ + HCl. Also a test for –OH. |
| Red phosphorus + iodine | Iodoalkane | PI₃ made in situ |

**With sodium (Edexcel).** 2CH₃CH₂OH + 2Na → 2CH₃CH₂O⁻Na⁺ + H₂. Fizzing, less vigorous than with water. Sodium ethoxide forms.

**With ethanoyl chloride and carboxylic acids (Eduqas OA2.1(b)).** Esters form (file 24).

**Purifying a liquid organic product (Edexcel 6.39, OCR A 4.2.3(a)).**

1. **Reflux** to complete the reaction (file 19).
2. **Distil** to separate the crude product from the mixture: heat in a flask with a still head, thermometer at the side arm and a condenser sloping down to a collecting flask; collect the fraction at the product's boiling point.
3. **Separating funnel** to wash: shake with sodium hydrogencarbonate solution to remove acid (release pressure often, CO₂ forms), then with water. Run off and keep the organic layer (check densities: the layer with the lower density is on top).
4. **Dry** with an anhydrous salt (anhydrous MgSO₄, CaCl₂ or Na₂SO₄) until the liquid is clear and the drying agent no longer clumps; decant or filter.
5. **Redistil**, collecting only within a narrow range around the boiling point.
6. **Boiling point** close to the data value, over a narrow range, shows purity.

#### Practical

**AQA RP5: distillation of a product from a reaction.** For example making cyclohexene from cyclohexanol and concentrated phosphoric acid: heat and distil off the cyclohexene (b.p. 83 °C), wash in a separating funnel, dry with anhydrous CaCl₂, redistil. Or distil ethanal from the oxidation of ethanol. Anti-bumping granules give smooth boiling; heat with a heating mantle or water bath because the products are flammable; do not seal the apparatus.

**Edexcel CP6: chlorination of 2-methylpropan-2-ol.** Shake the alcohol with concentrated hydrochloric acid in a separating funnel, releasing pressure. Separate the upper organic layer, wash with sodium hydrogencarbonate, dry with anhydrous sodium sulfate, and distil, collecting 2-chloro-2-methylpropane at about 50 °C.

**Eduqas: preparing an ester and separating by distillation** (file 24 for the reaction).

#### Common mistakes

- Sealing the apparatus during distillation or reflux.
- Putting the thermometer bulb in the liquid when distilling. It should be at the side arm, measuring vapour temperature.
- Keeping the wrong layer in a separating funnel.
- Saying the drying agent is "dehydrating" the product chemically. It absorbs water.

### Check questions

1. Classify butan-2-ol and 2-methylpropan-2-ol. (2 marks)
2. Give two advantages of making ethanol by fermentation rather than hydration of ethene. (2 marks)
3. Explain why ethanol made by fermentation may not be carbon neutral. (2 marks)
4. Describe how to make and collect propanal from propan-1-ol. Include reagents, conditions and the observation. (4 marks)
5. Explain why 2-methylpropan-2-ol is not oxidised by acidified dichromate. (1 mark)
6. Outline the mechanism for the acid-catalysed dehydration of propan-2-ol. (4 marks)
7. Name the alkenes formed when pentan-2-ol is dehydrated. (2 marks)
8. A crude ester is washed with sodium hydrogencarbonate solution in a separating funnel. Explain why, and why the tap must be opened often. (2 marks)
9. Describe how to dry an organic liquid. (2 marks)
10. Write an equation for the reaction of ethanol with PCl₅ and give an observation. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Butan-2-ol secondary (1); 2-methylpropan-2-ol tertiary (1).
2. Any two: renewable raw material; lower temperature / less energy; simple equipment; does not use crude oil.
3. Fossil fuels used in farming / fertiliser / transport / distillation (1); so overall CO₂ released (1).
4. Acidified potassium dichromate(VI) (1); warm, distil off product as it forms / excess alcohol (1); collect distillate at about 48 °C (1); orange to green (1).
5. No hydrogen on the carbon bonded to OH (1).
6. Lone pair on O to H⁺ (protonation) (1); water leaves, carbocation forms (1); C–H bond on adjacent carbon breaks to form C=C (1); H⁺ regenerated; product propene (1).
7. Pent-1-ene (1); E- and Z-pent-2-ene (1).
8. To neutralise / remove acid (1); CO₂ produced, pressure builds up (1).
9. Add anhydrous MgSO₄ / CaCl₂ until liquid clear / no longer clumps (1); filter or decant (1).
10. C₂H₅OH + PCl₅ → C₂H₅Cl + POCl₃ + HCl (1); steamy / misty fumes (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/25-aromatic-chemistry.md -->

## Aromatic chemistry: benzene, electrophilic substitution and phenol

Benzene's ring of delocalised electrons makes it unusually stable. This file covers the Kekulé and delocalised models and the evidence for delocalisation, why benzene undergoes substitution not addition, nitration, halogenation, Friedel–Crafts acylation and alkylation, sulfonation, and the chemistry of phenol, including directing effects.

### Overview

A-level for most boards. Reduction of nitrobenzene to phenylamine is in file 26; azo dyes are in file 37.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.10.1, 3.3.10.2: nitration and Friedel–Crafts acylation with mechanisms, and uses of nitration (explosives, making amines).
- Edexcel 18.1 to 18.7: adds combustion (smoky flame), bromination with a catalyst, Friedel–Crafts alkylation, and phenol with bromine water with the reason it reacts more easily than benzene.
- OCR A 6.1.1(a) to (l) and 6.2.4(d): naming aromatic compounds, halogenation with a halogen carrier, phenol (acidity, bromine, dilute nitric acid), 2- and 4-directing and 3-directing groups, Friedel–Crafts alkylation and acylation.
- OCR B WM(c) (AS: phenol acidity and the FeCl₃ test) and CD(d) to (g) (A-level: arenes, the two representations of benzene, naming, halogenation, nitration with mechanism, sulfonation and Friedel–Crafts reactions).
- Eduqas OA1.2(a) to (d) and OA2.1(c), (d): bonding in benzene, resistance to addition, electrophilic substitution, how substituent groups interact with the ring (phenol and bromine); acidity of phenol, reactions with bromine and ethanoyl chloride, and the FeCl₃ test.

### 3.3.10.1 Bonding

*Refs: AQA 3.3.10.1 · Edexcel 18.1 to 18.3 · OCR A 6.1.1(a) to (c), (f) · OCR B CD(d), CD(e), CD(f) · Eduqas OA1.2(a), (b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Benzene, C₆H₆, is a flat hexagon with all C–C bonds the same length (intermediate between single and double), with delocalised π electrons.
- The Kekulé structure and why it was replaced: bond lengths, enthalpy of hydrogenation, and resistance to addition.
- Naming aromatic compounds.

#### Explained

**Delocalised model.** Each carbon forms three σ bonds (two to C, one to H) at 120°. The remaining p orbital on each carbon overlaps sideways with both neighbours, forming a ring of π electron density above and below the plane. The six π electrons are spread over all six carbons. Drawn as a hexagon with a circle.

**Evidence against Kekulé (alternating single and double bonds):**

1. **Bond lengths.** X-ray diffraction shows all C–C bonds are 0.139 nm, between C–C (0.154 nm) and C=C (0.134 nm). Kekulé predicts two lengths.
2. **Enthalpy of hydrogenation.** Cyclohexene + H₂ → cyclohexane, ΔH = −120 kJ mol⁻¹. Kekulé benzene with three C=C would give 3 × −120 = −360 kJ mol⁻¹. Actual benzene: −208 kJ mol⁻¹. Benzene is about 152 kJ mol⁻¹ more stable than Kekulé predicts: the delocalisation (resonance) energy.
3. **Reactivity.** Kekulé benzene would decolorise bromine water like an alkene. Benzene does not; it reacts with bromine only with a catalyst, and by substitution.
4. Infrared spectra show no C=C absorption typical of alkenes (OCR A).

**Why substitution, not addition.** Addition would break the delocalised ring and lose the extra stability. Substitution replaces an H and keeps the ring.

**Why benzene is less reactive than alkenes towards electrophiles (Edexcel 18.3, OCR A 6.1.1(f)).** In an alkene, the π electrons are concentrated between two carbons: high electron density that can polarise Br₂. In benzene, the π electrons are spread over six carbons: lower electron density, not enough to polarise Br₂. A halogen carrier is needed.

**Naming.** Substituents as prefixes: chlorobenzene, nitrobenzene, methylbenzene. Some take benzene as a suffix-stem: phenol (C₆H₅OH), phenylamine (C₆H₅NH₂), benzoic acid (C₆H₅COOH), benzaldehyde. The C₆H₅– group is "phenyl". Number the ring to give lowest numbers: 2,4,6-tribromophenol; 1,3-dinitrobenzene.

**Combustion (Edexcel).** Arenes burn with a very smoky flame because of the high C : H ratio.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying benzene has "three double bonds".
- Saying the hydrogenation enthalpy is "more exothermic" than predicted. It is less exothermic, so benzene is more stable.
- Saying delocalisation makes benzene more reactive.

### 3.3.10.2 Electrophilic substitution

*Refs: AQA 3.3.10.2 · Edexcel 18.4 to 18.7 · OCR A 6.1.1(d), (e), (g) to (l), 6.2.4(d) · OCR B WM(c), CD(g) · Eduqas OA1.2(c), (d), OA2.1(c), (d). Level: A-level only (OCR B WM(c) AS).*

#### What you need to know

- Nitration (conc. HNO₃ and conc. H₂SO₄, below 55 °C): formation of NO₂⁺ and mechanism.
- Halogenation with a halogen carrier (FeBr₃, AlCl₃, or Fe): mechanism.
- Friedel–Crafts acylation (acyl chloride and AlCl₃): mechanism. Friedel–Crafts alkylation (halogenoalkane and AlCl₃) (Edexcel, OCR A, OCR B).
- Sulfonation (OCR B).
- Uses of nitration: explosives (TNT) and making aromatic amines for dyes.
- Phenol: weak acid; reactions with NaOH, Na, bromine water and dilute nitric acid; ethanoyl chloride; FeCl₃ test. Why phenol reacts more easily than benzene.
- Directing effects (OCR A).

#### Explained

**General mechanism.**

1. The electrophile, E⁺, is generated.
2. A curly arrow from the delocalised ring (inside the circle) to E⁺. The intermediate has a horseshoe of delocalisation over five carbons with a + charge; the sixth carbon is bonded to both E and H.
3. A curly arrow from the C–H bond back into the ring restores delocalisation; H⁺ is lost.

**Nitration.** Electrophile: nitronium ion, NO₂⁺.

HNO₃ + 2H₂SO₄ → NO₂⁺ + H₃O⁺ + 2HSO₄⁻

Sulfuric acid is the stronger acid and protonates nitric acid, which then loses water. The H⁺ released at the end reforms H₂SO₄ with HSO₄⁻ (catalyst). Below 55 °C to prevent multiple substitution.

C₆H₆ + HNO₃ → C₆H₅NO₂ + H₂O

Uses: nitrobenzene is reduced to phenylamine for dyes (files 26, 37); methylbenzene is nitrated three times to make TNT.

**Halogenation.** Electrophile: Br⁺ (as a polarised complex). The halogen carrier accepts a lone pair from Br₂:

Br₂ + FeBr₃ → Br⁺ + FeBr₄⁻

After substitution, H⁺ + FeBr₄⁻ → HBr + FeBr₃ (catalyst regenerated). Iron filings work because they react with bromine to form FeBr₃. AlCl₃ with Cl₂ gives chlorobenzene.

**Friedel–Crafts acylation.** Electrophile: acylium ion, RCO⁺.

CH₃COCl + AlCl₃ → CH₃CO⁺ + AlCl₄⁻

C₆H₆ + CH₃COCl → C₆H₅COCH₃ + HCl (phenylethanone). Conditions: anhydrous (AlCl₃ reacts with water), reflux at about 50 °C. Then H⁺ + AlCl₄⁻ → AlCl₃ + HCl.

**Friedel–Crafts alkylation.** Electrophile: carbocation from a halogenoalkane: CH₃CH₂Cl + AlCl₃ → CH₃CH₂⁺ + AlCl₄⁻. Gives ethylbenzene. It forms a C–C bond to the ring (OCR A 6.2.4(d)). Drawback: the alkylbenzene is more reactive than benzene, so it substitutes again.

**Sulfonation (OCR B).** Heat benzene with concentrated (or fuming) sulfuric acid: C₆H₆ + H₂SO₄ → C₆H₅SO₃H + H₂O. The electrophile is SO₃. Benzenesulfonic acids are used to make detergents and to make dyes water-soluble.

**Phenol.**

- **Acidity.** C₆H₅OH ⇌ C₆H₅O⁻ + H⁺. A lone pair on O overlaps with the ring's π system, so the negative charge on phenoxide is partly delocalised: phenol is a weak acid (stronger than alcohols, weaker than carboxylic acids). It reacts with NaOH (C₆H₅OH + NaOH → C₆H₅ONa + H₂O) and sodium (fizzing), but not with carbonates.
- **FeCl₃ test.** Neutral iron(III) chloride gives a purple colour.
- **Bromine water.** At room temperature, no catalyst: C₆H₅OH + 3Br₂ → 2,4,6-tribromophenol (white precipitate) + 3HBr. Bromine water decolorises.
- **Dilute nitric acid** at room temperature gives a mixture of 2-nitrophenol and 4-nitrophenol (OCR A).
- **Ethanoyl chloride** gives the ester phenyl ethanoate (Eduqas): C₆H₅OH + CH₃COCl → CH₃COOC₆H₅ + HCl. (Carboxylic acids react too slowly with phenol to make esters.)

**Why phenol reacts more easily than benzene.** A lone pair from the O is partly delocalised into the ring, increasing the electron density. The ring can now polarise Br₂ itself, so no halogen carrier is needed, and three H atoms are substituted.

**Directing effects (OCR A 6.1.1(k), (l)).** Electron-donating groups (–OH, –NH₂, –CH₃) activate the ring and direct new substituents to the 2- and 4-positions. Electron-withdrawing groups (–NO₂, –COOH, –CHO) deactivate the ring and direct to the 3-position. So nitrobenzene with Br₂/FeBr₃ gives 3-bromonitrobenzene; phenylamine with bromine water gives 2,4,6-tribromophenylamine.

#### Worked example

Suggest a two-step synthesis of phenylamine from benzene.

Step 1: concentrated HNO₃ and H₂SO₄ below 55 °C: nitrobenzene. Step 2: reduce with tin and concentrated HCl, then NaOH (file 26).

#### Common mistakes

- Drawing the intermediate with a full circle. It is a horseshoe (broken circle) with a + inside, not covering the carbon bonded to E and H.
- Starting the first arrow from a carbon or a bond in a Kekulé drawing. Start inside the delocalised ring.
- Forgetting to regenerate the catalyst.
- Saying phenol reacts with carbonates.

### Check questions

1. Describe the bonding in benzene. (3 marks)
2. Use enthalpy data to explain why benzene is more stable than the Kekulé structure suggests. Cyclohexene hydrogenation −120 kJ mol⁻¹; benzene hydrogenation −208 kJ mol⁻¹. (3 marks)
3. Explain why bromine reacts with cyclohexene without a catalyst but needs a catalyst to react with benzene. (3 marks)
4. Write an equation for the formation of the electrophile in nitration, and outline the mechanism of the nitration of benzene. (5 marks)
5. Give the reagents and conditions for making phenylethanone from benzene, and write equations for forming the electrophile and regenerating the catalyst. (4 marks)
6. Explain why phenol reacts with bromine water at room temperature but benzene does not. (3 marks)
7. Describe a test to distinguish phenol from cyclohexanol. (2 marks)
8. Predict the main product of the bromination of nitrobenzene with Br₂ and FeBr₃. (1 mark)

### Mark scheme

1. Each C forms three σ bonds (1); p orbitals overlap sideways (1); ring of delocalised π electrons above and below the plane (1).
2. Kekulé: 3 × −120 = −360 kJ mol⁻¹ expected (1); actual −208, less exothermic by 152 kJ mol⁻¹ (1); delocalisation makes benzene more stable (1).
3. C=C in cyclohexene has localised high electron density (1); can polarise Br₂ (1); in benzene electrons are delocalised / lower electron density, cannot polarise Br₂, so a halogen carrier is needed to make Br⁺ (1).
4. HNO₃ + 2H₂SO₄ → NO₂⁺ + H₃O⁺ + 2HSO₄⁻ (1); arrow from ring to NO₂⁺ (1); correct intermediate with horseshoe and + (1); arrow from C–H bond into ring (1); H⁺ lost, nitrobenzene formed (1).
5. Ethanoyl chloride, AlCl₃, anhydrous, reflux (1); CH₃COCl + AlCl₃ → CH₃CO⁺ + AlCl₄⁻ (1); H⁺ + AlCl₄⁻ → AlCl₃ + HCl (1); product phenylethanone (1).
6. Lone pair on O partly delocalised into ring (1); increases electron density (1); ring can polarise Br₂ (1).
7. Add neutral FeCl₃(aq) (1); phenol purple, cyclohexanol no colour (1). Accept bromine water: white precipitate with phenol.
8. 3-bromonitrobenzene (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/29-organic-synthesis.md -->

## Organic synthesis: routes, nitriles, C–C bond formation and practical techniques

Synthesis puts together the reactions from all the organic files to plan a route from a starting material to a target. This file gathers the reactions into maps, covers ways to lengthen a carbon chain (nitriles, hydroxynitriles, Grignard reagents, Friedel–Crafts), the reactions of nitriles, the mechanisms used across the course, and the practical techniques for making and purifying products.

### Overview

Mostly A-level. Every reaction here is explained in its own file (17 to 28); this file is for linking them.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.14: synthetic routes of up to four steps, aliphatic and aromatic; choosing reagents and conditions; the benefits of fewer steps and safer reagents.
- Edexcel 18.18 to 18.22: deducing formulae from data; planning schemes of up to four steps; increasing chain length with HCN and with Grignard reagents (18.20); choosing practical procedures; and techniques for solids (filtration under reduced pressure, recrystallisation, melting temperature).
- OCR A 4.2.3(b), (c) (AS: identifying functional groups and two-step routes) and 6.2.4, 6.2.5 (A-level: C–C bond formation with CN⁻ and HCN, reduction and hydrolysis of nitriles, multi-stage routes, preparing and purifying organic solids).
- OCR B WM(e), (f) (AS: techniques for making a solid and a liquid) and CD(j), (l) (A-level: using organic reactions in synthesis, and the full set of mechanisms).
- Eduqas OA2.3(h) to (j) (nitriles from halogenoalkanes and hydroxynitriles; hydrolysis of nitriles and amides; reduction of nitriles) and OA4(a), (b), (e). Specified practicals: synthesis of a liquid with a separating funnel; a solid with recrystallisation and melting temperature; a two-step synthesis.

### 3.3.14 Organic synthesis

*Refs: AQA 3.3.14 · Edexcel 18.18 to 18.22, CP16 · OCR A 4.2.3(b), (c), 6.2.4(a) to (c), 6.2.5(a) to (c) · OCR B WM(e), WM(f), CD(j), CD(l) · Eduqas OA2.3(h) to (j), OA4(a), (b), (e). Level: A-level only (OCR A 4.2.3, OCR B WM AS).*

#### What you need to know

- Reagents, conditions and equations for every conversion in the course.
- Plan multi-step routes, aliphatic and aromatic.
- Methods to increase chain length: KCN with halogenoalkanes, HCN with carbonyls, Grignard reagents (Edexcel), Friedel–Crafts (OCR A).
- Reactions of nitriles: hydrolysis to acids, reduction to amines.
- Practical techniques: reflux, distillation, separating funnel, drying, filtration under reduced pressure, recrystallisation, melting and boiling points.
- Chemists aim for fewer steps, high yield and atom economy, and safe, non-hazardous reagents and solvents.

#### Explained

**Aliphatic reaction map (key conversions).**

| From | To | Reagents and conditions | Type |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Alkane | Halogenoalkane | Cl₂ or Br₂, UV light | Radical substitution |
| Alkene | Alkane | H₂, Ni catalyst, 150 °C | Addition |
| Alkene | Halogenoalkane | HBr (room temperature) | Electrophilic addition |
| Alkene | Dihalogenoalkane | Br₂ | Electrophilic addition |
| Alkene | Alcohol | Steam, H₃PO₄, 300 °C, 60 atm (or conc. H₂SO₄ then water) | Electrophilic addition |
| Alkene | Diol | Cold acidified KMnO₄ | Oxidation |
| Halogenoalkane | Alcohol | NaOH(aq), warm, reflux | Nucleophilic substitution |
| Halogenoalkane | Nitrile | KCN in ethanol, reflux | Nucleophilic substitution |
| Halogenoalkane | Primary amine | Excess NH₃ in ethanol, heat in sealed tube | Nucleophilic substitution |
| Halogenoalkane | Alkene | KOH in ethanol, hot | Elimination |
| Alcohol | Alkene | Conc. H₃PO₄ or H₂SO₄, 170 °C | Elimination (dehydration) |
| Alcohol | Halogenoalkane | HBr (KBr + H₂SO₄), PCl₅, or conc. HCl (tertiary) | Substitution |
| Primary alcohol | Aldehyde | K₂Cr₂O₇/H₂SO₄, distil | Oxidation |
| Primary alcohol or aldehyde | Carboxylic acid | K₂Cr₂O₇/H₂SO₄, reflux | Oxidation |
| Secondary alcohol | Ketone | K₂Cr₂O₇/H₂SO₄, reflux | Oxidation |
| Aldehyde / ketone | Alcohol | NaBH₄ in water or methanol | Nucleophilic addition (reduction) |
| Aldehyde / ketone | Hydroxynitrile | KCN and dilute acid | Nucleophilic addition |
| Carboxylic acid | Ester | Alcohol, conc. H₂SO₄, warm | Esterification |
| Carboxylic acid | Acyl chloride | SOCl₂ or PCl₅ | Substitution |
| Carboxylic acid | Primary alcohol | LiAlH₄ in dry ether | Reduction |
| Acyl chloride | Ester / amide / acid | Alcohol / NH₃ or amine / water | Nucleophilic addition–elimination |
| Ester | Acid (or salt) + alcohol | Dilute acid, reflux (or NaOH(aq), reflux) | Hydrolysis |
| Nitrile | Primary amine | LiAlH₄ in dry ether, or H₂/Ni | Reduction |
| Nitrile | Carboxylic acid | Dilute HCl, reflux | Hydrolysis |
| Amide | Nitrile | P₄O₁₀, heat | Dehydration |

**Aromatic reaction map.**

| From | To | Reagents and conditions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Benzene | Nitrobenzene | Conc. HNO₃ + conc. H₂SO₄, below 55 °C |
| Nitrobenzene | Phenylamine | Sn + conc. HCl, reflux; then NaOH |
| Benzene | Bromobenzene | Br₂, FeBr₃ (or Fe) |
| Benzene | Phenyl ketone | RCOCl, AlCl₃, anhydrous, reflux |
| Benzene | Alkylbenzene | RCl, AlCl₃ |
| Methylbenzene | Benzoic acid | Alkaline KMnO₄, reflux; then acid |
| Phenylamine | Diazonium salt then azo dye | NaNO₂ + HCl below 10 °C; then phenol in NaOH (file 37) |
| Phenol | 2,4,6-tribromophenol | Bromine water |
| Phenylamine | N-phenylethanamide | Ethanoyl chloride |

**Nitriles.**

- Made from halogenoalkanes and KCN in ethanol (adds one carbon), or from carbonyls and HCN (hydroxynitriles).
- **Hydrolysis** by refluxing with dilute acid: CH₃CH₂CN + 2H₂O + H⁺ → CH₃CH₂COOH + NH₄⁺. With alkali, the carboxylate salt and NH₃ form.
- **Reduction** with LiAlH₄ or H₂/Ni: CH₃CH₂CN + 4[H] → CH₃CH₂CH₂NH₂.
- Hydroxynitriles give 2-hydroxy acids on hydrolysis: CH₃CH(OH)CN → CH₃CH(OH)COOH (lactic acid).

**Grignard reagents (Edexcel 18.20).** Made by reacting a halogenoalkane with magnesium in dry ether: CH₃CH₂Br + Mg → CH₃CH₂MgBr. The carbon bonded to Mg is δ−, so it acts as a nucleophile and forms a new C–C bond. Conditions must be dry (water destroys the reagent). After reaction, dilute acid is added.

- With CO₂ (dry ice): carboxylic acid with one more carbon. CH₃CH₂MgBr + CO₂ → CH₃CH₂COOMgBr; then acid → CH₃CH₂COOH.
- With methanal: primary alcohol (one more carbon).
- With other aldehydes: secondary alcohol.
- With ketones: tertiary alcohol.

**Mechanisms across the course (OCR B CD(l)).**

| Mechanism | Example | File |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Radical substitution | Alkane + Cl₂ | 18 |
| Electrophilic addition | Alkene + Br₂, HBr | 20 |
| Nucleophilic substitution (SN1, SN2) | Halogenoalkane + OH⁻ | 19 |
| Elimination | Halogenoalkane + ethanolic OH⁻; alcohol + acid | 19, 21 |
| Nucleophilic addition | Carbonyl + HCN, NaBH₄ | 23 |
| Nucleophilic addition–elimination | Acyl chloride + water, alcohol, amine | 24 |
| Electrophilic substitution | Benzene nitration, halogenation, Friedel–Crafts | 25 |

**Planning routes.** Work backwards from the target: which functional group is it, and what makes it? Count carbons: if the chain grows, a nitrile, hydroxynitrile, Grignard or Friedel–Crafts step is needed. Watch for groups that react with your reagents (protect or reorder).

**Choosing a route.** Fewer steps give higher overall yield (yields multiply: 80% × 80% × 80% = 51%). Prefer high atom economy, renewable starting materials, safe solvents and reagents, low temperatures, and catalysts (file 35).

**Deducing formulae (Edexcel 18.18).** Combine combustion data (empirical formula, file 2), Mᵣ from the mass spectrum, functional group tests and spectra.

#### Worked example

1. Plan a synthesis of propanoic acid from ethene.

   Ethene + HBr → bromoethane. Bromoethane + KCN (ethanol, reflux) → propanenitrile. Propanenitrile + dilute HCl, reflux → propanoic acid.

2. Plan a synthesis of 2-hydroxy-2-methylbutanoic acid from butan-2-ol.

   Oxidise butan-2-ol (acidified K₂Cr₂O₇, reflux) to butanone. Add HCN (KCN + acid) to give 2-hydroxy-2-methylbutanenitrile. Hydrolyse (dilute HCl, reflux) to the acid.

3. A route has three steps with yields 75%, 60% and 90%. What is the overall yield?

   0.75 × 0.60 × 0.90 = 0.405, so 40.5%.

#### Practical

**Making and purifying a liquid (OCR B WM(f), Eduqas):** reflux; distil; wash in a separating funnel (sodium hydrogencarbonate to remove acid, then water); dry with an anhydrous salt; redistil, collecting at the boiling point (file 21).

**Making and purifying a solid (OCR A 6.2.5(a), OCR B WM(e), Edexcel 18.22, Eduqas):** react; cool to crystallise; filter under reduced pressure with a Büchner funnel (faster and drier than gravity filtration); recrystallise from the minimum volume of hot solvent; dry; measure the melting point (file 24).

**Two-step synthesis (Eduqas):** for example, nitrobenzene to phenylamine to N-phenylethanamide, purifying and checking the melting point at the end.

**Choosing a solvent for recrystallisation:** the product must be very soluble in the hot solvent and much less soluble when cold; impurities should either be very soluble (stay in solution) or insoluble (removed by hot filtration).

#### Common mistakes

- Missing a step that adds a carbon.
- Giving reagents without conditions (for example "KOH" without saying aqueous or ethanolic).
- Using NaBH₄ to reduce a carboxylic acid (it cannot; use LiAlH₄).
- Adding yields instead of multiplying them.

### Check questions

1. Give the reagents and conditions for each step: 1-bromopropane → propan-1-ol → propanal → propanoic acid. (3 marks)
2. Plan a two-step synthesis of butylamine from 1-bromopropane. (3 marks)
3. Plan a synthesis of ethyl propanoate using ethene as the only organic starting material. (4 marks)
4. Write an equation for the acid hydrolysis of ethanenitrile. (2 marks)
5. Describe how a Grignard reagent can convert bromoethane into propanoic acid. (3 marks)
6. Plan a three-step synthesis of phenylamine's N-ethanoyl derivative (N-phenylethanamide) from benzene. (3 marks)
7. Explain why chemists prefer synthetic routes with fewer steps. (2 marks)
8. Explain why filtration under reduced pressure is used to collect crystals. (1 mark)

### Mark scheme

1. NaOH(aq), reflux (1); acidified K₂Cr₂O₇, distil (1); acidified K₂Cr₂O₇, reflux (1).
2. KCN in ethanol, reflux → butanenitrile (1)(1 for conditions); LiAlH₄ in dry ether / H₂ and Ni → butylamine (1).
3. Ethene + steam (H₃PO₄, 300 °C, 60 atm) → ethanol (1); ethene + HBr → bromoethane; + KCN in ethanol → propanenitrile (1); hydrolyse with dilute HCl → propanoic acid (1); propanoic acid + ethanol, conc. H₂SO₄ → ethyl propanoate (1).
4. CH₃CN + 2H₂O + HCl → CH₃COOH + NH₄Cl (2: products 1, balance 1).
5. React bromoethane with Mg in dry ether to give CH₃CH₂MgBr (1); add CO₂ (dry ice) (1); then dilute acid to give propanoic acid (1).
6. Conc. HNO₃ / conc. H₂SO₄ below 55 °C → nitrobenzene (1); Sn / conc. HCl then NaOH → phenylamine (1); ethanoyl chloride → N-phenylethanamide (1).
7. Overall yield higher (yields multiply) (1); less waste / energy / time / cost (1).
8. Faster and removes more solvent, giving drier crystals (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/22-organic-analysis-tests-ms-and-ir.md -->

## Organic analysis: test-tube tests, mass spectrometry and infrared

How do you find out what an unknown organic compound is? This file covers chemical tests for functional groups, mass spectrometry for relative molecular mass, molecular formula and fragments, and infrared spectroscopy for functional groups. It includes AQA required practical 6.

### Overview

AS content, with some A-level extensions. NMR, and combining all the evidence, is in file 30. Tests for aldehydes and ketones (Tollens', Fehling's, 2,4-DNPH, iodoform) are explained in file 23 and listed here.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.6.1 to 3.3.6.3 and RP6. AQA expects high-resolution mass spectrometry (precise Mᵣ) at AS.
- Edexcel 7.1, 7.2 (AS) and 19.1 (A-level: fragmentation, high resolution to find molecular formula).
- OCR A 4.2.4(a), (c) to (h) (AS) and 6.3.1(c) (A-level: the full scheme of organic tests). Absorption of IR by atmospheric gases (4.2.4(b)) is in file 35.
- OCR B WM(i), (j) (AS: mass spectra, IR) and PL(r) (A-level: further mass spectra interpretation).
- Eduqas C3.5(a), (b) and the specified practical: planning a sequence of tests to identify organic compounds.

### 3.3.6.1 Identification of functional groups by test-tube reactions

*Refs: AQA 3.3.6.1, RP6 · OCR A 6.3.1(c) · Eduqas OA4 (planning tests). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Tests for alkenes, alcohols (primary, secondary and tertiary), aldehydes, carboxylic acids and halogenoalkanes, with observations.

#### Explained

| Functional group | Test | Positive result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Alkene C=C | Shake with bromine water | Orange to colourless |
| Halogenoalkane | Warm with NaOH(aq) (or ethanol and water), acidify with HNO₃, add AgNO₃(aq) | White (Cl), cream (Br), yellow (I) precipitate |
| Primary or secondary alcohol | Warm with acidified K₂Cr₂O₇ | Orange to green |
| Tertiary alcohol | Same test | Stays orange |
| Alcohol (any) | PCl₅ (dry) | Steamy fumes of HCl |
| Aldehyde | Warm with Tollens' reagent (water bath) | Silver mirror |
| Aldehyde | Warm with Fehling's or Benedict's solution | Blue solution to red precipitate |
| Aldehyde or ketone | 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine | Orange / yellow precipitate (file 23) |
| Methyl ketone or ethanal; ethanol and secondary alcohols with CH₃CH(OH)– | Iodine and NaOH, warm | Pale yellow precipitate of CHI₃ (file 23) |
| Carboxylic acid | Add Na₂CO₃ or NaHCO₃ solution | Effervescence; gas turns limewater cloudy |
| Phenol | Neutral FeCl₃(aq) | Purple colour (file 25) |

**Distinguishing primary from secondary alcohols.** Both turn dichromate green. Distil off the product as it forms and test it: an aldehyde (from a primary alcohol) gives a silver mirror with Tollens' or red precipitate with Fehling's; a ketone (from a secondary alcohol) gives neither.

**Tollens' reagent.** Add NaOH(aq) to AgNO₃(aq) to give a brown precipitate, then add dilute ammonia dropwise until it just dissolves, giving [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺. Make fresh and warm in a water bath (it can form explosive compounds if stored).

#### Practical

**AQA RP6: tests for alcohol, aldehyde, alkene and carboxylic acid.** Use a few drops of each unknown, warm in a water bath (no naked flames; organic compounds are flammable), and record observations. Always compare with a control.

**Eduqas: planning a sequence of tests.** Order the tests so each result narrows the options, for example sodium carbonate (acid) first, then bromine water (alkene), then 2,4-DNPH (carbonyl), then Tollens' (aldehyde versus ketone), then dichromate (alcohol type).

#### Common mistakes

- Heating Tollens' over a Bunsen flame.
- Saying ketones give a positive Tollens' test.
- Forgetting that the dichromate test does not distinguish primary from secondary alcohols by itself.

### 3.3.6.2 Mass spectrometry

*Refs: AQA 3.3.6.2 · Edexcel 7.1, 19.1 · OCR A 4.2.4(f) to (h) · OCR B WM(i), PL(r) · Eduqas C3.5(a). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The molecular ion peak (M⁺) gives Mᵣ.
- High-resolution mass spectrometry gives Mᵣ to several decimal places, distinguishing compounds with the same nominal mass.
- Fragmentation peaks show the structure (OCR A, Edexcel, OCR B).
- The M+1 peak from ¹³C (not required by all boards).

#### Explained

**The molecular ion.** In electron impact, a high-energy electron removes an electron from the molecule: M + e⁻ → M⁺• + 2e⁻. The M⁺ peak is the one with the highest m/z (ignoring the small M+1 peak). Its m/z is Mᵣ.

**High resolution (AQA, Edexcel).** Precise atomic masses: ¹H 1.0078, ¹²C 12.0000, ¹⁴N 14.0031, ¹⁶O 15.9949. Molecules with the same nominal Mᵣ have different precise values. For Mᵣ = 44:

| Formula | Precise mass |
| --- | --- |
| CO₂ | 43.9898 |
| C₃H₈ | 44.0624 |
| C₂H₄O | 44.0261 |
| N₂O | 44.0011 |

A peak at 44.0262 shows C₂H₄O.

**Fragmentation.** The molecular ion can break into a positive fragment (detected) and a radical (not detected, being uncharged): M⁺ → X⁺ + Y•. Common fragments:

| m/z | Fragment |
| --- | --- |
| 15 | CH₃⁺ |
| 29 | C₂H₅⁺ or CHO⁺ |
| 31 | CH₂OH⁺ |
| 43 | C₃H₇⁺ or CH₃CO⁺ |
| 45 | COOH⁺ |
| 77 | C₆H₅⁺ |

Losses from M: M − 15 (lost CH₃), M − 17 (lost OH), M − 18 (lost H₂O, from alcohols), M − 29 (lost C₂H₅ or CHO), M − 45 (lost COOH).

Propanone (Mᵣ 58) has a big peak at 43 (CH₃CO⁺, lost CH₃). Propanal (also 58) has peaks at 29 (C₂H₅⁺ and CHO⁺) and 57. More stable carbocations (tertiary) and acylium ions (RCO⁺) give taller peaks.

**M+1 peak.** About 1.1% of carbon is ¹³C, so there is a small peak at M+1. Its height relative to M⁺ ≈ 1.1 × number of carbons (%).

**Halogens.** A compound with one Cl has M and M+2 peaks in ratio 3 : 1; one Br, M and M+2 in ratio 1 : 1.

#### Worked example

A compound of C, H and O has a molecular ion at m/z 74 and major peaks at 59, 45 and 29. Suggest its identity.

74 − 59 = 15 (lost CH₃); 45 (COOH⁺); 29 (C₂H₅⁺). Propanoic acid, CH₃CH₂COOH, fits: Mᵣ 74, C₂H₅⁺ and COOH⁺ fragments.

#### Common mistakes

- Taking the tallest peak (base peak) as M⁺.
- Writing fragments without a positive charge.
- Writing radical fragments as detected.

### 3.3.6.3 Infrared spectroscopy

*Refs: AQA 3.3.6.3 · Edexcel 7.2 · OCR A 4.2.4(a), (c) to (e), (h) · OCR B WM(j) · Eduqas C3.5(b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Bonds absorb infrared radiation at characteristic wavenumbers, making them vibrate (stretch and bend) more.
- Use an IR spectrum to identify functional groups, and the fingerprint region to identify a compound by comparison.
- IR is used to monitor gases such as CO and NO in exhausts, and alcohol in breath (OCR A).
- Links to the greenhouse effect (file 35).

#### Explained

An IR spectrum plots transmittance (%) against wavenumber (cm⁻¹; 4000 on the left to 500 on the right). Absorptions show as dips (troughs). Wavenumber is 1/wavelength.

| Bond | Wavenumber / cm⁻¹ | Appearance |
| --- | --- | --- |
| O–H (alcohol) | 3230 to 3550 | Broad |
| O–H (carboxylic acid) | 2500 to 3000 | Very broad, overlapping C–H |
| N–H (amine, amide) | 3300 to 3500 | Medium |
| C–H | 2850 to 3300 | Sharp |
| C≡N | 2220 to 2260 | Medium, sharp |
| C=O | 1680 to 1750 | Strong, sharp |
| C=C | 1620 to 1680 | Medium |
| C–O | 1000 to 1300 | Strong |

Use the values given on the data sheet in an exam.

- **Alcohol:** broad O–H at about 3300 and C–O.
- **Aldehyde or ketone:** C=O at about 1700, no O–H.
- **Carboxylic acid:** very broad O–H at 2500 to 3000 plus C=O.
- **Ester:** C=O and C–O, no O–H.

**Fingerprint region** (below about 1500 cm⁻¹): a complex pattern unique to each molecule. Comparing it with a database identifies the compound exactly; extra peaks show impurities.

**Monitoring gases (OCR A 4.2.4(e)).** Roadside sensors measure CO and NO in car exhausts by their IR absorptions. Police breathalysers measure the C–H (or C–O) absorption of ethanol; the intensity shows how much ethanol is in the breath.

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing the very broad acid O–H with the alcohol O–H.
- Trying to read structure from the fingerprint region. Use it only to compare with a known spectrum.
- Saying a peak at about 3000 alone proves an O–H: all organic compounds have C–H there.

### Check questions

1. Describe a test to show that a compound is an aldehyde and not a ketone. (2 marks)
2. Describe how to show an unknown liquid is a carboxylic acid. (2 marks)
3. A compound reacts with acidified potassium dichromate but its product does not react with Tollens' reagent. What type of compound is it? (1 mark)
4. Using precise masses (H 1.0078, C 12.0000, O 15.9949), show which of C₃H₆O and C₄H₁₀ has a high-resolution Mᵣ of 58.0419. (2 marks)
5. The mass spectrum of a ketone C₅H₁₀O has major peaks at 86, 57 and 29. Suggest its structure and explain. (3 marks)
6. Explain how an infrared spectrum could distinguish between propan-1-ol, propanal and propanoic acid. (3 marks)
7. Explain what the fingerprint region is used for. (2 marks)
8. A compound has a mass spectrum with peaks at m/z 122 and 124 of equal height. Explain what this shows. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Warm with Tollens' reagent (1); silver mirror with aldehyde, no change with ketone (1). Accept Fehling's: red precipitate vs stays blue.
2. Add sodium carbonate / hydrogencarbonate (1); effervescence, gas turns limewater cloudy (1).
3. Secondary alcohol (1).
4. C₃H₆O = 36.0000 + 6.0468 + 15.9949 = 58.0417 (1); C₄H₁₀ = 58.0780, so C₃H₆O (1).
5. Pentan-3-one, CH₃CH₂COCH₂CH₃ (1); 86 molecular ion (1); 57 = CH₃CH₂CO⁺ and 29 = C₂H₅⁺ (1).
6. Propan-1-ol: broad O–H at 3230 to 3550, no C=O (1). Propanal: C=O at about 1720, no O–H (1). Propanoic acid: very broad O–H at 2500 to 3000 and C=O (1).
7. Unique pattern for each compound (1); compared with database / known spectrum to identify / check purity (1).
8. Contains one bromine atom (1); ⁷⁹Br and ⁸¹Br isotopes in about 1 : 1 ratio (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/09-rate-equations.md -->

## Rate equations, orders, half-life and the Arrhenius equation

Rate equations turn "it goes faster with more acid" into numbers. This file covers rate equations and orders, the rate constant and its units, finding orders from initial rates and from graphs, half-life, the rate-determining step and mechanisms, and the Arrhenius equation. It includes AQA required practical 7.

### Overview

A-level only. It builds on collision theory in file 5. SN1 and SN2 mechanisms for halogenoalkanes (file 19) are the classic example of linking rate equations to mechanisms.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.9.1, 3.1.9.2 and RP7. AQA gives the Arrhenius equation in the data booklet and expects its use, including ln k against 1/T.
- Edexcel 16.1 to 16.12 and CP13a (continuous monitoring), CP13b (initial rate, clock reaction), CP14 (activation energy). Edexcel uses the hydrolysis of halogenoalkanes (16.11) and the iodination of propanone to link rate equations to mechanisms.
- OCR A 5.1.1(a) to (k), with half-life from concentration–time graphs (5.1.1(e), (f)) and rate constant from rate–concentration graphs (5.1.1(g)).
- OCR B CI(a) to (e) in the Chemical industry storyline: half-lives, techniques, the Arrhenius equation and the rate-determining step.
- Eduqas PI3(a) to (f) and C2.3(g) (colorimetry, AS), with the specified practical: determining the order of a reaction.

### 3.1.9.1 Rate equations

*Refs: AQA 3.1.9.1 · Edexcel 16.1, 16.2, 16.9 to 16.12 · OCR A 5.1.1(a) to (c), (i) to (k) · OCR B CI(a), CI(d), CI(e) · Eduqas PI3(c) to (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Rate = k[A]ᵐ[B]ⁿ. m and n are the orders with respect to A and B, usually 0, 1 or 2. The overall order is m + n.
- Orders can only be found by experiment, not from the balanced equation.
- k is the rate constant. Work out its units.
- k increases with temperature.
- The rate-determining step is the slowest step; the rate equation shows which species are in it (or in steps before it).
- The Arrhenius equation, k = Ae^(−Eₐ/RT), and ln k = ln A − Eₐ/RT.

#### Explained

**Orders.**

| Order in A | Effect of doubling [A] on rate |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | No change |
| 1 | Doubles |
| 2 | Quadruples |

A species with order zero does not appear in the rate equation, but it still reacts. It takes part after the rate-determining step.

**Units of k.** Rearrange: k = rate ÷ (concentration terms). Rate is in mol dm⁻³ s⁻¹.

| Overall order | Units of k |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | mol dm⁻³ s⁻¹ |
| 1 | s⁻¹ |
| 2 | dm³ mol⁻¹ s⁻¹ |
| 3 | dm⁶ mol⁻² s⁻¹ |

**Rate-determining step.** The overall rate cannot be faster than the slowest step. The species in the rate equation are those taking part in the rate-determining step (and any steps before it).

For the reaction 2NO₂ + F₂ → 2NO₂F, rate = k[NO₂][F₂]. A possible mechanism: NO₂ + F₂ → NO₂F + F (slow), then NO₂ + F → NO₂F (fast). The slow step contains one NO₂ and one F₂, matching the rate equation.

For the hydrolysis of 2-bromo-2-methylpropane, rate = k[(CH₃)₃CBr]. Only the halogenoalkane is in the slow step: it ionises to a carbocation first (SN1), and OH⁻ attacks afterwards. For 1-bromobutane, rate = k[C₄H₉Br][OH⁻], so both are in the single step (SN2). See file 19.

For the acid-catalysed iodination of propanone, CH₃COCH₃ + I₂ → CH₃COCH₂I + HI, rate = k[CH₃COCH₃][H⁺]. Iodine is zero order, so it is not in the slow step; H⁺ appears although it is a catalyst.

**Effect of temperature.** Raising the temperature increases k (the orders do not change). The Arrhenius equation:

k = Ae^(−Eₐ/RT)

- A is the pre-exponential (frequency) factor: related to collision frequency and orientation.
- e^(−Eₐ/RT) is the fraction of collisions with at least Eₐ.
- Taking logs: ln k = ln A − (Eₐ/R)(1/T). A graph of ln k (y) against 1/T (x) is a straight line with gradient −Eₐ/R and intercept ln A.

Since rate ∝ k, and for a clock reaction rate ∝ 1/t, ln(1/t) against 1/T also has gradient −Eₐ/R.

#### Worked example

1. Rate = k[A]²[B]. When [A] = 0.20 mol dm⁻³ and [B] = 0.50 mol dm⁻³, rate = 4.0 × 10⁻³ mol dm⁻³ s⁻¹. Find k with units.

   k = 4.0 × 10⁻³ ÷ (0.20² × 0.50) = 4.0 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.020 = 0.20 dm⁶ mol⁻² s⁻¹.

2. A plot of ln k against 1/T has gradient −6800 K. Find Eₐ.

   Eₐ = −gradient × R = 6800 × 8.31 = 56 500 J mol⁻¹ = 56.5 kJ mol⁻¹.

3. For a reaction with Eₐ = 50.0 kJ mol⁻¹, by what factor does k increase from 300 K to 310 K (assume A is constant)?

   ln(k₂/k₁) = (Eₐ/R)(1/T₁ − 1/T₂) = (50 000 ÷ 8.31)(1/300 − 1/310) = 6017 × 1.075 × 10⁻⁴ = 0.647. k₂/k₁ = e⁰·⁶⁴⁷ = 1.91. The rate nearly doubles.

#### Common mistakes

- Taking orders from the coefficients in the equation.
- Forgetting to convert Eₐ from J to kJ, or using T in °C.
- Saying a catalyst cannot be in the rate equation. It can (H⁺ above).
- Saying zero-order reactants do not react.

### 3.1.9.2 Determination of rate equation

*Refs: AQA 3.1.9.2, RP7 · Edexcel 16.3 to 16.8, 16.12, CP13a, CP13b, CP14 · OCR A 5.1.1(b), (d) to (h), (k) · OCR B CI(b), CI(c) · Eduqas PI3(a), (b), C2.3(g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Find orders from initial rate data in a table.
- Find orders from concentration–time graphs (shape and half-lives) and from rate–concentration graphs.
- Half-life of a first-order reaction is constant; t½ = ln 2 ÷ k.
- Methods: initial rates (including clock reactions) and continuous monitoring (gas volume, mass loss, colorimetry, titration of samples, pH, conductivity).
- Find Eₐ graphically from experiments at different temperatures.

#### Explained

**Initial rates method.** Run several experiments, changing one concentration at a time. Compare pairs where only one concentration changes.

**Graphs.**

| Graph | Zero order | First order | Second order |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Concentration against time | Straight line, falling | Curve with constant half-life | Steeper curve; half-life increases as concentration falls |
| Rate against concentration | Horizontal line | Straight line through origin (gradient = k) | Upward curve (parabola) |

**Half-life** is the time taken for the concentration of a reactant to halve. For first order it is the same whatever the starting concentration: k = ln 2 ÷ t½ = 0.693 ÷ t½. Radioactive decay is first order.

**Continuous monitoring.** One experiment, measuring concentration over time. Draw tangents to find rates at different concentrations, or check successive half-lives. Methods:

- Gas volume with a gas syringe, or mass loss on a balance (for reactions giving off a gas).
- **Colorimetry** (Eduqas C2.3(g)): if one species is coloured (for example iodine, or manganate(VII)), shine light of a suitable colour (the complementary colour) through the solution and measure absorbance. Absorbance is proportional to concentration, so make a calibration curve with known concentrations first.
- Withdraw samples at intervals, quench them (dilute in cold water or neutralise the catalyst) and titrate.
- pH meter or conductivity meter when the number of ions changes.

**Clock reactions (initial rates).** Measure the time, t, to reach a fixed point early in the reaction (a colour appears). Initial rate ∝ 1/t, assuming the rate is roughly constant over that short time.

#### Worked example

1. Find the rate equation from the data.

   | Expt | [A] / mol dm⁻³ | [B] / mol dm⁻³ | Initial rate / mol dm⁻³ s⁻¹ |
   | --- | --- | --- | --- |
   | 1 | 0.10 | 0.10 | 2.0 × 10⁻⁴ |
   | 2 | 0.20 | 0.10 | 8.0 × 10⁻⁴ |
   | 3 | 0.10 | 0.30 | 6.0 × 10⁻⁴ |

   Expt 1 → 2: [A] doubles, rate × 4, so second order in A. Expt 1 → 3: [B] × 3, rate × 3, so first order in B. Rate = k[A]²[B]. k = 2.0 × 10⁻⁴ ÷ (0.01 × 0.10) = 0.20 dm⁶ mol⁻² s⁻¹.

2. A first-order reaction has a half-life of 46 s. Find k, and the fraction of reactant left after 138 s.

   k = 0.693 ÷ 46 = 0.0151 s⁻¹. 138 s = 3 half-lives, so ⅛ is left.

#### Practical

**AQA RP7: measuring the rate of reaction by an initial rate method and by a continuous monitoring method.**

*Initial rate (iodine clock).* Mix potassium iodide, sodium thiosulfate and starch in a flask. Add hydrogen peroxide (or persulfate) and start the timer. Stop when the blue-black colour appears. Repeat with different iodide concentrations, keeping total volume constant by adding water. Plot 1/t against [I⁻]: a straight line through the origin shows first order.

*Continuous monitoring.* For example, iodine with propanone in acid: the brown iodine colour fades. Take samples every minute and quench with sodium hydrogencarbonate (to remove the acid catalyst), then titrate the iodine left with thiosulfate. Or follow the colour with a colorimeter. A straight-line concentration–time graph shows iodine is zero order.

**Edexcel CP13a, CP13b, CP14.** CP13a: continuous monitoring (for example propanone and iodine). CP13b: initial rate by a clock method. CP14: run a clock reaction at five or more temperatures (water bath), plot ln(1/t) against 1/T, and find Eₐ = −gradient × R.

**Eduqas: determining the order of a reaction** such as the oxidation of iodide ions by peroxodisulfate, using a clock method.

#### Common mistakes

- Changing two concentrations at once and claiming an order.
- Measuring half-life from the start only once and assuming first order. Check two or more successive half-lives.
- Reading the rate as the concentration rather than the gradient of the curve.

### Check questions

1. The rate equation for a reaction is rate = k[X][Y]². State the overall order and give the units of k. (2 marks)
2. Use the data to deduce the order with respect to P and Q, and write the rate equation. (4 marks)

   | Expt | [P] | [Q] | Rate / mol dm⁻³ s⁻¹ |
   | --- | --- | --- | --- |
   | 1 | 0.050 | 0.050 | 1.5 × 10⁻⁵ |
   | 2 | 0.100 | 0.050 | 3.0 × 10⁻⁵ |
   | 3 | 0.100 | 0.150 | 3.0 × 10⁻⁵ |

3. Calculate k from experiment 1 above, with units. (2 marks)
4. Sketch a rate–concentration graph for a reactant that is first order. (1 mark)
5. Explain how a concentration–time graph can show that a reaction is first order. (2 marks)
6. The reaction H₂O₂ + 2I⁻ + 2H⁺ → I₂ + 2H₂O has rate = k[H₂O₂][I⁻]. Suggest the species in the rate-determining step and why H⁺ does not appear. (2 marks)
7. In a set of experiments, ln k was plotted against 1/T. The line had a gradient of −9.40 × 10³ K. Calculate Eₐ in kJ mol⁻¹. (2 marks)
8. Describe how a colorimeter could be used to follow the reaction of a coloured reactant. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Third order (1); dm⁶ mol⁻² s⁻¹ (1).
2. P: [P] doubles, rate doubles, first order (1)(1 for reasoning). Q: [Q] trebles, rate unchanged, zero order (1). Rate = k[P] (1).
3. k = 1.5 × 10⁻⁵ ÷ 0.050 = 3.0 × 10⁻⁴ (1); s⁻¹ (1).
4. Straight line through the origin (1).
5. Measure successive half-lives (1); constant half-life shows first order (1).
6. One H₂O₂ and one I⁻ (1); H⁺ reacts in a step after the rate-determining step / fast step (1).
7. Eₐ = 9.40 × 10³ × 8.31 (1) = 78.1 kJ mol⁻¹ (1).
8. Choose a filter of the complementary colour / wavelength absorbed by the reactant (1); make a calibration curve of absorbance against known concentrations (1); measure absorbance at time intervals and convert to concentration (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/28-amino-acids-proteins-and-dna.md -->

## Amino acids, proteins, enzymes, DNA and anticancer drugs

Living things are built from condensation polymers. This file covers amino acids and zwitterions, peptides and protein structure, enzymes and inhibitors, molecular recognition in drug action, the structure of DNA, and how cisplatin stops cancer cells dividing.

### Overview

A-level only. Optical isomerism (amino acids other than glycine are chiral) is in file 23. Condensation polymerisation is in file 27. Chromatography of amino acids is in file 31. Cisplatin as a complex is in file 15.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.13.1 to 3.3.13.5.
- Edexcel 18.16, 18.17: amino acids (acidity and basicity, zwitterions, ninhydrin, optical activity) and the peptide bond. Edexcel does not require DNA or enzymes.
- OCR A 6.2.2(a): α-amino acids, reactions of the acid and amine groups. OCR A does not require proteins, enzymes or DNA in this detail.
- OCR B PL(a) to (g), (i): amino acids and proteins, DNA and RNA, base pairing and how DNA codes for proteins, molecular recognition (pharmacophores), the rate–substrate curve for enzymes, and enzyme characteristics.
- Eduqas OA3.2(a) to (f): α-amino acids, zwitterions, dipeptides, proteins and their structure, and the roles of proteins (enzymes).

### 3.3.13.1 Amino acids

*Refs: AQA 3.3.13.1 · Edexcel 18.16 · OCR A 6.2.2(a) · OCR B PL(a), PL(i) · Eduqas OA3.2(a), (b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The general structure of α-amino acids: RCH(NH₂)COOH.
- Amino acids are both acidic and basic (amphoteric) and form zwitterions.
- Structures at different pH values.
- Most are chiral (glycine, R = H, is not).
- Reactions: the –COOH with bases and alcohols, the –NH₂ with acids (OCR A).

#### Explained

In an α-amino acid, the NH₂ and COOH are on the same carbon. Glycine: H₂NCH₂COOH. Alanine: H₂NCH(CH₃)COOH. Systematic name of alanine: 2-aminopropanoic acid.

**Zwitterions.** The –COOH donates H⁺ to the –NH₂ in the same molecule: ⁺H₃N–CHR–COO⁻. This happens at the isoelectric point, the pH at which the amino acid has no overall charge. Strong ionic attractions between zwitterions give amino acids high melting points and make them soluble in water.

| Condition | Form |
| --- | --- |
| Acidic (low pH) | ⁺H₃N–CHR–COOH (the COO⁻ is protonated) |
| Isoelectric point | ⁺H₃N–CHR–COO⁻ |
| Alkaline (high pH) | H₂N–CHR–COO⁻ (the NH₃⁺ loses H⁺) |

**Reactions (OCR A 6.2.2(a)).**

- With NaOH: H₂NCH(R)COOH + NaOH → H₂NCH(R)COONa + H₂O.
- With an alcohol (acid catalyst): forms an ester, H₂NCH(R)COOCH₃ (the amine is protonated in the acid).
- With HCl: HOOCCH(R)NH₃⁺Cl⁻.

**Optical activity (Edexcel).** Naturally occurring amino acids are single enantiomers (L-amino acids).

#### Common mistakes

- Drawing the zwitterion with NH₂ and COO⁻ (no positive charge).
- Getting the low-pH and high-pH forms backwards.

### 3.3.13.2 Proteins

*Refs: AQA 3.3.13.2 · Edexcel 18.17 · OCR B PL(a), PL(b) · Eduqas OA3.2(c) to (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Proteins are polypeptides formed by condensation of amino acids; the peptide (amide) link.
- Draw dipeptides and tripeptides.
- Hydrolysis of proteins by hot concentrated hydrochloric acid gives amino acids (protonated).
- Primary, secondary and tertiary structure and the bonds involved (hydrogen bonds, ionic bonds, disulfide bridges).

#### Explained

**Peptide link.** The –COOH of one amino acid reacts with the –NH₂ of another, losing water: –CO–NH–. Two different amino acids give two possible dipeptides (Gly–Ala and Ala–Gly), because either can provide the –COOH.

**Hydrolysis.** Boil with 6 mol dm⁻³ HCl for several hours. The peptide bonds break, giving a mixture of amino acids as their ammonium salts. The amino acids can then be separated and identified by chromatography (file 31).

**Structure.**

| Level | What it is | Bonds that hold it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Primary | Sequence of amino acids | Peptide bonds (covalent) |
| Secondary | α-helix or β-pleated sheet | Hydrogen bonds between C=O and N–H of the backbone |
| Tertiary | Overall 3D folding | Interactions between R groups: hydrogen bonds, ionic bonds (–COO⁻ and –NH₃⁺), disulfide bridges (–S–S– between cysteines), and London forces |

Heat or extremes of pH break the weaker interactions and change the shape (denaturation).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying secondary structure involves R groups.
- Forgetting the second dipeptide when two different amino acids join.

### 3.3.13.3 Enzymes

*Refs: AQA 3.3.13.3 · OCR B PL(e) to (g) · Eduqas OA3.2(f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Enzymes are proteins that catalyse reactions. The active site is stereospecific.
- Inhibitors with a similar shape block the active site; drugs can act as enzyme inhibitors; computer modelling helps design them.
- OCR B: the rate–substrate concentration curve; specificity, temperature, pH and competitive inhibition; molecular recognition and pharmacophores.

#### Explained

The active site has a specific 3D shape formed by the tertiary structure. A substrate with a complementary shape binds by intermolecular forces (hydrogen bonds, ionic bonds, London forces) to form an enzyme–substrate complex, the reaction happens with lower activation energy, and the products leave.

**Stereospecificity.** Because the active site is chiral, an enzyme usually acts on only one enantiomer.

**Rate and substrate concentration (OCR B PL(f)).** At low substrate concentration, rate is proportional to concentration (first order in substrate): more substrate, more collisions with free active sites. At high concentration, all active sites are occupied, so rate stays constant (zero order in substrate).

**Temperature and pH (OCR B PL(g)).** Rate rises with temperature up to an optimum; above it, the tertiary structure is disrupted (denatured) and the active site changes shape. Changes in pH alter the charges on R groups (–COO⁻, –NH₃⁺), breaking ionic and hydrogen bonds.

**Competitive inhibition.** An inhibitor with a similar shape to the substrate binds to the active site, blocking it. Many drugs work like this. Computer modelling of the active site lets chemists design molecules that fit it.

**Molecular recognition and pharmacophores (OCR B PL(e)).** The pharmacophore is the part of a drug molecule that binds to the receptor and causes the effect. Other groups modify how well it dissolves, how well it is absorbed, or how strongly it binds. Binding depends on size, shape, the types of bond formed (hydrogen bonds, ionic attractions, London forces) and orientation in three dimensions.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying enzymes are "killed" by heat. They are denatured.
- Saying an inhibitor reacts with the substrate.

### 3.3.13.4 DNA

*Refs: AQA 3.3.13.4 · OCR B PL(c), PL(d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- DNA is a condensation polymer of nucleotides. Each nucleotide is a phosphate, the sugar 2-deoxyribose and a base: adenine, cytosine, guanine or thymine (RNA uses ribose and uracil).
- The sugar–phosphate backbone; two strands form a double helix held by hydrogen bonds between complementary bases: A with T (two hydrogen bonds), C with G (three).
- OCR B: base pairing and replication; how DNA codes for RNA, which codes for the amino acid sequence.

#### Explained

**Nucleotide.** The phosphate joins C5 of the sugar; the base joins C1. Nucleotides join by condensation between the phosphate of one and the C3 –OH of the next sugar, forming the sugar–phosphate backbone (phosphodiester links).

**Base pairing.** The two strands run in opposite directions. Bases point inwards and pair by hydrogen bonds: A–T (two) and C–G (three). The shapes and the positions of the N–H and C=O groups mean only these pairs fit.

**Replication (OCR B).** The hydrogen bonds are weak enough to break when the strands separate. Each strand acts as a template: free nucleotides pair with complementary bases, so two identical copies form.

**From DNA to protein (OCR B).** A sequence of three bases (a triplet, or codon) codes for one amino acid. DNA is copied into messenger RNA, and the RNA sequence is read to assemble the amino acids in order.

#### Common mistakes

- Pairing A with G.
- Saying the strands are held by covalent bonds.

### 3.3.13.5 Action of anticancer drugs

*Refs: AQA 3.3.13.5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Cisplatin binds to DNA and prevents replication.
- The balance between benefits and side effects.

#### Explained

Cisplatin, cis-[Pt(NH₃)₂Cl₂], enters the cell. A Cl⁻ ligand is replaced by water, and then the platinum forms a coordinate bond to a nitrogen atom on a guanine base. The second ligand is replaced by a bond to a nitrogen on a neighbouring guanine. This kinks the DNA so it cannot be copied, and the cell cannot divide (the cell then dies).

Side effects: cisplatin also affects healthy cells that divide rapidly (hair, blood, gut lining), causing hair loss, nausea and kidney damage. Doctors balance the benefit of killing cancer cells against these effects, using low doses and other drugs alongside.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying cisplatin binds through its chloride ligands. The chlorides are replaced; Pt bonds to guanine N atoms.

### Check questions

1. Draw the zwitterion of serine, H₂NCH(CH₂OH)COOH. (1 mark)
2. Draw the structure of alanine (2-aminopropanoic acid) in solution at pH 1 and at pH 12. (2 marks)
3. Explain why amino acids have high melting points. (2 marks)
4. Draw the two dipeptides formed from glycine and alanine. (2 marks)
5. Describe how a protein can be hydrolysed and the products identified. (3 marks)
6. Name the types of interaction responsible for the tertiary structure of a protein. (3 marks)
7. Explain the shape of a graph of rate against substrate concentration for an enzyme-catalysed reaction. (3 marks)
8. Explain how a competitive inhibitor reduces the rate of an enzyme-catalysed reaction. (2 marks)
9. Describe how the two strands of DNA are held together. (3 marks)
10. Explain how cisplatin prevents cancer cells dividing, and why it causes side effects. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. HOCH₂–CH(NH₃⁺)–COO⁻ (1).
2. pH 1: CH₃CH(NH₃⁺)COOH (1); pH 12: CH₃CH(NH₂)COO⁻ (1).
3. Exist as zwitterions (1); strong ionic attractions between them (1).
4. H₂NCH₂CONHCH(CH₃)COOH (1); H₂NCH(CH₃)CONHCH₂COOH (1).
5. Reflux with 6 mol dm⁻³ HCl (1); peptide bonds break to give amino acids (1); separate by TLC / paper chromatography, spray with ninhydrin and compare Rf values (1).
6. Hydrogen bonds (1); ionic bonds (1); disulfide bridges (1). Accept London forces for one mark if one is missing.
7. At low concentration rate proportional to substrate concentration / first order (1); active sites available (1); at high concentration all active sites occupied, rate constant / zero order (1).
8. Similar shape to substrate, binds to the active site (1); fewer active sites available to substrate / fewer enzyme–substrate complexes (1).
9. Hydrogen bonds (1); between complementary base pairs (1); A–T and C–G (1).
10. Ligand replaced; Pt bonds to N on two neighbouring guanine bases (1); DNA cannot replicate / unwind (1); so cell cannot divide (1); also affects healthy fast-dividing cells, e.g. hair loss / nausea (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/24-carboxylic-acids-and-derivatives.md -->

## Carboxylic acids, esters, acyl chlorides, anhydrides and amides

Carboxylic acids and their derivatives all contain the C=O group with something else on the same carbon. This file covers the acidity and reactions of carboxylic acids, making and hydrolysing esters, fats, soaps and biodiesel, acyl chlorides and acid anhydrides with the nucleophilic addition–elimination mechanism, making aspirin, and amides. It includes AQA required practical 10.

### Overview

A-level for most boards. Condensation polymers (polyesters, polyamides) are in file 27; nitriles are in file 29; amines are in file 26.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.9.1, 3.3.9.2 and RP10 (preparing a pure organic solid, such as aspirin, and a pure organic liquid, such as an ester).
- Edexcel 17.9 to 17.15 and CP16 (preparation of aspirin): adds hydrogen bonding and physical properties of acids (17.10), reduction of acids with LiAlH₄ and reaction with PCl₅ (17.12).
- OCR A 6.1.3(a) to (f) and 6.2.2(b): water solubility, reactions of acids, esterification with acids and with anhydrides, ester hydrolysis, acyl chlorides from SOCl₂ and their reactions; structures of primary and secondary amides.
- OCR B PL(h), (k) to (n): acidity of carboxylic acids, nomenclature and functional groups, hydrolysis of esters and amides, acyl chlorides with amines and alcohols.
- Eduqas C3.4(g), (h) (AS) and OA2.3(a) to (g) (A-level): relative acidity of acids, phenols and alcohols; LiAlH₄ reduction; aromatic acids by oxidising methylbenzene; decarboxylation; esters, acid chlorides, amides and nitriles from acids.

### 3.3.9.1 Carboxylic acids and esters

*Refs: AQA 3.3.9.1 · Edexcel 17.9 to 17.12, 17.15 · OCR A 6.1.3(a) to (d) · OCR B PL(h), PL(k) to (m) · Eduqas C3.4(g), (h), OA2.3(a) to (f). Level: A-level only (Eduqas C3.4 AS).*

#### What you need to know

- Carboxylic acids are weak acids; they react with carbonates to release CO₂, and with metals, bases and alkalis.
- Esterification with an alcohol and acid catalyst; naming esters.
- Uses of esters: perfumes, flavourings, solvents, plasticisers.
- Hydrolysis of esters in acid and in alkali.
- Fats and oils are esters of glycerol (propane-1,2,3-triol); alkaline hydrolysis makes soap; biodiesel is a mixture of methyl esters made from vegetable oils and methanol.

#### Explained

**Physical properties.** The –COOH group forms hydrogen bonds, so acids have high boiling points (often forming dimers) and small ones dissolve in water.

**Acidity.** CH₃COOH ⇌ CH₃COO⁻ + H⁺. Weak: only partly dissociated. The ethanoate ion is stabilised because the negative charge is delocalised over both oxygens.

**Relative acidity (Eduqas OA2.3(a)).** Carboxylic acids > phenols > water > alcohols. Phenol is acidic because the phenoxide ion's charge is partly delocalised into the ring (less than in carboxylate). Ethanol is not acidic because the ethyl group pushes electrons towards O⁻, destabilising the ion. Consequence: carboxylic acids react with carbonates (fizz), phenol does not; phenol reacts with NaOH; alcohols react with neither but do react with sodium.

**Reactions of acids.**

- 2CH₃COOH + Na₂CO₃ → 2CH₃COONa + H₂O + CO₂ (test for –COOH).
- CH₃COOH + NaOH → CH₃COONa + H₂O.
- 2CH₃COOH + Mg → (CH₃COO)₂Mg + H₂.

**Other reactions (Edexcel, Eduqas).**

- Reduction to primary alcohols with LiAlH₄ in dry ether: CH₃COOH + 4[H] → CH₃CH₂OH + H₂O.
- With PCl₅ (or SOCl₂) to give acyl chlorides: CH₃COOH + PCl₅ → CH₃COCl + POCl₃ + HCl.
- **Decarboxylation (Eduqas):** heating the sodium salt with soda lime (NaOH/CaO) removes CO₂: CH₃COONa + NaOH → CH₄ + Na₂CO₃. Benzoate gives benzene.
- **Aromatic acids (Eduqas):** methylbenzene is oxidised to benzoic acid by heating under reflux with alkaline KMnO₄, then acidifying.

**Esterification.** Carboxylic acid + alcohol ⇌ ester + water, with a concentrated sulfuric acid catalyst, warm. It is reversible, so yields are moderate.

CH₃COOH + CH₃CH₂OH ⇌ CH₃COOCH₂CH₃ + H₂O (ethyl ethanoate).

Naming: the alcohol part first (ethyl), the acid part second (-oate). HCOOCH₃ is methyl methanoate; CH₃CH₂COOCH₃ is methyl propanoate.

Esters can also be made from acid anhydrides (OCR A 6.1.3(c)(ii)) or acyl chlorides (below), which go to completion.

**Uses of esters.** Sweet, fruity smells: perfumes and food flavourings. Polar but cannot hydrogen bond to each other, so they are volatile solvents for glues and nail varnish. Plasticisers for PVC.

**Hydrolysis.**

- Acid hydrolysis (reflux with dilute H₂SO₄ or HCl): reversible, gives the acid and alcohol. CH₃COOCH₂CH₃ + H₂O ⇌ CH₃COOH + CH₃CH₂OH.
- Alkaline hydrolysis (reflux with NaOH(aq)): goes to completion, gives the carboxylate salt and alcohol. CH₃COOCH₂CH₃ + NaOH → CH₃COONa + CH₃CH₂OH. Add acid afterwards to get the free acid.

**Fats and oils.** Triglycerides: esters of propane-1,2,3-triol (glycerol) with three long-chain fatty acids. Oils are liquid (unsaturated chains with C=C kinks pack less well); fats are solid.

- Hydrolysis with hot NaOH gives glycerol and sodium salts of the fatty acids: **soap** (saponification). The long hydrocarbon tail dissolves in grease and the ionic head in water.
- **Biodiesel**: the oil reacts with methanol (KOH catalyst) to give methyl esters of the fatty acids and glycerol (transesterification).

#### Worked example

Name the ester formed from propan-1-ol and ethanoic acid, and give the products of its hydrolysis with sodium hydroxide.

Propyl ethanoate, CH₃COOCH₂CH₂CH₃. Hydrolysis gives sodium ethanoate and propan-1-ol.

#### Common mistakes

- Naming esters with the acid part first.
- Forgetting that alkaline hydrolysis gives the salt, not the acid.
- Saying carboxylic acids are strong because they fizz with carbonates.

### 3.3.9.2 Acylation

*Refs: AQA 3.3.9.2, RP10 · Edexcel 17.13, 17.14, CP16 · OCR A 6.1.3(c)(ii), (e), (f), 6.2.2(b) · OCR B PL(l), PL(n) · Eduqas OA2.3(f), (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Acyl chlorides (RCOCl) and acid anhydrides ((RCO)₂O) react with water, alcohols, ammonia and primary amines.
- The nucleophilic addition–elimination mechanism (acyl chlorides).
- Advantages of ethanoic anhydride over ethanoyl chloride in making aspirin.
- Structures of primary and secondary amides; making amides.
- Preparing a pure organic solid and testing purity by melting point.

#### Explained

**Making acyl chlorides (OCR A 6.1.3(e)).** RCOOH + SOCl₂ → RCOCl + SO₂ + HCl. Both by-products are gases, so they leave the mixture.

**Reactions of ethanoyl chloride.** All are vigorous at room temperature with steamy fumes of HCl.

| Nucleophile | Product | Equation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Water | Carboxylic acid | CH₃COCl + H₂O → CH₃COOH + HCl |
| Alcohol | Ester | CH₃COCl + CH₃OH → CH₃COOCH₃ + HCl |
| Ammonia | Primary amide | CH₃COCl + 2NH₃ → CH₃CONH₂ + NH₄Cl |
| Primary amine | N-substituted (secondary) amide | CH₃COCl + 2CH₃NH₂ → CH₃CONHCH₃ + CH₃NH₃⁺Cl⁻ |
| Phenol | Phenyl ester (file 25) | CH₃COCl + C₆H₅OH → CH₃COOC₆H₅ + HCl |

With ammonia and amines, the HCl reacts with a second molecule of base, so two are used.

**Mechanism (nucleophilic addition–elimination)**, ethanoyl chloride with methanol:

1. Addition: the lone pair on the O of methanol attacks the δ+ carbon of C=O (the carbon is very δ+ because both O and Cl pull electrons). Arrow from the C=O π bond to O.
2. Elimination: the O⁻ lone pair reforms C=O (arrow from O⁻ to the C–O bond); the C–Cl bond breaks (arrow to Cl), releasing Cl⁻.
3. Cl⁻ removes H⁺ from the oxygen that attacked (arrow from the O–H bond to O), giving HCl.

For ammonia and amines the nucleophile is the N lone pair, and H⁺ is removed in the same way.

**Acid anhydrides** react in the same way but more slowly, with RCOOH as the by-product instead of HCl:

(CH₃CO)₂O + CH₃OH → CH₃COOCH₃ + CH₃COOH

**Aspirin.** 2-hydroxybenzoic acid (salicylic acid) is reacted with ethanoic anhydride (with a little concentrated acid catalyst) to acylate the –OH: the product is 2-ethanoyloxybenzoic acid. Ethanoic anhydride is used industrially rather than ethanoyl chloride because it is cheaper, less corrosive, reacts less violently (safer, easier to control), does not react as fast with water, and its by-product is ethanoic acid rather than toxic, corrosive HCl gas.

**Amides.**

- Primary amide: RCONH₂ (ethanamide).
- Secondary (N-substituted) amide: RCONHR' (N-methylethanamide).
- Made from acyl chlorides with ammonia or amines, or by heating ammonium salts of carboxylic acids (Eduqas OA2.3(g)): CH₃COONH₄ → CH₃CONH₂ + H₂O. Amides can be dehydrated to nitriles with P₄O₁₀ (file 29).
- Amides are hydrolysed by acid (giving the acid and NH₄⁺) or alkali (giving the carboxylate and NH₃) (OCR B PL(m)).

#### Practical

**AQA RP10 (and Edexcel CP16): preparing a pure organic solid and testing its purity, and preparing a pure organic liquid.**

*Solid (aspirin):*

1. Warm 2-hydroxybenzoic acid with excess ethanoic anhydride and a few drops of concentrated phosphoric or sulfuric acid, in a water bath, for about 20 minutes.
2. Add cold water to destroy excess anhydride, cool in ice to crystallise.
3. Filter under reduced pressure (Büchner funnel and flask), washing with a little ice-cold water.
4. **Recrystallise:** dissolve in the minimum volume of hot solvent (so the solution is saturated when cooled; impurities stay in solution), filter hot if there are insoluble impurities, cool slowly, filter under reduced pressure, wash with a little cold solvent, and dry.
5. **Melting point:** pack a little dry solid in a capillary tube and heat slowly in a melting point apparatus. A pure solid melts sharply at the data-book value (aspirin about 136 °C); impurities lower the melting point and widen the range.

*Liquid (ester, such as ethyl ethanoate):* reflux ethanol, ethanoic acid and concentrated sulfuric acid; distil; wash with sodium carbonate solution in a separating funnel (venting CO₂); wash with calcium chloride solution to remove ethanol; dry with anhydrous CaCl₂; redistil, collecting at the boiling point (77 °C).

#### Common mistakes

- Missing the H⁺ loss step at the end of the addition–elimination mechanism.
- Using one ammonia molecule in the equation with acyl chlorides.
- Using too much solvent in recrystallisation (yield falls).
- Saying a broad melting range means a higher melting point. Impurities lower it.

### Check questions

1. Explain why ethanoic acid is a weak acid and why the ethanoate ion is stable. (2 marks)
2. Give the reagent and observation to distinguish ethanoic acid from phenol. (2 marks)
3. Write an equation for the formation of methyl propanoate and give the conditions. (3 marks)
4. Write an equation for the alkaline hydrolysis of a triglyceride of octadecanoic acid (C₁₇H₃₅COOH) and name the useful product. (3 marks)
5. Outline the mechanism for the reaction of ethanoyl chloride with ammonia. Name the organic product. (5 marks)
6. Give two reasons why ethanoic anhydride is preferred to ethanoyl chloride in making aspirin. (2 marks)
7. Describe how to recrystallise an impure organic solid. (4 marks)
8. Explain how melting point shows whether a product is pure. (2 marks)
9. Draw the structure of N-ethylpropanamide and state how it could be made from an acyl chloride. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Only partly dissociates (1); negative charge delocalised over both O atoms (1).
2. Sodium carbonate / hydrogencarbonate (1); ethanoic acid fizzes, phenol does not (1).
3. CH₃CH₂COOH + CH₃OH ⇌ CH₃CH₂COOCH₃ + H₂O (1); concentrated H₂SO₄ catalyst (1); warm / heat under reflux (1).
4. Triglyceride + 3NaOH → glycerol (propane-1,2,3-triol) + 3C₁₇H₃₅COONa (2: species 1, balance 1); soap / sodium octadecanoate (1).
5. Lone pair on N attacks δ+ C (1); C=O π electrons to O (1); C=O reforms and Cl⁻ leaves (1); H⁺ lost from N (to Cl⁻ or NH₃) (1); ethanamide (1).
6. Any two: cheaper; less corrosive; less vigorous / safer; does not produce HCl; reacts less readily with water.
7. Dissolve in minimum volume of hot solvent (1); filter hot to remove insoluble impurities (1); cool to crystallise, soluble impurities stay in solution (1); filter under reduced pressure, wash with cold solvent, dry (1).
8. Pure: sharp melting point matching data value (1); impure: lower and over a range (1).
9. CH₃CH₂CONHCH₂CH₃ (1); propanoyl chloride with ethylamine (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/26-amines.md -->

## Amines: preparation, basicity and nucleophilic reactions

Amines are derivatives of ammonia in which hydrogen atoms are replaced by carbon groups. The lone pair on nitrogen makes them bases and nucleophiles. This file covers naming and classifying amines, making them, comparing their base strength, and their reactions as nucleophiles with halogenoalkanes and acyl chlorides.

### Overview

A-level only. Halogenoalkanes are in file 19, acyl chlorides and amides in file 24, nitration in file 25, and diazonium salts in file 37.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.11.1 to 3.3.11.3, including quaternary ammonium salts as cationic surfactants.
- Edexcel 18.8 to 18.13: reactions of amines with water, acids, halogenoalkanes, acyl chlorides and copper(II) ions (18.9); making amides from acyl chlorides (18.13).
- OCR A 6.2.1(a), (b): basicity and salt formation; aliphatic amines from halogenoalkanes and ammonia or amines; aromatic amines by reducing nitroarenes with tin and HCl.
- OCR B PL(j): the basic nature of the amino group and its reaction with acids.
- Eduqas OA3.1(a) to (d): primary aliphatic amines from halogenoalkanes and nitriles, aromatic amines from nitrobenzenes, basicity and ethanoylation.

### 3.3.11.1 Preparation

*Refs: AQA 3.3.11.1 · Edexcel 18.8, 18.11, 18.12 · OCR A 6.2.1(b) · Eduqas OA3.1(a), (b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Primary, secondary and tertiary amines and quaternary ammonium ions.
- Primary aliphatic amines from halogenoalkanes and excess ammonia, and from reduction of nitriles.
- Aromatic amines from reduction of nitro compounds with tin and concentrated HCl.

#### Explained

**Classification.**

| Type | Example | N bonded to |
| --- | --- | --- |
