| Primary | CH₃NH₂ (methylamine) | One C |
| Secondary | (CH₃)₂NH (dimethylamine) | Two C |
| Tertiary | (CH₃)₃N (trimethylamine) | Three C |
| Quaternary ammonium ion | (CH₃)₄N⁺ | Four C, positive charge |

Names: ethylamine (or ethanamine); phenylamine (C₆H₅NH₂); N-methylethylamine for CH₃CH₂NHCH₃.

**From halogenoalkanes.** CH₃CH₂Br + 2NH₃ → CH₃CH₂NH₂ + NH₄Br. Use excess ammonia in ethanol, heated in a sealed tube. The amine product still has a lone pair, so it can react with more halogenoalkane to form secondary and tertiary amines and a quaternary salt (section 3.3.11.3). Excess ammonia makes the primary amine the main product, but a mixture still forms.

**From nitriles.** Reduce with LiAlH₄ in dry ether, or with hydrogen and a nickel catalyst (catalytic hydrogenation, cheaper for industry):

CH₃CN + 4[H] → CH₃CH₂NH₂

This gives only the primary amine, with no mixture. The nitrile comes from a halogenoalkane and KCN (file 19), so the chain is one carbon longer than the halogenoalkane.

**Aromatic amines.** Nitrobenzene is reduced with tin and concentrated hydrochloric acid under reflux; then sodium hydroxide is added to release the free amine from its salt (C₆H₅NH₃⁺Cl⁻):

C₆H₅NO₂ + 6[H] → C₆H₅NH₂ + 2H₂O

Phenylamine is used to make azo dyes (file 37).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying ammonia with a halogenoalkane gives only the primary amine.
- Writing 2[H] for the reduction of a nitrile or nitro group. Nitrile needs 4[H], nitrobenzene 6[H].

### 3.3.11.2 Base properties

*Refs: AQA 3.3.11.2 · Edexcel 18.9, 18.10 · OCR A 6.2.1(a) · OCR B PL(j) · Eduqas OA3.1(c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Amines are weak bases: the lone pair on N accepts a proton.
- Relative base strength: primary aliphatic amines > ammonia > aromatic amines. Explain using the availability of the lone pair.
- Amines react with acids to form salts.

#### Explained

CH₃NH₂ + H₂O ⇌ CH₃NH₃⁺ + OH⁻ (aqueous methylamine is alkaline; it smells fishy).

CH₃CH₂NH₂ + HCl → CH₃CH₂NH₃⁺Cl⁻ (ethylammonium chloride). Adding a strong alkali releases the amine again.

**Base strength depends on how available the N lone pair is to accept H⁺.**

- **Primary aliphatic amines** (methylamine) are stronger bases than ammonia. The alkyl group pushes electron density on to the nitrogen (positive inductive effect), making the lone pair more available.
- **Phenylamine** is a weaker base than ammonia. The N lone pair is partly delocalised into the benzene ring, so it is less available to accept a proton.

So: CH₃CH₂NH₂ > NH₃ > C₆H₅NH₂.

**With copper(II) ions (Edexcel 18.9).** A little butylamine solution gives a pale blue precipitate of copper(II) hydroxide (the amine acts as a base, like ammonia). Excess gives a deep blue solution as the amine acts as a ligand, like ammonia (file 16).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying phenylamine is weaker because the benzene ring is "big".
- Saying alkyl groups "donate electrons to the hydrogen". They push electron density towards N.

### 3.3.11.3 Nucleophilic properties

*Refs: AQA 3.3.11.3 · Edexcel 18.9, 18.13 · Eduqas OA3.1(d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Amines are nucleophiles; with halogenoalkanes they give secondary and tertiary amines and quaternary ammonium salts (mechanism).
- Quaternary ammonium salts are used as cationic surfactants.
- Primary amines react with acyl chlorides and anhydrides to give N-substituted amides (mechanism: nucleophilic addition–elimination, file 24).

#### Explained

**With halogenoalkanes** (nucleophilic substitution, same mechanism as ammonia):

CH₃CH₂NH₂ + CH₃CH₂Br → (CH₃CH₂)₂NH + HBr (the HBr reacts with more amine)
(CH₃CH₂)₂NH + CH₃CH₂Br → (CH₃CH₂)₃N + HBr
(CH₃CH₂)₃N + CH₃CH₂Br → (CH₃CH₂)₄N⁺Br⁻

Mechanism (each step): the N lone pair attacks the δ+ carbon; C–Br breaks; the N–H⁺ intermediate loses H⁺ to another amine molecule (no H⁺ to lose in the last step, so a salt forms).

**Quaternary ammonium salts as cationic surfactants.** Salts with one long hydrocarbon chain, such as (CH₃)₃N⁺(CH₂)₁₅CH₃ Br⁻, have a positive head that sticks to negatively charged surfaces (hair, fabric) and a non-polar tail. They are used in fabric softeners and hair conditioners, and some are disinfectants.

**With acyl chlorides.** CH₃COCl + 2CH₃CH₂NH₂ → CH₃CONHCH₂CH₃ + CH₃CH₂NH₃⁺Cl⁻ (N-ethylethanamide). Ethanoylation of primary amines (Eduqas OA3.1(d)).

#### Common mistakes

- Expecting the reaction of an amine and halogenoalkane to stop at one step.
- Using one amine molecule in the acyl chloride equation.

### Check questions

1. Classify (CH₃)₂NCH₂CH₃ and name it. (2 marks)
2. Give two methods of making propylamine from a suitable organic compound, with reagents and conditions. Explain which gives the purer product. (5 marks)
3. Give the reagents and conditions to convert nitrobenzene into phenylamine and write an equation using [H]. (3 marks)
4. Put ammonia, ethylamine and phenylamine in order of base strength and explain. (4 marks)
5. Write an equation for the reaction of methylamine with hydrochloric acid and name the product. (2 marks)
6. Outline the mechanism for the reaction of ethylamine with bromoethane. (4 marks)
7. Give one use of quaternary ammonium salts and explain how the structure suits the use. (2 marks)
8. Write an equation for the reaction of ethanoyl chloride with excess methylamine. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Tertiary (1); N,N-dimethylethylamine (accept dimethylethylamine) (1).
2. 1-bromopropane with excess ammonia in ethanol, heated in sealed tube (2). Propanenitrile reduced with LiAlH₄ in dry ether / H₂ and Ni (2). Nitrile reduction gives purer product; no further substitution (1).
3. Tin and concentrated HCl, reflux, then NaOH (2); C₆H₅NO₂ + 6[H] → C₆H₅NH₂ + 2H₂O (1).
4. Ethylamine > ammonia > phenylamine (1); ethyl group pushes electron density onto N (1); lone pair more available (1); in phenylamine lone pair delocalised into ring, less available (1).
5. CH₃NH₂ + HCl → CH₃NH₃⁺Cl⁻ (1); methylammonium chloride (1).
6. N lone pair attacks δ+ C (1); arrow from C–Br to Br (1); intermediate with N⁺ (1); H⁺ removed by another amine / ammonia giving diethylamine (1).
7. Fabric softener / hair conditioner (1); positive head attracted to negative surface, hydrocarbon tail gives smooth / water-repelling layer (1).
8. CH₃COCl + 2CH₃NH₂ → CH₃CONHCH₃ + CH₃NH₃⁺Cl⁻ (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/27-condensation-polymers.md -->

## Condensation polymers, biodegradability and disposal

Condensation polymers form when monomers join and a small molecule, usually water or HCl, is lost at each link. This file covers polyesters and polyamides (Terylene, nylon 6,6, Kevlar, poly(lactic acid)), drawing repeat units and monomers, hydrolysis, the forces between chains, and biodegradability and disposal compared with addition polymers.

### Overview

A-level only. Addition polymers are in file 20; esters and amides in file 24; proteins (natural polyamides) in file 28.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.12.1, 3.3.12.2: Terylene, nylon 6,6 and Kevlar; hydrogen bonding between chains; hydrolysis; biodegradability; advantages and disadvantages of landfill, incineration and recycling.
- Edexcel 17.16 (polyesters) and 18.14, 18.15 (polyamides and repeat units).
- OCR A 6.2.3(a) to (c): polyesters and polyamides from dicarboxylic acids or diacyl chlorides, or from one monomer with both groups (hydroxycarboxylic acids, amino acids); acid and base hydrolysis; predicting repeat units and monomers.
- OCR B PL(o), (p): differences between addition and condensation polymerisation; linking polymer structure to properties.
- Eduqas OA4(c), (d): condensation compared with addition polymerisation; how polyesters and polyamides form.

### 3.3.12.1 Condensation polymers

*Refs: AQA 3.3.12.1 · Edexcel 17.16, 18.14, 18.15 · OCR A 6.2.3(a) to (c) · OCR B PL(o), PL(p) · Eduqas OA4(c), (d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Condensation polymers form from monomers with two functional groups, losing a small molecule at each link.
- Polyesters: from a diol and a dicarboxylic acid (or diacyl chloride), or from a hydroxycarboxylic acid.
- Polyamides: from a diamine and a dicarboxylic acid (or diacyl chloride), or from an amino acid.
- Draw repeat units from monomers, and monomers from a section of polymer.
- Examples: Terylene (PET), nylon 6,6, Kevlar, poly(lactic acid).
- Hydrogen bonding between polyamide chains; the strength of Kevlar.

#### Explained

**Addition compared with condensation.**

| | Addition | Condensation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Monomer | Contains C=C | Two functional groups (or two different monomers each with two) |
| By-product | None | Small molecule (H₂O or HCl) |
| Link | C–C | Ester (–COO–) or amide (–CONH–) |
| Backbone | Only C | Contains O or N |

**Terylene (PET).** Benzene-1,4-dicarboxylic acid + ethane-1,2-diol:

n HOOC–C₆H₄–COOH + n HOCH₂CH₂OH → –[OC–C₆H₄–COO–CH₂CH₂–O]ₙ– + 2n H₂O

Repeat unit: –CO–C₆H₄–CO–O–CH₂CH₂–O–. Uses: bottles, clothing fibres.

**Nylon 6,6.** Hexane-1,6-diamine + hexanedioic acid (each monomer has 6 carbons):

n H₂N(CH₂)₆NH₂ + n HOOC(CH₂)₄COOH → –[NH(CH₂)₆NH–CO(CH₂)₄CO]ₙ– + 2n H₂O

Uses: clothing, ropes, carpets. Using hexanedioyl dichloride, ClOC(CH₂)₄COCl, instead of the acid works faster at room temperature and gives HCl.

**Kevlar.** Benzene-1,4-diamine + benzene-1,4-dicarboxylic acid (or its dichloride): –[NH–C₆H₄–NH–CO–C₆H₄–CO]ₙ–. The flat, rigid chains line up in parallel, held by many hydrogen bonds between C=O and N–H groups on neighbouring chains. This makes Kevlar very strong and light: used in bulletproof vests, helmets, ropes and tyres.

**From one monomer.**

- 2-hydroxypropanoic acid (lactic acid) forms poly(lactic acid), PLA: –[O–CH(CH₃)–CO]ₙ–. Made from corn starch; biodegradable; used in packaging, cups and dissolving stitches.
- Amino acids form polypeptides (file 28).

**Drawing repeat units.** Join the –COOH of one monomer to the –OH or –NH₂ of the next by removing H₂O (OH from the acid, H from the alcohol or amine). Show the link with open bonds at each end.

**Finding monomers from a polymer.** Break each ester or amide link: add –OH to the C=O side and –H to the O or N side.

**Hydrogen bonding.** In polyamides, the N–H of one chain hydrogen bonds to the C=O of another. Polyesters have only permanent dipole–dipole and London forces between chains.

#### Practical

**Making nylon (AQA).** Carefully pour a solution of hexanedioyl dichloride in cyclohexane on to an aqueous solution of hexane-1,6-diamine (with a little NaOH to neutralise the HCl). Nylon forms at the interface; use tweezers to pull a continuous thread and wind it on to a rod. Wear gloves and goggles: acyl chlorides are corrosive, the diamine is an irritant.

#### Common mistakes

- Including the H₂O in the repeat unit, or leaving an –OH or –H on the ends.
- Drawing two repeat units when asked for one.
- Getting the number of carbons in nylon 6,6 monomers wrong (the acid has 6 including both COOH carbons).

### 3.3.12.2 Biodegradability and disposal of polymers

*Refs: AQA 3.3.12.2 · OCR A 6.2.3(b) · OCR B PL(m). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Polyalkenes are chemically inert, so non-biodegradable.
- Polyesters and polyamides can be broken down by hydrolysis, so they biodegrade (slowly).
- Acid and base hydrolysis equations.
- Advantages and disadvantages of landfill, incineration and recycling.

#### Explained

**Why polyalkenes are inert.** Non-polar C–C and C–H bonds; no site for nucleophiles to attack.

**Why condensation polymers biodegrade.** The ester and amide links are polar; the δ+ carbon of C=O is attacked by water (catalysed by acid, alkali or enzymes in the environment). This is still slow in landfill.

**Hydrolysis.**

- Polyester with NaOH(aq): gives the diol and the sodium salt of the dicarboxylic acid.
- Polyester with acid: gives the diol and the dicarboxylic acid.
- Polyamide with HCl(aq): gives the dicarboxylic acid and the ammonium salt of the diamine (H₃N⁺(CH₂)₆NH₃⁺ 2Cl⁻).
- Polyamide with NaOH(aq): gives the sodium dicarboxylate and the free diamine.

**Disposal.**

| Method | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Landfill | Cheap, easy; little sorting | Takes up land; polymers last for centuries; leachate can pollute |
| Incineration | Energy recovered for electricity; reduces volume | CO₂ (greenhouse gas); toxic gases (HCl from PVC, which must be removed) |
| Recycling (mechanical) | Saves crude oil and energy; reduces landfill and CO₂ | Collection and sorting costly; quality can be lower; only limited times |
| Chemical recycling (hydrolysis or cracking) | Back to monomers or feedstock | Energy-intensive |

#### Common mistakes

- Saying condensation polymers biodegrade quickly. They can be hydrolysed but are still slow to break down in landfill.
- Writing the free diamine as the product of acid hydrolysis of a polyamide; in acid it is protonated.

### Check questions

1. State two differences between addition and condensation polymerisation. (2 marks)
2. Draw the repeat unit of the polyester formed from propane-1,3-diol and butanedioic acid. (2 marks)
3. Draw the monomers of the polymer –[NH(CH₂)₄NH–CO(CH₂)₈CO]ₙ–. (2 marks)
4. Draw the repeat unit of the polymer formed from 3-aminopropanoic acid. (1 mark)
5. Explain why Kevlar is very strong. (3 marks)
6. Give the products when nylon 6,6 is hydrolysed by hot hydrochloric acid. (2 marks)
7. Explain why polyesters are biodegradable but poly(ethene) is not. (3 marks)
8. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of recycling polymers rather than putting them in landfill. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Any two: addition monomers have C=C, condensation have two functional groups; condensation loses a small molecule (H₂O / HCl), addition does not; condensation forms ester / amide links.
2. –O–CH₂CH₂CH₂–O–CO–CH₂CH₂–CO– with open bonds (2: ester link 1, correct structure 1).
3. H₂N(CH₂)₄NH₂ (1); HOOC(CH₂)₈COOH or ClOC(CH₂)₈COCl (1).
4. –NH–CH₂CH₂–CO– (1).
5. Rigid, flat chains line up parallel (1); hydrogen bonds between C=O and N–H of neighbouring chains (1); many strong intermolecular forces (1).
6. Hexanedioic acid (1); hexane-1,6-diammonium chloride / ClH₃N(CH₂)₆NH₃Cl (1).
7. Ester link polar / δ+ carbon (1); can be hydrolysed (by water / enzymes) (1); poly(ethene) has non-polar C–C and C–H bonds, unreactive (1).
8. Advantage: saves raw materials / crude oil / energy / landfill space (1). Disadvantage: sorting / collection costs / lower quality product (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/23-optical-isomerism-aldehydes-and-ketones.md -->

## Optical isomerism, aldehydes and ketones

This file covers chirality and optical isomers, then the carbonyl compounds: how aldehydes and ketones are made, how they are told apart, their reduction by NaBH₄, nucleophilic addition of HCN (which links back to optical isomerism), and the 2,4-DNPH and iodoform tests.

### Overview

A-level only. Oxidation of alcohols to carbonyls is in file 21. Optical isomerism of complexes is in file 15 and of amino acids in file 28.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.7 and 3.3.8.
- Edexcel 17.1 to 17.8: optical activity data used to deduce SN1 or SN2 mechanisms (17.5); reactions with Fehling's or Benedict's, Tollens', acidified dichromate, LiAlH₄, HCN, iodine in alkali, and 2,4-DNPH (17.8).
- OCR A 6.1.2(a) to (e) and 6.2.2(c), (d): oxidation of aldehydes, NaBH₄, HCN, the nucleophilic addition mechanism, 2,4-DNPH and Tollens'; optical isomerism and identifying chiral centres.
- OCR B PL(q) (optical isomerism) and CD(i), (k) (reactions of carbonyl compounds and the nucleophilic addition mechanism).
- Eduqas OA1.1(a) to (d) and OA2.2(a) to (f), including the triiodomethane (iodoform) test (OA2.2(f)) and the specified practical: identifying aldehydes and ketones with 2,4-DNPH.

### 3.3.7 Optical isomerism

*Refs: AQA 3.3.7 · Edexcel 17.1 to 17.5 · OCR A 6.2.2(c), (d) · OCR B PL(q) · Eduqas OA1.1(a) to (d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Optical isomerism is a form of stereoisomerism. It occurs when a molecule has a chiral centre: a carbon atom with four different groups attached.
- Enantiomers are non-superimposable mirror images.
- Enantiomers rotate plane-polarised light in opposite directions by the same angle.
- A racemic mixture (racemate) contains equal amounts of both enantiomers and does not rotate plane-polarised light.
- Draw enantiomers in 3D (wedge and dashed bonds).
- Edexcel: use optical activity of products to tell SN1 from SN2.

#### Explained

**Chiral centre.** Butan-2-ol, CH₃CH(OH)CH₂CH₃: the second carbon has H, OH, CH₃ and C₂H₅, all different, so it is chiral. Mark it with an asterisk.

**Drawing.** Draw the chiral carbon with two bonds in the plane of the paper, one wedge (towards you) and one dashed (away). Draw the mirror image beside it with a dotted mirror line.

**Properties.** Enantiomers have identical physical properties (melting point, boiling point) and identical chemical properties with non-chiral reagents. They differ in:

- the direction they rotate plane-polarised light (one clockwise, +; the other anticlockwise, −). This is optical activity;
- how they react with other chiral molecules, such as enzymes and receptors in the body.

**Polarimeter.** Light is passed through a polariser so it vibrates in one plane, then through the sample, then an analyser that is turned to find the angle of rotation.

**Racemic mixtures.** Equal amounts of the two enantiomers rotate light equally in opposite directions, so there is no overall rotation. Reactions that go through a planar intermediate (carbocation, or a C=O carbon) usually give a racemic mixture, because attack from either face is equally likely.

**Using optical activity (Edexcel 17.5).** If a single enantiomer of a halogenoalkane is hydrolysed:

- by **SN2**, the nucleophile attacks from the opposite side to the leaving group, inverting the configuration. The product is a single enantiomer, still optically active (rotation of light changes).
- by **SN1**, the planar carbocation is attacked equally from both sides. The product is a racemic mixture, optically inactive.

**Why it matters.** Drugs often work because their shape fits a receptor. One enantiomer may be active and the other inactive or harmful. Thalidomide is the famous case: one enantiomer treated morning sickness, the other caused birth defects (and they interconvert in the body). Pharmaceutical companies now aim to make single enantiomers, which is costly but reduces side effects and dose (file 29).

#### Common mistakes

- Marking a carbon with two identical groups as chiral.
- Saying enantiomers have different boiling points.
- Saying a racemic mixture "has no isomers". It has both, in equal amounts.

### 3.3.8 Aldehydes and ketones

*Refs: AQA 3.3.8 · Edexcel 17.6 to 17.8 · OCR A 6.1.2(a) to (e) · OCR B CD(i), CD(k) · Eduqas OA2.2(a) to (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Aldehydes are made by oxidising primary alcohols; ketones by oxidising secondary alcohols.
- Aldehydes are further oxidised to carboxylic acids; ketones are not.
- Tollens' and Fehling's (or Benedict's) tests.
- Reduction by NaBH₄ to alcohols; mechanism (nucleophilic addition with H⁻).
- Nucleophilic addition of HCN (from KCN and acid) to form hydroxynitriles; mechanism; racemic product.
- Hazards of KCN.
- 2,4-DNPH test and identifying the carbonyl from melting points of derivatives.
- Iodoform test for CH₃CO– (Edexcel, Eduqas).
- LiAlH₄ as a reducing agent (Edexcel).

#### Explained

**Structure.** Aldehydes have C=O at the end of the chain (–CHO). Ketones have C=O inside the chain. The C=O bond is polar (C δ+), so nucleophiles attack the carbon. Unlike in C=C, the electron-rich region is not attractive to electrophiles in the same way; carbonyls undergo nucleophilic addition.

Small aldehydes and ketones dissolve in water because water hydrogen bonds to their oxygen, but they have lower boiling points than alcohols because they cannot hydrogen bond to each other.

**Oxidation.**

- RCHO + [O] → RCOOH, using acidified dichromate under reflux (orange to green).
- **Tollens'**: [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺ is reduced to silver: Ag⁺ + e⁻ → Ag. Silver mirror. In alkali, RCHO is oxidised to RCOO⁻.
- **Fehling's** (or Benedict's): Cu²⁺ complex (blue) reduced to Cu₂O, a brick-red precipitate.
- Ketones give no reaction with any of these.

**Reduction with NaBH₄.** In aqueous or methanol solution, NaBH₄ provides hydride ions (H⁻), a nucleophile.

- Aldehyde → primary alcohol: CH₃CHO + 2[H] → CH₃CH₂OH.
- Ketone → secondary alcohol: CH₃COCH₃ + 2[H] → CH₃CH(OH)CH₃.

Mechanism (nucleophilic addition):

1. Lone pair on H⁻ attacks the δ+ carbon of C=O. Curly arrow from H⁻ to C; curly arrow from the C=O π bond to O.
2. An alkoxide ion forms (O⁻).
3. The O⁻ lone pair takes H⁺ from water (curly arrow from O⁻ lone pair to H of H₂O).

**LiAlH₄ (Edexcel).** A stronger reducing agent, in dry ether. Reduces aldehydes and ketones, and also carboxylic acids (to primary alcohols, Eduqas OA2.3(c)).

**Addition of HCN.** CH₃CHO + HCN → CH₃CH(OH)CN (2-hydroxypropanenitrile). Conditions: KCN with dilute acid (provides CN⁻ and H⁺); HCN itself is a very toxic gas, so it is made in solution as needed and handled in a fume cupboard. KCN is also highly toxic.

Mechanism:

1. Lone pair on C of CN⁻ attacks the δ+ carbon. Arrow from the C=O π bond to O.
2. Alkoxide ion forms.
3. O⁻ takes H⁺ (from HCN or H⁺).

The carbonyl carbon is trigonal planar, so CN⁻ attacks from above or below with equal chance. If the product has a chiral centre (aldehydes other than methanal, and unsymmetrical ketones), a racemic mixture forms.

This reaction adds one carbon to the chain, and the nitrile can be hydrolysed to a carboxylic acid (2-hydroxy acid) or reduced to an amine (file 29).

**2,4-Dinitrophenylhydrazine (Brady's reagent).** Reacts with the C=O of aldehydes and ketones (but not carboxylic acids or esters) to give an orange or yellow precipitate, a 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazone. It is a condensation (addition then loss of water). To identify the compound: recrystallise the precipitate, measure its melting point and compare with a data table of derivatives.

**Triiodomethane (iodoform) test (Edexcel, Eduqas).** Warm with iodine in sodium hydroxide solution (or KI and NaClO). A pale yellow precipitate of CHI₃ with an antiseptic smell shows a CH₃CO– group (methyl ketones and ethanal), or a CH₃CH(OH)– group (ethanol and secondary alcohols like propan-2-ol, which are first oxidised to CH₃CO–). The other product is the carboxylate with one fewer carbon: CH₃COCH₃ + 3I₂ + 4NaOH → CHI₃ + CH₃COONa + 3NaI + 3H₂O.

#### Practical

**Eduqas: identifying aldehydes and ketones with 2,4-DNPH.** Add a few drops of the carbonyl to 2,4-DNPH solution. Filter the precipitate, recrystallise from the minimum volume of hot ethanol, dry, and measure the melting point. Compare with tables.

#### Worked example

A compound C₄H₈O gives an orange precipitate with 2,4-DNPH, no silver mirror with Tollens', and a yellow precipitate with iodine in alkali. Identify it.

2,4-DNPH: carbonyl. No Tollens': ketone. Iodoform positive: CH₃CO–. So butanone, CH₃COCH₂CH₃.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying NaBH₄ "adds hydrogen". Say hydride ion acts as a nucleophile.
- Drawing the first arrow from the C=O to the nucleophile.
- Saying HCN addition gives one optical isomer.
- Saying 2,4-DNPH distinguishes aldehydes from ketones. It reacts with both.

### Check questions

1. Draw the two optical isomers of 2-hydroxypropanoic acid (lactic acid) and mark the chiral centre. (2 marks)
2. Explain why a racemic mixture does not rotate plane-polarised light. (2 marks)
3. A single enantiomer of 2-bromobutane is hydrolysed. The product rotates plane-polarised light. Deduce the mechanism and explain. (3 marks)
4. Give the reagent and observations that distinguish propanal from propanone. (2 marks)
5. Outline the mechanism for the reduction of propanone by NaBH₄. Name the product. (4 marks)
6. Outline the mechanism for the reaction of ethanal with HCN. Explain why the product is optically inactive. (5 marks)
7. Give one safety precaution for the reaction in question 6 and explain why KCN with acid is used instead of HCN. (2 marks)
8. Explain how 2,4-DNPH can be used to identify an unknown ketone. (3 marks)
9. Which of these give a positive iodoform test: propanal, propanone, propan-1-ol, propan-2-ol? (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Two 3D mirror images with C bonded to H, OH, CH₃ and COOH (1); chiral centre marked (1).
2. Equal amounts of each enantiomer (1); rotate light by equal amounts in opposite directions, so effects cancel (1).
3. SN2 (1); one-step attack from opposite side gives a single (inverted) enantiomer (1); SN1 would give a planar carbocation and a racemic, inactive product (1).
4. Tollens' (or Fehling's) (1); propanal silver mirror (red precipitate), propanone no change (1).
5. H⁻ from NaBH₄ attacks δ+ C (1); arrow from C=O π bond to O (1); alkoxide takes H⁺ from water (1); propan-2-ol (1).
6. CN⁻ lone pair attacks δ+ C (1); arrow from C=O to O (1); intermediate takes H⁺ (1); product 2-hydroxypropanenitrile. C=O planar, so attack equally likely from either side (1); racemic mixture formed (1).
7. Fume cupboard / gloves (1); HCN is a toxic gas, so it is made in solution as needed (1).
8. Add 2,4-DNPH: orange precipitate (1); recrystallise and measure melting point (1); compare with known melting points of derivatives (1).
9. Propanone (1); propan-2-ol (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/30-nmr-spectroscopy.md -->

## NMR spectroscopy and combined structure determination

Nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) shows the different environments of carbon and hydrogen atoms in a molecule. This file covers how NMR works, carbon-13 spectra, proton spectra (chemical shift, integration and splitting), TMS and deuterated solvents, and how to combine NMR with other data to solve a structure.

### Overview

A-level for most boards. Mass spectrometry, infrared and test-tube tests are in file 22; empirical formula from combustion data is in file 2.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.15: ¹³C and ¹H NMR, chemical shift, integration, n+1 splitting, TMS, CDCl₃ and other solvents.
- Edexcel 19.2 to 19.5: ¹³C and high-resolution ¹H NMR.
- OCR A 6.3.2(a) to (e): adds the use of D₂O to identify O–H and N–H protons (6.3.2(b)), and combining elemental analysis, mass spectra, IR and NMR (6.3.2(e)).
- OCR B PL(s), (t): ¹H and ¹³C NMR and combining spectroscopic techniques.
- Eduqas C3.5(c) (AS: ¹³C and low-resolution ¹H) and OA4(f) (A-level: high-resolution ¹H).

### 3.3.15 Nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy

*Refs: AQA 3.3.15 · Edexcel 19.2 to 19.5 · OCR A 6.3.2(a) to (e) · OCR B PL(s), PL(t) · Eduqas C3.5(c), OA4(f). Level: A-level only (Eduqas C3.5(c) AS).*

#### What you need to know

- NMR gives information about the positions of ¹³C or ¹H atoms in a molecule.
- Chemical shift (δ, in ppm) depends on the environment; it is measured relative to TMS.
- ¹³C NMR: number of peaks = number of carbon environments.
- ¹H NMR: number of peaks = proton environments; integration (relative peak area) = ratio of protons; splitting by the n+1 rule.
- Why TMS is the standard; why CDCl₃ or other deuterated solvents are used.
- OCR A: D₂O exchange to identify –OH and –NH protons.

#### Explained

**How it works.** Nuclei with an odd mass number (¹H, ¹³C) have spin and behave like tiny magnets. In a strong magnetic field they line up with or against it. Radio waves of the right frequency flip them to the higher-energy state (resonance). The frequency needed depends on how much the nucleus is shielded by surrounding electrons. Electronegative atoms nearby pull electron density away (deshielding), so the nucleus resonates at a higher chemical shift.

**Chemical shift and TMS.** δ is measured relative to tetramethylsilane, Si(CH₃)₄, set at 0 ppm. TMS is used because:

- all 12 H (and 4 C) are in one environment, giving one strong peak;
- its peak is well away from most organic peaks (upfield, because Si is less electronegative than C);
- it is inert, non-toxic, and volatile so it is easily removed.

**Solvents.** Samples are dissolved in deuterated solvents such as CDCl₃, which have no ¹H atoms and so give no peak in ¹H spectra. (CDCl₃ still has carbon, but its ¹³C peak is known and ignored.) Deuterium (²H) does not show in ¹H NMR.

**Equivalent environments.** Atoms in the same environment are related by symmetry. Ethanol CH₃CH₂OH has 2 carbon environments and 3 proton environments. Propanone CH₃COCH₃ has 2 carbon environments and 1 proton environment.

**¹³C NMR.** Each carbon environment gives one peak (no splitting in the spectra used). Typical shifts:

| Carbon environment | δ / ppm |
| --- | --- |
| C–C (alkyl) | 5 to 40 |
| C–Cl, C–Br | 10 to 70 |
| C–N (amines) | 25 to 60 |
| C–O (alcohols, ethers, esters) | 50 to 90 |
| C=C (alkenes) | 90 to 150 |
| Aromatic C | 110 to 160 |
| C=O (acids, esters, amides) | 160 to 185 |
| C=O (aldehydes, ketones) | 190 to 220 |

Use the values on the data sheet in an exam.

**¹H NMR.** Typical shifts:

| Proton environment | δ / ppm |
| --- | --- |
| R–CH₃ | 0.7 to 1.2 |
| R–CH₂–R | 1.2 to 1.4 |
| H–C–C=O (next to carbonyl) | 2.1 to 2.6 |
| H–C–N | 2.3 to 2.9 |
| Benzene ring C–H attached to ring (Ar–CH) | 2.3 to 3.0 |
| H–C–O (alcohol, ether), H–C–Cl/Br | 3.1 to 4.2 |
| ester O–CH | 3.7 to 4.1 |
| Alkene C=C–H | 4.5 to 6.0 |
| R–OH | 1 to 5 (variable, broad) |
| Aromatic H | 6.5 to 8.0 |
| Aldehyde –CHO | 9.0 to 10.0 |
| Carboxylic acid –COOH | 10 to 12 |

**Integration.** The area under each peak is proportional to the number of H in that environment. Often shown as an integration trace or as relative numbers. For ethanol: 3 : 2 : 1.

**Splitting (n+1 rule).** A peak is split by the H atoms on neighbouring carbons (three bonds away). If there are n equivalent neighbouring H atoms, the peak splits into n + 1 lines.

| Neighbouring H | Pattern | Relative heights |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | Singlet | 1 |
| 1 | Doublet | 1 : 1 |
| 2 | Triplet | 1 : 2 : 1 |
| 3 | Quartet | 1 : 3 : 3 : 1 |

An ethyl group CH₃CH₂– shows a triplet (CH₃, next to 2 H) and a quartet (CH₂, next to 3 H). H atoms on O or N usually appear as singlets and do not split their neighbours (fast exchange).

**D₂O exchange (OCR A).** Shake the sample with D₂O. –OH and –NH protons are replaced by D: O–H + D₂O → O–D + HDO. Their peak disappears from the spectrum, identifying it.

**Solving structures.** Combine:

1. Molecular formula (from percentage composition and Mᵣ from mass spectrum).
2. IR for functional groups.
3. ¹³C NMR for the number and types of carbon.
4. ¹H NMR: shifts (environment), integration (how many H), splitting (how many neighbours).
5. Fragmentation in the mass spectrum to confirm.

#### Worked example

A compound C₄H₈O₂ has: IR C=O at 1740 cm⁻¹, no broad O–H. ¹H NMR: δ 4.1 (2H, quartet), 2.0 (3H, singlet), 1.3 (3H, triplet). Identify it.

- C=O and no O–H with two O: an ester.
- Quartet (2H) and triplet (3H): an ethyl group, CH₂ at 4.1 so bonded to O (O–CH₂CH₃).
- Singlet 3H at 2.0: CH₃ next to C=O with no neighbouring H.
- Ethyl ethanoate, CH₃COOCH₂CH₃.

#### Common mistakes

- Counting the H atoms on the carbon itself for splitting. Count the neighbours.
- Forgetting that CH₃ groups that are equivalent (as in propanone) give one peak.
- Saying TMS is used because it "has no protons". It has 12 equivalent protons, giving one peak at 0.
- Saying CDCl₃ is used because it is non-polar. It is used because it has no ¹H.

### Check questions

1. How many peaks are there in the ¹³C NMR spectrum of (a) propan-2-ol, (b) 2-methylpropane, (c) 1,4-dimethylbenzene? (3 marks)
2. Give two reasons why TMS is used as the standard. (2 marks)
3. Explain why CDCl₃ is used as the solvent in ¹H NMR. (1 mark)
4. Predict the ¹H NMR spectrum of propanal, CH₃CH₂CHO: number of peaks, approximate shift, integration and splitting of each. (4 marks)
5. A compound C₃H₆O has one peak in its ¹H NMR spectrum. Identify it. (1 mark)
6. A compound C₃H₇Br has ¹H NMR peaks: a doublet (6H) and a septet (1H). Identify it and explain the splitting. (3 marks)
7. Explain how D₂O can show which peak in a ¹H NMR spectrum is from an –OH group. (2 marks)
8. Compound X, C₄H₈O, gives an orange precipitate with 2,4-DNPH and no reaction with Tollens' reagent. Its ¹H NMR spectrum has a quartet (2H), a singlet (3H) and a triplet (3H). Identify X, explaining each piece of evidence. (5 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. (a) 2 (1); (b) 2 (1); (c) 3 (1).
2. Any two: single peak (all H equivalent); peak at 0, away from others; inert / non-toxic; volatile so easily removed.
3. No ¹H atoms so does not give a peak (1).
4. Three peaks (1); CH₃ about 1.0, triplet, 3H (1); CH₂ about 2.4, quintet / multiplet (split by 3 + 1), 2H (1); CHO about 9.8, triplet, 1H (1).
5. Propanone (1).
6. 2-bromopropane (1); 6H doublet: two equivalent CH₃ each next to one H (1); 1H septet: CH next to 6 H (1).
7. Shake with D₂O; OH replaced by OD (1); OH peak disappears (1).
8. 2,4-DNPH positive: carbonyl (1); Tollens' negative: ketone (1); quartet 2H and triplet 3H: ethyl group CH₂CH₃ (1); singlet 3H: CH₃ with no neighbouring H, next to C=O (1); butanone, CH₃COCH₂CH₃ (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/08-thermodynamics.md -->

## Thermodynamics: Born–Haber cycles, solutions, entropy and free energy

This file extends energetics (file 4). It covers lattice enthalpy and Born–Haber cycles, the perfect ionic model and polarisation, enthalpies of solution and hydration, entropy, and Gibbs free energy, which decides whether a reaction is feasible.

### Overview

A-level only. You need Hess's law and the enthalpy definitions from file 4.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.8.1 and 3.1.8.2. AQA defines lattice enthalpy as either formation or dissociation, so watch the sign in questions.
- Edexcel 13.1 to 13.22 define lattice energy as formation (exothermic). Edexcel adds polarising power and polarisability (13.6 to 13.8), the total entropy approach with ΔS(surroundings) = −ΔH ÷ T (13.15 to 13.18) and ΔG = −RT ln K (13.20).
- OCR A 5.2.1(a) to (e) (lattice enthalpy as formation, exothermic) and 5.2.2(a) to (f).
- OCR B O(a) to (h) set solubility, hydration, lattice enthalpy and entropy in the Oceans storyline and add the solubility product, Ksp (O(h)).
- Eduqas PI4.1(a) to (d) and PI4.2(a) to (e).

### 3.1.8.1 Born–Haber cycles

*Refs: AQA 3.1.8.1 · Edexcel 13.1 to 13.11 · OCR A 5.2.1(a) to (e) · OCR B O(a) to (c) · Eduqas PI4.1(a) to (d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Definitions: enthalpy of atomisation, ionisation energy, electron affinity, lattice enthalpy (formation and dissociation), enthalpy of hydration, enthalpy of solution.
- Construct Born–Haber cycles and use them to calculate any one missing quantity.
- Compare lattice enthalpies from Born–Haber cycles with values from the perfect ionic model to judge covalent character.
- Use cycles of solution, lattice and hydration enthalpies.
- Effect of ionic charge and radius on lattice and hydration enthalpies.

#### Explained

**Definitions.**

| Term | Definition | Sign |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Enthalpy of atomisation ΔatH | Forming 1 mol of gaseous atoms from the element in its standard state | + |
| First ionisation energy | Removing 1 electron from each atom in 1 mol of gaseous atoms | + |
| First electron affinity | Adding 1 electron to each atom in 1 mol of gaseous atoms to form 1 mol of gaseous 1− ions | − (usually) |
| Second electron affinity | Adding 1 electron to each ion in 1 mol of gaseous 1− ions | + (repulsion between ion and electron) |
| Lattice enthalpy of formation ΔLEH | 1 mol of ionic solid formed from its gaseous ions | − |
| Lattice enthalpy of dissociation | 1 mol of ionic solid separated into gaseous ions | + |
| Enthalpy of hydration ΔhydH | 1 mol of gaseous ions dissolved in water to form 1 mol of aqueous ions | − |
| Enthalpy of solution ΔsolH | 1 mol of solute dissolved in enough solvent that the ions are separated and do not interact | + or − |

Example equations for NaCl: Na(s) → Na(g); ½Cl₂(g) → Cl(g); Na(g) → Na⁺(g) + e⁻; Cl(g) + e⁻ → Cl⁻(g); Na⁺(g) + Cl⁻(g) → NaCl(s).

**Born–Haber cycle for NaCl** (lattice formation). Going round from the elements:

ΔfH(NaCl) = ΔatH(Na) + ΔatH(Cl) + IE₁(Na) + EA₁(Cl) + ΔLEH(NaCl)

Draw it with upward arrows for endothermic steps and downward for exothermic steps, keeping electrons and all species written on each level.

**Factors affecting lattice enthalpy.** Larger charges and smaller ions give a more exothermic lattice enthalpy of formation, because the ions attract more strongly and are closer. MgO (−3791 kJ mol⁻¹) is far more exothermic than NaCl (−787 kJ mol⁻¹).

**Perfect ionic model and covalent character.** A theoretical lattice enthalpy can be calculated assuming perfectly spherical ions and purely ionic bonding. If the Born–Haber (experimental) value is more exothermic than the theoretical one, the bonding has some covalent character. This happens when a small, highly charged cation **polarises** (distorts) a large anion, pulling its electron cloud towards itself.

- **Polarising power** of a cation increases with higher charge and smaller radius (high charge density). Al³⁺ and Mg²⁺ are strongly polarising.
- **Polarisability** of an anion increases with larger radius and higher charge. I⁻ is more polarisable than F⁻.

For NaCl the two values agree closely (nearly fully ionic). For AgI or MgI₂ the experimental value is noticeably more exothermic.

**Solution cycles.** Dissolving an ionic solid can be thought of as breaking up the lattice into gaseous ions then hydrating them:

ΔsolH = ΔLEH(dissociation) + ΣΔhydH

or, with lattice enthalpy of formation, ΔsolH = −ΔLEH(formation) + ΣΔhydH.

**Hydration enthalpy** is more exothermic for smaller and more highly charged ions, because they attract the polar water molecules more strongly (water's δ− oxygen faces cations; δ+ hydrogens face anions).

**Solubility (OCR B, Eduqas).** Whether a salt dissolves depends on the balance of lattice and hydration enthalpies, and on entropy (dissolving usually increases entropy). A small or negative ΔsolH favours dissolving. The solubility trend of Group 2 hydroxides and sulfates (file 13) comes from how lattice and hydration enthalpies change with ion size.

#### Worked example

1. Find the lattice enthalpy of formation of potassium chloride. Data (kJ mol⁻¹): ΔfH(KCl) −437, ΔatH(K) +89, ΔatH(Cl) +122, IE₁(K) +419, EA₁(Cl) −349.

   ΔLEH = −437 − (89 + 122 + 419 − 349) = −437 − 281 = −718 kJ mol⁻¹.

2. Find ΔsolH of MgCl₂. Lattice enthalpy of formation −2526; ΔhydH: Mg²⁺ −1921, Cl⁻ −364 (kJ mol⁻¹).

   ΔsolH = +2526 + (−1921 + 2(−364)) = +2526 − 2649 = −123 kJ mol⁻¹.

3. For MgO, the cycle needs two electron affinities of oxygen: O(g) + e⁻ → O⁻(g) (−141) and O⁻(g) + e⁻ → O²⁻(g) (+798), and two ionisation energies of magnesium. The second electron affinity is positive because an electron is being added to an ion that is already negative.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting that atomisation of Cl₂ gives one Cl per ½Cl₂. Use the enthalpy for one mole of Cl atoms; for MgCl₂, double it.
- Using the bond enthalpy of Cl₂ (242) instead of ΔatH (121) without halving.
- Mixing up lattice formation and dissociation signs.
- Saying a more exothermic experimental lattice enthalpy means "more ionic". It means some covalent character.

### 3.1.8.2 Gibbs free-energy change and entropy change

*Refs: AQA 3.1.8.2 · Edexcel 13.12 to 13.22 · OCR A 5.2.2(a) to (f) · OCR B O(d) to (h) · Eduqas PI4.2(a) to (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Entropy, S, is a measure of disorder: the number of ways particles and their energy can be arranged. Units J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹.
- Entropy increases from solid to liquid to gas, with more moles of gas, with dissolving, and with temperature.
- ΔS = ΣS(products) − ΣS(reactants).
- ΔG = ΔH − TΔS. A reaction is feasible when ΔG ≤ 0.
- Find the temperature at which a reaction becomes feasible.
- Edexcel: ΔS(total) = ΔS(system) + ΔS(surroundings); ΔS(surroundings) = −ΔH ÷ T; ΔG = −RT ln K.
- Feasible reactions may not happen because of a high activation energy (kinetic stability).
- OCR B: solubility product, Ksp.

#### Explained

**Why entropy.** Some endothermic reactions happen on their own (ice melting above 0 °C, ammonium nitrate dissolving), so ΔH alone cannot decide feasibility. Systems tend towards more disordered states because there are more ways to arrange them.

All substances above 0 K have positive entropy. A perfect crystal at 0 K has zero entropy.

**Predicting the sign of ΔS.** Look for a change of state or a change in gas moles. CaCO₃(s) → CaO(s) + CO₂(g): a gas forms, so ΔS is positive.

**Gibbs free energy.** ΔG = ΔH − TΔS, with ΔH in kJ mol⁻¹, so convert ΔS to kJ K⁻¹ mol⁻¹ (divide by 1000) and use T in K.

| ΔH | ΔS | Feasible? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| − | + | Always |
| + | − | Never |
| − | − | At low temperature (below T = ΔH/ΔS) |
| + | + | At high temperature (above T = ΔH/ΔS) |

The temperature where feasibility changes is when ΔG = 0, so T = ΔH ÷ ΔS.

A plot of ΔG against T is a straight line with gradient −ΔS and intercept ΔH.

**Total entropy (Edexcel).** ΔS(total) = ΔS(system) + ΔS(surroundings). An exothermic reaction releases heat, which increases the entropy of the surroundings: ΔS(surroundings) = −ΔH ÷ T. A reaction is feasible when ΔS(total) is positive. Since ΔG = −TΔS(total), the two approaches agree.

**ΔG and K (Edexcel).** ΔG = −RT ln K. A negative ΔG means K > 1 (products favoured); a very negative ΔG means the reaction goes almost to completion. A positive ΔG means K < 1.

**Limits.** ΔG says whether a reaction can happen, not how fast. Diamond turning into graphite has negative ΔG but is extremely slow because of a large activation energy. ΔH and ΔS also change slightly with temperature, so calculated feasibility temperatures are approximate. The calculation also assumes standard conditions.

**Solubility product (OCR B O(h)).** For a sparingly soluble salt in a saturated solution, AgCl(s) ⇌ Ag⁺(aq) + Cl⁻(aq), Ksp = [Ag⁺][Cl⁻]. For CaF₂, Ksp = [Ca²⁺][F⁻]². If the product of the ion concentrations in a mixture exceeds Ksp, a precipitate forms. Adding a common ion (for example Cl⁻ to a saturated AgCl solution) lowers the solubility.

#### Worked example

1. For CaCO₃(s) → CaO(s) + CO₂(g), ΔH = +178 kJ mol⁻¹. Entropies (J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹): CaCO₃ 93, CaO 40, CO₂ 214. Find ΔS, ΔG at 298 K, and the minimum temperature for decomposition.

   ΔS = (40 + 214) − 93 = +161 J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹ = 0.161 kJ K⁻¹ mol⁻¹.

   ΔG(298) = 178 − 298 × 0.161 = 178 − 48.0 = +130 kJ mol⁻¹, not feasible.

   T = 178 ÷ 0.161 = 1106 K.

2. (Edexcel) Find ΔS(surroundings) at 298 K for a reaction with ΔH = −92 kJ mol⁻¹.

   ΔS(surr) = −(−92 000) ÷ 298 = +309 J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹.

3. (Edexcel) ΔG for a reaction at 298 K is −20.0 kJ mol⁻¹. Find K.

   ln K = −ΔG ÷ RT = 20 000 ÷ (8.31 × 298) = 8.08. K = e⁸·⁰⁸ = 3.2 × 10³.

4. (OCR B) Ksp of BaSO₄ is 1.1 × 10⁻¹⁰ mol² dm⁻⁶. Find its solubility in mol dm⁻³.

   [Ba²⁺] = [SO₄²⁻] = s. s² = 1.1 × 10⁻¹⁰, s = 1.0 × 10⁻⁵ mol dm⁻³.

#### Common mistakes

- Not converting ΔS from J to kJ.
- Using °C for T.
- Claiming a reaction with negative ΔG must happen quickly.
- Forgetting that elements have non-zero standard entropies (unlike ΔfH).

### Check questions

1. Define first electron affinity and explain why the second electron affinity of oxygen is endothermic. (3 marks)
2. Calculate the lattice enthalpy of formation of sodium oxide, Na₂O. Data (kJ mol⁻¹): ΔfH(Na₂O) −414, ΔatH(Na) +107, ΔatH(O) +249, IE₁(Na) +496, EA₁(O) −141, EA₂(O) +798. (3 marks)
3. Explain why the lattice enthalpy of formation of MgO is much more exothermic than that of NaF. (2 marks)
4. The experimental lattice enthalpy of silver iodide is more exothermic than the value from the perfect ionic model. Explain. (3 marks)
5. Explain why the hydration enthalpy of Mg²⁺ is more exothermic than that of Na⁺. (2 marks)
6. Predict, with a reason, the sign of ΔS for N₂(g) + 3H₂(g) → 2NH₃(g). (1 mark)
7. For N₂(g) + 3H₂(g) → 2NH₃(g), ΔH = −92 kJ mol⁻¹ and ΔS = −199 J K⁻¹ mol⁻¹. Find the temperature above which the reaction is not feasible. (2 marks)
8. Explain why a reaction with a negative ΔG may not take place at room temperature. (2 marks)
9. Ammonium nitrate dissolves in water endothermically but spontaneously. Explain in terms of entropy. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Enthalpy change when one electron is added to each atom in one mole of gaseous atoms (1) to form one mole of gaseous 1− ions (1). Second: electron added to a negative ion, so repulsion must be overcome (1).
2. ΔLEH = −414 − [2(107) + 249 + 2(496) − 141 + 798] (1) = −414 − 2112 (1) = −2526 kJ mol⁻¹ (1).
3. Mg²⁺ and O²⁻ have larger charges than Na⁺ and F⁻ (1); stronger attraction between ions (and similar or smaller ions) (1).
4. Ag⁺ polarises the large I⁻ ion / I⁻ is easily polarised (1); electron density distorted towards Ag⁺ (1); so some covalent character / extra attraction not in the ionic model (1).
5. Mg²⁺ is smaller and has a higher charge (higher charge density) (1); attracts δ− oxygen of water more strongly (1).
6. Negative: 4 mol of gas form 2 mol of gas (fewer gas moles / less disorder) (1).
7. T = ΔH/ΔS = −92 ÷ −0.199 (1) = 462 K (1).
8. High activation energy (1); so reaction very slow / kinetically stable (1).
9. ΔS(system) is large and positive: ordered lattice becomes free aqueous ions (1); TΔS is greater than ΔH, so ΔG is negative (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/11-acids-bases-and-buffers.md -->

## Acids, bases, pH, Ka, titration curves and buffers

This file treats acids and bases as proton transfer equilibria. It covers Brønsted–Lowry acids and bases, pH and its calculation for strong acids, strong bases and weak acids, Kw, Ka and pKa, pH curves and indicators, and buffers. It includes AQA required practical 9.

### Overview

A-level for most boards (Eduqas has pH at AS). You need equilibrium constants from file 6 and titration skills from file 2.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.12.1 to 3.1.12.6 and RP9.
- Edexcel 12.1 to 12.24 and CP9 (finding Ka for a weak acid). Edexcel adds: enthalpy of neutralisation of weak acids (12.23), the carbonic acid–hydrogencarbonate buffer in blood (12.24), and finding Ka from the half-neutralisation point (12.22).
- OCR A 5.1.3(a) to (o): includes the limits of the weak-acid approximations (5.1.3(h)), making a buffer from excess weak acid and strong alkali (5.1.3(j)(ii)), and blood pH (5.1.3(m)).
- OCR B O(i) to (m) in the Oceans storyline: acidity of oceans and buffering are the context.
- Eduqas C2.1(e), (g) (AS) and PI5.2(a) to (i), with salt hydrolysis (PI5.2(h)) and buffers in living systems and industry (PI5.2(g)). Specified practical: a titration using a pH probe.

### 3.1.12.1 Brønsted–Lowry acid–base equilibria in aqueous solution

*Refs: AQA 3.1.12.1 · Edexcel 12.1 to 12.3, 12.7 · OCR A 5.1.3(a), (b) · OCR B O(i), O(j) · Eduqas PI5.2(a), C2.1(g). Level: A-level only (Eduqas C2.1(g) AS).*

#### What you need to know

- An acid is a proton (H⁺) donor; a base is a proton acceptor.
- Acid–base equilibria involve the transfer of protons. Identify conjugate acid–base pairs.
- Strong acids dissociate fully in water; weak acids partly.

#### Explained

HCl + H₂O → H₃O⁺ + Cl⁻. HCl donates a proton to water.

In NH₃ + H₂O ⇌ NH₄⁺ + OH⁻, water is the acid and NH₃ the base. Water can act as either (amphoteric).

**Conjugate pairs** differ by one H⁺. In CH₃COOH + H₂O ⇌ CH₃COO⁻ + H₃O⁺:

- CH₃COOH (acid 1) and CH₃COO⁻ (base 1)
- H₂O (base 2) and H₃O⁺ (acid 2)

In aqueous solution H⁺(aq) and H₃O⁺(aq) mean the same thing.

**Strong and weak.** A strong acid (HCl, HNO₃, H₂SO₄ first proton) is fully dissociated; its equilibrium lies completely to the right. A weak acid (carboxylic acids, carbonic acid) only partly dissociates. Strong and weak describe the extent of dissociation; concentrated and dilute describe the amount of acid per dm³.

**Reactions of acids (OCR A 5.1.3(b)).** In all acid reactions with metals, carbonates, bases and alkalis, the H⁺ ion is what reacts: 2H⁺ + CO₃²⁻ → H₂O + CO₂; H⁺ + OH⁻ → H₂O; 2H⁺ + Mg → Mg²⁺ + H₂.

#### Common mistakes

- Pairing an acid with a species that does not differ by exactly one H⁺.
- Using "strong" to mean "concentrated".

### 3.1.12.2 Definition and determination of pH

*Refs: AQA 3.1.12.2 · Edexcel 12.4 to 12.6, 12.8 · OCR A 5.1.3(d), (f) · OCR B O(l) · Eduqas C2.1(e), PI5.2(d). Level: A-level only (Eduqas C2.1(e) AS).*

#### What you need to know

- pH = −log₁₀[H⁺]; [H⁺] = 10⁻ᵖᴴ.
- Calculate pH of strong monoprotic acids.
- A tenfold change in [H⁺] changes pH by 1.

#### Explained

For a strong monoprotic acid, [H⁺] = [acid]. 0.010 mol dm⁻³ HCl has pH = −log(0.010) = 2.00.

For a strong diprotic acid assumed fully dissociated, [H⁺] = 2 × [acid] (for example sulfuric acid in simple questions).

pH values are usually given to 2 decimal places.

#### Worked example

1. pH of 0.25 mol dm⁻³ HNO₃: −log(0.25) = 0.60.
2. [H⁺] in a solution of pH 3.40: 10⁻³·⁴⁰ = 3.98 × 10⁻⁴ mol dm⁻³.
3. 25.0 cm³ of 0.100 mol dm⁻³ HCl is diluted to 500 cm³. New pH: [H⁺] = 0.100 × 25.0/500 = 5.00 × 10⁻³; pH = 2.30.

#### Common mistakes

- Using ln instead of log₁₀.
- Forgetting to account for dilution or volume change.

### 3.1.12.3 The ionic product of water, Kw

*Refs: AQA 3.1.12.3 · Edexcel 12.11 to 12.13 · OCR A 5.1.3(e), (f) · OCR B O(l) · Eduqas PI5.2(c), (d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Water dissociates slightly: H₂O ⇌ H⁺ + OH⁻.
- Kw = [H⁺][OH⁻] = 1.00 × 10⁻¹⁴ mol² dm⁻⁶ at 298 K.
- In pure water [H⁺] = [OH⁻] = √Kw.
- Calculate pH of strong bases using Kw.
- Kw changes with temperature. pKw = −log Kw.

#### Explained

Kw is derived from Kc: Kc = [H⁺][OH⁻] ÷ [H₂O], and [H₂O] is so large it is effectively constant, so it is absorbed into Kw.

Dissociation of water is endothermic, so Kw increases with temperature. At 50 °C, Kw ≈ 5.5 × 10⁻¹⁴, so pure water has pH ≈ 6.63. It is still neutral because [H⁺] = [OH⁻].

**Strong bases.** 0.050 mol dm⁻³ NaOH: [OH⁻] = 0.050. [H⁺] = 1.00 × 10⁻¹⁴ ÷ 0.050 = 2.0 × 10⁻¹³. pH = 12.70. For Ba(OH)₂ or Ca(OH)₂, [OH⁻] = 2 × concentration.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying water at 50 °C is acidic because its pH is below 7.
- Forgetting to double [OH⁻] for Group 2 hydroxides.

### 3.1.12.4 Weak acids and bases, Ka

*Refs: AQA 3.1.12.4 · Edexcel 12.9, 12.10, 12.13 to 12.15, CP9 · OCR A 5.1.3(c), (g), (h) · OCR B O(k), O(l) · Eduqas PI5.2(b), (d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- For HA ⇌ H⁺ + A⁻, Ka = [H⁺][A⁻] ÷ [HA]. Units mol dm⁻³.
- pKa = −log Ka; Ka = 10⁻ᵖᴷᵃ. A smaller pKa means a stronger acid.
- Calculate pH of a weak acid from Ka and concentration, and Ka from pH.
- The two approximations and their limits.

#### Explained

For a weak acid alone in water, assume:

1. [H⁺] = [A⁻] (dissociation of water is negligible).
2. [HA] at equilibrium ≈ initial concentration (so little dissociates).

Then Ka = [H⁺]² ÷ [HA], so [H⁺] = √(Ka × [HA]).

**Limits (OCR A 5.1.3(h)).** Approximation 2 breaks down for "stronger" weak acids (Ka above about 10⁻²) or very dilute solutions, where a significant fraction dissociates. Approximation 1 breaks down for very weak or very dilute acids, where H⁺ from water matters.

**Weak bases** such as ammonia only partly accept protons; their pH is calculated with Kb at some boards, but AQA, OCR and Edexcel only require qualitative treatment.

#### Worked example

1. pH of 0.100 mol dm⁻³ ethanoic acid, Ka = 1.74 × 10⁻⁵ mol dm⁻³.

   [H⁺] = √(1.74 × 10⁻⁵ × 0.100) = √(1.74 × 10⁻⁶) = 1.32 × 10⁻³. pH = 2.88.

2. A 0.0500 mol dm⁻³ solution of a weak acid has pH 3.10. Find Ka and pKa.

   [H⁺] = 7.94 × 10⁻⁴. Ka = (7.94 × 10⁻⁴)² ÷ 0.0500 = 1.26 × 10⁻⁵ mol dm⁻³. pKa = 4.90.

#### Practical

**Edexcel CP9: finding Ka for a weak acid.** Method 1: measure the pH of a solution of known concentration with a calibrated pH meter and use Ka = [H⁺]² ÷ [HA]. Method 2: titrate a weak acid with NaOH to find the end-point volume, then add half that volume of NaOH to a fresh sample of acid; at half-neutralisation [HA] = [A⁻], so pH = pKa. Calibrate the pH meter with buffer solutions of known pH (for example pH 4, 7 and 10) first, and rinse the probe with distilled water between readings.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the approximations in a buffer calculation (they only work for the weak acid alone).
- Forgetting to square-root.

### 3.1.12.5 pH curves, titrations and indicators

*Refs: AQA 3.1.12.5, RP9 · Edexcel 12.16, 12.17, 12.22, 12.23 · OCR A 5.1.3(n), (o) · Eduqas PI5.2(e), (h), (i). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The shapes of pH curves for strong acid–strong base, strong acid–weak base, weak acid–strong base and weak acid–weak base titrations.
- Equivalence point and the vertical section.
- Choose an indicator whose colour change (pH range) lies within the vertical section.
- For a weak acid–strong base titration, pH at half-neutralisation = pKa.
- Edexcel: why weak acids have less exothermic enthalpies of neutralisation.
- Eduqas: salt hydrolysis gives non-neutral solutions at the equivalence point.

#### Explained

Typical curves (acid in the flask, base added):

| Titration | Start pH | Equivalence point pH | Vertical section | Indicator |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Strong acid–strong base | ~1 | 7 | About 3 to 11 | Either |
| Weak acid–strong base | ~3 | ~9 | About 7 to 11 | Phenolphthalein (8.3 to 10) |
| Strong acid–weak base | ~1 | ~5 | About 3 to 7 | Methyl orange (3.1 to 4.4) |
| Weak acid–weak base | ~3 | ~7 | No sharp vertical section | None suitable; use a pH meter |

For a weak acid–strong base curve, there is a buffer region at the start where pH rises slowly. Half-way to the equivalence point, [HA] = [A⁻], so Ka = [H⁺] and pH = pKa.

**Indicators** are weak acids whose HIn and In⁻ forms differ in colour: HIn ⇌ H⁺ + In⁻. The colour changes over about 2 pH units around the pKa of the indicator. The end point (colour change) should match the equivalence point.

**Salt hydrolysis (Eduqas).** At the equivalence point of a weak acid–strong base titration the solution contains A⁻ ions, which accept protons from water: A⁻ + H₂O ⇌ HA + OH⁻. So sodium ethanoate solution is alkaline. Ammonium chloride solution is acidic (NH₄⁺ ⇌ NH₃ + H⁺). Sodium chloride solution is neutral.

**Enthalpy of neutralisation (Edexcel 12.23).** Strong acid with strong base: about −57 kJ mol⁻¹, the same for all because the reaction is always H⁺ + OH⁻ → H₂O. A weak acid gives a less exothermic value, because some energy is used to dissociate the acid as it reacts.

#### Practical

**AQA RP9: investigate how pH changes when a weak acid reacts with a strong base and when a strong acid reacts with a weak base.** Calibrate the pH meter. Pipette 25.0 cm³ of acid into a beaker with a magnetic stirrer. Add base from a burette in 1 cm³ portions, reading pH after each, and in 0.1 cm³ portions near the equivalence point. Plot pH against volume. Find the equivalence point (middle of the vertical section) and, for the weak acid, read pKa at half the equivalence volume.

**Eduqas: titration using a pH probe**, for example a weak acid against a weak base, shows the small change at equivalence.

#### Common mistakes

- Choosing methyl orange for a weak acid–strong base titration.
- Saying the equivalence point is always pH 7.
- Reading pKa at the equivalence point instead of half-way.

### 3.1.12.6 Buffer action

*Refs: AQA 3.1.12.6 · Edexcel 12.18 to 12.21, 12.24 · OCR A 5.1.3(i) to (m) · OCR B O(m) · Eduqas PI5.2(f), (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A buffer resists changes in pH when small amounts of acid or base are added, or on dilution.
- Acidic buffers: a weak acid and one of its salts (or excess weak acid partly neutralised by a strong alkali). Basic buffers: a weak base and its salt (ammonia and ammonium chloride).
- How buffers work, in terms of the equilibrium and the large reservoirs of HA and A⁻.
- Calculate the pH of acidic buffers, including after adding small amounts of acid or base.
- Uses: blood (carbonic acid–hydrogencarbonate), shampoos, biological washing powders, food, fermentation.

#### Explained

**Ethanoic acid / sodium ethanoate buffer.** CH₃COOH ⇌ CH₃COO⁻ + H⁺. The sodium ethanoate supplies lots of CH₃COO⁻; the acid supplies lots of CH₃COOH.

- Add a little acid (H⁺): it reacts with CH₃COO⁻ to make CH₃COOH. The equilibrium shifts left; [H⁺] barely changes.
- Add a little alkali (OH⁻): it reacts with H⁺ (H⁺ + OH⁻ → H₂O); more CH₃COOH dissociates to replace the H⁺. Also CH₃COOH + OH⁻ → CH₃COO⁻ + H₂O directly. [H⁺] barely changes.

Because both [HA] and [A⁻] are large, their ratio changes little, so pH changes little.

**Basic buffer.** NH₃ + H⁺ ⇌ NH₄⁺. Added acid is taken up by NH₃; added alkali reacts with NH₄⁺: NH₄⁺ + OH⁻ → NH₃ + H₂O.

**Calculating buffer pH.** [H⁺] = Ka × [HA] ÷ [A⁻]. Do not use the weak-acid approximations; use the actual amounts of acid and salt. Because both are in the same volume, mole ratios can be used. (Equivalent form: pH = pKa + log([A⁻] ÷ [HA]).)

**Blood (Edexcel 12.24, OCR A 5.1.3(m), Eduqas).** Blood pH is kept at 7.35 to 7.45 mainly by H₂CO₃ ⇌ H⁺ + HCO₃⁻. Extra acid reacts with HCO₃⁻ to form H₂CO₃; the H₂CO₃ is converted to CO₂ and water and the CO₂ is breathed out. Extra alkali reacts with H₂CO₃. Proteins and phosphate ions also buffer.

#### Worked example

1. A buffer contains 0.200 mol dm⁻³ ethanoic acid and 0.150 mol dm⁻³ sodium ethanoate. Ka = 1.74 × 10⁻⁵. Find the pH.

   [H⁺] = 1.74 × 10⁻⁵ × 0.200 ÷ 0.150 = 2.32 × 10⁻⁵. pH = 4.63.

2. To 1.00 dm³ of that buffer, 0.0100 mol of HCl is added. Find the new pH.

   H⁺ reacts with A⁻: A⁻ = 0.150 − 0.010 = 0.140 mol; HA = 0.200 + 0.010 = 0.210 mol. [H⁺] = 1.74 × 10⁻⁵ × 0.210 ÷ 0.140 = 2.61 × 10⁻⁵. pH = 4.58. A change of only 0.05.

3. 50.0 cm³ of 0.200 mol dm⁻³ ethanoic acid is mixed with 25.0 cm³ of 0.100 mol dm⁻³ NaOH. Find the pH.

   HA = 0.0100 mol; OH⁻ = 0.00250 mol. After reaction: HA = 0.00750 mol, A⁻ = 0.00250 mol. [H⁺] = 1.74 × 10⁻⁵ × 0.00750 ÷ 0.00250 = 5.22 × 10⁻⁵. pH = 4.28.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a buffer keeps the pH constant. It resists change; large additions overwhelm it.
- Using [H⁺] = √(Ka[HA]) for a buffer.
- Forgetting to subtract the reacted amounts before using the buffer equation.

### Check questions

1. Identify the two conjugate acid–base pairs in HNO₃ + H₂SO₄ ⇌ H₂NO₃⁺ + HSO₄⁻. (2 marks)
2. Calculate the pH of 0.0350 mol dm⁻³ Ba(OH)₂ at 298 K. (2 marks)
3. At 40 °C, Kw = 2.92 × 10⁻¹⁴ mol² dm⁻⁶. Calculate the pH of pure water and explain why it is neutral. (3 marks)
4. Calculate the pH of 0.150 mol dm⁻³ propanoic acid (Ka = 1.35 × 10⁻⁵ mol dm⁻³). (2 marks)
5. Sketch the pH curve when 0.100 mol dm⁻³ NaOH is added to 25.0 cm³ of 0.100 mol dm⁻³ ethanoic acid, and label the equivalence point and the half-equivalence point. (3 marks)
6. Explain why phenolphthalein is suitable for the titration in question 5 but methyl orange is not. (2 marks)
7. Explain how an ammonia–ammonium chloride buffer resists a change in pH when a small amount of acid is added. (2 marks)
8. A buffer is made by adding 0.0300 mol of sodium methanoate to 500 cm³ of 0.100 mol dm⁻³ methanoic acid (Ka = 1.78 × 10⁻⁴). Calculate its pH. (3 marks)
9. Explain why the enthalpy of neutralisation of ethanoic acid with NaOH is less exothermic than that of HCl with NaOH. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. HNO₃ (base) and H₂NO₃⁺ (its conjugate acid) (1); H₂SO₄ (acid) and HSO₄⁻ (its conjugate base) (1). Here HNO₃ acts as a base because it accepts a proton.
2. [OH⁻] = 0.0700 (1); [H⁺] = 1.43 × 10⁻¹³, pH = 12.85 (1).
3. [H⁺] = √(2.92 × 10⁻¹⁴) = 1.71 × 10⁻⁷ (1); pH = 6.77 (1); [H⁺] = [OH⁻] (1).
4. [H⁺] = √(1.35 × 10⁻⁵ × 0.150) = 1.42 × 10⁻³ (1); pH = 2.85 (1).
5. Starts about pH 3, gradual rise (buffer region) (1); vertical section about pH 7 to 11 at 25.0 cm³ (1); half-equivalence at 12.5 cm³ labelled (pH = pKa, about 4.8) (1).
6. Vertical section is in pH range about 7 to 11 (1); phenolphthalein changes in this range (8.3 to 10); methyl orange changes at 3.1 to 4.4, outside it (1).
7. H⁺ reacts with NH₃ to form NH₄⁺ (1); large reservoir of NH₃, so [H⁺] / ratio barely changes (1).
8. n(HCOOH) = 0.0500 mol (1); [H⁺] = 1.78 × 10⁻⁴ × 0.0500 ÷ 0.0300 = 2.97 × 10⁻⁴ (1); pH = 3.53 (1).
9. Ethanoic acid is weak / partly dissociated (1); energy needed to dissociate it as it reacts (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/10-electrode-potentials-and-cells.md -->

## Electrode potentials and electrochemical cells

An electrochemical cell separates the two halves of a redox reaction so the electrons flow through a wire. This file covers half-cells, the standard hydrogen electrode, standard electrode potentials, cell diagrams, calculating cell EMF, predicting feasibility, and commercial cells including fuel cells. It includes AQA required practical 8.

### Overview

A-level only. You need half-equations from file 7. Redox titrations and the redox chemistry of transition metals use these ideas in file 15.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.11.1, 3.1.11.2 and RP8.
- Edexcel 14.1 to 14.17 and CP10. Edexcel adds: Eₒ cell is proportional to ΔS(total) and ln K (14.11); limits of predictions from E⦵ (14.12); disproportionation and E⦵ (14.14); the hydrogen–oxygen fuel cell in acid and alkali (14.17); the importance of conditions (14.9).
- OCR A 5.2.3(f) to (j), with storage and fuel cells (5.2.3(j)).
- OCR B DM(d) to (f) in Developing metals: simple cells, electron flow, and standard electrode potentials.
- Eduqas PI1.1(c) to (f), including the benefits and risks of the hydrogen fuel cell (PI1.1(f)), and the specified practical: building cells and measuring Ecell.

### 3.1.11.1 Electrode potentials and cells

*Refs: AQA 3.1.11.1, RP8 · Edexcel 14.1 to 14.14, CP10 · OCR A 5.2.3(f) to (i) · OCR B DM(d) to (f) · Eduqas PI1.1(c) to (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- IUPAC cell diagrams.
- The standard hydrogen electrode (SHE) and why it is the reference.
- Standard electrode potential, E⦵: the EMF of a half-cell connected to the SHE under standard conditions.
- Standard conditions: 298 K, 100 kPa, 1.00 mol dm⁻³ solutions.
- The electrochemical series; E⦵ values to predict the direction of reactions.
- E(cell) = E(right) − E(left), or E(reduced half) − E(oxidised half).
- Different types of half-cell: metal/metal ion, two ions of the same element (platinum electrode), gas/ion (platinum electrode).

#### Explained

**Half-cells.** A zinc rod in Zn²⁺(aq) sets up the equilibrium Zn²⁺ + 2e⁻ ⇌ Zn. Zinc tends to lose electrons, so the rod becomes negative. A copper rod in Cu²⁺ is less inclined to lose electrons. Join them by a wire and a salt bridge (filter paper soaked in saturated potassium nitrate) and electrons flow from zinc to copper.

The salt bridge completes the circuit by letting ions move between the solutions without mixing them. KNO₃ is used because its ions do not react with the half-cell ions (KCl would precipitate Ag⁺, for example).

**Standard hydrogen electrode.** H₂ gas at 100 kPa bubbled over a platinum electrode (coated with platinum black for a large surface) dipped in 1.00 mol dm⁻³ H⁺ (for example HCl), at 298 K. Its potential is defined as 0.00 V. Platinum is inert and conducts.

When there is no solid metal in the half-cell (Fe³⁺/Fe²⁺, or Cl₂/Cl⁻), use a platinum electrode. For Fe³⁺/Fe²⁺, both ions are 1.00 mol dm⁻³.

**Cell diagrams.** Write the oxidised half-cell on the left, with the reduced form at the electrode:

Zn(s) | Zn²⁺(aq) ‖ Cu²⁺(aq) | Cu(s)   E(cell) = +1.10 V

- A single line is a phase boundary; the double line is the salt bridge.
- With platinum: Pt(s) | H₂(g) | H⁺(aq) ‖ Fe³⁺(aq), Fe²⁺(aq) | Pt(s). Species in the same phase are separated by a comma.

**The electrochemical series.** Half-equations written as reductions, listed by E⦵:

| Half-equation | E⦵ / V |
| --- | --- |
| Li⁺ + e⁻ ⇌ Li | −3.04 |
| Zn²⁺ + 2e⁻ ⇌ Zn | −0.76 |
| Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻ ⇌ Fe | −0.44 |
| 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ ⇌ H₂ | 0.00 |
| Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ ⇌ Cu | +0.34 |
| I₂ + 2e⁻ ⇌ 2I⁻ | +0.54 |
| Fe³⁺ + e⁻ ⇌ Fe²⁺ | +0.77 |
| Ag⁺ + e⁻ ⇌ Ag | +0.80 |
| Br₂ + 2e⁻ ⇌ 2Br⁻ | +1.09 |
| Cr₂O₇²⁻ + 14H⁺ + 6e⁻ ⇌ 2Cr³⁺ + 7H₂O | +1.33 |
| Cl₂ + 2e⁻ ⇌ 2Cl⁻ | +1.36 |
| MnO₄⁻ + 8H⁺ + 5e⁻ ⇌ Mn²⁺ + 4H₂O | +1.51 |
| F₂ + 2e⁻ ⇌ 2F⁻ | +2.87 |

The more positive E⦵, the stronger the oxidising agent on the left (it is more easily reduced). The more negative E⦵, the stronger the reducing agent on the right.

**Predicting feasibility.** A reaction is feasible if E(cell) is positive, calculated as E(half being reduced) − E(half being oxidised). A quick rule: the species on the left of the more positive half-equation will oxidise the species on the right of the less positive one ("anticlockwise rule").

Example: will Cl₂ oxidise Fe²⁺? Cl₂/Cl⁻ +1.36 is more positive than Fe³⁺/Fe²⁺ +0.77. E(cell) = 1.36 − 0.77 = +0.59 V, feasible: Cl₂ + 2Fe²⁺ → 2Cl⁻ + 2Fe³⁺.

**Links to thermodynamics (Edexcel 14.11).** E(cell) is proportional to ΔS(total) and to ln K for the cell reaction: a large positive E(cell) means a large K, and a reaction that goes nearly to completion. (ΔG = −nFE(cell).)

**Limits of predictions (Edexcel 14.12, OCR A).** E⦵ shows whether a reaction is thermodynamically feasible, not how fast it goes. A feasible reaction may be too slow because of a high activation energy. Also, real conditions are often not standard: different concentrations or temperatures change the electrode potentials. If [Zn²⁺] is lowered, the equilibrium Zn²⁺ + 2e⁻ ⇌ Zn shifts left, so E becomes more negative.

**Disproportionation (Edexcel 14.14).** Cu⁺ disproportionates in solution because E⦵(Cu⁺/Cu) = +0.52 V is more positive than E⦵(Cu²⁺/Cu⁺) = +0.15 V: E(cell) = 0.52 − 0.15 = +0.37 V for 2Cu⁺ → Cu + Cu²⁺.

#### Worked example

1. Calculate E(cell) and write the overall equation for a cell made from Ag⁺/Ag (+0.80 V) and Zn²⁺/Zn (−0.76 V).

   Ag⁺ is reduced (more positive). E(cell) = 0.80 − (−0.76) = +1.56 V. Zn + 2Ag⁺ → Zn²⁺ + 2Ag.

2. Write the cell diagram for question 1.

   Zn(s) | Zn²⁺(aq) ‖ Ag⁺(aq) | Ag(s).

3. Will iodine oxidise Fe²⁺ ions?

   I₂/I⁻ (+0.54) is less positive than Fe³⁺/Fe²⁺ (+0.77). E(cell) = 0.54 − 0.77 = −0.23 V. Not feasible. The reverse happens: Fe³⁺ oxidises I⁻.

#### Common mistakes

- Doubling E⦵ when a half-equation is multiplied by 2. E⦵ does not depend on amount.
- Forgetting a platinum electrode for an ion/ion or gas/ion half-cell.
- Writing a salt bridge as a wire, or saying electrons flow through the salt bridge (ions do).
- Saying a positive E(cell) means the reaction is fast.

#### Practical

**AQA RP8: measuring the EMF of an electrochemical cell.** Clean metal strips with emery paper (removes oxide layer) and degrease with propanone. Put each in 1.00 mol dm⁻³ of its sulfate (or nitrate) solution in a beaker. Join with a salt bridge of filter paper soaked in saturated KNO₃. Connect the metals to a high-resistance voltmeter (so almost no current flows and the EMF is measured, not a lower voltage). Record the reading. A new salt bridge is needed for each cell. The measured value differs from the data value if solutions are not exactly 1 mol dm⁻³, the temperature is not 298 K, or the metal surface is not clean.

**Edexcel CP10; Eduqas specified practical.** The same method, building several cells and comparing measured and calculated values, including cells with a concentration change and with a non-metal half-cell (such as Fe³⁺/Fe²⁺ with platinum).

### 3.1.11.2 Commercial applications of electrochemical cells

*Refs: AQA 3.1.11.2 · Edexcel 14.15 to 14.17 · OCR A 5.2.3(j) · Eduqas PI1.1(f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Electrochemical cells are used as non-rechargeable cells, rechargeable cells and fuel cells.
- Rechargeable cells: the reactions can be reversed by applying a current.
- Given data, deduce the electrode reactions and the EMF.
- The hydrogen–oxygen fuel cell: electrode reactions in acid and in alkali; benefits and risks.

#### Explained

**Non-rechargeable** cells (for example zinc–carbon or alkaline batteries) run until a reactant is used up. The products cannot be turned back into reactants by charging.

**Rechargeable** cells, such as lithium-ion and lead–acid, have reactions that reverse when charged. A lithium-ion cell: during discharge, at the positive electrode Li⁺ + CoO₂ + e⁻ → Li⁺[CoO₂]⁻; at the negative electrode Li → Li⁺ + e⁻. EMF about 3.6 V. Charging reverses both.

**Fuel cells** use a continuous supply of fuel and oxygen; they do not need recharging. The fuel is not burned; the chemical energy becomes electrical energy directly, so they are more efficient than engines.

Hydrogen fuel cell, **alkaline** electrolyte (KOH):

- Negative electrode: H₂ + 2OH⁻ → 2H₂O + 2e⁻ (E⦵ of the reduction is −0.83 V)
- Positive electrode: O₂ + 2H₂O + 4e⁻ → 4OH⁻ (E⦵ +0.40 V)
- Overall: 2H₂ + O₂ → 2H₂O, E(cell) = 1.23 V.

**Acid** electrolyte (a polymer membrane that lets H⁺ through):

- Negative: H₂ → 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ (0.00 V)
- Positive: O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ → 2H₂O (+1.23 V)
- Overall the same, E(cell) = 1.23 V.

Fuel cells can also use methanol or ethanol: CH₃OH + H₂O → CO₂ + 6H⁺ + 6e⁻ at the negative electrode (Edexcel 14.16).

**Benefits and risks.**

| Benefits | Risks and drawbacks |
| --- | --- |
| Only product is water: no CO₂ or NOₓ at point of use | Hydrogen is mostly made from fossil fuels (methane), which releases CO₂, or by electrolysis using electricity that may come from fossil fuels |
| More efficient than combustion engines | Hydrogen is flammable and explosive; hard to store (high pressure or very low temperature) |
| Quiet, few moving parts | Fuel cells use expensive catalysts such as platinum and have a limited life |

Cells in general: useful, but used cells contain toxic or flammable materials (lithium, cadmium, lead) and must be recycled.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a fuel cell stores electricity. It converts chemical energy continuously while fuel is supplied.
- Saying hydrogen cars produce no CO₂ at all. The hydrogen may be made in ways that release CO₂.

### Check questions

1. Describe the standard hydrogen electrode. (4 marks)
2. State the purpose of the salt bridge and suggest why potassium nitrate is used. (2 marks)
3. Write the cell diagram for a cell made from Fe³⁺/Fe²⁺ (+0.77 V) and Cu²⁺/Cu (+0.34 V), and calculate E(cell). (3 marks)
4. Use E⦵ values from this file to predict whether bromine will oxidise iron(II) ions. Write the equation if it does. (3 marks)
5. Explain why the measured EMF of a Zn/Cu cell might differ from the calculated standard value. (2 marks)
6. Explain why a high-resistance voltmeter is used. (1 mark)
7. Write the electrode equations for a hydrogen–oxygen fuel cell with an alkaline electrolyte. (2 marks)
8. Give one advantage and one disadvantage of hydrogen fuel cells compared with petrol engines. (2 marks)
9. The concentration of Cu²⁺ in a Zn/Cu cell is increased. State and explain the effect on E(cell). (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Hydrogen gas at 100 kPa (1); bubbled over platinum electrode (1); in 1.00 mol dm⁻³ H⁺ / HCl (1); at 298 K, potential defined as 0.00 V (1).
2. Completes the circuit / allows ions to move between half-cells (1); KNO₃ ions do not react with the ions in the half-cells / no precipitates (1).
3. Cu(s) | Cu²⁺(aq) ‖ Fe³⁺(aq), Fe²⁺(aq) | Pt(s) (2: 1 for correct order, 1 for Pt and comma); E(cell) = 0.77 − 0.34 = +0.43 V (1).
4. Br₂/Br⁻ +1.09 V more positive than Fe³⁺/Fe²⁺ +0.77 V (1); E(cell) = +0.32 V, so feasible (1); Br₂ + 2Fe²⁺ → 2Br⁻ + 2Fe³⁺ (1).
5. Any two: solutions not 1.00 mol dm⁻³; temperature not 298 K; dirty / oxidised electrodes; resistance in circuit / current drawn.
6. So no (negligible) current flows, giving the true EMF (1).
7. H₂ + 2OH⁻ → 2H₂O + 2e⁻ (1); O₂ + 2H₂O + 4e⁻ → 4OH⁻ (1).
8. Advantage: only water produced / more efficient (1). Disadvantage: hydrogen hard to store / flammable / made from fossil fuels / expensive platinum (1).
9. E(cell) increases (1); Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ ⇌ Cu shifts right, so copper electrode potential more positive (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/chemistry/37-colour-dyes-and-fats.md -->

## Colour, dyes, diazonium coupling and fats and oils

Why are some organic compounds coloured, and how are dyes made and fixed to cloth? This file covers the origin of colour in organic molecules (chromophores and delocalisation), making diazonium salts and coupling them to form azo dyes, how dye structure controls colour, solubility and binding to fibres, and the structure of fats and oils.

### Overview

A-level, OCR B and Eduqas only. Colour in transition metal complexes is in file 15; benzene and phenol in file 25; amines in file 26; esters and soaps in file 24.

#### Board differences

- OCR B CD(a) to (c), (h), (m): dyes and fibres, chromophores and modifying groups, fats and oils as mixed esters of propane-1,2,3-triol, diazonium compounds and azo dyes, and the origins of colour and UV absorption.
- Eduqas OA3.1(e) to (h): primary amines with cold nitric(III) acid, coupling benzenediazonium salts with phenols and aromatic amines, the –N=N– chromophore, and colour from the wavelengths of visible light absorbed.

### CD Origin of colour in organic molecules

*Refs: OCR B CD(m) · Eduqas OA3.1(g), (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A coloured compound absorbs some wavelengths of visible light; we see the complementary colour (the light not absorbed).
- Absorption promotes electrons to higher energy levels: ΔE = hf.
- The chromophore is the part of the molecule responsible for absorption; it usually contains a delocalised electron system (C=C, C=O, N=N, benzene rings).
- The more extensive the delocalisation, the smaller the energy gap, so the lower the frequency (longer wavelength) of light absorbed.

#### Explained

Electrons in molecules occupy energy levels. Light whose photon energy matches a gap is absorbed and promotes an electron. In small molecules the gaps are large, so they absorb in the ultraviolet and look colourless (benzene absorbs only UV). As the delocalised system grows (more alternating double and single bonds, more rings joined through –N=N–), the gap between levels gets smaller, so lower-energy light is absorbed, moving from UV into the visible region. The compound then looks coloured.

**Complementary colours.** If a dye absorbs blue light (about 450 nm), it looks orange or yellow. If it absorbs green, it looks red or purple.

| Absorbed | Seen |
| --- | --- |
| Violet | Yellow-green |
| Blue | Orange / yellow |
| Green | Red / magenta |
| Yellow | Violet / blue |
| Red | Blue-green |

**The azo chromophore.** –N=N– linking two benzene rings gives an extended delocalised system across the whole molecule, which absorbs visible light. Azo dyes are typically yellow, orange or red.

**Modifying groups (OCR B CD(b)).** Groups attached to the chromophore change its colour and intensity: –OH, –NH₂ and –NR₂ have lone pairs that join the delocalised system, extending it and shifting absorption to longer wavelengths (deepening the colour). Changing pH can protonate or deprotonate such groups, which is why some dyes act as indicators (methyl orange is an azo dye).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a red dye absorbs red light.
- Saying more delocalisation means a bigger energy gap.

### OA3.1 Diazonium salts and azo dyes

*Refs: OCR B CD(h) · Eduqas OA3.1(e), (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Primary aromatic amines react with nitric(III) (nitrous) acid below 10 °C to form diazonium salts.
- Diazonium salts couple with phenols (in alkali) or aromatic amines to form azo dyes.
- Primary aliphatic amines with nitrous acid give nitrogen gas and alcohols (Eduqas).

#### Explained

**Making nitrous acid.** It is unstable, so it is made in situ from sodium nitrite (sodium nitrate(III)) and hydrochloric acid: NaNO₂ + HCl → HNO₂ + NaCl.

**Diazotisation.** Phenylamine + nitrous acid + HCl, kept below 10 °C in an ice bath:

C₆H₅NH₂ + HNO₂ + HCl → C₆H₅N₂⁺Cl⁻ + 2H₂O

The product is benzenediazonium chloride, containing –N≡N⁺. Below 10 °C because above that it decomposes, giving off nitrogen gas and forming phenol: C₆H₅N₂⁺ + H₂O → C₆H₅OH + N₂ + H⁺. (Dry diazonium salts can explode, so they are used in solution straight away.)

**Aliphatic amines (Eduqas OA3.1(e)).** Their diazonium ions are too unstable even when cold: nitrogen gas is given off immediately, and an alcohol forms. CH₃CH₂NH₂ + HNO₂ → CH₃CH₂OH + N₂ + H₂O. The steady effervescence of N₂ distinguishes primary aliphatic from aromatic amines.

**Coupling.** The diazonium ion is a weak electrophile. It attacks a very electron-rich ring (a phenol in alkaline solution, as the phenoxide ion, or an aromatic amine), substituting at the 4-position (or 2 if 4 is blocked):

C₆H₅N₂⁺ + C₆H₅O⁻ → C₆H₅–N=N–C₆H₄–OH (4-hydroxyazobenzene, a yellow-orange dye)

With naphthalen-2-ol in alkali, a bright red dye forms. With phenylamine, a yellow dye (4-aminoazobenzene) forms.

**Practical (OCR B).** Dissolve phenylamine in hydrochloric acid, cool in ice, add cold sodium nitrite solution dropwise, keeping below 10 °C. Add this to a cold alkaline solution of a phenol: a coloured precipitate forms at once. Filter and use to dye cloth. Phenylamine is toxic; wear gloves.

#### Common mistakes

- Doing diazotisation at room temperature.
- Writing nitric(V) acid (HNO₃) instead of nitric(III) acid (HNO₂).
- Coupling with benzene, which is not reactive enough.

### CD Dyes and fibres

*Refs: OCR B CD(a), CD(b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How dyes attach to fibres: intermolecular forces (hydrogen bonds, London forces), ionic bonds and covalent bonds.
- The parts of a dye: the chromophore; groups that modify it; groups that affect solubility; groups that let it bond to fibres. (Structures are given in questions.)

#### Explained

**Groups in a dye molecule.**

| Purpose | Groups |
| --- | --- |
| Chromophore (colour) | Extended delocalised system: –N=N– between rings, C=C, C=O |
| Modify colour | –OH, –NH₂, –NR₂ (extend delocalisation) |
| Make soluble in water (so it can be applied) | –SO₃⁻ Na⁺ (sulfonate), –COO⁻ Na⁺, –OH |
| Bond to the fibre | Depends on the fibre (below) |

**Fibres and how dyes attach.**

| Fibre | Structure | How dyes attach |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cotton (cellulose) | Many –OH groups | Hydrogen bonds; reactive dyes form covalent bonds with –OH (very colourfast) |
| Wool, silk, nylon (proteins and polyamides) | –NH₃⁺ and –COO⁻ groups, amide links | Ionic bonds between –SO₃⁻ on the dye and –NH₃⁺ on the fibre (acid dyes); hydrogen bonds |
| Polyester | Few polar groups | Small non-polar ("disperse") dyes held by London forces and permanent dipole forces |

The stronger the bond between dye and fibre, the less the colour washes out (colourfastness): covalent > ionic > hydrogen bonds > London forces.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a dye attaches to all fibres in the same way.
- Saying the sulfonate group causes colour. It mainly makes the dye soluble (and can form ionic bonds).

### CD Fats and oils

*Refs: OCR B CD(c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Fats and oils are mainly mixed triesters of propane-1,2,3-triol (glycerol) with long-chain fatty acids, with different degrees of unsaturation.

#### Explained

Each triglyceride has a glycerol backbone joined by three ester links to three fatty acid chains, which can differ (mixed esters). Fatty acids are saturated (no C=C, such as stearic acid, C₁₇H₃₅COOH), mono-unsaturated (one C=C) or polyunsaturated (several C=C).

- Saturated chains are straight and pack closely, with more London forces, so saturated fats (animal fats) are solid at room temperature.
